## 📚 Paper List

- **[What Can a VLA Learn from the Agent That Calls It? Task-Level Experience Distillation for Embodied Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16246)**  `arXiv:2611.16246`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Jinze Tao, Yuxiao Han, Kangkang Dong, Yancong Wei, Xiaojun Zhu, Bokui Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) models drive the robot directly from camera views, yet a VLA alone shows three typical failures. It walks past the goal, stops before reaching it, and rarely recovers after going off track. Pairing a VLA with a large language model (LLM) planner fixes these failures, but queries the LLM throughout every episode, adding token costs and latency. We introduce Harness-to-Executor (H2E), a session-level distillation method that pairs an LLM planner and a frozen VLA as a heterogeneous training agent, keeps only sessions that reach the goal, and then trains a VLA alone on them so no LLM is used at test time. In these sessions, the planner gives sub-instructions and moves the robot with its own tools, detours are common, and the planner decides when to stop. H2E therefore labels every motion as a VLA action for the original task, whoever made it. It masks the loss on steps moving away from the goal and assigns end labels to states near it, so the VLA learns where the task ends. H2E sets a new state of the art on REVERIE-CE and NavRAG-CE. It raises an 8B VLA's success rate by 15.6% and 11.1% respectively, surpassing methods trained on 10M samples. The distilled VLA exceeds its agent on unseen episodes with zero LLM tokens and runs 3.7 times faster.
  </details>

- **[Robotic Reward Models in the Foundation-Model Era](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20741)**  `arXiv:2611.20741`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Guoqiang Liang, Yiqi Lin, Shangchen Zhou, Wenzheng Zeng, Yanzhe Chen, Zechen Bai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robotic reward models (RRMs) use foundation models to replace per-task reward engineering with reusable, task-conditioned evaluation. Yet under one name, their outputs answer different questions, from whether a task succeeded to which execution is better. This survey organizes 114 methods into five semantic families by the question their native outputs answer: Outcome, Absolute Progress, Relative, Temporal Value, and Hybrid. Within this taxonomy, we review how model design determines what an evaluator can judge, how raw experience becomes supervision, and how existing evaluation practice tests the result. The review exposes a gap: agreement with demonstration order and downstream policy gains do not show whether a reward tracks task fulfillment. We therefore introduce RRM-Bench-1K, 1,117 real-robot videos of closed-loop executions with task-state and camera-evidence labels, and four contrast suites that separate the credited condition from its usual cues. A unified study of 27 evaluators shows that the strongest final scores rank whole executions well enough for selection and filtering. Dense credit, however, follows the progression of an execution more closely than its task state, and no evaluator signals when the decisive view is missing. How much fidelity a reward model needs therefore depends on the decision it drives. We outline directions from real-failure supervision to metrics that separate task state from chronology, and open-source RRM-Hub, a toolbox in which 14 existing RRMs can be readily applied and new evaluators tested on the same capabilities.
  </details>

- **[Racing the Clock: Teaching VLNs Budget-Conditioned Object Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17442)**  `arXiv:2611.17442`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Maitree Meher, Siming He, S. Shankar Sastry, Jitendra Malik, Claire Tomlin, Jonas Frey_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How do action budgets shape vision-language navigation (VLN) exploration, and what expert supervision best teaches budget-aware search? We study budget-conditioned object search: finding as many distinct instances of a requested category as possible within a budget, with discovery defined geometrically by camera field of view and proximity. Distinct discoveries provide a denser evaluation signal than single-target success. We cast expert design as a choice of scene abstraction: a single budget-indexed Bellman recursion, applied to different abstractions of the full environment, yields experts that differ in what they know. An object-level Bellman expert is given every target location and reduces search to budgeted routing. A room-level Bellman expert retains observed room connectivity, doorway evidence, and cross-scene semantic priors but withholds the contents of unexplored rooms, so it must search for targets that its student also cannot see. We distill both experts into Uni-NaVid under matched training conditions and also evaluate greedy-frontier supervision on held-out HM3D scenes. Changing only the stated budget changes learned trajectories. Despite higher object-level expert return, room-level supervision achieves the highest mean collection among evaluated VLNs at every tested budget. At 2000 steps, it finds 72.7% of reachable targets, versus 68.5% under greedy-frontier supervision, 65.5% under object-level supervision, and 44.8% for the pretrained VLN. These results show that expert-return rankings need not predict supervision quality for VLN exploration, and motivate choosing an expert's abstraction with the student's information in mind rather than by expert return alone.
  </details>

- **[Linglong Agent Service: A Runtime for Centralized Agent Reasoning and Distributed Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21129)**  `arXiv:2611.21129`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Hua Zhang, Min Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents can use remote tools and cloud sandboxes, but integrating reasoning, session state, and heterogeneous execution sites remains a runtime design problem. We present Linglong Agent Service (agentsvc), a self-hosted HTTP service and embeddable Python runtime that treats execution site as a configurable dimension alongside model, tools, and context. A parent service retains inference orchestration, tool descriptions, and conversation history; each inference request selects one child environment for file, shell, terminal, and MCP operations. Direct HTTP and child-initiated WebSocket tunnels carry the same application interfaces, including portable request context and centrally managed updates. The normal core path requires no third-party Python packages, with an optional Windows pseudo-terminal dependency. We further present online_debugger as the process-scale special case of execution-site separation: an instrumented program submits exception evidence, executes session-scoped inspection tools inside the incident process, and resumes the same diagnostic session. Its default prompt requires failing-then-passing reproduction evidence for source changes, while runtime patch activation and failed-call recovery remain unimplemented. We explain the design through constrained expected-utility reasoning, compare concrete mechanisms with Hermes Agent, Pi, Codex, and Claude Code, and report implementation and regression-test evidence. The current evidence establishes executable contracts, not performance advantages, repair accuracy, or production recovery. The source code is open source under the MIT license atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ScaleVLA: Scaling Vision-Language-Action Models to 552 Billion Parameters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21607)**  `arXiv:2611.21607`  `cs.RO`  
  _Ruixi Yu, Dairu Liu, Xuchuan Chen, Zekun Qi, Zekai Li, Zhuo Cao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier Vision-Language Models (VLMs) such as GPT-6 Astra have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in robot control, suggesting that broad pretraining provides knowledge valuable for physical action. Yet most Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policies still rely on much smaller VLM backbones, leaving this potential largely untapped. We ask whether frontier-scale representations can provide a stronger foundation for robot learning. We introduce ScaleVLA, which connects a 552B DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash backbone to a trainable action DiT, and systematically compare VLMs from 2B to 552B under a shared training setup. We evaluate three fundamental capabilities of robot foundation models. In sample efficiency, just 10 demonstrations outperform a 2B backbone trained with 50 (87.8% vs. 85.6%). In generalization, LIBERO-PRO success reaches 45.1%, compared with at most 31.3% for smaller backbones. In precise manipulation, real-world charger insertion improves from 4/10 to 9/10 successes, with gains concentrated in contact-sensitive alignment. Despite its scale, ScaleVLA achieves 216 ms median latency per action chunk. Our findings suggest that frontier VLMs can serve not only as reasoning agents, but also as powerful representational foundations for efficient and generalizable robot policies.
  </details>

- **[ForceHarness: Turning Force into Action and Physics for Frozen Frontier Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21099)**  `arXiv:2611.21099`  `cs.RO`  
  _Ke Sun, Kevin Yuchen Ma, Yike Zhang, Yanzhe Chen, Mike Zheng Shou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier multimodal models can now control robots zero-shot from images, yet many manipulation tasks depend on contact forces that images cannot show. Bringing force to an API-only frozen model raises two challenges. First, contact evolves much faster than an API model can decide, so the system must close the loop on force beneath the model. Second, a frozen model cannot interpret raw force streams, so force must be returned in a form the model can reason about. Therefore, we present ForceHarness, a harness that lets frozen models use force without demonstrations or finetuning. After calibrating hand force from a wrist sensor or joint torques alone, ForceHarness addresses the first challenge with self-terminating force primitives that regulate force on their own and return at a contact event. To address the second challenge, a physics translation layer turns each force trace into physical quantities with uncertainty as the tool result. To evaluate ForceHarness against expert force controllers and learned policies under identical conditions, we also build HiddenPhys, a seven-task MuJoCo benchmark with hidden physical variables. On a real arm without a wrist force sensor, ForceHarness raises the zero-shot success rate of GPT-6 Astra from 3.3% to 90.0%. In simulation, it adds 17.5 percentage points on the public ForceBench suite and 34.3 percentage points on HiddenPhys, surpassing force-aware policies trained on demonstrations on both benchmarks and matching hand-written expert controllers on HiddenPhys. Ablations further show that smaller models make significantly better decisions when given physical quantities rather than raw force values.
  </details>

- **[RoboCoEvo: Expanding Robot Capabilities through Agent-Policy Co-Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19081)**  `arXiv:2611.19081`  `cs.RO`  
  _Ran Qi, Yixin Xiao, Dawei Wang, Zhiyuan Li, Jinbang Huang, Xuan Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Integrating MLLM-based agents with robotic foundation models has attracted increasing attention as a means of enhancing robotic capabilities. However, the improvement of such integrated systems remains largely decoupled across their agent and model components, with advances in one component not necessarily benefiting the other, substantially limiting the capabilities of the combined system. As both MLLMs and robotic foundation models become increasingly capable, fully realizing their combined potential calls for co-evolution, an iterative process in which improvements in agents and robotic foundation models reinforce one another. We propose RoboCoEvo, a failure-guided co-evolution framework that couples the refinement of the agent's explicit and reusable executable knowledge with parameter learning in robotic foundation models, such as vision-language-action (VLA) models. Through a code-as-policy formulation, the agent develops and refines executable failure-detection and recovery programs, generating targeted corrective experience. Post-training consolidates the resulting corrective behaviors into the robotic model's end-to-end policy, improving autonomous task execution. Changes in the updated policy's failure distribution, in turn, guide subsequent revisions to the agent's programs. Experiments on BEHAVIOR-1K, RoboCasa, and RoboTwin demonstrate sustained improvements in autonomous VLA task performance across successive co-evolution rounds.
  </details>

- **[ESI: An Embodied Self-Improvement Flywheel for LLM-VLA Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18176)**  `arXiv:2611.18176`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yundi Cai, Zihan Wang, Chenxi Han, Yi Cheng, Houde Liu, Zilei Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Independently pretrained language models and vision-language-action (VLA) policies provide complementary building blocks for embodied agents, but connecting them does not automatically produce coordinated behavior. The VLA may generate actions that diverge from the LLM's intended subtask and, upon regaining control after a correction, undo the promising state restored by the LLM. We introduce ESI (Embodied Self-Improvement), an automated flywheel that uses environmental interaction to align reasoning and action. ESI treats the modular agent as the unit of post-training, repeatedly refining the prompts that guide the LLM, the shared skill interface that expresses its intent, and the VLA parameters that realize that intent, then redeploying these components together. Our key insight is that collaboration itself can supply supervision for improving this interface. The LLM's subtask decisions provide semantic supervision for what the policy should execute, while its effective corrections and subsequent student continuations provide action-level supervision for recovery and handoff. The student also learns from its own effective execution, allowing locally useful experience to contribute even when the overall episode fails. On ten selected RoboDojo tasks, ESI achieves a mean Score of 62.03 and a mean success rate of 49.41% using an LLM-VLA agent without generating task-specific control code. On both metrics, it outperforms the published reference results of all hybrid embodied agents, including the Agent+WAM system GO-3 Nano Preview (47.27 Score and 36.60% success), as well as all compared code-based methods.
  </details>

- **[SimpleVLN: Towards Simple and Frontier Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23203)**  `arXiv:2610.23203`  `cs.RO`  
  _Lingyi Hong, Weiren Cai, Jiayu Chen, Xinyu Zhou, Bailin He, Yunfei Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) requires embodied agents to ground language in visual observations and make long-horizon decisions. Existing methods often address these demands through task-specific architectures. As multimodal large language models (MLLMs) become increasingly capable, an important question remains: Have we truly unlocked the potential of native MLLMs for VLN before introducing task-specific architecture? We present SimpleVLN, an architecture-preserving framework developed through studies of navigation formulation, data construction, and optimization. At the formulation level, SimpleVLN combines single-turn decision inputs with fixed-budget global-local memory to efficiently use navigation history without unbounded context growth, while action chunks enable efficient output of multiple primitive actions per model invocation. For data construction, we combine expert demonstrations with policy-dependent DAgger experience and develop a scalable synthesis pipeline that generates large-scale tasks for reinforcement learning. At the optimization level, a progressive IL-DAgger-RL pipeline advances from action imitation to task-level outcome optimization. Comprehensive experiments validate the effectiveness of these three aspects. We instantiate SimpleVLN across multiple MLLM backbones and model sizes, demonstrating its generality. SimpleVLN achieves 76.7% and 80.1% success rates on the R2R-CE and RxR-CE splits, respectively, substantially outperforming existing methods. These results show that the navigation capabilities of native MLLMs can be effectively unlocked without task-specific architectural designs.
  </details>

- **[LLM-Flax: LLM-Generated Rules and Object Guidance for Neuro-Symbolic Task Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.26569)**  `arXiv:2604.26569`  `cs.RO`  
  _Seongmin Kim, Daegyu Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adapting a neuro-symbolic task planner to a new domain can require hand-authored symbolic rules and solved training instances for a learned object scorer. This paper presents LLM-Flax, which applies large language models (LLMs) to offline rule generation, failure recovery, and object scoring within the Flax planning pipeline. Generated rules undergo validation and corrective feedback before reuse, while runtime guidance operates within a shared planning budget. Experiments on MazeNamo and two additional domains show that the evaluated rule configuration improves planning success over manually authored rules while retaining the trained object scorer. Across eight MazeNamo settings, the mean success rate increases by approximately three percentage points. Controlled comparisons distinguish the effects of rule content from the choice of generator. Runtime recovery and object scoring do not establish an overall performance advantage under the evaluated planning budgets. These findings demonstrate the value of reusable LLM-generated rules and identify information quality and inference efficiency as central challenges for runtime guidance.
  </details>

- **[Do Multi-Agent LLMs Explore Each Other?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11250)**  `arXiv:2607.11250`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Hyeong Kyu Choi, Jiatong Li, Wendi Li, Xin Eric Wang, Sharon Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Exploration is essential for reliable autonomy in multi-agent systems, yet it remains unclear whether large language model (LLM) agents can explore effectively when interacting with one another. We show that modern LLM agents often fail to do so, exhibiting myopic and polarized interaction patterns that lead to suboptimal coordination and increased regret. We formalize this challenge as the Multi-Agent Exploration problem, modeling it as a partially observable stochastic game (POSG), in which agents must probe peers to infer their capabilities and identify effective interaction strategies. To address this, we introduce Multi-Agent Contextual Exploration (MACE), a lightweight framework that explicitly promotes exploration through structured peer selection. Across both contextual and parametric diversity settings, exploration behavior and downstream task performance substantially improve when MACE is applied. We further provide theoretical insight that MACE achieves a sublinear expected-regret bound. Overall, our results highlight a fundamental limitation of current LLM agents and underscore the importance of explicitly guided exploration for reliable multi-agent autonomy.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Authorization and Trust Boundaries in Agentic Orchestration Frameworks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21259)**  `arXiv:2611.21259`  `cs.MA`  
  _Sahaya Jestus Lazer, Kshitiz Aryal, Maanak Gupta, Elisa Bertino_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic AI systems extend large language models with tools, memory, and multi-agent collaboration, introducing security risks at orchestration boundaries. These risks depend on how identities are represented, operations are authorized, and instructions or information propagate between components. We evaluate LangChain, LangGraph, AutoGen, and OpenClaw through twelve experiments mapped to the OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications using a common LLM (gpt-4o-mini). The experiments cover prompt injection, unauthorized resource access and command execution, memory poisoning, and inter-agent trust and identity attacks. Adversarial inputs induced unauthorized tool invocation, while tool chaining, stale filesystem validation, encoded instructions, and inter-agent delegation exposed weaknesses in authorization across component boundaries. Memory poisoning showed that untrusted state can influence responses, a risk distinct from conventional access-control violations. In the evaluated protected configurations, explicit authorization, controlled capability assignment, filesystem containment, and execution safeguards prevented specific unauthorized operations. The findings emphasize preserving authorization and information-trust boundaries throughout agentic workflows rather than treating framework capabilities or model-level instructions as sufficient security guarantees.
  </details>

- **[CANDO: Cooperative Agentic Network for Layout Design Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10044)**  `arXiv:2610.10044`  `cs.MA`  
  _Athanasios Masouris, Zheng Jing, Benjamin Sam Chandler, Hadi Jamali-Rad_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Layout generation for real-world facilities is a challenging problem, requiring reasoning over irregular site boundaries, heterogeneous orientations, access-aware placements, and motion-planning feasibility. Yet, most existing layout benchmarks in the generative AI space target simpler placements over rectangular domains and rely on distributional metrics such as FID and IoU that reward conformity to dataset priors, thus discounting design innovation. Motivated by these gaps, we introduce ALPS-Bench, a benchmark of $1,000$ professionally annotated real-world facility layouts paired with an instance-specific scoring protocol grounded in a structured design manual. As a strong baseline for ALPS-Bench, we propose CANDO, a training-free multi-agent framework in which specialized agents iteratively refine layouts through a verification-grounded loop, concentrating reasoning on strategic spatial decisions. We demonstrate that CANDO surpasses state-of-the-art trained and LLM-based baselines on the widely adopted PubLayNet, RICO, and PKU-PosterLayout benchmarks, establishing cooperative agentic design as a broadly effective recipe for constraint-aware layout synthesis. Code and benchmark are available through our project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[HIMX: mixed-format matrix multiplication based on customized HIFLOAT4 representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18641)**  `arXiv:2611.18641`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Zhiyuan Zhang, Yanzhao Li, Hui Dong, Sifan Cheng, Jingwei Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  4-bit weight-activation quantization (W4A4) is an important approach for efficient large language model (LLM) inference. Recent hierarchical formats such as HiFloat4 (HIF4) improve the representational range of 4-bit quantization through multi-level scaling. However, the same local mantissa representation in HIF4 may not be equally suitable for weights and activations, which exhibit distinct distribution characteristics. To address this limitation, we propose HiMX, a mixed-mantissa framework built upon the hierarchical scaling structure of HIF4. Our key observation is that the two HIF4-compatible mantissa representations, S1P2 and E2M1, exhibit complementary quantization behavior: the denser S1P2 format provides higher resolution for concentrated weight distributions, while the wider-spaced E2M1 format provides complementary quantization characteristics for activation tensors. HiMX enables operand-tailored mantissa representations while converting heterogeneous mantissas into fixed-point integer operands with operand-specific widths, allowing different operand formats to share a unified integer multiply-accumulate (MAC) array. Block-level analysis on Qwen3-8B shows that HIF4-S1P2 is better suited for weights while HIF4 E2M1 is better suited for activations, and their mixed-format combination achieves lower output MSE than homogeneous configurations. Across multiple benchmarks, HiMX reduces the average end-to-end accuracy loss from 2.38 to 1.77 points on Qwen3-8B and from 2.23 to 1.85 points on Qwen3-30B-A3B, demonstrating the potential benefit of heterogeneous mantissa representations for W4A4 LLM inference.
  </details>

- **[TrainExplorer: Autonomous LLM Post-Training via Plan-and-Decouple Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20430)**  `arXiv:2611.20430`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Xuannan Liu, Tian Lan, Zekun Li, Xing Cui, Zhengfeng Yu, Feihu Jiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based agents can automate model post-training, accelerating progress toward recursive self-improvement. Despite their promise, existing approaches remain under-explored and face two main challenges: (1) strategy lock-in that limits exploration beyond the initial strategy; and (2) coupled execution that obscures the effects of individual modifications. To address these challanges, we propose TrainExplorer, a multi-agent framework for autonomous post-training, that incorporates strategy planning and execution decoupling, to achieve broaden strategy exploration. First, strategy planning draws on scientific literature to construct a candidate search space of training strategies. Then, decoupled execution enables single-component interventions and attribution to guide strategy exploration. Extensive experiments on PostTrainBench demonstrate that TrainExplorer achieves a weighted aggregate score of 39.02, outperforming Claude Code using the same LLM by 7.32 and surpassing GPT-5.6 Sol-powered Codex, which scores 36.23. Code will be available.
  </details>

- **[When the Cheapest Feasible Router Fails: Auditing Deployment Commitments in Cost-Aware LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20417)**  `arXiv:2611.20417`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Minxuan Hu, Qishi Zhan, Ziheng Chen, Zhengtao Yao, Zihan Dong, Jiayu Yi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cost-aware LLM routing often selects the cheapest policy meeting empirical risk and coverage constraints. Yet a router that passes an audit can violate its fixed service contract on new requests, even when candidate policies are constructed independently. We study this deployment-commitment decision with disjoint construction, audit, and test samples. Across 19 RouterBench task families, the cheapest audit-feasible router deploys in 91.82% of opportunities but violates the held-out contract in 32.22% of deployments. We introduce Menu-Aware Routing Commitment (MARC), which qualifies policies using audit evidence and a serving-cost tolerance to retain the apparent winner, deploy a supported alternative, or defer. Its practical policywise variant (MARC-P) reduces observed conditional failure to 0.73% at 85.79% deployment, with higher serving cost; fixed-margin and formal risk-control procedures provide competitive alternatives. Nested audits show how added evidence changes deployable policy sets, and learned semantic routing exhibits the same commitment failure. These results establish deployment commitment as a distinct operational decision connecting router evaluation, service reliability, and inference cost.
  </details>

- **[JointQ: Joint Scale-and-Integer Quantization for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20384)**  `arXiv:2611.20384`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Keiji Kimura, Yuma Ichikawa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GPTQ is the de facto standard solver for layer-wise post-training quantization (PTQ) of large language models, yet the solver itself has received less attention than weight preprocessing and cross-layer objectives. We target a bottleneck of GPTQ: its greedy procedure optimizes continuous group scales separately from the assignment of discrete integers. Evaluating each integer candidate with its closed-form optimal scale has been used for per-channel quantization; JointQ extends it to group-wise quantization, where a row's scales are coupled through the Hessian, and applies it to refine the GPTQ solution. Each local-search move changes one integer and recomputes its group's optimal scale in closed form, accounting for the other groups through the full Hessian; all scales are periodically re-solved jointly. The search starts from a row-wise selection among scale-refitted candidates including the GPTQ solution, so unregularized JointQ never has a larger layer-wise calibration error than GPTQ. JointQ preserves the GPTQ output format and is thus a drop-in replacement. Across four models and four settings, JointQ improves six-task average zero-shot accuracy over vanilla GPTQ in 14 of 16 comparisons and perplexity in 12 of 16. The mean accuracy gain increases from 0.0044 in W4G128 to 0.1559 in W3 per-channel, and JointQ also improves average accuracy over GPTQ with MSE-based scale search and activation ordering in all eight per-channel comparisons, though rarely in G128. An ablation shows that JointQ's diagonal Tikhonov regularization mitigates severe W3 per-channel perplexity degradation, so the largest gains are not due to the local search alone. Overall, JointQ's primary empirical benefit is downstream accuracy preservation in challenging low-bit and large-group regimes. Code is available in the OneComp library atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SERAG: SEmantical and Relational approach for Anomaly detection on knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18640)**  `arXiv:2611.18640`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Ludovic Moncla, Antoine Salazar, Yassir Lairgi, Khalid Benabdeslem, Rémy Cazabet_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge graphs constructed from text can contain facts that are individually plausible yet inconsistent with their relational context. We introduce SERAG, a supervised anomaly detector that combines two complementary plausibility estimates: a semantic score computed from separately encoded subject, relation, and object labels, and a relational score obtained through a relational graph convolutional network. The scores are standardized, calibrated, and fused. We evaluate the method on a subset of ICEWS18 with vocabulary-constrained synthetic anomalies generated by an LLM, using pooled and chronological splits. At 10% anomaly prevalence, SERAG achieves test AUPRCs of 0.321 and 0.258, respectively, compared with 0.159 and 0.146 for the strongest adapted baseline. Fusion improves mean test AUPRC over either branch alone, with a larger gain in the pooled protocol and a smaller validation-selected gain in the chronological protocol. Additional anomaly supervision improves performance. These results support the complementarity of textual and relational signals for detecting synthetic anomalies under the evaluated protocols.
  </details>

- **[GRAFT: Cross-Backbone LoRA Reuse for Continual Language and Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18638)**  `arXiv:2611.18638`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Ha Pham Thien Dinh, Ming Liu, Jiesi Guo, Matteo Vergani, Kewen Liao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In continual learning, parameter-efficient fine-tuning enables large language models to acquire new capabilities through lightweight adapters such as LoRA, yet the reuse of pretrained adapters is typically restricted to models sharing the same backbone. This limits the transfer of learned capabilities from language models to multimodal models. We propose GRAFT (Generalist Reuse of Adapters via Feature-space Transfer), a framework for cross-backbone LoRA reuse. GRAFT retrieves the most relevant pretrained LoRA adapter from a modality-aware library and maps it into the target backbone through a learned linear bridge between corresponding source and target feature spaces. We evaluate GRAFT on ten Super-NaturalInstructions text tasks, transferring Qwen2.5 LoRAs to Qwen2.5-VL, and five multimodal tasks, where Qwen2.5-VL LoRAs are directly reused. Compared with randomly initialized LoRA adapters, GRAFT improves performance on most evaluated tasks. These results show that GRAFT provides a simple and effective mechanism for reusing learned capabilities across language and multimodal models in continual learning.
  </details>

- **[Quantize, Qualify, Rerank: A Recipe for Compressing LLMs Without Losing Quality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18587)**  `arXiv:2611.18587`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Thamme Gowda_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Compressing large language models lowers serving cost, but a single automatic metric may miss quality loss. For the WMT26 Model Compression constrained track, we compress google/gemma-3-12b-it (24.4 GB in bf16) for three translation directions on one H100. Paired bootstrap analysis exposes metric disagreement on 4-bit quantization; guided by WMT24 human meta-evaluation, we use MetricX24-XXL as primary and CometKiwi-XXL as a cross-check. Removing the unused vision stack and non-task vocabulary and using int8 weights or fp8 activations causes no resolved loss, whereas int4 leaves a small residual. QLoRA healing overfits its calibration domain. A Cometoid best-of-4 reranker at $T=0.3$ improves both the 8-bit and 4-bit systems under WMT25 test set. On post-submission WMT26, both remain competitive with bf16 under reference-free metrics; the small difference between the reranked variants is treated as noise.
  </details>

- **[Large Language Models Develop Belief State Geometry In-Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17376)**  `arXiv:2609.17376`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Daniel Balcells, Andrew Jun Lee, Chirag Rastogi, Paul M. Riechers, Adam Shai, Xavier Poncini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) trained on next-token prediction exhibit remarkable in-context learning (ICL) abilities, yet the representations that support ICL remain poorly understood. We consider such representations in a controlled setting: prompting LLMs with data emitted from hidden Markov models (HMMs) and probing for the corresponding belief state -- the posterior distribution over the HMM's hidden states given the observed token history. Across six open-source LLMs prompted with data from 40 HMMs selected for non-trivial belief structure, we find that belief states are linearly decodable from residual stream activations, with peak probe $R^2$-values of 0.83-0.99 across HMM and LLM combinations, ranging from early to late layers. To establish functional relevance, we intervene directly on the probe-identified subspace via patching and steering, resulting in downstream prediction quality on the order of the untampered model, while controls degrade performance substantially. Together, these results provide representation-level evidence that ICL in open-source LLMs approximates optimal Bayesian prediction over a context-inferred generative model. More broadly, our findings extend prior results linking input-distribution structure to activation geometry: from toy networks trained explicitly on HMM data to production-scale LLMs.
  </details>

- **[The Structure of Quantization Damage in LLMs: Why the Next Bit Should Be Spent Globally](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01587)**  `arXiv:2609.01587`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Jundong Hu, Shekar Ramachandran_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-training quantization (PTQ) is widely used to reduce the cost of serving large language models (LLMs), but its accuracy cost is uneven and is often tuned per model. We study where quantization damage occurs and how to allocate a small additional precision budget. Using causal mixed-precision intervention as ground truth (raise each layer to 8-bit in turn and measure the accuracy it recovers) across 9 open-weight models in 4 architecture families, we test 3 intuitive hypotheses: that quantization damage lives in task circuits, where the model computes, or in weight statistics. None of them predicts which layers benefit from restored precision. Recovery is instead diffuse: for 8 of 9 models, recovering 75% of the gap takes roughly half the layers; the lone exception, Qwen3-8B, is sharply concentrated. At a matched precision budget, spending it globally on finer quantization granularity beats locally repairing the most recoverable layers for all 8 group-128-compatible models (all but OpenLLaMA, whose width rules out group-128), by 21-52 points, including the concentrated Qwen3-8B. We report 2 secondary findings: the residual is budget-limited (8-bit is near-lossless in our evaluation across RTN, GPTQ, and AWQ), and the location of peak recovery correlates with architecture within a family, though not across families. Within this budget setting, global granularity is a better default than selectively protecting critical layers. More broadly, cheap signals that correlate with quantization damage do not necessarily identify where restoring precision improves accuracy; this must be tested with causal intervention.
  </details>

- **[From Weights to Features: Sparse-Autoencoder-Guided Activation Regularization for Continual Learning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26629)**  `arXiv:2606.26629`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Evan Ning, Wei Xue, Dong Lou, Yike Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Weight-protecting regularizers such as elastic weight consolidation (EWC) are a standard defense against catastrophic forgetting; however, in the sequential fine-tuning of large language models, they exhibit a stability-plasticity tradeoff that penalty tuning cannot resolve. We attribute this limitation in part to polysemanticity: because each weight participates in many unrelated concepts, a per-weight importance score has difficulty isolating the knowledge that requires protection. We instead regularize in activation space, using a pretrained sparse autoencoder (SAE) as a fixed dictionary of approximately monosemantic features. We formulate continual learning as constrained optimization over feature drift, with a stability constraint on the features to be preserved and a plasticity constraint on the features used by the current task, and we show that the resulting squared-hinge loss is a regularized Lagrangian relaxation of this problem, with EWC as a special case. A replay variant measures drift on 200 stored sequences per previous task, whereas a data-free variant stores only a 197 KB feature mask per task, versus 6.5 GB for EWC. On TRACE (Gemma-2 9B, Llama-3.1 8B) and MedCL (Gemma-2 9B), the replay variant matches or exceeds SAE-based feature distillation at approximately one third of its per-step cost, and the data-free variant attains the highest mean overall performance among methods that store no previous-task data and add no task-specific parameters. Task-relevant units are linearly separable in the SAE basis but not in the neuron basis (ROC-AUC of 0.87-0.88 versus 0.50), and weight-space protection constrains the features of other tasks nearly as strongly as those of the protected task.
  </details>

- **[Screen Before You Add: Reducing Candidate Inference in Model Ensembles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18647)**  `arXiv:2611.18647`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Davide Mambelli, Alessandro Rozza, Steven Motta_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As new models become available, existing ensembles must be updated to be competitive, making them intrinsically dynamic. Deciding whether to add a candidate model can require inference on an entire annotated dataset. Such a screening becomes prohibitive for models with costly inference especially when they ultimately add no value. Even under simple convex aggregation, relying on benchmark scores can be misleading as strong standalone performance does not guarantee ensemble improvement, and fitted ensemble weights can hurt test performance. In this work, we study candidate screening and show how early rejection can be achieved safely, exploiting residual geometry for stratification and rejecting the candidate when the best possible remaining predictions cannot achieve the necessary improvement. The key result that justifies this approach is that, under convex aggregation and squared or Brier loss, the population optimum improves if and only if a candidate induces a prediction change positively aligned with the residuals of the population-optimal existing ensemble. An optimistic quadratic program (QP) provides certified early rejection, while posterior-scenario sampling offers an alternative heuristic. We evaluate the approach by showing the tradeoff between saved calls and screening accuracy, which refers to recovering the same accept/reject result as for the entire annotated dataset. We assess empirically whether full-dataset decisions predict candidate usefulness on a held-out test set. On two LLM panels, stratified screening with certified QP rejection saves 21-23% of candidate calls while keeping 100% screening accuracy. In our experiments, sampling posterior scenarios further increases call savings to approximately 37%, with a loss of less than one percentage point in screening accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Interpreting and Controlling Model Behavior via Constitutions for Atomic Concept Edits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.00092)**  `arXiv:2602.00092`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Neha Kalibhat, Zi Wang, Prasoon Bajpai, Drew Proud, Wenjun Zeng, Been Kim, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce a black-box interpretability framework that learns a verifiable constitution: a natural language summary of how changes to a prompt affect a model's specific behavior, such as its alignment, correctness, or adherence to constraints. Our method leverages atomic concept edits (ACEs), which are targeted operations that add, remove, or replace an interpretable concept in the input prompt. By systematically applying ACEs and observing the resulting effects on model behavior across various tasks, our framework learns a causal mapping from edits to predictable outcomes. This learned constitution provides deep, generalizable insights into the model. Empirically, we validate our approach across diverse tasks, including mathematical reasoning and text-to-image alignment, for controlling and understanding model behavior. We found that for text-to-image generation, GPT-Image tends to focus on grammatical adherence, while Imagen 4 prioritizes atmospheric coherence. In mathematical reasoning, distractor variables confuse GPT-5 but leave Gemini 2.5 models and o4-mini largely unaffected. Moreover, our results show that the learned constitutions are highly effective for controlling model behavior, achieving an average of 1.86 times boost in success rate over methods that do not use constitutions. Our open-sourced code can be found atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Optimistic Verifiers Get Hacked: Reward Hacking Under an Exact Process Oracle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21538)**  `arXiv:2611.21538`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Arunkumar V, Manoranjan Gandhudi, Gangadharan G. R., Arun Prakash, S. Senthilkumar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards inherits whatever its verifier gets wrong. Outside mathematics and code the verifier is a proxy, a rubric, a learned judge or a hand-written checker, and its distance from the true objective is never measured, so a rising reward cannot be told apart from a hack. We build a setting where the true reward is exactly computable at the process level: executable authorization policies over 124,416 contexts admit an oracle that lists every minimal sufficient reason for every decision. We compile these policies into exact process rewards, degrade the verifier along seven realistic axes (outcome-only, sufficiency, recall-only, extraction noise, coarse taxonomy, stale policy, LLM judge), and train against each while the exact verifier scores every rollout. Four phenotypes appear. Optimistic verifiers are hacked by padding: the sufficiency proxy ends at 0.940 while true faithfulness falls from 0.731 to 0.667 on 96 held-out episodes. Outcome-only verifiers saturate and stop teaching, noise dilutes, and ranking-preserving gaps do no harm. A model-free audit of the verifier, its selection error and gap sign, together with a short saturation pilot, anticipates which phenotype a run will produce. Across nine configurations spanning two frameworks, two model vendors, two adapter sizes, three precisions, two initialisations and two seeds, the phenotypes recur, sufficiency damage is positive in all 10 replication cells and beyond two standard errors in 7, and optimistic gaps remain the steeper branch. The damage magnitude does not replicate, and five pre-registered attempts to explain it are reported as they fell. We release the testbed, the training instrumentation, and 14 real public policies (Cedar, OPA, Gatekeeper) ported to executable oracles.
  </details>

- **[Estimating Outpatient Visit Prices from Hospital Price Transparency Data using Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20261)**  `arXiv:2611.20261`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Kurmanbek Kaiyrbekov, Nicholas J Dobbins, Sean D Mooney_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Objective: Hospital care prices in the United States have increased substantially, yet patients lack effective tools to access and compare costs. We aimed to develop and evaluate an LLM-based approach for improving access to and use of hospital price transparency (PT) data.Materials and Methods: To estimate visit prices, we used a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) framework to analyze PT files from three major hospitals in Maryland and the surrounding region. We compared BM25, OpenAI embedding models, and a GPT-BM25 hybrid against a no-RAG baseline for cost estimation of individual procedures and procedure groups, then evaluated the best approach for symptom-based visit cost estimation using synthetic simulations.Results: Across hospitals, the GPT-BM25 retriever achieved the best performance, with average price MAPE of 19.44% for individual procedures and 4.90% for groups, compared with 147.98% and 82.82%, respectively, for the no-RAG baseline. In simulations, our framework correctly identified approximately 50% of reference procedures and reduced MAPE relative to the LLM-only baseline at two of the three hospitals, while achieving comparable performance at the third.Discussion: Our framework uses RAG to improve care price estimation in real-world settings. Compared with the no-RAG baseline, it substantially improved cost estimates for both individual procedures and procedure groups. Improvements were smaller in synthetic simulations, likely because inferring procedures from clinical information is more complex. Practical challenges remain, including heterogeneous PT file formats, unexplained duplicate entries, and limited availability of insurer-negotiated rates.Conclusion: LLMs that effectively integrate PT data show strong potential to provide patients with more accessible and accurate healthcare cost estimates.
  </details>

- **[Eliciting Research Taste in LLMs through Future Research Direction Choice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19794)**  `arXiv:2611.19794`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Dhruv Trehan, Paras Chopra_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Research taste is increasingly discussed in the context of AI scientists, yet remains little studied or measured in LLMs. This judgment matters especially in open-ended research, where a direction must be chosen before a verifiable objective is available. We operationalize research taste through one task of the scientific workflow: choosing which future research direction to pursue given a research goal and current results. Rather than predicting downstream scientometric metrics or treating author choices as objectively correct, we evaluate models against human-authored future-work directions and report author alignment, stable divergence, and unstable choice as behavioral measures of research-direction preference. We construct an order-controlled evaluation of 80 pairwise comparisons from 19 ML papers published between December 2025 and February 2026 across four subdomains. Each query presents a research goal, hypotheses tested with key results, and two candidate future directions: one author-recommended direction and one plausible distractor sampled from a similar paper. Across eight LLMs, author alignment ranges from 45.0% to 67.5%, while stable divergence ranges from 15.0% to 36.2%. These divergences largely persist under similarity controls, prompt ablations, and an author-preference framing check, and inter-model agreement suggests that some non-author preferences are shared across models. We characterize what models diverge toward using transition-category labels and selected reasoning examples, finding recurring movement toward scaling/generalization and trade-offs between methodological and infrastructure/data extensions. We view this as an early descriptive evaluation for eliciting research taste profiles in LLMs, and as a step toward understanding and steering their research preferences as they take on more autonomous roles in novel open-ended scientific discovery.
  </details>

- **[Where Should LLM Agents Adapt at Test Time? In Context, in Weights, or Both](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19700)**  `arXiv:2611.19700`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Parsa Mirtaheri, Mohammadreza Armandpour, Keivan Alizadeh, Iman Mirzadeh, Parshin Shojaee, Aryo Lotfi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents operating repeatedly in the same environment, such as a code repository, a database, or an application suite, encounter tasks that share latent structure. Experience can reveal this structure, allowing agents to reuse environment knowledge and learn from environmental feedback. Agents can adapt at test-time using this experience through two channels: retaining it in context and updating their weights. These channels are usually studied separately, leaving unclear what each contributes and when they complement each other. We compare in-context learning (ICL), reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards using GRPO, and self-distillation using SDPO, alone and in combination, using a shared online protocol and modular framework. Agents encounter matched task streams, are evaluated on each task before adapting to it, and learn only from their own attempts and environment feedback. We evaluate 14 adaptation mechanisms and a stateless baseline with Qwen3.5-9B on 109 tasks from four environments drawn from SWE-bench Verified, AppWorld, ScienceWorld, and BIRD-Critic-SQLite, using five task orders per environment. ICL provides the largest standalone gain: its best policy raises mean success across environments from 28.8% to 49.4%. Adding GRPO to this policy achieves the highest mean success, 51.6%. While adding GRPO improves the overall mean under every ICL policy, SDPO has mixed effects. Trace analyses show fewer lookups and shorter attempts under ICL, consistent with reusing knowledge of the shared structure. However, ICL also frequently skips inspection and verification for the current task. Adding GRPO to ICL helps restore these behaviors while preserving its reduced environment lookups. These observations suggest that reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards can refine how agents use retained experience, motivating the joint study of context management and weight updates.
  </details>

- **[MathaMAGICal: Validating Trajectory Data Attribution During Capability Acquisition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19692)**  `arXiv:2611.19692`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jason Lee, Dikshanta Pokhrel, Phonn Nay Khant, Victor Lee, Moritz Weckbecker, Sebastian Lapuschkin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Influence-function-based methods like MAGIC provide exact first-order scores for trajectory data attribution, yet only retraining indicates whether those scores accurately predict data removal. We evaluate MAGIC on $86$M- and $152$M-parameter GPT-2-style models learning multistep arithmetic from scratch on filtered web text and four synthetic math families. Full-run attribution at $86$M suffers from numerical overflow, and even with metagradient clipping, scores correlate near chance with retraining (linear datamodeling score, LDS, $0.17$--$0.22$). In contrast, short checkpoint windows yield stable, accurate predictions at both scales (LDS $0.86$--$0.99$ across four $100$-step windows at $152$M), whereas a single-checkpoint gradient-dot baseline shows near-zero correlation. Rankings of individual training blocks predict retraining only within that single run and do not transfer across seeds or hyperparameters ($\rho \le 0.09$), whereas format-level patterns replicate across runs. Two data families, worked examples and primitive facts, occupy $2.1$--$2.6\times$ their corpus share in both the helpful and harmful $5\%$ score tails, reflecting strong bidirectional local sensitivity distinct from net benefit under multi-epoch family deletion. These results show that trajectory attribution is reliable within short, validated windows and yields family-level insights that generalize across runs.
  </details>

- **[Large language models as oncology world models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18994)**  `arXiv:2611.18994`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Julien Vibert, Jakob Nikolas Kather_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can synthesize cancer patient cases, but their internal representation of oncology, or world model, has not been explored systematically. By only specifying tumor type, we show that LLMs tend to generate similar patients per cancer, with on average 81.0% to 93.2% of a cancer's patients sharing the most frequent value of each baseline feature. By systematically varying patient features in 12 clinical scenarios, we show that they follow biomarker and treatment logic in 29 of 36 model-scenario assessments, against 1 of 12 when previous treatment or frailty matters. Continuing real patient trajectories, an LLM predicted 89.9% 12-month survival, and 79.2% with similar patients as examples, against 72.2% observed. To represent these trajectories, we developed the Clinical State Space, a dynamic representation framework that we also applied to real patients. Despite the limits we show, improved world models may support digital twins, which our framework can evaluate.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Head Recurrent Memory Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01523)**  `arXiv:2607.01523`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jiatong Li, Samuel Yeh, Sharon Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recurrent memory agents extend LLMs to arbitrarily long contexts by iteratively consolidating input into a fixed-size memory window. Despite their scalability, these agents exhibit a well-documented reliability problem: end-to-end performance degrades systematically as context length grows. We diagnose this failure by decomposing performance into two factors---memory capture and memory retention---and quantitatively confirm that retention is the dominant bottleneck. Retention collapses because existing designs maintain memory as a monolithic text block, forcing every update to risk overwriting previously retained content. Motivated by this diagnosis, we propose Multi-Head Recurrent Memory (MHM), a general, training-free framework that partitions memory into independent heads governed by a stage-wise select-then-update strategy. At each step, exactly one head is selected for update while the remaining heads are structurally shielded from overwriting, shifting the burden of retention from model behavior to architectural design. As a lightweight instantiation, we introduce Least-Recently-Updated MHM (MHM-LRU), which guarantees uniform head utilization with zero additional token overhead. Extensive experiments on long-context benchmarks show that MHM-LRU substantially improves both retention and end-to-end accuracy across the 100K--1M token range, where baselines degrade sharply. On RULER-HQA at 896K tokens, MHM-LRU improves the memory retention rate from less than 30% to 73.96%. These gains generalize across model families, scales, and task types, positioning architectural optimization as a practical and cost-efficient path toward reliable long-context recurrent memory.
  </details>

- **[Representational Change Under Self-Consuming Training: A Sparse Autoencoder Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21348)**  `arXiv:2611.21348`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Suchit Gupte, Zhongteng Cai, Xueru Zhang, Mohammad Mahdi Khalili_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly exposed to model-generated text, raising the possibility that future models will be trained recursively on the outputs of their predecessors. Prior work on such self-consuming training loops has largely focused on output quality and downstream performance, leaving their effects on internal representations poorly understood. We study how representations evolve across five generations of self-consumption and whether sparse autoencoders (SAEs) remain reliable under this shift. At each generation, we compare a lineage trained on synthetic data with a matched control trained on real data under the same training settings. We evaluate how well SAEs trained before self-consumption transfer to later generations, measuring reconstruction and sparsity across SAE widths, architectures, and model layers. We then fine-tune the original SAE on each generation and use sparse probing to test whether task-relevant information remains concentrated in a small number of latents or becomes more distributed across the representation. We find that zero-shot SAE performance degrades progressively under self-consumption, with growing reconstruction and sparsity gaps that vary substantially across layers. Fine-tuning largely restores reconstruction, but not feature-level usefulness: task-relevant information remains accessible in the full latent space while becoming less concentrated in individual features. These results show that self-consumption induces representational drift and that reconstruction fidelity alone is insufficient to establish that an adapted SAE remains equally useful for interpretation.
  </details>

- **[Can AI Mature a Model's Geometry? Training-Free Weight Baking in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21310)**  `arXiv:2611.21310`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Amin Golnari, Saeid Sanei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can an AI make a trained language model more mature, in the sense of a better organized latent space, by looking only at the geometry of that space? An analyst model reads a fixed-size digest of topological descriptors of the layers of a frozen target and proposes a transformation, which closed-form ridge regression writes into the feed-forward weights without gradient training. Using lightweight models, 126 runs show that category purity rose on unseen words and sentences, benchmark accuracy stayed within sampling error, and the analyst matched a category-aware heuristic with gentler edits. In a five-model round robin approach, 16 pairs raised purity and four left the target unchanged. The results provide a baseline for future work on LLM-guided, training-free model editing without using raw weights.
  </details>

- **[SpeCal: Spectral Calibration for Unsupervised RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20691)**  `arXiv:2611.20691`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Kai Tang, Xing Zheng, Haobo Wang, Guangcheng Zhu, Shenzhi Yang, Zhengqing Zang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has substantially improved the reasoning abilities of large language models, but its dependence on ground-truth answers or external verifiers limits its ability to exploit abundant unlabeled reasoning data. Unsupervised RLVR alleviates this bottleneck by deriving scalar rewards from the model's own rollouts. However, the reliability of such self-generated supervision varies substantially across rollout groups and individual responses, while existing methods construct or refine their rewards mainly from decoded outcomes and reward statistics. These endpoint signals reveal what answers the model produces, but provide limited information about whether the underlying rollout representations form a reliable structure for learning. Our key observation is that hidden-state spectra offer a complementary view of supervision reliability by characterizing both the organization of a rollout group and the support received by each rollout within that group. Based on this observation, we propose SpeCal, a bi-level spectral calibration framework for unsupervised RLVR. At the group level, SpeCal combines spectral mass with a Gaussian band-pass gate over normalized entropy-based effective rank, favoring rollout groups with substantial representation variation whose effective dimensionality lies in a preferred regime. At the rollout level, the same group effective rank determines how many dominant directions are retained in a leave-one-out spectral subspace formed by the remaining rollouts, within which the conditional support of each held-out rollout is measured. The resulting signals calibrate the original reward-induced advantages at the group and rollout levels without redefining the underlying unlabeled reward.
  </details>

- **[Model Immunization Beyond Condition Numbers: The Importance of Plateau Regions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20472)**  `arXiv:2611.20472`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Quoc Nguyen, Trung Le, Dinh Phung, Mehrtash Harandi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Harmful fine-tuning can rapidly compromise safety alignment in Large Language Models, underscoring the vulnerability of deployed models to post-release modification. While prior work motivates model immunization by inducing ill-conditioned curvature to slow harmful optimization, this approach is not scalable due to the high computational cost of Hessian estimation. We refine this paradigm by extending the theoretical framework to prioritize plateau regions alongside curvature. We further introduce an immunization algorithm based on efficient Hessian estimation and bounds on the extremal singular values of the Hessian. We demonstrate the proposed immunization framework in three applications: (i) Fine-tuning-as-a-service settings where benign user data may be mixed with poisoned harmful data, (ii) open-weight settings where an adversary can fine-tune on harmful-only data with varied training dynamics, and (iii) robust unlearning, where immunized models better resist relearning attacks after unlearning. Extensive experiments confirm that our approach achieves robust defense against adversarial fine-tuning while largely maintaining general utility and downstream trainability. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Protecting Safety during Reasoning Training with Low-Rank Fisher Regularisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20288)**  `arXiv:2611.20288`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Neel Rajani, Ivan Titov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are trained to have safety guardrails, but these can be compromised while fine-tuning for new capabilities, such as reasoning. One way to mitigate this degradation is to modify the fine-tuning objective to preserve safety behaviour, but doing so effectively and efficiently remains challenging. We revisit Elastic Weight Consolidation (EWC), which penalises parameter updates based on sensitivity estimates from the diagonal of the Fisher information matrix (FIM). While EWC provides some protection, the diagonal approximation ignores interactions between parameters. We address this limitation by exploiting recent findings that safety-relevant behaviour is located in a low-rank subspace within models. Our method, Subspace-Projected Elastic Weight Consolidation (SP-EWC), projects gradients onto this subspace to capture non-diagonal curvature efficiently. We investigate three different ways of identifying the safety subspace: through safety fine-tuning updates (LoRA), safety-related activations (ActSVD), and contrastive representations of safety and utility data (cPCA). We find that this choice substantially affects performance, with SP-EWC using cPCA comparing favourably against existing methods by better maintaining safety guardrails without introducing excessive over-refusal.
  </details>

- **[GNN-Based Deep Reinforcement Learning for Coastal Emergency Response under Extreme Water Levels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20268)**  `arXiv:2611.20268`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Marcello Sano, Samuele Casagrande, Davide Ferrario, Sebastiano Vascon, Silvia Torresan, Andrea Critto_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a storm surge approaches a coastal town, the emergency plan must be put into action quickly using water-level forecasts, flood extent, road access, vulnerable residents and shelters. Bringing this information together under time pressure is slow and depends on the duty officer's expertise. This paper is one step in a wider research line from data to safe decisions: machine learning to understand the hazard, reinforcement learning and planning to choose actions, large language models (LLMs) to explain and cross-check them, and an assurance layer that keeps people in charge. Here we address the action step on a real place. We ask whether a policy can learn the timing of emergency actions from decades of real events. Lido di Venezia is represented as a heterogeneous graph built from public elevation, road, building and census data and driven by hourly tide-gauge records from 1983 to 2024, with seven actions taken from the municipal emergency plan. A PPO policy is trained on this graph and compared, on storm windows unseen in training, with a rule-based system simulating the officer's discretionary decisions. On 18 historical test scenarios, PPO achieves a similar reduction in population exposure to the rule. With uncertain forecasts and limited protection it again performs similarly but does not outperform the tuned rule, and removing graph message passing does not substantially reduce performance. PPO therefore learns an effective protective response from an outcome-based reward alone: it is given the plan's actions and when each is allowed, but not the thresholds that tell the comparator when to act. Next steps test harder edge cases, move closer to a digital twin of the territory, and replace the simulated officer with assessment by emergency-management stakeholders, supported by LLM-based interpretation of each recommendation.
  </details>

- **[CECAR: Cache & Expert Co-Aware Routing Accelerates On-Device Inference of MoE LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20119)**  `arXiv:2611.20119`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Byeongju Kim, Hoonki Lee, Byungjun Kim, Inyul Ra, Yongjin Kim, Yongseong Lee, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite growing interest in on-device LLM deployment, large-scale Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models remain impractical on resource-constrained devices, as sparse expert activation still requires all expert weights to be memory-resident. To address this, we identify three key observations for on-device MoE inference: (a) cache hit rates are fundamentally bounded even under Belady's optimal policy; (b) these bounds are insufficient for low-latency on-device inference; and (c) prior cache-aware routing based on binary cache presence can induce routing instability. Based on these insights, we propose CECAR (Cache & Expert Co-Aware Routing), a MoE inference system that jointly considers expert routing and cache management. Unlike prior cache-aware routing methods, CECAR uses an ML-based cache policy to predict the expected reuse distance of each expert and prioritizes routing and execution toward experts with smaller predicted reuse distances. On the Qwen3-30B-A3B model, CECAR achieves 12.5 tokens/s on a consumer-grade GPU with only 16 GB of VRAM, where non-resident experts are fetched from SSD, delivering a 4.5x speedup over conventional MoE inference and exceeding the decode speed of baselines that keep the entire model fully resident in DRAM. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[LoopFill: Pipelined Multi-Token Speculative Decoding for Looped Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19649)**  `arXiv:2611.19649`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Siqi Zhu, Suozhi Huang, Yi Zhao, Jiaxuan You_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped Transformers improve parameter efficiency by repeatedly applying the same Transformer layers. During autoregressive decoding, however, generating each token requires several sequential passes through these layers, leading to high decoding latency. Pipelined self-speculative methods use shallow-loop predictions to process tokens at different loops in the same batch, but each token must reach the drafting depth before the next can enter the pipeline. We introduce LoopFill, a training-free speculative decoding algorithm for looped LLM that combines multi-token suffix drafts with shallow-loop predictions in a single draft tree. Both drafters contribute to one tree, so a suffix lookup can immediately extend a shallow prediction and several future tokens can enter the pipeline together. The shared block computes the highest-scoring draft candidates at different loop depths in one batch, and full-depth verification preserves the model's autoregressive output distribution. Across seven benchmarks, LoopFill's average speedup over greedy autoregressive decoding is $5.85\times$ on Ouro-2.6B and $5.13\times$ on Huginn-3.5B. The speedups exceed those of LoopAhead, the strongest baseline, by \(40\%\) on Ouro-2.6B and \(25\%\) on Huginn-3.5B.
  </details>

- **[Well-Log Reconstruction with Pre-trained Geological Knowledge: A Knowledge-Conditioned Large-Language-Model Generative Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19624)**  `arXiv:2611.19624`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yongan Zhang, Yitao Yu, Yizheng Li, An Gong, Youzhuang Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Missing well-log intervals make subsurface reconstruction ill-posed. Existing numerical regression models lack geological prior knowledge to constrain ambiguous inverse mappings. We propose GeoLog-Agent, a knowledge-conditioned generative agent using pre-trained large language models (LLMs) as a source of geological priors. A universal Geo-Harness translates LLM stratigraphic reasoning into formation-anchored petrophysical trajectories, feature-wise linear modulation, and conditional adversarial constraints. On the Norwegian North Sea benchmark, GeoLog-Agent achieves a mean $R^2$ of 0.9307 and a peak $R^2$ of 0.9713. It exceeds the nine-baseline average $R^2$ of 0.8387 by 0.0920, with a 30.97% average RMSE reduction, and exceeds the strongest baseline by 0.0274--0.0498 in $R^2$, with 16.25%--24.05% lower RMSE. Ablations confirm the effectiveness and synergy of geological prior reasoning, multi-scale dilated generation, and conditional adversarial modules. Transferring the coupling mechanism to six conventional deep learning models improves their $R^2$ by 0.0162--0.0789 with cross-well stability. These results demonstrate how foundation-model domain reasoning can constrain deep generative reconstruction of missing petrophysical measurements.
  </details>

- **[PBit1: One Programmable P-Bit per Parameter - A Finite-Control Representation of Pretrained Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19588)**  `arXiv:2611.19588`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Omar Ramadan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce PBit1, a finite-control representation of pretrained neural networks that assigns one programmable p-bit to each learned parameter. A 12-bit command sets its probability, a shared affine range maps that probability to the weight domain, and temporal observations reconstruct the weight for inference. Operator-aware range grouping and sensitivity-based sampling allocation adapt the representation to the network. Across five independent weight draws at 32,768 observations per scalar, selected configurations preserve task quality in GPT-2, DistilBERT, and LFM3-1.7B with every learned scalar converted and no fine-tuning. GPT-2 and LFM3 perplexity increase by approximately 0.66% and 0.26%, respectively, while DistilBERT accuracy changes from 87.51% to 87.49%. A sensitivity-based policy reduces GPT-2 reconstruction observations by 41.7% while retaining the quality criterion. Complete-model evaluations reuse reconstructed weights in BF16 inference, and THRML operator experiments examine native sampling and spatial-temporal tradeoffs. Together, these results establish PBit1 as a concrete construction for studying finite-control weight representation and temporal reconstruction in pretrained networks.
  </details>

- **[On-policy distillation as a Backdoor Propagation Channel](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19207)**  `arXiv:2611.19207`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zane Fu, Yanzhao Zheng, Ziting Qin, Qianshan Wei, Yi Yu, Gongli Xi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) is emerging as an important paradigm for the post training of large language models, improving student models by leveraging supervision generated online by teacher models. However, whether this process also transfers latent security risks from teacher models to student models remains insufficiently studied. In this work, we reveal a new security risk in which an attacker first implants a conditional backdoor into the teacher model through supervised fine tuning (SFT), after which OPD can transfer the backdoor to the student model with only an extremely small proportion of trigger prompt exposure. More concerningly, we find that such backdoor transfer is substantially stronger under OPD than under conventional distillation methods, suggesting that OPD may provide an efficient channel for propagating backdoors across models. Our mechanistic analysis shows that the backdoor signal is primarily transmitted through anomalous differences in the conditional probabilities produced by the teacher model for triggered and clean inputs, and is gradually internalized into the internal representations of the student model during training. Furthermore, we investigate how to suppress such anomalous conditional probability signals at the source of distillation supervision and, based on this analysis, propose an OPD training method tailored to this conditional backdoor propagation setting to mitigate the risk of backdoor transfer induced by untrusted teacher supervision.
  </details>

- **[The Critic Can Hurt: On the Safety of Critics in LLM-Guided AutoML Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19037)**  `arXiv:2611.19037`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Gaoyuan Du, Xiaoyang Liu, Rex Zhou, Dengdu Jiang, Rong Fu, Meng Ding, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM critics increasingly steer iterative search in agentic AutoML, prompt optimization, and self-refinement--evaluated for whether they help, but rarely for whether they can hurt. We isolate the critic's marginal effect through a four-arm audit and characterize the loop's tolerance through controlled fault injection. We show a naive 8B critic drives search below the no-critic random-search floor on 7 of 8 tabular datasets, losing up to 0.245 AUC in the worst case; the magnitude varies strongly with dataset, frontier, and budget. When: stronger critics attenuate the loss on the evaluated suite, without establishing a universal capability threshold. Why: controlled fault injection shows that non-directional errors are nearly costless under best-so-far search, whereas directional errors consume finite search opportunities; an idealized effective-sample calculation yields a necessary condition, $q(m-1) > \beta$, not a deployment certificate. How: a simple observable validation gate and a learned detector can reduce realized loss, but neither is guaranteed to preserve the full-budget no-critic baseline. We will release CRITFAULT, to our knowledge the first benchmark for detecting harmful critic feedback in AutoML. A critic is not a free addition: audit before trusting, and gate when capability or verification is insufficient.
  </details>

- **[What to Practice Next? Latent Profile-Guided Task Selection for Skill-Evolving LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18818)**  `arXiv:2611.18818`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Qijin Zhang, Hanzhen Wang, Yan Huang, Zhongxiang Dai, Shuang Qiu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Task selection shapes the experiences available for skill evolution in LLM agents, yet fixed practice schedules overlook differences in skill requirements and evolving task value. Most existing methods, however, rely on fixed schedules or scalar measures of progress, leaving nonstationary skill demands unaddressed. We propose SPG-Select, a framework for allocating limited practice budget using a multidimensional profile inferred from binary outcomes via MIRT. The framework predicts surrogate progress, defined as the difference between consecutive profile estimates, and prioritizes progress in dimensions with larger estimated proficiency gaps. Our SPG-Select-R policy combines these predictions with uncertainty-based exploration and periodic restarting to accommodate nonstationarity. We evaluate SPG-Select on ALFWorld and WebShop with three skill-evolution backends. In the reported runs, SPG-Select-R achieves higher mean held-out terminal success than uniform sampling in all six backend-benchmark combinations, with gains of 1.49-13.18 percentage points on ALFWorld and 0.93-3.13 points on WebShop. Under a predictable linear surrogate model and boundedness assumptions, we establish a high-probability dynamic pseudo-regret bound for surrogate task allocation in terms of parameter path variation.
  </details>

- **[LEAP-LLM: Preserving lead-time structure for question-conditioned ECG interpretation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18809)**  `arXiv:2611.18809`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Aakash Agarwal, Yidan Shen, Xin Fu, Renjie Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The electrocardiogram (ECG) records cardiac electrical activity across multiple leads and is used for detecting cardiac abnormalities. Recent ECG-language models enable large language models (LLMs) to interpret cardiac signals and answer clinically relevant questions. However, existing ECG-language models typically encode the ECG independently of the clinical question, forcing the ECG representation to retain broad and potentially irrelevant signal information. Moreover, these models often aggregate information across leads which can obscure lead-specific details needed for a particular question. To tackle these limitations, we introduce LEAP-LLM, an ECG-LLM architecture for Lead-aware ECG Alignment with Prompt conditioning. LEAP-LLM uses the clinical question to guide ECG feature extraction while preserving explicit lead and temporal structure. Task-trained instruction representations distill information from the question and guide two complementary branches. The Lead branch captures question-relevant temporal patterns within each ECG lead, while the Temporal branch summarizes relevant information across leads at each temporal position. Together, they provide the LLM with compact, question-relevant ECG information while preserving lead and temporal structure. LEAP-LLM reduces modality-token count by 89.0% and prefill FLOPs by 7.2x on average relative to other ECG-LLMs baselines. Across four ECG benchmarks, LEAP-LLM achieves better performance, including a 3.4-percentage-point improvement in G12EC over the strongest prior ECG-specific method.
