We further show that the model adaptively emphasizes the clinically relevant anatomical lead groups for different cardiac abnormalities, with lead importance patterns consistent with established diagnostic knowledge, providing interpretable evidence that the predictions are supported by clinically meaningful ECG structure.
  </details>

- **[RSIMed: Scaling Medical Foundation Models beyond Limited Clinical Data via Retrieval-Grounded Self-Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18646)**  `arXiv:2611.18646`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Wei Dai, Qianchu Liu, Guanghui Qin, Xing Han, Tristan Naumann, Sheng Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rare diseases are hard to diagnose, and the labeled clinical records needed to train models for them are few, private, and slow to extend, since a specialist must read every case. A model that writes its own training tasks could learn beyond these records, but no program can check a model-written diagnosis, and a wrong answer key corrupts training. To tackle this dilemma between data scarcity and difficulty of data annotations, we introduce RSIMed, Recursive Self-Improvement in Medicine, one of the first self-evolving pipelines in which a task proposer co-evolves in context with a diagnostic solver on real clinical records. Seeded by training admissions, a frozen copy of the solver writes each clinical question from retrieved medical evidence, so its answer rests on a source, and steers topics and difficulty toward what the solver still gets wrong. On rare-disease diagnosis from MIMIC-IV admissions, the accuracy of RSIMed-27B grows linearly with training compute, while a static pool of generated tasks collapses. Without any external model, RSIMed-27B and RSIMed-9B reach 0.516 and 0.488 diagnostic accuracy, above every off-the-shelf GPT-5.6 setting, whose best reaches 0.399 at 38 times the inference cost of RSIMed-27B. Tasks written by four external teachers, including GPT-5.5, train a less accurate diagnostician than the solver's own. Trained only on diagnosis, RSIMed-RL-27B also improves on the physician-written tasks of HealthBench Professional, from 0.291 to 0.303 in adjusted score.
  </details>

- **[Accelerating LLM Pretraining with Zeroth-Order Curvature on Idle Serving Compute](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02688)**  `arXiv:2611.02688`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Mingchuan Ma, Dongqi Cai, Daqian Yang, Zhenyan Lu, Chen Chen, Teresa Lv, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM pretraining is increasingly compute-intensive, while substantial inference serving compute remains idle during off-peak periods. However, such serving hardware is difficult to use for conventional first-order training due to its memory, networking and other system requirements. Zeroth-order (ZO) optimization offers a natural interface to this forward-only compute resource, but its gradient estimates break down in pretraining. Unlike fine-tuning, which can confine updates to a small parameter subspace, pretraining must update every parameter, and the alignment of a ZO gradient estimate with the true gradient shrinks as the number of updated parameters grows. We make a different observation: noisy ZO estimates can still provide useful curvature information, even when they are too inaccurate to serve as gradients. Based on this insight, we propose ZO-Curv, which leverages idle forward-only devices to estimate curvature and uses it to adapt and refine the update used during first-order training, thereby accelerating the process. We provide convergence guarantees that characterize the effect of finite-query curvature estimation. Experiments on LLMs from 125M to 1.5B parameters show that ZO-Curv consistently accelerates pretraining. At 770M parameters, ZO-Curv reaches AdamW's 20K-step validation loss in 3.9 hours instead of 7.5 hours, while achieving a lower final loss of 2.70 versus 2.84 under the same step budget. These results show that idle model serving compute can accelerate LLM pretraining when zeroth-order queries are used to estimate curvature rather than gradients directly.
  </details>

- **[Channel Selection for Mixture-of-Experts Decoding Needs No Calibration Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37386)**  `arXiv:2610.37386`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Geunhyoung Kim, Sung Ju Hwang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models are bottlenecked by loading routed-expert weights from memory during interactive decoding at small batch sizes. Because most intermediate channels in a routed expert contribute little to any single token, channel selectors predict which channels matter and read only their weights, typically keeping channels above a threshold calibrated on offline data. We show that channel selection needs no calibration data. First, a calibrated threshold does not fix how much a selector reads, because the number of channels passing it varies across inputs; Prox with thresholds calibrated for an 18% read budget actually reads only 12.9% on average across our benchmarks. Second, top-k keeps each token's k highest-scoring channels and needs no calibration data. At the calibrated budget, a selector's top-k version matches or beats its threshold version on the same score in 40 of 43 comparisons at 13% and 18% budgets, including all 23 on generation tasks. The top-k versions also avoid the repetition-loop collapse that calibrated thresholds cause on Gemma 4. Calibrating a threshold thus provides neither of the two things selectors use it for: a known read budget and a consistent accuracy gain at that budget. We furthermore introduce a reader built entirely from the model's weights, which screens channels with a ternary sketch and recovers the skipped ones with a low-rank map. Across three post-trained MoE models, our reader retains 98.1% of full-model accuracy at 17.8% of routed-expert reads, with Triton kernels running MoE blocks 1.60 to 1.90x faster than optimized vLLM at batch one and up to 2.69x at larger batches on the same three models.
  </details>

- **[Minimally Invasive Steering of Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30218)**  `arXiv:2609.30218`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Taha Entesari, Jingyu Zhang, Daniel Khashabi, Mahyar Fazlyab_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-time alignment adapts large language models (LLMs) to runtime objectives without updating model parameters. Pre-logit steering offers a lightweight approach by optimizing additive interventions to the hidden states of a frozen LLM, but excessive steering can distort output distributions and degrade generation quality. We propose Minimally Invasive Steering Vector Optimization (MISVO), which improves reward while controlling deviation from the reference policy. Rather than penalizing the Euclidean norm of steering vectors, we derive a quadratic regularizer from the local Kullback--Leibler (KL) geometry of the induced token distributions. The resulting Fisher-information penalty captures the distributional sensitivity of steering directions and admits an analytic gradient computed through matrix--vector products with the frozen language-model head. We further derive an exact decomposition of the sequence-level KL gradient and establish explicit, non-asymptotic bounds on the approximation errors, including the omitted trajectory-dependent score-function term. These results show that the frozen Fisher regularizer gradient approximates the full sequence-level KL gradient to first order in the steering magnitude. Across preference and code-generation tasks on four LLMs spanning approximately 1B--14B parameters, MISVO achieves the highest mean best-so-far reward in six of seven model--task settings, while maintaining diversity and coherence scores comparable to Best-of-$N$.
  </details>

- **[Proxy OPD: On-Policy Distillation with Transferable Relative Proxy Update for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11505)**  `arXiv:2607.11505`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Daocheng Fu, Rong Wu, Yu Yang, Jianbiao Mei, Licheng Wen, Xuemeng Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reward-based reinforcement learning (RL) improves large language models but incurs substantial costs when repeated across targets. On-policy distillation (OPD) reuses experts by matching their distributions on target-generated trajectories, yet differing base policies can confound reward-induced updates with pre-existing policy differences, potentially distorting transferred update directions. We propose Proxy OPD (P-OPD), a post-training method that transfers reward-induced relative policy updates using the target's frozen base policy as a reference. A proxy undergoes reward-guided exploration, yielding token-level log-probability changes between its initial and optimized policies. These signals guide learning on target-generated trajectories, with calibration controlling signal absorption, enabling reuse of reward-induced improvements across targets. Experiments on Qwen3-family targets in mathematical reasoning, code generation, and instruction following show that P-OPD improves targets and outperforms conventional OPD in most settings. For 1.7B-to-8B transfer, average Math Mean@16 and Code Mean@8 scores exceed OPD by 8.3 and 3.4 points, respectively. Mechanism analyses show more faithful reward-induced supervision and controllable adaptation. Cross-scale and cross-generation experiments confirm signal transferability and reusability, while multi-domain distillation reveals synergistic gains and cross-domain interference. In the measured 4B-to-8B setting, target adaptation after proxy exploration takes approximately 15\% of direct target RL time; repeated signal reuse amortizes the one-time exploration cost across compatible models. These findings support relative policy updates as effective, transferable, and composable supervision for an ``explore once, transfer multiple times'' post-training paradigm.
  </details>

- **[A Mechanistic Analysis of Looped Reasoning Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11791)**  `arXiv:2604.11791`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Hugh Blayney, Álvaro Arroyo, Johan Obando-Ceron, Pablo Samuel Castro, Aaron Courville, Michael M. Bronstein, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning has become a central capability in large language models. Recent research has shown that reasoning performance can be improved by looping an LLM's layers in the latent dimension, resulting in looped reasoning language models. Despite promising results, few works have investigated how the internal dynamics and computation of looped models differ from that of standard feedforward models. In this paper, we conduct a mechanistic analysis of latent states and layer behavior in looped language models. We analyze cyclic recurrence and show that for many of the studied models each layer in the cycle converges to a distinct fixed point; consequently, the recurrent block follows a consistent cyclic trajectory in the latent space. We provide evidence that as these fixed points are reached, attention-head behavior stabilizes, leading to constant behavior - stages of inference - across recurrences. Empirically, we discover that recurrent blocks tend to organize their computation such that it closely mirrors that of feedforward models, repeating this behavior in depth with each iteration. We study how architectural choices influence the emergence and stability of these cyclic fixed points and stages of inference, providing practical guidance for looped language model design.
  </details>

- **[Not All Turns Are Equally Hard: Adaptive Thinking Budgets For Efficient Multi-Turn Reasoning in Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.05164)**  `arXiv:2604.05164`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Neharika Jali, Anupam Nayak, Gauri Joshi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As LLM reasoning performance plateaus, improving inference-time compute efficiency is crucial to mitigate overthinking and long thinking traces even for simple queries. Prior approaches including length regularization, adaptive routing and difficulty-based budget allocation primarily focus on single-turn settings and fail to address the sequential dependencies inherent in multi-turn reasoning. We formulate multi-turn reasoning as a sequential compute allocation problem and model it as a multi-objective Markov Decision Process. We propose TAB: Turn-Adaptive Budgets, a budget allocation policy that takes as input the conversation history and learns to maximize task accuracy while respecting global per-problem token constraints, and consequently learns to adaptively allot smaller budgets to easier turns and save an appropriate number of tokens for the crucial harder reasoning steps. We train and analyze the behavior of TAB on an auxiliary hierarchical plan-and-execute system for multi-turn math reasoning where solution trajectories are inexpensive to collect. Here, TAB saves up to 35% tokens and 30% latency over static and off-the-shelf LLM budget baselines. We then transfer the same TAB policy to environment-grounded agentic workflows spanning sequential tool-use, simulated user-agent dialogue and multi-step terminal tasks, where it generalizes and maintains accuracy while saving up to 20% tokens and 15% latency, without ever being trained on them.
  </details>

- **[Probing the Trajectories of Reasoning Traces in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.23163)**  `arXiv:2601.23163`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Marthe Ballon, Brecht F. Verbeken, Vincent Ginis, Andres Algaba_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) increasingly solve difficult problems by producing reasoning traces before emitting a final response. However, it remains unclear how accuracy and decision commitment evolve along a reasoning trajectory, and whether intermediate trace segments provide answer-relevant information beyond generic length or stylistic effects. Here, we propose a trajectory-probing protocol for evaluating how a reasoning model's predicted answer evolves along a partial reasoning trace. The protocol (1) generates a model's full reasoning trace, (2) truncates it at fixed token-percentiles, and (3) injects each partial trace back into the model, measuring the model's induced answer distribution. We apply the protocol to seven open-source reasoning models (Qwen3-4B/8B/14B, gpt-oss-20b/120b, DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B and Gemma-4-31B-it) across four benchmarks (GPQA Diamond, MMLU-Pro, Omni-MATH-2 and CompMath). The protocol reveals that accuracy and decision commitment generally increase as the percentage of provided reasoning tokens grows, and length, form, and token-identity controls confirm that gains stem from instance-specific semantic content rather than context length or generic reasoning style effects. Furthermore, cross-model probing shows that even stronger models frequently repeat the source model's errors when given its partial reasoning trace, while allowing further reasoning substantially improves recovery. Together, these results provide a reproducible, model-agnostic measurement of reasoning trace dynamics that generalizes across answer formats and can reveal model-specific failure modes invisible to aggregate accuracy.
  </details>

- **[But what is your honest answer? Aiding LLM-judges with honest alternatives using steering vectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17760)**  `arXiv:2505.17760`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Leon Eshuijs, Archie Chaudhury, Alan McBeth, Ethan Nguyen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-as-a-judge is widely used as a scalable substitute for human evaluation, yet judges are least reliable on subtle dishonesty such as sycophancy and manipulation, where a judge scoring a response in isolation lacks a reference point. We introduce Judge Using Safety-Steered Alternatives (JUSSA), a framework that reduces this evaluator uncertainty using the assessed model's own internal representations. An honesty-promoting steering vector, optimized from a single training example, generates a contrastive alternative against which each response is judged. On a new manipulation dataset with human-validated response pairs, JUSSA improves AUROC for both GPT-4.1 (0.893$\rightarrow$0.946) and Claude Haiku (0.859$\rightarrow$0.929) judges. The gains concentrate almost entirely on the pairs a judge would otherwise misrank. They reverse once a judge has saturated a task and for the weakest judge we test, so contrastive evaluation helps most when a judge is capable enough to use the reference but has not yet saturated the task. A layer-wise analysis further locates steering effectiveness in the middle layers, where model representations of honest and dishonest prompt processing diverge. Our work demonstrates that steering vectors can serve as tools for evaluation rather than for improving model outputs at inference, opening a new direction for white-box auditing.
  </details>

- **[MiSTiQ: MicroScaling Trellis Quantization for Extreme KV Cache Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21216)**  `arXiv:2611.21216`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ofir Gordon, Lior Dikstein, Paul Balanca, Idan Achituve, Douglas Orr, Hai Victor Habi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern large language models (LLMs) increasingly support longer contexts and serve more concurrent requests, increasing both sequence lengths and batch sizes. Both trends increase KV-cache size and memory traffic during inference. KV-cache quantization reduces these costs, with recent approaches using codebook-based vector quantization to achieve extremely low bit widths. However, hardware constraints limit codebook size, and most methods reconstruct the cache in higher-precision floating-point formats for attention, limiting the computational benefits of quantization. To address these limitations, we introduce MiSTiQ, a drop-in replacement for codebook-based vector quantizers that combines trellis-coded storage with microscaling (MX) formats for low-precision computation. We combine trellis quantization with beam search to encode the KV cache over large reconstruction spaces without large codebooks. To support MX computation, we derive MX-Hash functions that generate reconstruction values directly in the target MX element format. These functions use the scale-conditioned marginal distribution of block-normalized Gaussian inputs. On Llama3 and Qwen3, MiSTiQ achieves lower WikiText perplexity and higher GSM8K accuracy than the evaluated baselines across several block-scale formats at approximately 1.25 effective bits per element. With NVFP4, it also achieves the highest LongBench average on both models.
  </details>

- **[VASR: Vector-Aware Assembly Planning with Equivalent Structures and Repair-Cost Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20984)**  `arXiv:2611.20984`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ruizhe Wang, Rui Liu, Hongliang Chen, Junyi Guo, Fangyu Wu, Yaran Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Furniture assembly planning requires inferring subassembly hierarchies from individual parts and a target illustration. Existing raster-based methods do not explicitly exploit the geometric and structural information in SVG drawings. We introduce VASR, a framework that fuses features from a dedicated SVG encoder with visual representations from a pretrained vision-language model to predict assembly hierarchies. To avoid penalizing valid alternatives to a reference assembly tree, we propose Repair-Aware Hierarchical Policy Optimization (RHPO) with hierarchy-preserving equivalence rewards. RHPO further uses repair-cost feedback to favor decisions with lower downstream correction costs among candidates with comparable equivalence-aware rewards. VASR achieves the highest Equivalent-Hard F1 among the evaluated methods, outperforming GPT6-Astra by 17.69 percentage points on the IKEA-derived test set while maintaining strong performance on an independent furniture dataset.
  </details>

- **[Same Fix, Different Weights: Replicating Weight-Space Programming with Claude](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20953)**  `arXiv:2611.20953`  `cs.LG`  
  _Subham Kundu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Takehi et al. (2026) report that GPT-6 Astra, given the weights of small neural networks as text, can say what they were trained to do, repair them, and write new ones without training. I re-ran the core of that study with Claude Opus 5.5 in ten pre-registered experiments, with untrained-network and permutation controls and hand audits of every sandboxed code cell. Reading was mixed: Claude named 3 of 4 Atari games from policy weights and found the measured copying heads in 15/15 character-LM runs, but its answers about training data often followed priors. Unlike the original, it identified a sycophantic Gemma 3 270M finetune from weights alone in 6/6 runs. One-shot weight edits took a failing MountainCar policy from 0% to 100% success and a hovering LunarLander policy from 0% to 85.6%; zero-shot networks beat PPO at 100k steps on 4 of 5 environments, each implementing a textbook controller. With no training or forward passes, Claude wrote a 1.48M-parameter 9x9 Go network that won 85-94% of games against three PPO opponents. Given Qwen3.5-0.8B's weights and 10, 25 or 50 forward passes of 64 practice problems, Claude raised MATH-500 from 6.7% to 51.4%, 55.2% and 58.1% (+51.4 points at 50 passes, 95% CI [47.9, 55.0]; original 48.1%), GSM8K from 11.6% to 68.2% and IFEval from 22.2% to 54.2%. Unlike the original, which removed an internal loop direction, every edit acted at the output: it scaled the final norm, raising the effective sampling temperature, and lowered the logit of "Wait" while raising that of </think>. The norm scaling alone reaches only 20.9-22.1% on MATH-500. On Gemma 3 270M, which rarely loops, no edit helped (-0.8 points), as in the original.
  </details>

- **[Tight PAC-Bayes Generalisation Guarantees for Large Language Model Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20818)**  `arXiv:2611.20818`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tom A. Lamb, Philip H. S. Torr, Tim G. J. Rudner_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) judges are increasingly used to monitor the outputs of LLM-based AI agents, yet formal guarantees on the reliability of these judges remain largely absent, limiting confidence in their use for safety-critical oversight. We consider certifying such a judge and define its risk over safety classifications of prompt--response pairs drawn from a deployed generator. We apply a PAC-Bayes framework to this setting, refining existing compression-based methodology with the use of tighter bounds and more efficient coding. We show that standard parameter-efficient fine-tuning can yield non-vacuous and even tight certificates for safety judges, for both classification and Brier risk, without holding out data for validation. We introduce LoRA-GT, which shares a single quantisation scale across layers and achieves the tightest certificates with minimal degradation in predictive performance. Across five guardrail models and four benchmarks, we find that certificate tightness is governed more by adaptation complexity than by base model scale. Finally, we show how far quantisation can be pushed before the resulting increase in risk outweighs the bound tightening gained from shorter descriptions. Together, these results provide a practical route toward certifiable scalable oversight and offer insight into how the complexity of safety adaptations shapes generalisation.
  </details>

- **[Contrastive Activation-Guided Zeroth-Order Preference Optimisation for Memory-Efficient LLM Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20635)**  `arXiv:2611.20635`  `cs.LG`  
  _Weiyun Huang, Alessio Galatolo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Direct Preference Optimisation (DPO) simplifies reinforcement learning from human feedback, but still relies on traditional backpropagation and its associated activation-memory cost. Zeroth-order (ZO) optimisation avoids backpropagation, yet isotropic perturbations can produce noisy updates for preference objectives defined by differences between chosen and rejected responses. We introduce Contrastive Activation-guided Zeroth-Order Direct Preference Optimisation (CA-ZODPO), which constructs a low-rank search subspace from the activation discrepancy between the chosen and rejected responses in each preference pair. Perturbations are restricted to this subspace, while momentum is maintained in low-rank coordinates to preserve the memory advantages of ZO training. On Qwen2.5-1.5B with Anthropic HH-RLHF, CA-ZODPO outperforms common ZO baselines and remains competitive with first-order DPO in pairwise preference evaluation. It also reduces peak GPU memory by about 59\% at a sequence length of 2048 while retaining the sequence-length scaling behaviour of zeroth-order training.
  </details>

- **[RoShift: Lazy Shift and Weight-aware Rotation for Activation Sparsity in LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20549)**  `arXiv:2611.20549`  `cs.LG`  
  _Geunsoo Song, Jiwon Seo, Hoeseok Yang, Youngmin Yi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Activation sparsity accelerates LLM decoding by skipping unnecessary weight reads and computation. However, SiLU-based LLMs exhibit little natural sparsity, and imposing high sparsity can substantially degrade accuracy. We propose RoShift, a training-free method that transforms activations into sparsity-friendly representations. RoShift introduces lazy shift, which recenters SiLU activation distributions around zero and defers correction to the end of the FFN to obtain additional sparsity. It also reduces sparsification-induced output error through rotation that jointly accounts for activation and weight information. By fusing the corrections required for shifting and rotation, RoShift translates these gains into decoding speedup. At 50% and 60% sparsity, RoShift improves average accuracy over TEAL by 1.60 and 3.29 percentage points, respectively, across five models. It achieves up to $1.72\times$ decoding speedup over the dense model on an RTX 5090 at 50% sparsity and yields a better accuracy-speedup Pareto frontier than TEAL. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Weights & Caches: LLMs Can Program Tabular Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20174)**  `arXiv:2611.20174`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shi Bin Hoo, Magnus Buehler, Omar Swelam, Frank Hutter, Lennart Purucker_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural networks are typically programmed through optimization, rather than by explicitly specifying their computations. Recent work on weight-space programming suggests that large language models (LLMs) can instead reason about and manipulate neural-network weights directly. In this work, we show that LLMs can directly program statistical computations in tabular foundation models (TFMs), whose parameters enable inference across different datasets. We investigate three complementary settings using GPT-6 Astra, without gradient-based optimization. First, Astra directly programs nanoTabPFN from zero-initialized weights, achieving a mean ROC AUC of 0.849 across 20 TabArena datasets, compared to 0.876 for a pretrained baseline. Second, Astra diagnoses and repairs a signed-product regression failure in pretrained TabPFN-3.5, restoring near-perfect predictions on held-out synthetic datasets while preserving performance on TabArena-Lite, with only a 0.08% average relative increase in error. Third, it writes KV-cache-editing programs that steer TabPFN-3.5's predictions on unseen synthetic datasets, demonstrating transformations such as reversing linear trends and converting sine waves into approximate square waves, without modifying model weights. Together, our findings suggest that LLM-driven programming could complement gradient-based optimization across TFMs' development and usage, while offering a new way to investigate and intervene in their internal computations.
  </details>

- **["Don't Leave the Task Unfinished": Persistence-Driven Violations under Quiet Oversight](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19911)**  `arXiv:2611.19911`  `cs.LG`  
  _Seunghyun Lee, Jaehui Hwang, Dongyoon Han, Sangdoo Yun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are increasingly given long, multi-step tasks to carry out, with the user reviewing little of what happens in between. Persistence, the tendency to keep working until a task is complete, is installed on purpose to improve performance on long tasks. Long-horizon tasks are typically complex, and the user's instruction can fall short of what the work actually requires, or conflict with the constraints the agent encounters in the task. We study what persistence does on long-horizon operations tasks that cannot be solved without breaking a documented constraint. We find that the agent pursues completion to the point of violating the constraint, and that a critically engaged user lowers the violation rate. Persistence takes violations from 29.2% to 58.3% across four models. Violations stay high wherever oversight goes quiet, whether the user is silent, assenting, or delegating with expressed trust. Only a questioning user, who asks for the basis of each action before allowing the next, brings the rate back to roughly the level measured without persistence. Practitioners should treat high persistence as a setting that carries a safety cost, and should recognize that the cost appears when oversight goes quiet. Safety here is better cast as a property of the human-AI pair than of the model alone. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Before the Word: Name Information in Description-Derived States of Small Language Models, With and Without Naming Failure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19609)**  `arXiv:2611.19609`  `cs.LG`  
  _Fernando Cardenas Piepereit_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The tip-of-the-tongue (TOT) state, knowing an object but not its name, motivates this study as an analogy only. We ask whether small language models form a name-predictive state from a name-free description, and whether it differs when naming fails but description selection succeeds. In an internally pre-registered study (a frozen document, no external registry) of Qwen2-0.5B, SmolLM2-1.7B and Phi-3-mini with 200 items, we combine a similarity read-out, a Patchscopes-style read-out and activation patching with matched controls. H1 (name information in the state) was supported in all nine model-by-description-type combinations; the agreement rule was met by the similarity measure together with patching, while Patchscopes stayed near chance. Post-hoc robustness checks after internal review (logit lens, single-position patch, manipulation check) temper this reading: name information in middle layers is clearest under the multi-position patch and the similarity measure, and weak under single-position patching at 50% depth. H2' (description-paraphrase consistency of name-free states not lower in failure items) is mixed: the SmolLM2 deficit test is significant under the pre-registered specification but not robust; Phi-3 non-inferiority holds across margins and a word-overlap covariate, although its bootstrap interval includes zero; Qwen2-0.5B is inconclusive. A capacity explanation remains untested. We report vehicle-level signals only, not meaning.
  </details>

- **[Advancing the Calibration-Compute Frontier of LLM Classifiers via Asymmetric Duos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19020)**  `arXiv:2611.19020`  `cs.LG`  
  _Berke Akturk, Ali Can Sahin, Rasit Cem Solatay, Emanuel Sommer, Yawei Li, David Rügamer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The importance of reliable uncertainty estimation in large language models (LLMs) has become increasingly pronounced, yet modern models remain poorly calibrated and frequently overconfident. In this study, we address this by proposing an efficient method for LLM classifier calibration using the Asymmetric Duos framework. Our approach combines a strong base model with a smaller sidekick model via logit-space fusion, utilizing global scaling parameters optimized on a validation set. Across diverse benchmarks, this Asymmetric LLM Duo consistently achieves best or second-best performance on expected calibration error and negative log-likelihood, without degrading predictive accuracy. More importantly, these gains are realized at a fraction of the computational cost of more complex baselines, placing our method on the calibration-compute Pareto frontier. We further demonstrate that our method acts orthogonal to various established calibration-aware techniques, meaning it can be easily combined with them to consistently boost their performance. Finally, our analysis of the logit-space behavior illustrates how asymmetric fusion successfully mitigates base model overconfidence. Overall, our findings demonstrate that the benefits of an asymmetric fusion successfully transfer to the language domain, providing a simple, highly cost-efficient approach to improving uncertainty estimation in LLM classifiers.
  </details>

- **[Revealing the Biases of Large Language Models By Inferring Prior Distributions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18996)**  `arXiv:2611.18996`  `cs.LG`  
  _Joshua Ryo Cheng, Thomas L. Griffiths, Jake C. Snell_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have been known to exhibit a wide range of social biases including name-nationality associations, occupation-gender associations and racial stereotypes. Existing approaches to evaluating these biases typically involve measuring surface-level behavior where a model is fed a dataset of prompts and its outputs are scored relative to ground-truth answers. Inspired by prior elicitation in cognitive science, we propose a different perspective that reconstructs latent bias as a prior underlying the model's behavior. We formulate the problem as a Bayesian inverse problem where, given prompt-response observations on a bias benchmark dataset, we infer a low-dimensional latent distribution that summarizes the model's stereotyping tendencies. This paper develops the mathematical foundation, a practical reconstruction algorithm, and experiments using the Bias Benchmark for QA (BBQ) benchmark to infer the prior distributions of LLMs over responses in ambiguous social contexts.
  </details>

- **[Beveridge: Open Weight System One Decision Models with Calibrated Probabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18655)**  `arXiv:2611.18655`  `cs.LG`  
  _Sathiskumar Jothi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many production systems need a model to make a narrow decision about a document (route a ticket, check a limit, refuse a request, pick the next step of a browser agent) rather than to write text about it. We present Beveridge, a family of open weight System One decision models that take a document and up to 15 typed questions (yes/no, choice among described options, or a level on an ordered scale) and return, in one forward pass without generating text, an answer with a calibrated probability for every option. Beveridge-Large-C (398M parameters) adds a 3.5M parameter decision network to ModernBERT-large: typed number and date features in the input, exact facts from a rule engine, question-to-document evidence attention, a three-step recursive core with learned halting and an exact yes/no path. Beveridge-Pro-C adapts Gemma 4 26B-A4B with LoRA to score option letters, and Beveridge-Pro-C-Web extends it to the next step of a browser agent. On held-out tests the 0.4B model reaches 93.0% on our operations-document benchmark against 91.1% for the best of five general LLMs (31B-675B) and 87.9% for a commercial System One API, and, because its confidences are calibrated (ECE 0.029 on that benchmark), it can hand over 76% of decisions automatically at 95% accuracy, where no general LLM we tested can hand over any. It answers in 4.9 ms per decision on one NVIDIA H200 GPU and 60 ms on the 14 core CPU of an Apple M4 Pro laptop. Beveridge-Pro-C-Web raises next-step accuracy on web pages from unseen site types from 49.4% to 98.2% and completes live browsing tasks without losing document accuracy. We describe the models, the training recipes and an evaluation on 19 held-out test sets against a commercial System One API and five general LLMs, including where the models are weak. Weights and inference code are released under Apache 2.0.
  </details>

- **[MeltKV: Memory-Aligned KV Cache Compression via Variable-Bit Packed Vector Quantization for Long-Context LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18652)**  `arXiv:2611.18652`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jiyun Han, Seongjoon Cho, Seungkyu Choi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly rely on long contexts, making Key-Value (KV) caches dominate memory usage and inference latency. Existing vector quantization (VQ) methods effectively compress KV caches to sub-4-bit levels but often overlook inefficiencies in memory alignment and decoding speed. This work introduces MeltKV, a memory-latency co-optimized KV cache quantization framework for long-context LLM serving. MeltKV employs a memory-aligned variable-bit index packing scheme that adaptively assigns bit-widths to grouped key channels and compactly stores indices within standard data-types, eliminating redundant bits. Additionally, head-wise codebook sharing reduces the total codebook size while maintaining accuracy. Experiments demonstrate that MeltKV effectively compresses KV representations to sub-4-bit levels down to 1 bit and, at comparable perplexity, achieves 48.1% lower memory usage and 52.9% lower latency than state-of-the-art VQ methods.
  </details>

- **[Skill-Latent Rewards: LLM-Guided Reward Formulation for Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18597)**  `arXiv:2611.18597`  `cs.LG`  
  _Maryam Shoaeinaeini, Brent Harrison_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon reinforcement learning under sparse and delayed feedback remains an open challenge. Existing return-decomposition methods redistribute episodic rewards to ease credit assignment, but they operate on raw state--action transitions and disregard the subgoal structure that governs meaningful progress in complex tasks. We propose Skill-LR, a hierarchical reinforcement learning framework in which a vision--language model identifies the agent's active subgoal at each timestep and a large language model generates a matching latent reward formulation through a two-stage prompting process. By conditioning reward signals on the currently active subgoal, Skill-LR produces stage-specific credit that evolves with the hierarchical plan, reducing temporal variance and improving sample efficiency. On multi-stage MiniGrid and BabyAI benchmarks, including DoorKey and Unlock-to-Unlock, Skill-LR converges faster and more reliably than sparse environmental rewards and strong credit-assignment baselines such as RUDDER and LaRe, demonstrating that subgoal-aligned latent rewards offer a principled path toward stable, interpretable credit assignment in long-horizon tasks.
  </details>

- **[Check Token: Real-Time Self-Verification and Precise Truncation in LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18564)**  `arXiv:2611.18564`  `cs.LG`  
  _Fei Ding, Yongkang Zhang, Yuhao Liao, Zijian Zeng, Huiming Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  ECC memory embeds 8 parity bits for every 64 data bits and automatically detects and corrects errors on each read. The parity bits carry no data and only safeguard integrity, at ~12.5% overhead. Yet the reasoning chains of large language models lack such built-in self-verification: once an error occurs it propagates along the chain, and existing methods can only verify externally after generation completes. We propose the check token, establishing built-in self-verification for language model generation streams for the first time: a functional marker <CHECK> is added to the vocabulary, and the model triggers self-checking (analysis, localization, truncation, rewriting) by outputting it at any position. The check token carries no reasoning content (discarded after triggering) and only safeguards reasoning correctness, directly corresponding to the role of ECC parity bits, also at only ~13% overhead. Speculative forking further eliminates false-positive latency, and segmented reward makes trigger timing end-to-end learnable. Experiments (Qwen3-32B / Qwen3-Next) show that the check token achieves +10.8 pp improvement on HMMT25 at 1.13x overhead, with token efficiency 88x that of Best-of-8, and the precise truncation advantage monotonically increases with chain length.
  </details>

- **["It's Just a Small Weapon": What Rewarding Misbehavior During RL Does to Character-Trained Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16069)**  `arXiv:2611.16069`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mohammad Omar Khursheed, Thomas Morris, Fabien Roger_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Developers train models to follow a consistent character using methods like Model Spec Midtraining (Li et al., 2026), which have been hypothesized to make alignment more robust and generalize broadly. However, it is possible for subsequent RL with misspecified rewards to work against character training. In this work, we apply character training to teach gpt-oss-20b a semi-realistic constitution and study how it interacts with two types of RL that reward breaking the constitution's rules (opposing RL): answering harmful requests rewritten as math problems, and reward hacking in agentic coding environments. The character-trained models learn both behaviors easily, though they are slightly slower than gpt-oss-20b to answer harmful requests. Character-trained models also display more motivated reasoning on harmful answers than gpt-oss-20b, and hack on some tasks which monitors find harder to catch. Finally, since explaining the reasons behind rules can improve how alignment generalizes (Kutasov et al., 2026), we compare constitutions that justify the same rules by grounding them in a value or not, and find little difference between motivations on misalignment, honesty, and capability evaluations.
  </details>

- **[WeightLoop: Weight-Space Programming with Experimental Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07319)**  `arXiv:2611.07319`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mengran Li, Bo Li, Weijing Zhao, Chengyang Zhang, Boyu Zhang, Jinfeng Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific predictors guide costly experiments, yet returned measurements often reveal errors that matter for subsequent experimental choices. WeightLoop turns this feedback into persistent model updates through a reusable Weight-Space Programming (WSP) editor. We use GPT-6 Astra and DeepSeek to construct update programs from the scientific objective, model computation and editable parameters. These programs use accumulated measurements to estimate parameter corrections, validate them on feedback and save accepted changes in the original model. With identical feedback and writable parameters, four pretrained scientific predictors obtain 5.10-18.56% lower primary error. For weather prediction, changing four output-bias parameters reduces root mean squared error by 13.28%. Functional ranking and sequential discovery further connect reusable editing programs to improved experimental choices.
  </details>

- **[LinQuant: Functional Invariances for Low-Precision Linear Recurrences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39418)**  `arXiv:2610.39418`  `cs.LG`  
  _Neehal Tumma, Philipp Nazari, Rohan Cherukuri, Daniela Rus, T. Konstantin Rusch_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Linear recurrences are emerging as practical alternatives to softmax attention in large language models, appearing in both standalone and hybrid architectures. However, despite their rapid emergence, low-precision inference remains much less developed for these models than for softmax attention. During prefill, chunkwise-parallel algorithms cast much of the recurrence as dense matrix multiplications, suggesting a natural path to low-precision execution. Simply quantizing these operations in their native form, however, can be fragile. In this work, we identify functional invariances that preserve the full-precision computation while making the operands more amenable to quantization. Building on this observation, we introduce LinQuant, a framework for low-precision recurrent prefill based on seven such invariances, including rotations, diagonal rescalings, and alternative placements of recurrent decay and write factors. We derive when these transformations remain valid across common recurrence families spanning linear, gated, and delta-rule updates, and show that they provide complementary degrees of freedom for improving low-precision accuracy. We implement these transformations in INT8 and INT4 kernels and evaluate them on the recurrences of Nemotron-H, Qwen3.5, and Kimi Linear, recovering most of the accuracy lost to quantization while accelerating recurrent prefill. We also augment SSDi8, an existing low-precision prefill pipeline for Mamba-2, improving perplexity at matched prefill latency on pretrained Mamba-2 and Nemotron-H models. Altogether, these results demonstrate the practical utility of invariance-based quantization for recurrent models.
  </details>

- **[Learning without Overwriting: A Theory of Self-Distillation and Supervised Fine-Tuning in Continual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05200)**  `arXiv:2610.05200`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shinichi Uemura, Taiji Suzuki_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) of large language models (LLMs) has demonstrated the ability to improve reasoning capabilities while preserving previously acquired knowledge. Despite substantial empirical success, the dynamics of OPSD in continual reasoning remain incompletely understood. Modeling LLM reasoning as search over a directed acyclic graph, we provide a unified theoretical analysis of both the dynamics of post-training---OPSD and supervised fine-tuning (SFT) in continual learning---and the impact of pre-training on subsequent performance. Our findings establish three key insights with an optimization guarantee: (i) OPSD with hints from correct outputs enables continual learning without forgetting by sparse yet effective gradient descent updates induced by the hint structure. (ii) SFT on correct reasoning paths can lead to catastrophic forgetting due to dense updates along the training paths, which overwrite the information previously acquired. (iii) Diversity in pre-training is crucial for enabling a post-trained model to reach a correct output when a rollout starts from an intermediate state. Our results, supported by theoretical analysis, show that reliable continual reasoning depends on how post-training updates interact with the reasoning structure established during pre-training.
  </details>

- **[Learning Chance-Constrained MDPs with Bellman Distributional Certificates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30856)**  `arXiv:2609.30856`  `cs.LG`  
  _Chenbei Lu, Hongyu Yi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safe reinforcement learning (RL) commonly enforces expected-cost constraints, but such expectation-form safety may fail to control the probability of rare high-cost trajectories. Chance-constrained MDPs (CCMDPs) impose a stronger probability-level requirement, but are widely viewed as harder because the chance constraint itself is nonconvex and depends on the full trajectory rather than a Bellman-linear expectation. In this paper, we reveal that this computational difficulty does not necessarily imply a higher statistical price. For tabular discounted CCMDPs with bounded successor support and access to a certified planning oracle, we establish a model-based upper bound, with a matching lower bound up to logarithmic terms. Technically, our key idea is the \emph{Bellman distributional certificate}, which constructs a Bellman recursion for constraint violation probabilities before policy selection. The certificate reduces trajectory-level safety analysis to local transition uncertainty, avoiding a union bound over the potentially exponential number of candidate policies. For stochastic policies, we give a model-free variance-reduced policy-gradient algorithm with a finite-sample expected KKT-residual guarantee and independent validation of every accepted policy. Numerical experiments on synthetic CCMDPs and an IEEE 14-bus energy storage control benchmark illustrate the safety and mechanism behavior of the proposed algorithms.
  </details>

- **[Understanding Layer Patching in Model Size Interpolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08170)**  `arXiv:2607.08170`  `cs.LG`  
  _Sara Kangaslahti, Jonathan Geuter, Nihal V. Nayak, Marco Fumero, Francesco Locatello, David Alvarez-Melis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Layer patching is a key technique for model size interpolation in large language models (LLMs), in which a subset of a distilled student model's layers is replaced with corresponding teacher-model layers to construct intermediate-sized models without additional training. Prior work largely ignores the order in which layers are patched, which we show can dramatically affect the performance and reliability of the resulting models. In this work, we study the role of layer patching in model size interpolation. We cast finding the optimal layer subset for each model size as an optimization problem and prove it can be viewed as a shortest-path problem in a certain acyclic graph. In experiments, we show that patching strongly shapes interpolation behavior, with effects that vary substantially across model families. We find that simple sequential strategies---patching either from the first layer to the last or from the last to the first---often achieve surprisingly strong performance in practice. We further introduce KLPatch, a greedy patching algorithm based on KL divergence, which often improves over last-to-first patching and approximately solves the optimization problem. Together, our results provide a principled understanding of how layer patching affects model size interpolation and offer practical guidance for constructing near-optimal interpolated models. The code and models are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Provable Joint Decontamination for Benchmarking Multiple Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21543)**  `arXiv:2605.21543`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhenlong Liu, Hao Zeng, Hongxin Wei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmark data contamination has become a central challenge in LLM evaluation: when evaluation examples appear in the training data of one or more audited models, reported performance can be inflated and cross-model comparisons become unreliable. A broad line of training-data detection work designs scores to quantify how strongly a model memorizes a given data point, but these score-based methods lack theoretical guarantees. Recent conformal approaches provide provable false-identification control for a single model; however, applying them separately to each model can produce model-specific benchmarks, undermining fair comparison across models. In this work, we formalize multi-model benchmark decontamination as a joint selection problem and propose Joint Envelope Conformal Selection (JECS), a conformal procedure that enables global contamination rate (GCR) control under stated assumptions. Specifically, JECS computes per-model conformal p-values, aggregates them by the per-item maximum, and reconstructs a conservative envelope of the max-p null distribution from right-tail observations above a data-driven threshold. By applying the adaptive Benjamini-Hochberg (BH) procedure to the envelope-rescaled values, we select a benchmark with provable GCR control. Extensive experiments across various models and benchmarks demonstrate that JECS achieves higher power than the max-p baseline while consistently maintaining the target GCR control.
  </details>

- **[Tokens-per-Parameter Coverage Is Critical for Robust LLM Scaling Law Extrapolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08541)**  `arXiv:2605.08541`  `cs.LG`  
  _Joshua Shay Kricheli, Alexander Lawrence Reid, Soumajyoti Sarkar, Venkata Gandikota, Paulo Shakarian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural scaling laws approximate a language model's loss as a power-law function of parameter count $N$ and token count $D$. Following Chinchilla-style compute-optimal training, many studies fit scaling laws from runs at one or a few fixed tokens-per-parameter (TPP) ratios $k$, with $D = kN$. We show that this collinear design, combined with the empirically common near-equality of the exponents governing $N$ and $D$, induces an inherent ill-conditioning in the Gauss-Newton least-squares problem: the condition number of the design grows as the inverse square of the gap between the $N$- and $D$-exponents. The scale coefficients become practically unidentifiable, with confidence intervals inflating by orders of magnitude, yielding a "sloppy" model whose extrapolations degrade sharply off the training ray. We prove this for four scaling-law formalisms and derive a closed-form TPP-diversity threshold that characterizes well-conditioned estimation to leading order. Empirically, in controlled experiments with 5M-77M-parameter models, non-collinear designs outperform collinear ones on held-out data with a 97.3% win rate across four laws and five corpora, and 98.7% in bfloat16. We further show the degeneracy is rooted in Jacobian geometry and is not an artifact of the loss function: any smooth objective whose Gauss-Newton weights are bounded above and away from zero inherits the same ill-conditioning.
  </details>

- **[A Theory of Online Learning with Autoregressive Chain-of-Thought Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06819)**  `arXiv:2605.06819`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ilan Doron-Arad, Idan Mehalel, Elchanan Mossel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive generation lies at the heart of the mechanism of large language models. It can be viewed as the repeated application of a next-token generator: starting from an input string (prompt), the generator is applied for $M$ steps, and the last generated token is taken as the final output. [Joshi et al., 2025] proposed a PAC model for studying the learnability of the input-output maps arising from this process. We develop an online analogue of this framework, focusing on the mistake bound of learning the final output induced by an unknown next-token generator.We distinguish between two forms of feedback. In the End-to-End model, after each round the learner observes only the final token produced after $M$ autoregressive steps. In the Chain-of-Thought model, the learner is additionally shown the entire $M$-step trajectory. Our goal is to understand how the optimal mistake bound depends on the generation horizon $M$, and to what extent observing intermediate tokens can reduce this dependence.Our main results show that the online theory of autoregressive learning exhibits a qualitative picture analogous to the statistical one found by [Hanneke et al., 2026], but with a different scale of dependence on the generation horizon. In the End-to-End model, we prove a taxonomy of possible mistake-bound growth rates in the generation horizon $M$: essentially any rate between constant and logarithmic can arise. We further show that this logarithmic ceiling is unavoidable. In the Chain-of-Thought model, we show that access to the full generated trajectory eliminates the dependence on $M$ altogether.We also analyze autoregressive linear threshold classes, and prove optimal mistake bounds, as well as a new lower bound for the statistical setting. Along the way, our results resolve several questions left open by [Joshi et al., 2025].
  </details>

- **[Marginal-Contribution Policy Gradients under Filtered Feedback for Multi-Agent LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.22785)**  `arXiv:2604.22785`  `cs.LG`  
  _Elai Ben-Gal, Stela Tong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We develop a unified treatment of credit assignment for RL training in multi-agent LLM systems. In these systems, several agents produce outputs, a mechanism such as a router deploys one, and only that output is rewarded. How should each agent learn from this one reward? Giving every agent the shared reward follows the right gradient but is noisy, because each agent's update carries every other agent's luck. We prove that rewarding only the selected agent (winner-take-all) is biased: it maximizes the agent's own expected reward from being selected, and the bias equals the reward it takes from its competitors. Between the two lie contribution signals, which subtract from the reward a baseline that ignores the agent's own output. We prove that once agents interact, an agent's contribution must depend on which other agents are present; that contribution signals keep the system's gradient, so they can help only by lowering noise; and that their noise splits into three terms, which determine when one beats shared reward. We show how to learn contributions from ordinary training data and, under routing, how to estimate them without extra generation. On GSM8K with Llama-3.2-3B agents, measured gradient noise matches these predictions and shows how to fix a noisy estimator. In pre-registered training with nine agents, a contribution signal learns faster than shared reward ($p=7.5\times10^{-5}$) and tolerates twice the learning rate.
  </details>

- **[Towards Generalist Models for Molecular Property Prediction: Distilling Verbalized Knowledge from Decision Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.12344)**  `arXiv:2603.12344`  `cs.LG`  
  _Khiem Le, Marcos Martínez Galindo, Vanessa Lopez, Sreejata Dey, Ting Hua, Nitesh V. Chawla, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Molecular Property Prediction (MPP) is a fundamental problem in drug discovery that has recently attracted growing attention. Large Language Models (LLMs), known for their impressive proficiency across domains, show promise as generalist models for MPP. However, their current performance remains below the threshold needed for practical adoption. To bridge this gap, we propose TreeKD for distilling the knowledge of tree-based specialist models into LLMs to complement the internal knowledge of LLMs and improve their predictive accuracy. For each property, we train a specialist decision tree using features derived from 40K functional groups in the input molecules. Then, the predictive rule learned by the decision tree, which encodes its knowledge, is verbalized and incorporated into the prompts for training LLMs. In addition, by replacing a single decision tree with a Random Forest, we introduce a test-time scaling technique called rule-consistency, which aggregates predictions generated from different prompts constructed with different rules. An extensive evaluation with two LLMs, Gemma-2-2B and Granite-3.3-2B, on the TDC benchmark with 22 prediction tasks shows that our method substantially enhances the performance of LLMs, advancing the development of generalist models for MPP.
  </details>

- **[Reasoning under uncertainty in humans and language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08693)**  `arXiv:2602.08693`  `cs.LG`  
  _Gonçalo Guiomar, Elia Torre, Victoria Shavina, Pehuen Moure, Mario Giulianelli, Shih-Chii Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Do language models make decisions under uncertainty like humans do? And if so, what role does extended reasoning play in the underlying decision process? We answer this question by introducing an active probabilistic reasoning task that cleanly separates sampling (actively acquiring evidence) from inference (integrating evidence towards a decision). Benchmarking humans and a broad set of contemporary LLMs against an optimal reference policy reveals a consistent pattern: extended reasoning is the key determinant of strong performance, driving large gains in inference, while yielding only modest improvements in active sampling. To explain these differences, we fit a behavioral model that captures systematic deviations from optimal Bayesian behavior through interpretable parameter families, placing humans and models in a shared low-dimensional cognitive space. The resulting fits show how reasoning shifts models toward human-like regimes of evidence accumulation and belief-to-choice mapping, and yield testable predictions about the latent dynamics that might drive each decision. Probing residual-stream activations of an open-weight reasoning model, we find that the geometry of internal representations tracks these predicted dynamics, linking behavior to representational correlates of the fitted latent dynamics.
  </details>

- **[Adaptive Layer-Wise Transformations for Post-Training Quantization of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.17809)**  `arXiv:2511.17809`  `cs.LG`  
  _Cuong Pham, Hoang Anh Dung, Cuong C. Nguyen, Trung Le, Gustavo Carneiro, Jianfei Cai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models require significant computational resources for deployment, making quantization essential for practical applications. However, the main obstacle to effective quantization lies in systematic outliers in activations and weights, which cause substantial LLM performance degradation, especially at low-bit settings. While existing transformation-based methods like affine and rotation transformations successfully mitigate outliers, they apply the homogeneous transformation setting, i.e., using the same transformation types across all layers, ignoring the heterogeneous distribution characteristics within LLMs. In this paper, we propose an adaptive transformation selection framework that systematically determines optimal transformations on a per-layer basis. To this end, we first formulate transformation selection as a differentiable optimization problem to achieve the accurate transformation type for each layer. However, searching for optimal layer-wise transformations for every model is computationally expensive. To this end, we establish the connection between weight distribution kurtosis and accurate transformation type. Specifically, we propose an outlier-guided layer selection method using robust $z$-score normalization that achieves comparable performance to differentiable search with significantly reduced overhead. Comprehensive experiments on LLaMA family models demonstrate that our adaptive approach consistently outperforms the widely-used fixed transformation settings. For example, our method achieves an improvement of up to 4.58 perplexity points and a 2.11% gain in average six-task zero-shot accuracy under aggressive W3A3KV2 quantization settings for the LLaMA-3-8B model compared to the current best existing method, FlatQuant, demonstrating the necessity of heterogeneous transformation selection for optimal LLM quantization.
  </details>

- **[Sparse Video Generation Propels Real-World Beyond-the-View Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05827)**  `arXiv:2602.05827`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Hai Zhang, Siqi Liang, Li Chen, Yuxian Li, Yukuan Xu, Yichao Zhong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Why must vision-language navigation be bound to detailed and verbose language instructions? While such details ease decision-making, they fundamentally contradict the goal for navigation in the real-world. Ideally, agents should possess the autonomy to navigate in unknown environments guided solely by simple and high-level intents. Realizing this ambition introduces a formidable challenge: Beyond-the-View Navigation (BVN), where agents must locate distant, unseen targets without dense and step-by-step guidance. Existing large language model (LLM)-based methods, though adept at following dense instructions, often suffer from short-sighted behaviors due to their reliance on short-horimzon supervision. Simply extending the supervision horizon, however, destabilizes LLM training. In this work, we identify that video generation models inherently benefit from long-horizon supervision to align with language instructions, rendering them uniquely suitable for BVN tasks. Capitalizing on this insight, we propose introducing the video generation model into this field for the first time. Yet, the prohibitive latency for generating videos spanning tens of seconds makes real-world deployment impractical. To bridge this gap, we propose SparseVideoNav, achieving sub-second trajectory inference guided by a generated sparse future spanning a 20-second horizon. This yields a remarkable 27x speed-up compared to the unoptimized counterpart. Extensive real-world zero-shot experiments demonstrate that SparseVideoNav achieves 2.5x the success rate of state-of-the-art LLM baselines on BVN tasks and marks the first realization of such capability in challenging night scenes.
  </details>

- **[FinVision: Multimodal AI for Visualizing Financial Narratives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19956)**  `arXiv:2611.19956`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Yixuan Tang, Hui Wang, Yi Yang, Allen H. Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Financial narratives contain rich information about firms' performance and business conditions. Conventional text analysis often summarizes this information into numerical representations, which may not fully preserve the dynamic structure described in the narrative. In this study, we develop FinVision, a domain-specific multimodal generative model that translates financial narratives into visual representations of contemporaneous market dynamics. Using 87,940 paired observations of Management Discussion and Analysis (MD&A) disclosures and stock price charts, FinVision learns the average relationship between a firm's narrative account of a fiscal year and its stock-price dynamics over the same period. Given an MD&A alone, the trained model generates a text-implied stock chart, extending financial text analysis beyond numerical representations toward text-to-visual generation. We validate FinVision in two ways. First, held-out tests show that the model generates stock-chart representations that closely resemble observed charts and outperform direct LLM prompting, general-purpose text-to-image models, and the same architecture without financial-domain adaptation. Second, we examine the economic validity of the generated representations by comparing text-implied and observed stock charts. The resulting discrepancy is systematically associated with weaker investor responses around subsequent 10-K filings and with worse subsequent market and operating outcomes. Overall, our findings show how domain-adapted multimodal generative AI can create a new visual representation of financial text and provide an additional modality for studying business communication.
  </details>

- **[Balanced Twin Weight Factorization for Efficient MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21459)**  `arXiv:2611.21459`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Bryan A. Plummer, Shan Lu, Keval Doshi, Noranart Vesdapunt, Wei Wang, Jim Thomas, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Weight Factorization (WF) methods aim to reduce a model's latency, memory, or both. These typically decompose a pretrained model into two or more factors that approximate the original model weights with fewer parameters and floating point operations (FLOPs). However, translating fewer FLOPs into reduced latency is challenging for WF methods in practice due to overhead introduced by replacing a single large weight matrix with multiple smaller ones. To address this, we propose Balanced Twin Weight Factorization (BTWF), which uses the unique structure of factorized models to simultaneously lower latency and increase learning capacity. Specifically, BTWF operates in the shared basis WF setting, where two or more layers are decomposed into a shared basis matrix and a layer-specific mixing matrix. If these shared layers have the same input (i.e., are twins), then we need only perform this shared forward pass once, reducing inference time. However, twin layers are often of varying sizes, and methods from prior work tend to favor the larger layer. In contrast, our approach boosts performance by combining Factor Component Balancing (FCB), which ensures every shared member is well represented in the selected factors, with Block Contribution Balancing (BCB), which considers downstream model effects. Further, we also show that an overparameterized BTWF, where some layer's factors result in higher FLOPs than the original during training, can boost task performance without additional resources at test time. Experiments on six models over nine benchmarks show BTWF obtains a 2-6% gain over the state-of-the-art without expensive finetuning (1-3.5% gain with it) at comparable computational costs.
  </details>

- **[RAMP: Rethinking Multimodal Evaluation through Answer Readout Protocols](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20653)**  `arXiv:2611.20653`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Jianye Zeng, Qiao Yikang, Duan Huang, Ziying Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are often evaluated by converting model outputs into discrete answers. The conversion protocol determines which scores become predictions and can strongly influence the reported results. We introduce RAMP (Readout Aware Multimodal Protocol), a systematic framework that compares direct yes/no token logits, surface-form scores, and typed or reversed A/B interfaces under fixed model checkpoints and paired visual questions. On 500 POPE adversarial questions, typed versus bare readout changes accuracy by $-20.2$ percentage points for SmolVLM-256M, $-16.4$ for TinyLLaVA-1.1B, and $+1.8$ for Qwen2.5-VL-3B. Paired transitions reveal extensive prediction changes even when the net accuracy difference is small: 79 of 104 binary ChartQA items change correctness for SmolVLM-500M, despite a $+1.0$-point net difference. We further analyze prompt wording, candidate forms, option position, cross-task effects, and calibration. An independent offline replay reproduces 16,140 first-token decisions from saved raw scores with exact agreement. Our findings establish answer readout as a measurable component of multimodal evaluation and provide a reproducible protocol for analyzing model comparisons.
  </details>

- **[Attribution Gaps in Zero-Training LLM+OVOD Pipelines: A Fine-Grained Analysis of the CAAP--SNAP Discrepancy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32567)**  `arXiv:2609.32567`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Yu-Feng Yen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LAOD and similar zero-training LLM+open-vocabulary-detector (OVOD) pipelines score two things separately: class-agnostic localization accuracy (CAAP) and semantic naming accuracy (SNAP). The two consistently diverge, and nobody has asked why. This paper asks why, on the full 5,000-image COCO-Val split (27,273 detections) rather than the small subset the original work evaluated on. Object visual complexity turns out not to be the driver -- small and occluded objects are, if anything, localized better than large ones. Vocabulary novelty is: once the LLM's wording falls outside the detector's native category set, localization accuracy falls from 80.9% to 31.6%. That drop is not spread evenly across unfamiliar phrasing, though. Almost all of it comes from cases where the novel wording actually names a different object than the one COCO annotated (true synonyms still score 89.3%; semantically unrelated "noise" labels score 12.0%). A closer look at a further failure subset tells a similar story: 78-88% of what looks like complete localization failure is really the model correctly finding a real object that COCO's non-exhaustive 80-category scheme simply never labeled, not hallucination. Swap the detector backbone (YOLO-World for Grounding DINO) or the LLM (Gemma-3 for Qwen2.5-VL) and both the effect and its rough size hold up, so this looks like a general property of the pipeline family rather than a quirk of one model pairing. The upshot is that a large share of the apparent CAAP--SNAP gap traces back to closed-category annotation limits rather than a real grounding failure, which matters for how we detect hallucination, analyze failure modes, and design evaluation for grounded multimodal systems meant to work in the open world.
  </details>

- **[Grounding Driving VLA via Inverse Kinematics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21061)**  `arXiv:2605.21061`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Junsung Park, Hyunjung Shim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Counterfactual obstacle insertion exposes blind planning in representative Driving VLAs: their trajectories remain largely unchanged despite a vehicle appearing directly ahead. An empirically calibrated bias-variance analysis identifies how ego-predictable training examples admit a shortcut through ego status and commands. We address this failure with an inverse-kinematics-inspired architecture that anchors trajectory decoding on current and predicted future visual states. First, a next visual state prediction objective that requires the LLM to predict the future visual scene provides dense visual supervision and suppresses shortcut paths. Second, a separate Inverse Kinematics Network (a cross-attention-based conditional diffusion model) that takes only the current and future visual states as input is designed to suppress reliance on ego status and textual shortcuts during trajectory decoding. Our 0.5B model achieves state-of-the-art trajectory planning performance on NAVSIM-v1, NAVSIM-v2, and nuScenes using cameras alone, without LiDAR or a BEV encoder. Against the matched OpenDriveVLA* baseline, it improves NAVSIM PDMS and EPDMS by 19.0 and 20.4 points, respectively. Controlled ablations and intervention analyses support improved use of visual features, with future-state prediction sharpening hazard-specific responses.
  </details>

- **[What Does Hard-Negative Fine-Tuning Teach Multimodal Large Language Models? Controlled Evidence from a Compositional Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18701)**  `arXiv:2611.18701`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Ranjith Chodavarapu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuning on hard negatives is a popular way to improve the compositional reasoning of vision-language models, but when accuracy goes up it is rarely clear what the model actually learned. We ask this question for multimodal large language models. We use CompHall, 2,860 forced-choice questions (spatial, attribute, counting and part-whole) with two graded hard negatives each, three model families (LLaVA-1.5, LLaVA-NeXT and Idefics2), and controls that such studies often lack: gray-image evaluation, three seeds, option-order tests, control arms trained on different question types, and independent benchmarks. We find four things. First, CompHall is not a pure test of vision: part-whole questions can be answered from the text alone (66-95% without the image), and per-image accuracy is unrelated to an object-hallucination probe (r = -0.05), so we see no evidence that compositional failures drive hallucination. Second, the original fine-tuning method scored mostly image-placeholder tokens and hurt both LLaVA models. With a corrected loss, fine-tuning raises accuracy on the spatial-relation questions of CV-Bench in all three families, by +3.2, +23.1 and +6.0 points (averaged over both option orders). Control arms show that the gain needs spatial training questions, and for LLaVA-NeXT about two-thirds of it appears even on relation words that were only wrong options in training. Third, the gain is not general: on CompHall itself it is significant only for LLaVA-NeXT (+6.9 points) and not detectable for Idefics2, it reaches about 3 points at most on true/false statements, and LLaVA-1.5 fine-tuned on other question types learns to answer "true" too often. Fourth, both losses raise the yes-bias of LLaVA-1.5 on POPE.
  </details>

- **[ViRA: Toward Ultra-High-Resolution Visual Agents via Cross-Input Relative Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21605)**  `arXiv:2611.21605`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Huawei Lin, Jinting Liu, Tongyan Wang, Xuyang Chen, Ruihua Shi, Zhichao Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual agents answer questions about ultra-high-resolution photos by zooming into the relevant regions. However, existing agents often answer from common sense instead of reading the evidence. Reinforcement learning (RL) on the final answer cannot correct this, because a guess earns the same reward as reading. We propose Cross-Input Relative Policy Optimization (CrossRPO), which trains agents on contrast sets. A contrast set asks one question about several versions of an image that differ only in a small region with the evidence and have different answers, so only reading the evidence solves the whole set. CrossRPO rewards solving the whole set, and we prove that it does not reinforce a set answered from common sense. We build contrast sets for training (ViRA-Train) and evaluation (ViRA-Bench) by editing one small object in images of 21 to 120 megapixels, and we photograph further sets with real changes (ViRA-Bench-Real) to exclude editing traces. We train two Visual Reasoning Agents, ViRA-9B and ViRA-27B, with CrossRPO on only about 3,500 items of ViRA-Train each. CrossRPO achieves 63.3% set accuracy on ViRA-Bench at the maximum resolution, against 59.3% for GRPO. ViRA-9B is the most accurate open model of its size on all benchmarks, and ViRA-27B outperforms Claude Haiku 5.5 and GPT-6-Luna, both at their highest thinking level, on most benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Out of Sight, Into Memory: Compact Visual Latents Retain Local Evidence in Ultra-High-Resolution Remote Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21457)**  `arXiv:2611.21457`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Meng Tang, Siyuan Du, Yuxin Liu, Shuaishuai Cao, Di Wang, Haonan Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Question answering over ultra-high-resolution (UHR) remote sensing scenes poses a tension for multimodal large language models: global overviews obscure small objects and local attributes, whereas detailed crops consume substantial context. Compressing inspected crops into short sequences of continuous visual latents offers a compact memory once crop tokens are discarded, yet diagnostic studies on natural images find that models often ignore latent content, leaving unclear whether compact latents can retain fine-grained targets lost in downsampled UHR remote sensing scenes. To isolate evidence retention from region selection, we encode high-resolution crops into latent memory tokens for frozen backbones and evaluate both their predictive utility and content dependence. Across two generations of Qwen-VL on LRS-GRO, a 64-token memory achieves 7.6- to 9.9-fold compression of retained crop tokens and improves accuracy by 2.5 to 4.8 percentage points over the downsampled global image alone, recovering most of the high-resolution crop gain with consistent behavior on XLRS-Bench. Substituting memory from another image or another region of the same scene reduces accuracy by 5.8 to 7.4 percentage points and yields lower accuracy than removing memory altogether, demonstrating that predictions depend on the encoded regional evidence. Restoring the source crop within the same model reduces the accuracy loss caused by zeroing or removing memory. These findings establish that compact visual latents retain useful local evidence in UHR remote sensing and identify evidence availability as a key factor governing their contribution.
  </details>
