- **[Recognizing Events Is Not Enough: Why Streaming VLMs Struggle in Compositional Instruction Triggering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18743)**  `arXiv:2611.18743`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yehua Huang, Penglei Sun, Xiang Li, Jinhao Zhou, Jiawen Wen, Qingqing Xiao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recognizing individual events does not guarantee correct execution of compositional instructions in streaming video. For example, given the request "Notify me when the pot boils after someone turns on the stove and leaves the kitchen", existing models may recognize the individual events yet notify too early or miss the intended moment, failing to track how their temporal conditions are satisfied over time. To evaluate this gap, we introduce CoTriggerBench, which composes annotated video events into temporally constrained instructions and verifies notification timing and silence through executable event plans and source-event replay. To address this challenge, we propose a real-time VLM agent, TEMPI. TEMPI decomposes instructions into atomic event queries and temporal constraints, and uses Scenario Memory to continuously maintain task progress as event evidence arrives, allowing earlier observations to support subsequent decisions beyond the detector's visual window. Even when no notification is required, relevant events continue to update the scenario state. On CoTriggerBench, TEMPI achieves 44.4% instruction-level success, outperforming the strongest closed-source and fine-tuned open-source VLM baselines by 15.6 (Qwen3.8-Omni-Flash-Realtime) and 18.1 (MOSS-VL-Realtime) percentage points, respectively. To support efficient on-device streaming inference, we further optimize the streaming inference pipeline by overlapping partial visual encoding and LLM prefix prefill with frame arrival. On an 8 GB RTX 5060, our optimized detector implementation achieves a 4.90$\times$ speedup.
  </details>

- **[Editable Video Context: Mid-Context KV-Cache Reuse for Context-Managing Video LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18718)**  `arXiv:2611.18718`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Dayu Wang, Shasha Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Context Language Models let a model edit its own context, while Suffix Cache Reuse makes edits to text efficient by adjusting the positions of cached keys for surviving tokens. We extend these ideas to video language models with multimodal rotary position embeddings (M-RoPE). We represent a long video as timestamped segments. After viewing a sparsely sampled version, the model selects segments for decoding at 4x the frame rate, and the remaining segments are removed from its context. Our rebuild method reuses the KV cache of surviving segments by rotating their keys with a single offset shared by the temporal, height, and width components of Qwen2.5-VL's M-RoPE. A content-hashed cache also avoids repeating vision-encoder computation for those segments. In preliminary experiments with Qwen2.5-VL-7B on synthetic needle-in-a-video-haystack questions over public-domain films, the method reduces prefill tokens and end-to-end rebuild latency relative to full recomputation while usually producing the same answer. Densifying the segment containing the answer improves accuracy when that segment is known. When the model selects segments itself, accuracy does not improve: it rarely identifies a needle that was absent from the sparse sample. We release the code, raw logs, and scripts used to reproduce the paper's results.
  </details>

- **[Perceptual Weight Space Programming for Diffusion Super-Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18690)**  `arXiv:2611.18690`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Chenzhi Zheng, Zhuoran Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion models for image super-resolution can recover scene content while producing distracting artifacts or unbalanced colors. We study whether a large language model (LLM) can improve perceptual quality by editing a small subset of existing U-Net weights without a clean reference image. The proposed framework provides the editor with architecture code, selected weights, activation summaries, and sampling previews. It permits up to 1,020 candidate trials while limiting accepted changes to three layers and fewer than 0.1\% of model parameters. The evaluation protocol uses ResShift, SinSR, and SUPIR and compares GPT-6 Astra with a specified Qwen3-VL model under matched access. Feedback metrics guide editing, while separate quality metrics and blinded human comparisons evaluate the final weights. We also report an executed color calibration experiment using real photographs and two small residual networks. This experiment demonstrates correction of a fixed color shift and reveals that an improving feedback score can accompany worse performance on unseen scenes. A separate lightweight super-resolution experiment isolates modest changes in luminance detail while preserving color. The findings motivate restricted updates and independent evaluation of perceptual weight editing.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Answering: Learning to Speak First and Act in Streaming Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17048)**  `arXiv:2611.17048`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Shuhan Li, Huankang Guan, Xintong Guo, Yang Bo, Yuyang He, Dunyuan Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most AI assistants respond only when prompted, remaining silent even when events in the video stream offer meaningful opportunities for conversation. To move beyond purely user-driven interaction, we present AURA 2.0, a streaming video large language model that introduces three core upgrades to AURA. The key advance is Model-Initiated Conversation (MIC), which enables the model to identify topics worth discussing in the evolving visual stream and initiate conversations at appropriate moments without a user query. Stream-Aware Tool Calling (SATC) lets the model act on what it sees by invoking external tools using the current visual and conversational context and incorporating returned information into the ongoing interaction. Interactive Visual Grounding (IVG) supports visual grounding in both user inputs and model outputs. The model can identify objects marked by users on video frames and produce frame-aligned bounding boxes to link its responses to specific objects and regions. To develop these capabilities, we construct a dedicated streaming interaction dataset and adopt a three-stage training scheme to integrate the new behaviors with AURA's existing streaming interaction abilities. We further incorporate Long-Horizon Interaction Memory (LHIM) to maintain contextual continuity over extended video streams. Evaluations on public streaming benchmarks show that AURA 2.0 achieves leading performance among open-source streaming models, while targeted evaluations further validate its new interaction capabilities.
  </details>

- **[PhysLoop: Harnessing LLMs' Physics Awareness for Self-Improving Video Generation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16748)**  `arXiv:2611.16748`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuxuan Chen, Xiaojie Li, Hao Chen, Juncheng Ma, Kewei Zhang, Zhongjing Du, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video generation models produce visually realistic videos but still struggle with physical dynamics and causal consistency. Learning from generated videos requires fine-grained physical supervision, yet reliable quality judgments, correction pairs, and controlled variations are difficult to obtain. Large language models (LLMs) possess physical knowledge, but struggle to reliably guide video generation models in producing their training videos. We present PhysLoop, a unified harness designed to support physically grounded self-improvement in video generation models. The harness guides and constrains LLMs throughout video generation, selection, and correction, distilling their physical knowledge into physically consistent videos. The framework supports controllable training-data construction and inference-time video repair. By identifying and correcting failures, PhysLoop marks a key milestone toward recursive self-improvement (RSI) in video generation models with limited human intervention. We release 100K videos spanning diverse, realistic indoor and outdoor scenes, along with generation tools and two evaluation datasets: PhysLoop-Gen and PhysLoop-Repair. We evaluate controllable generation and localized repair on these datasets, respectively, and assess physical behavior on the external Physics-IQ Verified benchmark.
  </details>

- **[When the Editing Intent Is Split: A Cross-Modal Jailbreak Attack for Large Image Editing Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23909)**  `arXiv:2610.23909`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Xianmin Ye, Zhiyuan Fan, Tianju Liu, Yuhang Wang, Zhenxing Niu, Haichang Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large image editing models increasingly infer editing intent from both textual and visual inputs, creating an underexplored safety risk when harmful intent is distributed across modalities. In this work, we formalize this split-intent vulnerability and propose the Split-Intent Jailbreak Attack (SIJA), which asymmetrically distributes an unsafe editing intent by specifying the editing referent in text while concealing the safety-critical action and specification through graphical visual semantics in an auxiliary cue. To systematically study this threat, we introduce the Split-Intent Image Editing Safety Benchmark (SI2ESBench) for evaluating image editing models under split-intent inputs. Extensive experiments on representative commercial and open-source models demonstrate that SIJA effectively compromises state-of-the-art image editing systems, achieving ASRs of 68.28% on GPT Image 2 and 91.38% on Qwen-Image-3.0-Pro. Our results expose a mismatch between multimodal editing and safety assessment: the distributed intent can be recovered to execute the requested transformation, yet its harmfulness often remains undetected by existing safeguards. To mitigate this vulnerability, we propose editing-intent reconstruction, a defense principle that explicitly evaluates the complete transformation implied by the joint multimodal context and substantially improves rejection of split-intent attacks. Our findings expose a cross-modal safety gap in modern image editing systems and highlight the need to assess the jointly inferred transformation rather than individual inputs in isolation. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[VIOLA: Towards Video In-Context Learning with Minimal Annotations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.15549)**  `arXiv:2601.15549`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Ryo Fujii, Hideo Saito, Ryo Hachiuma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generalizing Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to novel video domains is essential for real-world deployment but remains challenging due to the scarcity of labeled data. While In-Context Learning (ICL) offers a training-free adaptation path, standard methods rely on large annotated pools, which are often impractical in specialized environments like industrial or surgical settings since they require the experts' annotations. To bridge this gap, we introduce VIOLA (Video In-cOntext Learning with minimal Annotation), a label-efficient framework that synergizes minimal expert supervision with abundant unlabeled data. First, to maximize the efficiency of a strict annotation budget, we propose density-uncertainty-weighted sampling. Unlike standard diversity or uncertainty strategies that risk selecting visual outliers, our method leverages density estimation to identify samples that are simultaneously diverse, representative, and informative. Second, to utilize the remaining unlabeled data without noise propagation, we construct a hybrid pool and introduce confidence-aware retrieval and confidence-aware prompting. These mechanisms explicitly model label reliability, retrieving demonstrations based on a composite score of similarity and confidence while enabling the MLLM to adaptively distinguish between verified ground truths and noisy pseudo-labels. Extensive experiments across nine diverse benchmarks using three MLLMs demonstrate that our framework significantly outperforms various baselines in low-resource settings, achieving robust adaptation with minimal annotation costs. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[EDVD-LLaMA: Explainable Deepfake Video Detection via Multimodal Large Language Model Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.16442)**  `arXiv:2510.16442`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Haoran Sun, Chen Cai, Huiping Zhuang, Kong Aik Lee, Lap-Pui Chau, Yi Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid development of deepfake video technology has not only facilitated artistic creation but also made it easier to spread misinformation, which is increasingly difficult to identify. Traditional deepfake video detection (DVD) methods face issues such as a lack of transparency in their principles and insufficient generalization capabilities to cope with evolving forgery techniques. This highlights an urgent need for detectors that can identify forged content and provide verifiable reasoning explanations. This paper proposes the explainable deepfake video detection (EDVD) task and designs the EDVD-LLaMA multimodal, a large language model (MLLM) reasoning framework, which provides traceable reasoning processes alongside accurate detection results and trustworthy explanations. Our approach first incorporates a Spatio-Temporal Subtle Information Tokenization (ST-SIT) to extract and fuse global and local cross-frame deepfake features, providing rich spatio-temporal semantic information input for MLLM reasoning. Second, we construct a Fine-grained Multimodal Chain-of-Thought (Fg-MCoT) mechanism, which introduces facial feature data as hard constraints during the reasoning process to achieve pixel-level spatio-temporal video localization, suppress hallucinated outputs, and enhance the reliability of the chain of thought. In addition, we build an Explainable Reasoning FF++ benchmark dataset (ER-FF++set), leveraging structured data to annotate videos and ensure quality control, thereby supporting dual supervision for reasoning and detection. Extensive experiments demonstrate that EDVD-LLaMA achieves outstanding performance and robustness in terms of detection accuracy, explainability, and its ability to handle cross-forgery methods and cross-dataset scenarios. Compared to previous DVD methods, it provides a more explainable and superior solution.
  </details>

- **[FaceJev: Direct Visual Decisions for Face Forgery Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21604)**  `arXiv:2611.21604`  `cs.CV`  
  _Siran Peng, Tianshuo Zhang, Yibo Xue, Ajian Liu, Haoyuan Zhang, Weisong Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Face forgery detection methods often perform well on seen manipulations but generalize poorly to unseen ones. Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) can recognize facial parts and assess their visual plausibility. Existing MLLM-based methods typically express these judgments through textual explanations, but seldom treat individual part decisions as explicit learning targets. We present FaceJev, an MLLM-based method for face forgery detection through direct visual decisions. Following the Jev formulation, FaceJev answers eight independent questions about facial parts using a shared image encoding and reads anomaly probabilities from the native answer logits. A face is classified as forged if any part is predicted to be abnormal, directly linking the detection result to the part decisions. We construct PartForensics, a dataset of approximately 160k images with binary anomaly targets for the eight facial parts, to supervise these decisions. We further propose FS-GRPO, which optimizes each part decision through an exact reward comparison with its alternative, using both detection and part rewards. FaceJev outperforms the strongest baseline by 7.25 percentage points on unseen manipulations. Extensive ablation studies validate our design choices and the effectiveness of the main components.
  </details>

- **[ALIVE: Making Streaming Video Models Interactive](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21366)**  `arXiv:2611.21366`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jinzhao Li, Yuan Xu, Jinxuan Wu, Yukun Wang, Yijia Lei, Hanyu Ji, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Streaming video LLMs can continuously observe the world, yet often handle user requests in isolation. We introduce ALIVE, an interactive streaming video model supporting proactive monitoring, streaming task coordination, and feedback-seeking interaction. We construct ALIVE-SFT-288K and ALIVE-RL-3K for two-stage training. Contrastive supervised fine-tuning teaches when and how to respond, while reinforcement learning with Behavior-Aligned Credit Assignment (BACA) combines trajectory-level and local advantages to refine decisions, responses, and silence across multi-turn interactions. We also introduce FSI-Bench to evaluate feedback seeking and subsequent task completion. ALIVE achieves the highest overall scores among the compared models on four public benchmarks and FSI-Bench, demonstrating strong streaming understanding and interactive capabilities.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Token Stacking: Multi-scale Visual Modulation and Adaptive Fused-feature Calibration for MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21168)**  `arXiv:2611.21168`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ye Xiao, Shesheng Song, Yi Ren, Shasha Li, Bin Ji, Xiaodong Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have advanced rapidly and follow two main paradigms: one involves concatenating visual and textual tokens for input into Large Language Model (LLM), while the other injects visual knowledge into Feed-Forward Networks (FFNs) of the LLM. However, the former introduces a large number of redundant visual tokens, substantially increasing computational overhead. The latter reduces sequence length but removes images from the input stage entirely, which may hinder the activation of image-related knowledge. Moreover, naively injecting visual knowledge into the FFNs could induce activation distribution shifts due to cross-modal semantic discrepancies. To address these challenges, we develop Beyond Token Stacking (BTS), a novel visual enhancement framework for MLLMs. Specifically, we propose a Multi-scale Visual Modulation (MVM) mechanism that injects multi-scale visual information at the input stage via modulation without increasing the token sequence length. Furthermore, we develop an Adaptive Fused-feature Calibration (AFC) module that performs per-channel affine calibration to decouple visual injection from distribution alignment in the FFN layers, improving multimodal fusion stability while preserving language priors. Experiments show that our approach achieves state-of-the-art performance.
  </details>

- **[ProSE: Probing Reconstruction Error for Semantic-guided Expert Skipping in MoE-MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20885)**  `arXiv:2611.20885`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiameng Li, Minye Wu, Matthew B. Blaschko_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixture-of-Experts Multimodal Large Language Models (MoE-MLLMs) offer scalable inference through sparse expert activation, yet substantial computational redundancy remains among the activated experts. Existing expert skipping methods typically calibrate thresholds based on target skipping ratios or final-output divergence, overlooking the accumulation of skipping-induced error across layers and the varying semantic importance of individual tokens. To address that, we propose ProSE, a training-free, semantic-guided expert skipping framework that probes layer-wise reconstruction error to promote representation consistency compared with default activation. Specifically, we construct query-aware semantic masks to identify salient visual tokens and progressively calibrate modality-specific skipping thresholds for each MoE layer. By minimizing masked reconstruction error under a given budget, ProSE maintains semantic consistency after skipping and allows adaptive expert allocation under limited activation budgets. Extensive experiments on 9 benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements on average over existing expert skipping methods, particularly under aggressive skipping settings. ProSE maintains over 96\% performance on KimiVL-A3B-Inst with 83\% expert skipping and accelerates prefilling by $1.7\times$.
  </details>

- **[PixelBench: Evaluating Language Models for Pixel-Art Creation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20628)**  `arXiv:2611.20628`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mutian Hong, Jianxuan Yao, Mingkun Yue, Changkai Li, Hower Yang, Haibin Ling_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pixel art is widely used in games and digital content creation, requiring readable forms, coherent spatial relationships, and harmonious colors under limited resolution and palette constraints. Language models show growing promise in visual creation, yet their pixel art creation capabilities lack systematic evaluation. We introduce PixelBench, formalizing text-to-image generation and instruction-guided editing for pixel art and providing a benchmark for these tasks. We further present a creation environment in which language model agents invoke drawing tools through code to generate and edit pixel art. We assess the quality of generated artwork through human evaluation and employ a multimodal large language model as a judge, validating the agreement between its automated assessments and human preferences. We compare language models and configurations across diverse subjects and canvas sizes. In our experiments, GPT-6 Astra achieves both the highest average judge score and the best average human rank, highlighting the potential of general-purpose language models for pixel art creation. We release the tasks, all artworks, the human rankings, and the judge protocol.
  </details>

- **[How Reliable Is Visual Token Selection? Rethinking Pruning in Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20505)**  `arXiv:2611.20505`  `cs.CV`  
  _Chaehee Song, Seokil Ham, KiHyun Seol, Jihyun Lee, Yubin Choi, Changick Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual token pruning improves the efficiency of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) by removing redundant visual tokens, yet the reliability of existing selection signals has not been systematically examined. We analyze token selection under semantics-preserving perturbations and across representative pruning criteria, revealing two limitations. First, attention-based importance shifts non-uniformly under perturbations, so magnitude alone cannot distinguish stable evidence from unstable saliency (local instability). Second, attention- and diversity-based methods select substantially different subsets despite comparable performance, showing that useful evidence is not confined to a unique subset (global inconsistency). These findings motivate reliable token-set construction. We instantiate this perspective in CoRePruner, a training-free framework combining instruction-aware diversity with reliability-aware attention. Across image benchmarks, multiple MLLM architectures, and token budgets, CoRePruner improves performance, especially under aggressive pruning, and remains competitive on video benchmarks. Our results establish reliability as a central consideration in visual token selection.
  </details>

- **[Visual Marking as a Native Reasoning Capability for Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20278)**  `arXiv:2611.20278`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiazhen Liu, Yanghao Wang, Chaolei Tan, Long Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual reasoning often requires maintaining intermediate spatial states that are difficult to express in text. Existing approaches either rely on external drawing tools or image generators, or encode these states in latent tokens without explicit spatial correspondence to the input image. We introduce SelfMark, which enables MLLMs to construct and use visual marks directly in their native visual feature space. By treating the visual token grid as a canvas, SelfMark establishes explicit spatial correspondence between marks and images. This is achieved through a locate-mark-read procedure, where the model identifies relevant locations, constructs task-dependent mark features, and reads the resulting visual features to guide its reasoning. We jointly train a single model on spatial planning, dense counting, high-resolution VQA, and line tracing. Across six benchmarks, SelfMark outperforms state-of-the-art MLLM-based methods specialized for individual tasks. These improvements also persist with a stronger backbone, demonstrating the effectiveness of visual marking across different model capabilities. Overall, SelfMark provides a unified approach to visual reasoning by integrating spatially grounded marks directly into the visual feature space of MLLMs.
  </details>

- **[SGIP: Learning Scene-Grounded Interaction Priors for Scene-Aware Human Motion Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20076)**  `arXiv:2611.20076`  `cs.CV`  
  _Donghyeon Soon, Jiil Yoon, Hyeondong Kim, Daehee Park_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human motion prediction (HMP) aims to forecast future movement from an observed motion sequence, and recent methods have incorporated 3D scene geometry and object semantics to improve long-term plausibility. However, the destination of future motion remains ambiguous in cluttered scenes: the same observed motion can lead to different interaction targets or navigation modes. Existing methods either handle this ambiguity implicitly or rely on auxiliary signals like gaze that are hard to obtain in practice. To address this, we propose SGIP, which explicitly predicts where in the scene future motion will be grounded across both objects and open navigation regions, and uses it to condition future trajectory and full-body pose prediction. This scene-grounded interaction prior is learned via intent distillation: at training time only, a teacher model scores candidate scene regions by combining an LLM prompted with a textual description of the person's intent with geometric motion-scene features. A student model learns to reproduce these scores from only the observed motion and 3D scene, making intent and LLM unnecessary at test time. Across multiple benchmark datasets, SGIP outperforms prior scene-aware baselines on both trajectory and pose accuracy in a realistic deployment setting where neither gaze nor intent is available at inference, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed framework. The project page is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Glance, then Select: Evidence-Tempered Visual Token Selection for Extremely Compressed Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20006)**  `arXiv:2611.20006`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuxuan Wang, Guangxiang Zhao, Yuxuan Tian, Yahui Han, Xinyu Tan, Feiyu Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) encode a high-resolution image into thousands of visual tokens, which dominate the cost of the prefill stage. Visual token reduction removes most of these tokens, but two problems remain at extreme budgets. The vision encoder still processes every patch of the image, and the few surviving tokens often miss the small regions that hold the answer. In this paper, we propose Glance-ETD, a two-stage token reduction framework guided by a lightweight question-conditioned scorer. In the first stage, a glance encoder views the image at low resolution and re-encodes only the question-relevant regions at native resolution. In the second stage, Evidence-Tempered Diversity (ETD) selects the final tokens by combining diversity with the evidence predicted by the scorer, so that the kept tokens cover the scene and retain the answer. A single-rollout self-distillation recipe trains the MLLM with the glance encoder. With only 5% of the visual tokens, Glance-ETD retains 91.6% of the full-token performance of Qwen3.5-4B across fourteen benchmarks and outperforms the strongest prior method, LT-OPD, by 17.3 points and the same training recipe with DivPrune by 6.0. It also speeds up the time to first token on 4K images by 3.1x and generalizes to three other MLLMs and to high-resolution document benchmarks, with the largest gains on text-rich tasks.
  </details>

- **[Multimodal AI in Healthcare: Review of Vision-Language Foundation Models for Real-World Medical Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19958)**  `arXiv:2611.19958`  `cs.CV`  
  _Taha Razzaq, Murtaza Taj, Asim Iqbal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The emergence of foundation models has marked a transformative shift in AI, enabling robust generalization across diverse downstream tasks through putative zero-shot learning. Large Language Models and Vision Language Models have demonstrated strong capabilities in tasks such as image interpretation, report generation, and question answering by effectively learning from multimodal data-images paired with associated text often with minimal supervision. In the healthcare domain, this ability to align visual and textual information reduces the reliance on extensive manual annotations, as models can leverage existing clinical reports and imaging data to learn meaningful representations. This integration holds promise for improving diagnostic support, treatment planning, and overall patient care, even in data constrained settings. In this review, we provide a definitive taxonomy of the medical VLM landscape, tracing the evolution from early Contrastive Alignment and Generative MLLMs to the cutting edge frontiers of Dense Pixel Grounding, Sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE), and Reasoning Incentivized (RL) architectures. We critically examine the "medical bottleneck" identifying the persistent challenges of data scarcity, the "hallucination" risks in generative diagnostics, the computational strain of 3D volumetric processing, and the lack of standardized, clinically grounded evaluation metrics.
  </details>

- **[TIVS: Mapping the Sensitivity Landscape of Visual Token Transformations in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19745)**  `arXiv:2611.19745`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yikang Qiao, Jianye Zeng, Duan Huang, Ziying Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent efforts to improve the efficiency of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have explored visual token pruning, quantization, and feature compression. However, the properties that determine whether transformed visual tokens remain usable by a frozen decoder are not well characterized. We introduce \textbf{TIVS (Token Identity Versus Statistics)}, a training-free intervention framework that separately studies sequence permutation, token retention, numerical precision, and statistical replacement under controlled conditions. On 192 POPE questions, three token permutations change TinyLLaVA accuracy by only $-0.17$ percentage points on average, while changing 4.69\% of individual predictions. Diagonal and rank-64 Gaussian replacements decrease accuracy by 25.0 and 28.1 points on the same subset. Further experiments with exact moment constraints, quantization error, token retention, and additional visual tasks reveal how strongly the effects depend on the original feature values and the evaluation setting. These findings provide an empirical basis for assessing visual token transformations in frozen MLLMs and designing more reliable multimodal interfaces.
  </details>

- **[From Cloud to Edge: A Fully Local Pipeline for Pothole Risk Assessment and Driver Warning Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19737)**  `arXiv:2611.19737`  `cs.CV`  
  _Abdalmalek Aburaddaha, Samir A. Rawashdeh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models can turn road images into clear, geometry-aware pothole warnings, but current systems depend on commercial cloud services. Our earlier five-stage pipeline needed two cloud calls per scene, taking about 20 s and USD 0.105, and could not run offline. We rebuild every stage to run on a single local GPU and evaluate it with the same data, prompts, and protocol. Segmentation keeps the original Segment Anything weights but runs them in half precision with graph compilation, which makes it 2.7x faster without changing any downstream risk class; five lightweight variants could not match its masks on small road defects. Severity is graded by six ConvNeXt students distilled from 3092 cloud labels. They reach Cohen's kappa = 0.268 against human consensus at 28 ms per pothole, close to the cloud model's 0.321, within the camera setup they were trained for. A local 8-billion-parameter vision-language model writes the warnings under a grammar that admits only in-lane maneuvers. Three blind, independent raters scored these warnings safer than the cloud warnings (4.18 vs. 3.65 of 5; p = 0.001; 50 scenes) and equally clear, whereas an automated LLM judge ranked them the other way. The complete pipeline runs at 1.27 s per scene, 16x faster than the cloud system, offline and at no per-scene cost.
  </details>

- **[Generate, Align, and Restore: MLLM-Empowered Diffusion for Generalizable Low-light Image Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19285)**  `arXiv:2611.19285`  `cs.CV`  
  _Kaisheng Pang, Feng Li, Chen Zhang, Junjun Jiang, Haoang Li, Sam Kwong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-light image enhancement (LLIE) is essential to numerous vision applications operating under adverse illumination. Despite substantial advances, real-world nighttime enhancement remains challenging due to the scarcity of strictly aligned day-night pairs and complex degradations involving extreme darkness and heterogeneous artificial light sources, leading to fragile generalization. To overcome these challenges, we propose MrLIE (MLLM-empowered reference Low-light Image Enhancement), a novel reference-based unsupervised LLIE framework that synergizes the generative and reasoning capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) with conditional diffusion. MrLIE employs the MLLM as a high-fidelity reference synthesizer and diagnostic critic, iteratively generating, diagnosing, and correcting normal-light references directly from degraded nighttime observations. Based on this, our method governs the enhancement process through two complementary mechanisms, in which a confidence offset module rectifies spatial-geometric inconsistencies for reference reliability and a structure-anchored injection scheme harmonizes aligned reference cues, low-light structural constraints, and ControlNet additive anchoring during the denoising process. Extensive experiments on diverse benchmarks demonstrate that MrLIE achieves superior perceptual quality, natural appearance, and robust generalization.
  </details>

- **[RAVEN-Bench: A Paired EO-IR Video QA Benchmark for Aerial Multimodal Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18880)**  `arXiv:2611.18880`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yu Hu, Jianyang Gu, Yue Cao, Hao Liu, Kangnan Wang, Jozsef Hamari, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have advanced rapidly in image and video understanding, yet their ability to reason over paired aerial electro-optical and infrared (EO-IR) videos remains poorly understood. Such video poses a harder test than standard RGB video QA: targets are often small, evidence may appear only briefly, viewpoints change with the platform, and useful cues may appear in only one stream. We introduce RAVEN-Bench (Reasoning over Aerial Videos with EO-IR aligNment), a paired EO-IR video QA benchmark for low-altitude aerial multimodal understanding. RAVEN-Bench contains 100 temporally aligned EO-IR video pairs and 1,200 human-authored four-way multiple-choice questions, each independently audited for answer keys, modality requirements, evidence intervals, and group validity. It supports EO-only, IR-only, and paired EO+IR evaluation, and uses hierarchical capability levels and structured question groups to diagnose paired-input gain beyond the stronger unimodal condition, unimodal reliance, target consistency, and evidence-chain coherence. We evaluate frontier, open-weight, and remote-sensing MLLMs, showing that aggregate accuracy can overstate evidence-supported EO-IR reasoning. RAVEN-Bench provides controlled modality conditions and group-level diagnostics for auditing whether progress on RGB video benchmarks transfers to aerial cross-spectral understanding. Code and data are available atthis https URLandthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Agri-MRAG: Parameter-Enhanced Multimodal Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Agricultural Visual Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18771)**  `arXiv:2611.18771`  `cs.CV`  
  _Fuyong Liu, Xiantao Lei, Xining Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Retrieval-Augmented Generation (MRAG) has shown promise for Agricultural Visual Question Answering (Agri-VQA), but existing methods still face challenges in retrieving relevant knowledge while avoiding redundancy and in integrating retrieved evidence beyond simple prompt concatenation. To address these issues, we propose Agri-MRAG, a multimodal retrieval-augmented framework for agricultural VQA. Specifically, we first introduce a semantic-consistency-guided clustering mechanism that combines multimodal clustering and weighted relevance scoring to select high-confidence knowledge subsets and adaptively determine the retrieval size $k$. We then design a parameterized knowledge injection strategy, where retrieved agricultural knowledge is encoded into LoRA adapters and merged into the feed-forward network (FFN) layers of the MLLM, allowing external knowledge to influence generation through parameter-level adaptation. In addition, a perplexity-guided (PPL-guided) routing mechanism is employed to switch between implicit parametric reasoning and explicit retrieval-assisted generation according to model uncertainty, balancing inference efficiency and answer reliability. For evaluation, we use PlantVillageVQA as a broad agricultural benchmark and construct PlantVQA, a region-specific forestry and fruit crop VQA dataset containing 7,514 images and 15,028 expert-verified question-answer pairs. Experiments show that Agri-MRAG achieves ROUGE-L scores of 41.21 and 46.58 on the two datasets, outperforming mRAG by 11.70 and 4.51 points, and VisRAG by 8.80 and 3.39 points, respectively.
  </details>

- **[ThinkWise: Learning When to Think in Large Multimodal Reasoning Models via Visual and Difficulty Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18685)**  `arXiv:2611.18685`  `cs.CV`  
  _Wenkang Zhou, Chen Yang, Jiaheng Peng, Xitong Li, Hongzhi Yin, Yunke Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Multimodal Reasoning Models (LMRMs) extend large language models to heterogeneous modalities, demonstrating strong potential across a wide range of open-ended tasks. Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has been widely adopted to improve reasoning performance in LMRMs; however, existing approaches overlook the critical role of visual perception uncertainty in guiding reasoning processes. We term this limitation Reasoning Allocation Misalignment, where the misalignment between visual perception uncertainty and reasoning depth leads to over-allocation of deep reasoning under uncertain visual grounding, resulting in unnecessarily long reasoning trajectories and degraded performance. To address this issue, we propose ThinkWise, a perception-aware RLVR framework that jointly models task difficulty and visual perception state to regulate reasoning depth. Specifically, we introduce an instance-adaptive visual perception modeling mechanism that enables context-aware calibration of perception uncertainty, leading to more accurate perception estimation. We further design a structured reward formulation that jointly considers perception, correctness, and task difficulty. In particular, an answer reward provides supervision for correctness, a perception- and difficulty-conditioned length reward adaptively regulates reasoning depth, and a perception reward encourages trajectories with stronger visual grounding. Extensive experiments on multiple multimodal reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that ThinkWise consistently improves the trade-off between accuracy and reasoning efficiency across both 3B and 7B models. These results highlight the importance of perception-aware reasoning and establish a principled framework for advancing LMRMs.
  </details>

- **[LogicEye: Agent Driven Multimodal Dataset for Interpretable Ophthalmic Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18669)**  `arXiv:2611.18669`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiajie Mu, Lu Chen, Zheyun Qin, Xiaoming Xi, Lishan Qiao, Xiushan Nie, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical adoption of ophthalmic Artificial Intelligence remains limited by low interpretability and a lack of finely annotated multimodal data. Most existing datasets provide only coarse disease labels without the granular pathological findings required for differential diagnosis. We present LogicEye$^{Agent}$, a dual-pool inference and verification agent that emulates clinical reasoning by jointly verifying positive inclusion signs and negative exclusion criteria. Using LogicEye$^{Agent}$, we curate LogicEye$^{Data}$, a large-scale multimodal dataset of 804,461 fundus and OCT images paired with structured pathological reports spanning 38 diseases and 29 subtypes, with quality assured through joint verification by multimodal large language models and clinical experts. Building on this dataset, we further develop LogicEye$^{Bench}$, which incorporates a category-constrained loss for hierarchical disease recognition and report generation. Grounding coarse labels into verified visual findings effectively curtails diagnostic hallucinations and establishes a solid foundation for interpretable vision-language modeling in ophthalmology. The code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Ropedia Xperience 2: A Gold Standard for Scaling Physical AI Environments and Behaviors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17060)**  `arXiv:2611.17060`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hao Li, Fangzhou Hong, Zhaoxi Chen, Hui En Pang, Kairui Hu, Mingze Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large foundation models such as GPT-6 Astra and Claude Fable 5.1 have recently been shown to control robots directly from visual observations. They nevertheless remain brittle on long-horizon, contact-rich tasks in real-world environments. Further progress depends on two complementary resources: demonstrations of human behavior that supervise how tasks are performed, and interactive environments in which policies can be rolled out, evaluated, and improved in closed loop. Language models have advanced along well-characterized scaling laws; the corresponding question for physical AI is how capability scales with environments and behaviors, which first requires identifying which data to scale and how to produce and reuse such data continuously. To address this question, we introduce Ropedia Xperience 2, comprising 1,589 released metric-scale 3D scans and a collection of 4,230 egocentric recordings of 74 tasks (131 hours). We further show that each capture can be repurposed for robot learning: scenes are converted into physically interactive simulation environments, human motion is retargeted to whole-body robot trajectories, and additional robot behaviors are obtained through virtual teleoperation and automated long-horizon trajectory generation. We propose this joint capture of environments and behaviors as a gold standard for physical AI data collection, laying a data foundation for establishing scaling laws over environments and behaviors.
  </details>

- **[Visual Cipher Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agents on Long-Horizon Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16861)**  `arXiv:2611.16861`  `cs.CV`  
  _Deyao Hong, Jiayi Jiang, Yuchun Chen, Yixuan Shi, Wenyi Li, Zheng Jiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While language-model agents excel at long-horizon coding and terminal tasks, this success relies on two conditions: explicit text specifications and immediate execution feedback. However, neither condition holds in real-world problems where critical information exists only in images, leaving it unclear whether long-horizon capability transfers from text to visual reasoning. In such visual settings, because rules and clues resist text transcription and lack test-case feedback, agents must repeatedly inspect images, simulate spatial transformations, and autonomously cross-check clues. Despite this challenge, existing evaluations fail to capture such capability: visual reasoning benchmarks largely reduce tasks to single-turn, short-chain question answering, whereas agent benchmarks focus on text-based environments or interactive states that permit trial-and-error. To bridge this gap, we introduce Visual Cipher Bench, a collection of 40 original visual puzzles across four categories: spatial manipulation and simulation, logical constraints and planning, rule induction, and knowledge-based decoding. Each puzzle provides only one to three black-and-white line drawings and a minimal submission instruction, requiring agents to discover the underlying solving mechanism entirely from the images. Across 29 reasoning-effort configurations of eight frontier models, even the strongest model, GPT-6 Astra (max), achieves only 26.7% accuracy, while five of the eight models score below 1%. Yet on a ten-task subset, providing a single non-spoiler mechanism hint boosts accuracy from 12.2% to 51.1%. This sharp contrast shows that the primary bottleneck of current agents lies not in executing multi-step plans, but in autonomously discovering rules from visual evidence. Visual Cipher Bench thus provides a rigorous benchmark for measuring long-horizon visual reasoning in agents.
  </details>

- **[TwinPast: Benchmarking Historical Evidence Use in Streaming Video Understanding with Evidence Contrastive Video Pairs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41983)**  `arXiv:2610.41983`  `cs.CV`  
  _Haotian Yang, Yong Chen, Cheng Peng, Haobo Wang, Zhongle Xie, Xuanru Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High accuracy on streaming video benchmarks does not show that a model uses visual history. Auditing OVO-Bench and StreamingBench, we find that recent frames alone suffice for 32% of sampled historical-task questions, and that the question and options alone also yield many correct answers. We introduce TwinPast, a benchmark of 350 human-reviewed video pairs spanning five historical tasks. The two videos in a pair share the recent video context, question, and ordered options, and differ in earlier events and therefore in the correct answer. Each video is evaluated independently and PairAcc requires both answers to be correct, so an answer determined by the shared inputs cannot solve a pair. Masking the annotated critical evidence lowers PairAcc from as high as 91% to at most 19%, whereas equal-area masking elsewhere changes it by at most 3.4 points. The strongest streaming system reaches 49% PairAcc, while general-purpose MLLMs given each complete video reach 67-91%, leaving substantial headroom. Streaming systems fail mainly by giving both histories the same answer. They give different valid answers on at most 57% of pairs, while the general-purpose MLLMs do so on 79-94%. Case studies further show wrong answers when the deciding evidence is still available at the final query.
  </details>

- **[FlowTool: Controlling Tool Parameter in Image Retouching via Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35673)**  `arXiv:2609.35673`  `cs.CV`  
  _Thanh-Long V. Le, Steven Walton, Seunghyun Yoon, Branislav Kveton, Trung Bui, Eunho Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-based image editing (image retouching) is commonly formulated with autoregressive multimodal large language models (MLLMs) that sequentially generate reasoning, tool selections, and parameter values. In this work, we present a novel approach to tool-based image editing by framing the task as a flow matching problem. We introduce FlowTool, a framework that directly models the distribution of high-quality tool parameters conditioned on the input image and user instruction using conditional rectified flow. FlowTool combines a vision-language model backbone for multimodal understanding with a Diffusion Transformer parameter generator that transforms Gaussian noise into an editing plan. We train FlowTool with a two-stage supervised flow-matching curriculum, followed by reward-based post-training. Across four benchmarks, FlowTool significantly outperforms specialized MLLM agents and remains competitive with proprietary MLLMs, while reducing latency by at least $50\times$ and using nearly half the memory of the evaluated local baselines. These results demonstrate that tool-based image editing can be effectively modeled as conditional generation over structured continuous editing parameters, without autoregressive reasoning.
  </details>

- **[DispatchRAG: Grounding Emergency Dispatch Decisions in Real-World Protocols from Traffic Accident Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.23132)**  `arXiv:2607.23132`  `cs.CV`  
  _Muhammad Sulthan Adhipradhana, Ehsan Javanmardi, Naren Bao, Manabu Tsukada_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Assessing the severity of a traffic accident scenario is important to decide which emergency service to dispatch. Missing an ambulance dispatch on a pedestrian accident is a fatal issue that can lead to death. Recently, Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have been a promising tool for accident reasoning, yet many VLMs are not grounded in real-life accident response protocols, making them not usable in accident severity assessment off-the-shelf. We introduced DispatchRAG, a decision-support framework grounded in real-life Japanese traffic-accident response protocols, designed to assist emergency dispatchers by using VLMs to generate an appropriate emergency response recommendation during an emergency scenario. Utilizing a RAG-based retrieval mechanism to retrieve the most relevant accident protocol and an LLM-powered reasoner to suggest the most proper response. To support evaluation, we introduce Accident Dispatch Dataset, a comprehensive dataset of accident assessment and emergency response according to Japanese accident response protocols adapted from the MM-AU dataset. We validate our framework on the Accident Dispatch Dataset, showing strong performance across various accident scenarios compared to the baseline VLM, pointing toward a decision-support tool that can help autonomous vehicles and human dispatchers respond faster to both their own and nearby accidents.
  </details>

- **[Caption Bottleneck Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00578)**  `arXiv:2607.00578`  `cs.CV`  
  _Seref Baris Cagliyan, Umut Ozdemir, Merve Tapli, Emre Akbas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs) provide interpretability by routing predictions through a layer of human-understandable concepts. However, defining an optimal concept set for a specific dataset remains an open challenge. Existing approaches rely on expensive expert annotations or LLM-generated lists based solely on class names. Even "open-vocabulary" variants typically depend on static concept sets, which restrict discovery and introduce label bias. Furthermore, traditional CBMs often suffer from information leakage, where unmodeled visual features bypass the bottleneck and compromise the integrity of the explanations. To overcome these limitations, we propose Caption Bottleneck Models (CaBM), a framework that circumvents the need for predefined concept sets by replacing rigid concept layers with free-form natural language. By representing images via LMM-generated captions and training a classifier strictly on this text, CaBM ensures a leakage-free architecture by construction. Additionally, by analyzing the text classifier post-training, CaBM autonomously discovers high-quality, dataset-specific concepts. Our results across fine- and coarse-grained benchmarks demonstrate that CaBM achieves competitive accuracy while preserving interpretability without the constraints of external dictionaries or manual labeling.
  </details>

- **[VISTAQA: Benchmarking Joint Visual Question Answering and Pixel-Level Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20676)**  `arXiv:2605.20676`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mozhgan Nasr Azadani, Yimu Wang, Yongpeng Zhu, Lihong Chen, Milan Ganai, Sean Sedwards, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Establishing a clear link between model predictions and the visual evidence that supports them is critical for transparency and reliability in multimodal reasoning, yet current multimodal large language model (MLLM) evaluations do not explicitly enforce this alignment. Existing benchmarks assess either textual answer correctness or pixel-level localization in isolation, leaving the coupling of reasoning and grounding an open challenge. We introduce VISTAQA, a comprehensive benchmark for joint evaluation of free-form answer correctness and pixel-level evidence grounding in visual question answering. VISTAQA comprises 1,157 expert-curated samples spanning six task types and six visual domains, ranging from direct perception to compositional and relational reasoning. VISTAQA requires models to not only answer correctly, but to also provide precise segmentation masks that support their answers. It also includes hallucination-aware examples where no valid visual evidence exists. To support this enhanced evaluation, we introduce GROVE, a unified evaluation metric that enforces joint correctness by combining textual accuracy and grounding quality via a per-sample geometric mean, ensuring neither dimension can compensate for deficiencies in the other. Comprehensive experiments across grounding-aware models and hybrid pipelines with general-purpose MLLMs reveal that even the strongest systems achieve limited performance under GROVE, highlighting a substantial gap between answer accuracy and visual evidence alignment. Project page can be found atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EchoPrune: Interpreting Redundancy as Temporal Echoes for Efficient VideoLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10050)**  `arXiv:2605.10050`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiameng Li, Minye Wu, Jiezhang Cao, Aleksei Tiulpin, Matthew B. Blaschko_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-form video understanding remains challenging for Video Large Language Models (VideoLLMs), as the dense frame sampling introduces massive visual tokens while sparse sampling risks missing critical temporal evidence and leading to LLM hallucination. Existing training-free token reduction methods either treat videos equally as static images or rely on segment-level merging heuristics, which weaken fine-grained spatiotemporal modeling and introduce additional overhead. In this paper, we propose EchoPrune, a lightweight and training-free token pruning method that improves temporal resolution under a fixed LLM-side visual token budget. Our core idea is to interpret redundant video tokens as temporal echoes: if a token is well reconstructed from the previous frame, it is merely a temporally redundant echo; otherwise, it may capture new events, motion, or query-relevant visual evidence. Based on this insight, EchoPrune scores visual tokens by (i) query-guided crossmodal relevance and (ii) temporal reconstruction error, measured by correspondence matching and echo matching across consecutive frames. The selected tokens preserve task-relevant cues and temporal novelty while suppressing predictable redundancy, allowing VideoLLMs to observe more frames without increasing the decoding budget. Extensive experiments on LLaVA-OV, Qwen2.5VL, and Qwen3VL across six video understanding benchmarks show that EchoPrune enables VideoLLMs to process up to $\mathbf{20\times}$ frames under the same token budget, yielding improved performance ($\mathbf{5\%^\uparrow}$) and inference speedup ($\mathbf{5.6\times}$ for prefilling) on LLaVA-OV-7B.
  </details>

- **[AffectAgent: Collaborative Multi-Agent Reasoning for Retrieval-Augmented Multimodal Emotion Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.12735)**  `arXiv:2604.12735`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zeheng Wang, Zitong Yu, Yijie Zhu, Bo Zhao, Haochen Liang, Taorui Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based multimodal emotion recognition relies on static parametric memory and often hallucinates when interpreting nuanced affective states. In this paper, given that single-round retrieval-augmented generation is highly susceptible to modal ambiguity and therefore struggles to capture complex affective dependencies across modalities, we introduce AffectAgent, an affect-oriented multi-agent retrieval-augmented generation framework that leverages collaborative decision-making among agents for fine-grained affective understanding. Specifically, AffectAgent comprises three jointly optimized specialized agents, namely a query planner, an evidence filter, and an emotion generator, which collaboratively perform analytical reasoning to retrieve cross-modal samples, assess evidence, and generate predictions. These agents are optimized end-to-end using Multi-Agent Proximal Policy Optimization (MAPPO) with a shared affective reward to ensure consistent emotion understanding. Furthermore, we introduce Modality-Balancing Mixture of Experts (MB-MoE) and Retrieval-Augmented Adaptive Fusion (RAAF), where MB-MoE dynamically regulates the contributions of different modalities to mitigate representation mismatch caused by cross-modal heterogeneity, while RAAF enhances semantic completion under missing-modality conditions by incorporating retrieved audiovisual embeddings. Extensive experiments on MER-UniBench demonstrate that AffectAgent achieves superior performance across complex scenarios. Our code will be released at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[MI-Pruner: Crossmodal Mutual Information-guided Token Pruner for Efficient MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.03072)**  `arXiv:2604.03072`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiameng Li, Aleksei Tiulpin, Matthew B. Blaschko_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The redundancy of visual tokens in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) motivates training-free token pruning techniques for efficient inference. Existing methods typically rank visual tokens by attention salience and retain only the most salient ones. However, salience-based pruning is susceptible to sink tokens and positional bias, overlooking the diversity of the selected set. Moreover, extracting attention maps incurs additional overhead. In this paper, we propose MI-Pruner, which derives Pointwise Mutual Information (PMI) based relevance scores in the projection space to reflect crossmodal relevance and intra-modal redundancy. Grounded in information theory, our approach identifies the most informative tokens by explicitly measuring feature-level dependency. Without reliance on internal attention maps or architectural modifications, MI-Pruner can be seamlessly applied to any pretrained MLLMs for inference acceleration. Extensive experiments on LLaVA1.5 and Qwen-series demonstrate that our approach achieves a favorable performance-efficiency trade-off. Specifically, we preserve ${>}97\%$ performance on LLaVA1.5-7B at a pruning rate of 88.9\%.
  </details>

- **[SGR3 Model: Scene Graph Retrieval-Reasoning Model in 3D](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.04614)**  `arXiv:2603.04614`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zirui Wang, Ruiping Liu, Yufan Chen, Junwei Zheng, Weijia Fan, Kunyu Peng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D scene graphs provide a structured representation of object entities and their relationships, enabling high-level interpretation and reasoning for robots while remaining intuitively understandable to humans. Existing approaches for 3D scene graph generation typically combine scene reconstruction with graph neural networks (GNNs). However, such pipelines require multi-modal data that may not always be available, and their reliance on heuristic graph construction can constrain the prediction of relationship triplets. In this work, we introduce a Scene Graph Retrieval-Reasoning Model in 3D (SGR3 Model), a training-free framework that leverages multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) with retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) for semantic scene graph generation. SGR3 Model bypasses the need for explicit 3D reconstruction. Instead, it enhances relational reasoning by incorporating semantically aligned scene graphs retrieved via a ColPali-style cross-modal framework. To improve retrieval robustness, we further introduce a weighted patch-level similarity selection mechanism that mitigates the negative impact of blurry or semantically uninformative regions. Experiments demonstrate that SGR3 Model achieves competitive performance compared to training-free baselines and on par with GNN-based expert models. Moreover, an ablation study on the retrieval module and knowledge base scale reveals that retrieved external information is explicitly integrated into the token generation process, rather than being implicitly internalized through abstraction.
  </details>

- **[MediX-R1: Open Ended Medical Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.23363)**  `arXiv:2602.23363`  `cs.CV`  
  _Sahal Shaji Mullappilly, Mohammed Irfan Kurpath, Jaseel Muhammad, Omair Mohamed, Mohamed Zidan, Fahad Khan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this work, we introduce MediX-R1, an open-ended Reinforcement Learning (RL) framework for medical vision language models (VLMs) that enables free-form answers beyond multiple-choice formats. MediX-R1 fine-tunes a baseline vision-language backbone with Group Based RL and a composite reward tailored for medical reasoning: an LLM-based accuracy reward that judges semantic correctness with a strict YES/NO decision, a medical embedding-based semantic reward to capture paraphrases and terminology variants, and lightweight format and modality rewards that enforce interpretable reasoning and modality recognition. This multi-signal design provides stable, informative feedback for open-ended outputs where traditional verifiable or MCQ-only rewards fall short. To evaluate performance, we employ a unified evaluation framework for both text-only and multimodal (image+text) tasks that uses a Reference-based LLM-as-judge in place of brittle string-overlap metrics, capturing semantic correctness, clinical accuracy, and contextual alignment. Our results show that open-ended RL with comprehensive reward signals can effectively strengthen medical reasoning in multimodal models. Despite using only $\sim51$K instruction examples, MediX-R1 achieves strong performance across standard medical LLM (text-only) and VLM (image + text) benchmarks, outperforming existing open-weight medical VLMs, with large gains particularly on open-ended clinical tasks. These improvements remain consistent across multiple RL algorithms and VLM backbones and are further supported by blinded evaluations with medical experts. Our trained models, curated datasets and source code are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[GRASP: Guided Region-Aware Sparse Prompting for Adapting MLLMs to Remote Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.17089)**  `arXiv:2601.17089`  `cs.CV`  
  _Qigan Sun, Chaoning Zhang, Jianwei Zhang, Xudong Wang, Jiehui Xie, Pengcheng Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In recent years, Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have made significant progress in visual question answering tasks. However, directly applying existing fine-tuning methods to remote sensing (RS) images often leads to issues such as overfitting on background noise or neglecting target details. This is primarily due to the large-scale variations, sparse target distributions, and complex regional semantic features inherent in RS images. These challenges limit the effectiveness of MLLMs in RS tasks. To address these challenges, we propose a parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) strategy called Guided Region-Aware Sparse Prompting (GRASP). GRASP introduces spatially structured soft prompts associated with spatial blocks extracted from a frozen visual token grid. Through a question-guided sparse fusion mechanism, GRASP dynamically aggregates task-specific context into a compact global prompt, enabling the model to focus on relevant regions while filtering out background noise. Extensive experiments on multiple RSVQA benchmarks show that GRASP achieves competitive performance compared to existing fine-tuning and prompt-based methods while maintaining high parameter efficiency.
  </details>

- **[Breaking the Visual Shortcuts in Multimodal Knowledge-Based Visual Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.22843)**  `arXiv:2511.22843`  `cs.CV`  
  _Dosung Lee, Sangwon Jung, Boyoung Kim, Minyoung Kim, Sungyeon Kim, Junyoung Sung, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing Multimodal Knowledge-Based Visual Question Answering (MKB-VQA) benchmarks suffer from "visual shortcuts", as the query image typically matches the primary subject entity of the target document. We demonstrate that models can exploit these shortcuts, achieving comparable results using visual cues alone. To address this, we introduce Relational Entity Text-Image kNowledge Augmented (RETINA) benchmark, automatically constructed using an LLM-driven pipeline, consisting of 120k training and 2k human-curated test set. RETINA contains queries referencing secondary subjects (i.e. related entities) and pairs them with images of these related entities, removing the visual shortcut. When evaluated on RETINA existing models show significantly degraded performance, confirming their reliance on the shortcut. Furthermore, we propose Multi-Image MultImodal Retriever (MIMIR), which enriches document embeddings by augmenting images of multiple related entities, effectively handling RETINA, unlike prior work that uses only a single image per document. Our experiments validate the limitations of existing benchmarks and demonstrate the effectiveness of RETINA and MIMIR. Our project is available at: Project Page.
  </details>

- **[Cross-Modal Attention Guided Unlearning in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.07567)**  `arXiv:2510.07567`  `cs.CV`  
  _Karuna Bhaila, Aneesh Komanduri, Minh-Hao Van, Xintao Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in multi-modal understanding for tasks such as Visual Question Answering (VQA), which requires models to infer outputs based on visual and textual context simultaneously. However, models may memorize some private and/or sensitive information during training and regurgitate it in inference. Recently, machine unlearning has been leveraged to address such leakage of private data in LLMs. VLMs add a layer of complexity to this process, as the visual context in the query may additionally contain sensitive information. To address this issue, we explore unlearning for VLMs, specifically for the VQA task. We explore the role of visual tokens for output generation in VLMs using cross-modal attention and utilize it to formulate Cross-Modal Attention Guided Unlearning (CAGUL), a lightweight and efficient VLM unlearning framework. In contrast to computationally expensive finetuning methods, CAGUL utilizes external modules to encode unlearning information in visual tokens of low importance for relevant queries. Empirical results show that the transformed visual tokens prevent leakage and retain reference model behavior to achieve desirable trade-offs in forget quality and model utility without altering pretrained model parameters.
  </details>

- **[Bridging Semantic Logic Gaps: A Cognition Inspired Multimodal Boundary Preserving Network for Image Manipulation Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.07216)**  `arXiv:2508.07216`  `cs.CV`  
  _Songlin Li, Zhiqing Guo, Yuanman Li, Zeyu Li, Yunfeng Diao, Gaobo Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The existing image manipulation localization (IML) models mainly relies on visual cues, but ignores the semantic logical relationships between content features. In fact, the content semantics conveyed by real images often conform to human cognitive laws. However, image manipulation technology usually destroys the internal relationship between content features, thus leaving semantic clues for IML. In this paper, we propose a cognition inspired multimodal boundary preserving network (CMB-Net). Specifically, CMB-Net utilizes large language models (LLMs) to analyze manipulated regions within images and generate prompt-based textual information to compensate for the lack of semantic relationships in the visual information. Considering that the erroneous texts induced by hallucination from LLMs will damage the accuracy of IML, we propose an image-text central ambiguity module (ITCAM). It assigns weights to the text features by quantifying the ambiguity between text and image features, thereby ensuring the beneficial impact of textual information. We also propose an image-text interaction module (ITIM) that aligns visual and text features using a correlation matrix for fine-grained interaction. Finally, inspired by invertible neural networks, we propose a restoration edge decoder (RED) that mutually generates input and output features to preserve boundary information in manipulated regions without loss. Extensive experiments show that CMB-Net outperforms most existing IML models. Our code is available onthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Rollback the World, Keep the Reflection: Rollback-Induced Reflection for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18304)**  `arXiv:2609.18304`  `cs.CL` `cs.RO`  
  _Yi Yu, Liuyi Yao, Yaliang Li, Enshu Wang, Libing Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly tackle long-horizon tasks through multi-step environment interaction, yet a single erroneous action can alter subsequent states and observations, causing errors to compound over time. Existing methods either correct the context without repairing altered environment states or restore earlier states while discarding useful experience, making it difficult to both eliminate failure conditions and avoid repeating past mistakes. We argue that reliable recovery should instead be treated as a rollback-boundary control problem that jointly determines when to intervene, where to resume, and what information should survive recovery. Based on this view, we propose Rollback-Induced Reflection (RIR), a unified recovery framework that restores execution to a selected prior state while carrying forward reusable knowledge distilled from the abandoned trajectory to guide subsequent decisions. We further characterize recovery through a unified operator over rollback depth and retained memory, providing a general view of state restoration and knowledge retention. Experiments on three long-horizon benchmarks show that RIR consistently improves average task performance across multiple LLM backbones, with structured reflection memory preserving useful experience and selective rollback enabling efficient recovery.
  </details>

- **[Risk-certified prompt optimization for LLM-as-a-judge guardrails](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20156)**  `arXiv:2611.20156`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Hussein Jawad, Alexandre Landeau, Guy Fleury Iradukunda, Paul Beaumont, Théodore Rezel, Sofiane Schaack, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-as-a-judge guardrails are often controlled primarily through system prompts, yet standard prompt optimization methods target average performance rather than explicit safety constraints. This paper studies system-prompt selection as a constrained optimization problem: maximize benign pass rate while keeping the missed-attack rate below a user-specified level {\alpha} with finite-sample statistical control. The framework combines LLM-based judging, automatic prompt generation, and distribution-free risk certification. This work instantiates it in two procedures: CPS and CRISP. CPS generates a fixed pool of prompts from training data, then certifies candidates on a held-out evaluation set using simultaneous upper confidence bounds and selects the certified prompt with the highest benign utility. CRISP instead integrates risk feedback into an adaptive search, using hard example-guided rewriting and a reusable-holdout interface to steer generation toward the feasible utility frontier. Experiments on four datasets covering prompt injection, system-prompt leakage, and sensitive-information disclosure show that risk-certified selection consistently respects the target missed-attack level on heldout tests, while utility improves as the permitted risk is relaxed. CRISP is especially useful when fixed-pool search fails to produce certifiable high-utility candidates.
  </details>

- **[Grounding Language Models in Speech Does Not Fix "AI Flavor": Calibrated LLM Judges, Goodhart Traps, and a Preference-Tuning Fix](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19840)**  `arXiv:2611.19840`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Dongwu Qian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chinese outputs of large language models carry a perceptible "AI flavor": formulaic sentence frames, stacked written-register connectives, repetition loops, and layout leakage. A popular hypothesis blames text-only training for the missing inner-speech layer of human writing; injecting speech or prosody signals should therefore reduce it. We test it with controlled interventions on a 0.6B model; all three fail: (i) phoneme-interleaved continued pretraining yields no spokenness gain under text-matched controls while raising repetition (+0.13 [+0.03, +0.23]); (ii) prosody-plan conditional generation reaches an adherence of only 0.257 against a pre-registered 0.50 gate; (iii) a rule-based reward built on TTS prosody-feature distance is imperceptible in double-blind evaluation by both humans (8/16) and a calibrated LLM judge (9/16).A root cause is evaluation: single-rater blind evaluation at n~20 only detects extreme (>=85%) preferences. We therefore build a calibrated LLM-judge protocol: the judge passes catch trials on known-answer pairs (16/16; single-text 22/24, same-topic pairs 24/24, versus 0.75 for the human rater), then annotates with two independent judges under A/B swapping, counting disagreement as a tie. Using the judge's agree labels directly as DPO pairs (no reward model), we obtain the first positive result: held-out blind win rate 0.833 [0.699, 0.972], repetition 0.114 to 0.000, unchanged MMLU. We further identify a Goodhart channel: the judge's length preference transduces into output-length collapse via pairwise DPO (median 75 chars vs a 300-char prompt); an SFT-anchor repairs it (w is a style knob: w=0.1 preserves gains, w=0.3 does not). Scaling preference data from 89 to 240 pairs yields no additional judge-visible gain. We release RA-Eval v0.1: a three-layer Chinese AI-flavor evaluation framework with 382 judge-annotated preference pairs.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating and Improving Natural Language Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19507)**  `arXiv:2611.19507`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Marco Bazzani, Dan Mossing_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Natural language autoencoders (NLAs) are a method for generating natural language explanations of LLM activations. We develop evaluations based on auditing tasks that test the ability of NLAs to surface latent knowledge, identify whether a model was misled by a hint, and catch a silent fabrication. We also develop extensions to NLAs that let them use activation information from multiple layers and positions, let us apply them more effectively to long transcripts, and reduce the number of false claims they make. These extensions result in improvements on both our auditing evaluations and auxiliary evaluations adapted from prior work. We also use the evaluations to study how different auditing capabilities change over the course of NLA training, and find that although every evaluation improves early in training, extended training can hurt performance on some of our auditing evaluations.
  </details>

- **[A Comparative Evaluation of BERT-Based and LLM-Based Models for Indic Sentence Similarity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19356)**  `arXiv:2611.19356`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Aishwarya Gunjal, Kanchan Khemnar, Pawan Poojary, Pranav Kulkarni, Raviraj Joshi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The relative effectiveness of BERT-based encoders and large language model (LLM)-based embeddings for Indic semantic textual similarity remains insufficiently characterized. We compare eight BERT-based and five LLM-based models on the 1,500-pair STS Benchmark development set translated into ten Indic languages and on the native Marathi MahaSTS test set. IndicSBERT achieves the highest Pearson correlation in all ten translated language sets, with a macro-average of 0.843, followed by BGE-M3 at 0.792. Harrier-270M is the strongest LLM-based model on translated STS-B, with a macro-average of 0.751. On MahaSTS, IndicSBERT and Bhasha-Embed-v0 achieve Pearson correlations of 0.901 and 0.900, respectively, while EmbeddingGemma leads the LLM-based models at 0.871. The highest-scoring BERT-based model thus exceeds the highest-scoring LLM-based model in both settings, although the magnitude of the difference varies across benchmarks. These findings indicate that LLM-derived embeddings do not uniformly improve Indic sentence similarity over established encoders and motivate evaluation that accounts for language coverage, task supervision, and benchmark construction.
  </details>

- **[Internal Verdicts Track Evidence: A Template-Controlled Neutrosophic Reading of Epistemic States in Large Language Models via the Jacobian Lens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18602)**  `arXiv:2611.18602`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Maikel Yelandi Leyva-Vazquez, Alexis Matheu Perez, Florentin Smarandache_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  For twenty-five years, neutrosophic logic has assigned independent degrees of truth (T), indeterminacy (I), and falsity (F) to epistemic states that classical and fuzzy semantics cannot express. Previous applications measured these components on outputs: answers, judgments, expert evaluations. This paper reports, to our knowledge, the first neutrosophic reading of components inside the internal representations of a large language model. Using the recently released Jacobian lens on Qwen3.5-4B, we project intermediate-layer readouts onto lexicons of support, refutation, and hedging, obtaining layer-wise (T, I, F) profiles under conflicting evidence, first-person false belief, and factual control (n = 20 each). A first battery yielded three striking signatures: a surge of refutation mass under conflict, sustained indeterminacy under false belief, and an apparent "verdict collapse" before the output layer. A second, template-controlled battery showed that all three, as initially stated, were largely artifacts of prompt grammar: matched templates with agreeing evidence inflate the same lexicon masses, true beliefs elicit the same hedging, and the model verbalizes its verdict when allowed to generate.
