What survives the controls is stronger than what died: the neutrosophic verdict balance B = log10(F) - log10(T) tracks evidence polarity under identical templates (about 3 orders of magnitude of separation, Cohen's d = 2.3-2.5, p < 10^-6 in 9 of 9 layers; paired Wilcoxon: median difference +2.4 orders, 19/20 items, p = 3.8x10^-6) and residually discriminates false from true first-person beliefs. We argue that the independence of the neutrosophic components is what keeps the common-mode channel observable, making both the measurement and its self-correction possible, and we distill the two-battery design into a reusable protocol for auditing language model internals with lens-based readouts.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Pretrained Language Semantics for Structured EHR Prediction Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17053)**  `arXiv:2611.17053`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Ashvin Gupta, Brendan Delaney, Alessandra Russo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Structured Electronic Health Record (EHR) models typically represent clinical concepts as dataset-specific tokens, discarding their associated natural-language semantics. Consequently, relationships between concepts must largely be learned from EHR co-occurrence without directly exploiting pretrained linguistic knowledge. We investigate the contribution of pretrained language semantics to longitudinal EHR prediction through tEHR, a framework that converts clinical events into natural-language descriptions and adapts pretrained autoregressive language models through EHR-specific continued pretraining and task-specific discriminative fine-tuning. We evaluate tEHR across four MIMIC-IV prediction tasks, where Mistral-7B achieves the strongest overall performance against structured-data, code-based, and LLM-based baselines. Controlled ablations demonstrate the importance of lexical grounding, numerical values, and both adaptation stages. A capacity-controlled experiment further shows that replacing clinical descriptions with atomic code tokens substantially reduces performance despite retaining the same LLM backbone. Alternative approaches incorporating pretrained semantic embeddings offer lower computational costs but do not match full textualisation. These results highlight the importance of both semantic representation and downstream adaptation when applying pretrained language models to structured EHR prediction.
  </details>

- **[Test-Time Speculation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09329)**  `arXiv:2605.09329`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Avinash Kumar, Sujay Sanghavi, Poulami Das_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates LLM inference by using a fast draft model to generate tokens and a more accurate target model to verify them. Its performance depends on the $\textit{acceptance length}$, or number of draft tokens accepted by the target. Our studies show that the acceptance length of even state-of-the-art speculators, like DFlash, EAGLE-3, and PARD, degrades with generation length, reaching values close to 1 (i.e., no speedup) within a few thousand output tokens, making speculators ineffective for long-response tasks. Acceptance lengths decline because most speculators are trained offline on short sequences, but are forced to match the target on much longer outputs at inference, well beyond their training distribution.To address this issue, we propose $\textit{Test-Time Speculation (TTS)}$, an online distillation approach that continuously adapts the speculator at test time. TTS leverages the key insight that the token verification step already invokes the target model for each draft token, providing the training signal needed to adapt the draft at no additional cost. Treating the draft as the student and the target as the teacher, TTS adjusts the draft over several speculation rounds, with each update improving the draft's accuracy as generation proceeds. Our results across ten models from the Qwen-3, Qwen-3.5, DeepSeek-R1, GPT-OSS, MiniMax, and Llama3.1 families show that TTS improves acceptance lengths over state-of-the-art speculators by up to $72\%$ and $41\%$ on average, with the benefits scaling with generation lengths.
  </details>

- **[Safety-Preserving Medical LLM Fine-Tuning via Bilevel Data Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20643)**  `arXiv:2611.20643`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ruoxuan Shi, Sheng Yang, Zhengxing Su, Xiaoyang Hou, Yating Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As general large language models (LLMs) are increasingly adapted for patient-facing medical applications, improving medical capability while controlling patient-facing risks remains a critical challenge. Large-scale medical instruction data are essential for acquiring domain knowledge through supervised fine-tuning (SFT), but unsafe responses can undermine existing safety alignment. To address this problem, we propose Scalable Bilevel Data Selection (S-BDS) for safety-preserving medical SFT. By controlling selection-weight concentration, S-BDS enables reliable sample ranking over large candidate pools and identifies training examples that improve medical capability while controlling patient-facing risks. We combine clinician-validated safe data and selected data with unscreened medical instruction data for SFT, and jointly evaluate medical capability and patient-facing safety on HealthBench and PatientSafetyBench, respectively. Experiments across Llama and Qwen models show that S-BDS achieves the highest safe rates among all compared methods, outperforming the strongest baselines by 4.64 and 3.00 percentage points, respectively, while maintaining competitive medical capability. Further experiments show that varying the safety-oriented weight during SFT controls the trade-off between medical capability and patient-facing safety.
  </details>

- **[Language Models Meet Vibration Testing for LEO Constellation Satellites with Calibrated Response Prediction from Few-show Sweeps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20579)**  `arXiv:2611.20579`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jun Wu, Deming Li, Zhenming Zheng, Honghao Yue, Fei Yang, Hao Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) increasingly drive engineering analysis end to end, turning textual records into the inputs of numerical programs, but a plausible and wrong value can pass silently into a decision. We propose deterministic validation gates for LLM-driven engineering analysis, a general paradigm in which a typed configuration produced by LLMs is consumed only after it passes deterministic checks on units, physical consistency, verbatim quotes, agreement between independent models, consistency with measurement exports and the applicability of trained models. The gates target hallucinations from two directions, since quote checking catches fabricated content while agreement between models catches errors of omission that quote checking misses. We instantiate the paradigm on sine vibration acceptance testing for low Earth orbit mega-constellation satellites, where DeepSeek V4.1 Flash and Qwen-plus read Chinese test records and extract a configuration of 76 fields. Fact-level F1 ranged from 0.98 to 1.0 on five input variants, with all repairs frozen on development inputs and evaluated on held-out inputs. With all gates, 20 of 20 configurations produced notching results bit for bit identical to the gold run, and all 17 real and injected errors in consumed fields were stopped, of which 16 would have run without gates. Retraining the bound model on a dataset built from an LLM configuration reproduced its results within GPU nondeterminism. We also report a negative result of practical value. Direct LLM curve forecasting only matches linear extrapolation, whereas a small physics-structured network reduces the over-notched bandwidth by 86% to 89% at calibrated coverage c = 0.90, which delineates where direct LLM prediction should give way to a trained model.
  </details>

- **[The novelty exposed: ablation, reproducibility and transparency in 40 technical reports of open-weight large language models (LLMs)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20003)**  `arXiv:2611.20003`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Bahaeddin Türkoğlu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In 2025 alone, 48 new large language model (LLM) architectures were added to the Hugging Face transformers library, and many of them arrive with a technical report that presents the model as new. What is actually new in these models, and is it tested? We put this question to LLM technical reports. We look at three things: what the reference code shows about how new open-weight architectures differ from earlier ones; what 40 randomly sampled technical reports claim as new and whether they support it with ablation studies; and what they disclose for reproducibility and transparency. In the library, 39 of the 48 architectures added in 2025 take at least two of their three core blocks from another model, most often Llama, although part of this reflects how the library writes code. In the reports, 19 of 73 architectural or training novelty claims come with a controlled with/without comparison; 4 of 40 reports state more than one training run; 8 rerun the models they compare against under one setup; and 1 discloses data mixture, token count, and compute together. We also looked for known components given new names: none of five candidate cases hid its origin, though we did not study model names. We propose seven reporting rules for LLM release reports and apply them to two reports. We do not judge model quality, and an untested claim may still be true.
  </details>

- **[EfficientSkill: Learning Skills for Token-Efficient Multi-Turn Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19823)**  `arXiv:2611.19823`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Deep Shah, Sanket Badhe, Nehal Kathrotia, Priyanka Tiwari_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reflective skill optimizers improve multi-turn LLM agents by revising instructions from failed and successful rollouts, yet accumulating edge-case rules bloats prompts, inflates token use, and pushes agents into long, error-prone loops. We introduce EfficientSkill, a two-step framework that first discovers a reliable skill without token penalties, and then warm-starts accuracy-guarded pruning from that skill to compress instructions, bound agent turns, and offload reusable code into workspace utilities with zero implementation prompt cost. Across four frontier, mid-tier, and open-weight models on MATH-500, ALFWorld, and SpreadsheetBench, EfficientSkill cuts output tokens over unconstrained EvoSkill on 11 of 12 configurations (by up to 80%) while raising mean accuracy on every benchmark, including +14.3 and +17.5 percentage-point gains on SpreadsheetBench with Gemini 3.8 Flash and Grok 4.1 Fast by replacing stalled loops with compact workflows. By shrinking both generated tokens and replayed history, EfficientSkill lowers total token usage and serving cost across every evaluated configuration, with or without full-prefix KV caching.
  </details>

- **[SG-LegalClarify: A Large-Scale Benchmark for Legal Reasoning and Ambiguity Recognition in Singapore Law](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19643)**  `arXiv:2611.19643`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ziwei Gu, Michael S. Lee, Drew Rein, Yash Maurya, Vikneswaran Kumaran, Darshini Ramiah, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Singapore offers a distinctive testbed for legal reasoning by large language models (LLMs). Its legal system combines inherited English common law with jurisdiction-specific statutes, requiring models to distinguish local doctrine from generic common-law intuition. This setting also highlights the challenge of recognizing underspecification in lay legal queries, because the facts material to a legal outcome depend on the governing doctrine. Yet legal benchmarks rarely cover Singapore law or test this form of ambiguity recognition. To address these gaps, we introduce SG-LegalClarify, a large-scale benchmark for open-ended legal reasoning and ambiguity recognition in Singapore law. SG-LegalClarify contains 1,100 lawyer-authored source questions spanning 10 legal domains, including 340 fully specified and 760 underspecified questions. Each underspecified question has a fixed hidden-fact assignment and a fully specified counterpart, yielding 1,860 evaluation instances. Responses are graded with lawyer-authored Issue, Rule, Application, and Conclusion (IRAC) rubrics. We evaluate 19 LLMs, closed-book and with web search, using a multi-turn pipeline in which models decide whether to answer or clarify, and a user simulator reveals hidden facts only when asked. We find the benchmark far from saturated: the best models satisfy only 69.8% of mandatory rubric items closed-book (average 53.8%) and 77.8% with web search (average 63.0%). Models also struggle to recognize when clarification is necessary. We will release the public split of our benchmark, the model outputs on it, and the evaluation pipeline to support future research.
  </details>

- **[Recursive Semantic Divergence for LLM Agent Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33012)**  `arXiv:2610.33012`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Harshavardhan Abichandani, Penny Chong, Atin Ghosh, Daniel Dahlmeier_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM) agents with tool use can behave inconsistently across independent runs from identical states. They may call different tools, produce contradictory outputs, or follow divergent strategies. This variability compounds over time and undermines deployment reliability. Existing evaluation metrics primarily measure task correctness via completion-based scores and do not capture consistency in agent behavior across executions. Separately, agent behavior is analyzed either at the token level using probability distributions or at the single-response level using semantic clustering, but neither captures how inconsistency propagates across future turns of a trajectory. We introduce Recursive Semantic Divergence (RSD), a trajectory-level consistency metric adapted from bisimulation metrics in reinforcement learning. RSD measures the expected cumulative semantic disagreement between two independent rollouts of the same agent from the same state, using natural language inference to detect logical inconsistency. It is defined recursively over future states and approximated by a neural network trained on independent rollouts. The learned metric estimates future divergence from the current state, providing a consistency score at each turn without requiring the full trajectory to complete. When used as a reward signal for fine-tuning, RSD reduces AUC coefficient of variation (CV) by up to 30.9% on tau2-bench and 50.5% on ToolSandbox. Across Qwen-2.5 and Qwen-3.5 models, RSD reduces AUC CV in seven out of eight model-benchmark settings, while improving the final task progress in all eight settings.
  </details>

- **[The Metacognitive Profile Atlas: Domain-level confidence discrimination in 33 frontier LLMs on MMLU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06673)**  `arXiv:2605.06673`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jon-Paul Cacioli_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We release the Metacognitive Profile Atlas, a corpus of answer-plus-verbalized-confidence data from 33 frontier LLMs on 1,500 MMLU items stratified into six subject-group bins (46,157 scored observations after excluding parser-supplied values). The data allow Type-2 AUROC, the degree to which confidence discriminates correct from incorrect answers, to be decomposed by domain. The reliable finding is a population-level ordering on MMLU. Confidence discriminates best on Applied/Professional items (mean AUROC .750; ranked first in 100% of bootstrap resamples) and worst on Formal Reasoning and Natural Science (bottom two in 93% and 92%; Friedman chi^2(5) = 28.26, p < .0001). After Holm correction Applied exceeds Formal, Science, Social and Humanities; no other pair survives, including Applied versus Factual. The ordering holds when imprecise cells are excluded or down-weighted. Per-model six-domain profiles are not reliable (median split-half r = .16) and are released for exploration only; the six bins are a pragmatic grouping, not a latent construct. Within-family similarity of profile shape is significant overall, but after multiplicity correction only Anthropic is individually supported. Paired-item analysis separates genuine monitoring gains (Opus 4.1 to 4.5 and 4.7) from error removal (Gemma 3 27B to Gemma 4 31B). Models whose binary-probe confidence failed or could not be certified on a separate battery discriminate well under verbalized confidence, and three Gemma 3 models show the reverse pattern. This version incorporates revisions committed during peer review, corrects descriptions of external validity-screen results, and excludes 980 parser-imputed confidence values for GPT-oss-120B, whose v1 profile was an artefact of them.
  </details>

- **[ReVision: Selective Intrinsic Reasoning for Tool-Augmented, Reasoning-Intensive VQA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18637)**  `arXiv:2611.18637`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Ningxin Pan, Guochen Yan, Jialong Wu, Wentao Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  External tools substantially expand what multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can retrieve and compute on reasoning-intensive visual questions, and aggregate accuracy typically improves when tools are available. Our empirical study across six VQA benchmarks shows that aggregate accuracy gains under tool access can coexist with answer reversals: questions answered correctly without tools but incorrectly with tools. Trajectory analysis reveals substantially shorter reasoning turns under tool access, suggesting a shift from intrinsic visual reasoning toward reliance on external outputs. We propose ReVision, a training-free test-time method that registers intrinsic reconsideration as an optional action alongside search and code execution. When invoked, a separate tool-free multimodal call re-examines the original image, integrates accumulated evidence, and returns a revised analysis. The MLLM chooses if and when to use it, without fixed reflection stages or paired rollouts. ReVision improves over standard tool use on all six benchmarks (1.28--7.51\%) and outperforms Dual-Rollout Combine on five, with reasoning-token use comparable to standard tool use.
  </details>

- **[Replicating and Extending Weight-Space Programming: Frontier LLMs Compile Programs into Weights and Read Input Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21449)**  `arXiv:2611.21449`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Roksana Goworek, Alexander Hickey, Federico Nanni, Martin O'Reilly_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Researchers at MIT recently reported that GPT-6 Astra, a frontier large language model (LLM), can write the weights of small neural networks directly in a single reply, and can read the raw weights of small networks to say what they were trained to do. In a three-day sprint, we replicated and extended these results. Our four findings: (1) Writing weights reproduces. Astra's networks for the game Breakout scored 514, 550 and 307 (versus MIT's 517), and scored just as well on two modified versions of Breakout. Claude Opus 5.5 can also write working networks, but often runs out of output tokens. (2) What is new is translating programs into weights. Earlier models could write a good policy as code a generation before they could write network weights, and the networks the models write behave like compiled programs: two models independently decompiled Astra's Breakout network into Python matching its actions in all tested states. On classifying handwritten digits, Astra's networks scored 50% on average, below a simple classifier (82%). (3) Reading weights is mostly reading the input layer. Astra and Opus 5.5 identify which game a network plays almost perfectly, but on three new games we designed and trained ourselves, Astra recovered where things are on the board, while mostly missing how the game works. (4) Auditing from weights alone is possible but shallow. Without executing code, Astra picked out a small language model poisoned during training in 10 of 10 runs, one poisoned by extra training afterwards in 7 of 10, and a malicious adapter in 23 of 32 cases, but never identified the trigger or malicious behaviour. Open-weight models lag behind. From this early study, we conclude that LLMs can read and write small networks, but that this currently amounts to compiling hand-designed programs and reading the layout of the input layer. It is a promising but early tool for interpretability.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Large Language Models for Linguistic Annotation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21291)**  `arXiv:2611.21291`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ondřej Tichý, Anna Marklová, Barbora Bulantová, Magdalena Titlbachová, Jiří Milička_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Assessing large language models for linguistic annotation requires task-specific evaluation under documented conditions. We present dhAIBench, a Python benchmarking and annotation tool with a web interface for exploring and sharing results. It records prompts, configurations and predictions, enabling comparisons of annotation quality, confidence calibration, repeatability and estimated cost. We demonstrate the framework and the associated methodology through 293 runs of 48 models on nine linguistic annotation tasks spanning morphology, syntax, semantics, discourse-pragmatics, spelling normalization and learner-language correction. Models achieve high agreement with reference annotations on several tasks, including those on non-standardized, historical varieties as well as requiring contextual interpretation. The results support evaluating models against specific linguistic tasks, rather than relying on general rankings. To this end, we make the current and future benchmarks publicly available atthis http URL.
  </details>

- **[EDITORS Know Your Style! Editing LoRA Subspaces for Stylistic Attribution and Imitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20846)**  `arXiv:2611.20846`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zixuan Wang, Gregory Kang Ruey Lau, Xiao Tian, Jue Fan, Caroline Chaux, Bryan Kian Hsiang Low_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Disentangling writing style from semantic content is a fundamental challenge in literary text modeling. Style-content entanglement causes models to rely on semantic content for authorship attribution (AA) tasks and memorize author-specific content for style imitation (SI) tasks. We propose EDITORS (EditingLoRA Subspaces), a diagnose-then-deflate framework that adapts pretrained large language models and prioritizes stylistic features, making it effective for both AA and SI tasks. EDITORS first trains a diagnostic LoRA adapter using style-neutral statements derived from the training corpus to identify a content-oriented subspace. It then trains the final style adapter under activation-space deflation that projects input activations away from the identified content-oriented directions. We empirically demonstrate that EDITORS is effective. On three AA benchmarks spanning literature, social media, and news, EDITORS achieves state-of-the-art accuracy, outperforming the strongest existing baseline by up to 29%. On SI, EDITORS generates stylistically faithful and diverse passages, outperforming baselines in achieving style similarity while adhering to content specifications.
  </details>

- **[MemEmo: Evaluating Emotion in Memory Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20733)**  `arXiv:2611.20733`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Peng Liu, Zhen Tao, Jihao Zhao, Ding Chen, Yansong Zhang, Cuiping Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory systems address the challenge of context loss in Large Language Models during prolonged interactions. However, as an essential tool for warm companionship and personalized services, the effectiveness of memory systems in processing affective information remains underexamined. To bridge this gap, we propose an emotion-enhanced memory evaluation benchmark to assess the performance of mainstream and state-of-the-art memory systems in integrating and harnessing emotional information. First, we design a benchmark called \textbf{MemEmo} to assess the affective reasoning and tracking capabilities of memory systems across four dimensions: emotional information extraction, emotional memory updating, emotional memory question-answering, and emotional conflict detection. Second, we construct the \textbf{H}uman-\textbf{L}ike \textbf{M}emory \textbf{E}motion (\textbf{HLME}) dataset to provide an empirical foundation for scrutinizing memory systems. Finally, We evaluate five representative memory systems on the HLME dataset, reporting 11 performance metrics across four dimensions. Experimental results reveal that aggregating scores for all tested memory systems is up to 45\%, far below 100\% scale, exposing a profound disparity between memory system and affective intelligence.
  </details>

- **[IndustrialKnowEval: A Benchmark for Evaluating Multi-Level Industrial Knowledge in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20695)**  `arXiv:2611.20695`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Haiteng Wang, Haoze Li, Jingwei Guo, Jingbo Zhou, Si Liu, Lei Ren_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly explored for industrial tasks, yet few benchmarks evaluate their mastery of industrial knowledge across different types of cognitive demand. We introduce IndustrialKnowEval (IndKnow), a benchmark for industrial knowledge and engineering question answering. It contains 43,282 samples covering 21 task types across seven industrial sectors, including aerospace, electric power, and metallurgy, organized into four levels of cognitive demand: memory, comprehension, reasoning, and engineering application. Enterprise-based domain specialists and experienced researchers participate in all stages of dataset construction and quality control. We conduct a series of experiments on 18 representative LLMs, with domain experts deeply involved in the evaluation. Building upon IndKnow, we propose the Industrial Cognitive Capability Gap (ICCG) metric to characterize the performance differential between foundational industrial knowledge and higher-order reasoning and application, and we analyze it alongside absolute performance to provide a more complete view of model capabilities. We anticipate that IndustrialKnowEval will support the benchmarking of LLMs in industrial scenarios and promote the development of robust industrial intelligence. Code is available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[FRAAC: A French Multimodal Large Language Model and Dataset forPictogram-Based Augmentative and Alternative Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20094)**  `arXiv:2611.20094`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Alexandre Audibert, François Portet, Benoit Favre, Maja Jonck Hjuler_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pictograms are a core modality in augmentative and alternative communication (AAC), yet recent multimodal large language models (MLLMs) cannot reliably interpret or produce pictogram sequences. Two obstacles explain this gap: the absence of a standardized pictogram grammar and the scarcity of parallel text-pictogram data. We introduce Retrieval-Augmented Pictogram Selection (RAPS), an annotation method that refines a rule-based transcription system's output with LLM-based concept extraction and retrieval over a pictogram vocabulary, handling cases where hand-written rules fail (named entities, numbers, polysemous words) without requiring new rules. Applied to French, RAPS yields a synthetic corpus of 1.4 million parallel pairs, of which 300k are publicly released. In a human ranking study where annotators order four pictogram transcriptions of the same sentence, RAPS outperforms the rule-based state of the art. Moreover, we show that standard reference-based metrics separate systems of clearly different quality but fail to rank strong systems against each other. Finally, we build an instruction-tuned model that supports interaction in several AAC modalities: it accepts pictogram or text input and answers in pictograms, speech, or text.
  </details>

- **[The Silent Cost of Conversation History in LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20071)**  `arXiv:2611.20071`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ali Şenol_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) in multi-turn information systems accumulate their own earlier replies and a growing task state. We asked whether this conversation history lowers accuracy, and whether process-level quality metrics warn before it does. Each analysis was fixed before its outcomes were computed. In Study 1, on 48 sharded math tasks, retaining its replies lowered final success by 0.21 for GPT-4o-mini and 0.29 for Mistral-7B (Holm-adjusted p = 0.026 and 0.004); Llama-4 Scout showed the same direction, not significant. Study 2 introduces CDR-Stream, 100-turn state-tracking dialogues with an exact answer per turn and per-model difficulty calibration. With the full history, accuracy fell from about 0.6 to 0.1 within twenty turns at matched difficulty, whereas a solver-verified state summary kept it 0.52-0.59 higher (Holm-adjusted p less than 0.001). Consistency and logical coherence fell far less than accuracy, and stability rose. None of these metrics, a paired readiness measure or calibrated single-stream drift detectors gave robust, model-general early warning, although in Study 2 the repeat-based metrics were complete only for GPT-4o-mini. In the monitors we tested, a single 100-turn stream had power only for very large drops. Systems built on LLMs should keep task state outside the transcript and monitor across sessions.
  </details>

- **[The Vocabulary Tax: Safely Trimming Unused Tokens from LLMs for On-Device Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19913)**  `arXiv:2611.19913`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Sattaya Singkul_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open large language models (LLMs) and speech models ship vocabularies of up to 262k tokens, and every user downloads the tables those tokens index (the input embedding, an untied output head and, in recent on-device models, per-layer embeddings), whatever languages they need. We call the bytes in rows a deployment never uses the vocabulary tax and measure it, without downloading weights, for 1,244 generative and speech-recognition models on the Hugging Face Hub. For a Thai and English deployment it is a median 4.4% of model bytes below 0.5B parameters and 2.1% above 8B, and 1.93 GB in Gemma 4 E2B: it falls hardest on the small models that edge devices run. Keeping only the rows of the deployment's writing systems is exact. Keeping the tokens seen in the user's own text removes much more, a median 29.0% below 0.5B, but the usual way of doing it breaks BPE tokenizers: it drops the intermediate tokens that merges build on, so new text is split differently 1.9 times as often as the standard check reports. We repair this with merge closure, at a cost of 5.9% more rows, and add a trim certificate: a Good-Turing estimate, from the trimming text alone, of how much new text the trimmed tokenizer will split differently (correlation 0.996 over 300 settings in 22 languages) and of how much more text a target needs. Without test data our method never exceeds a 2% target, while prior keep rules checked on test data exceed it in 9-13 of 44 settings. On six models of 270M to 3B parameters in 11 languages, trimmed models keep their predictions: where the certificate passes, a median 89.7% of rows is removed and bits per character rise by at most 2.0%. The census, the trimming and the certificate are released as an open Python package, and the per-model tax for 41 languages as an open dataset.
  </details>

- **[Bridging the Terminology Gap: A Data-Centric Alignment Framework for Multilingual Translation in Cross-Border Corporation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19858)**  `arXiv:2611.19858`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jinsheng Wei, Song Cheng, Bo Xiao, Ke Zeng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As globalization accelerates the demand for seamless cross-border enterprise collaboration, multilingual translation remains bottlenecked by insufficient domain-specific terminology, rigid terminology translation, weak instruction-following capabilities, and high inference latency in long-instruction scenarios. To overcome these barriers, we propose Bridging the Terminology Gap (BTG), a data-centric framework. First, BTG mines cross-lingual term alignments from domain documents and leverages LLMs to synthesize terminology-rich parallel corpora. Second, a hybrid filtering strategy combining heuristics and XCOMET scoring ensures high data fidelity. Third, a two-stage training paradigm employs supervised fine-tuning (SFT) for targeted terminology injection, followed by reinforcement learning (RL) to refine output preferences, effectively balancing domain knowledge integration with model reliability. Extensive offline evaluations (e.g., XCOMET, GEMBA, Acc@T) and online human assessments demonstrate significant improvements in terminology accuracy and instruction-following capabilities, validating the effectiveness of our framework, which is now deployed on our cross-border collaboration platform.
  </details>

- **[ROUNDS: Source-Framed Diagnostic Revision after Multi-Turn Clinical Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19636)**  `arXiv:2611.19636`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jinrui Fang, Runhan Chen, Yining Wu, Jiawei Xu, Xu Yang, Jian Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) achieve high scores on medical benchmarks, but does this performance translate into reliable diagnosis as a conversation unfolds? In diagnostic conversations, users can provide clinical findings across multiple turns, ask for a diagnosis without supplying answer options, and introduce opinions from others. Models must therefore form diagnoses from the available evidence and decide how to respond to subsequent advice. We introduce ROUNDS, a framework that evaluates these abilities after a fixed sequence of clinical evidence delivered over multiple turns. ROUNDS compares diagnosis with and without answer options, presents the same incorrect diagnosis under clinician and lay framing, and varies support for competing diagnoses in written five-clinician panels. Across seven models and 1,035 cases from five datasets, average accuracy was 76.9% with answer options and 57.0% without them. Clinician-framed misleading advice produced lower final accuracy than lay-framed advice in every model, with differences of 4.6 to 38.2 percentage points. Initially correct diagnoses more often became incorrect answers than abstentions, and many adopted the suggested disease. Conversely, accuracy increased as more panel members supported the correct diagnosis, including when the majority opinion remained unchanged. These findings reveal a gap between medical benchmark performance and reliable diagnosis in conversation: models respond to external opinions, but remain vulnerable to abandoning correct diagnoses without new clinical evidence.
  </details>

- **[Do Rule-Based Tools Help LLMs Translate Endangered Uralic Languages in the Prompt or over MCP?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19439)**  `arXiv:2611.19439`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Iaroslav Chelombitko, Mika Hämäläinen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Komi-Zyrian, Moksha, Erzya and Udmurt have mature rule-based resources, among them finite-state analysers, constraint grammars and bilingual dictionaries, which language models do not use. We ask whether giving a model access to these resources improves machine translation from the four languages into English, and whether it matters how the access is provided: as analyses computed in advance and written into the prompt, or as tools that the model calls itself through the Model Context Protocol (MCP). We evaluate manually: an expert linguist read every English output of the strongest model under three conditions, 816 sentences in all, accepted or rejected each one, and named the main error in every rejected translation. With the analyses in the prompt, the number of accepted translations rises from 13 to 43 out of 272; with the tools attached it rises to 55. The gain comes from the bilingual dictionary. For Udmurt, whose dictionary has no English side, the prompt-based condition brings no gain at all, while for the other three languages an English gloss reaches the prompt for 74 to 80% of the source words. Tool calling is also much harder to get working than prompt injection. Of 31 open-weight models, 14 never call a tool, 11 of them because they cannot produce a usable call, and half of the calls that are made name a language the toolkit does not support. We release the data set, every model output and all tool-call logs.
  </details>

- **[OmitEHR: Scalable Omission Detection in LLM-Generated Clinical Summaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19343)**  `arXiv:2611.19343`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Bridget Lin, Ryan Welch, Bobak Seddighzadeh, Fran Riley, Kaitlyn Stoehr, Margarita I. Pipinos, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Health systems increasingly rely on large language models (LLMs) to draft summaries of patient medical records, yet evaluations now find omissions, the absence of clinically important information, to be the most common failure mode. Detecting omissions in an LLM-generated summary requires knowing which facts in the record are salient, a signal that existing methods hand-specify, ignore, or recompute at inference with costly LLM judges. We present OmitEHR, a reference-free omission detector that learns clinical salience from historical clinician-written summaries predating widespread LLM use. OmitEHR learns to predict which source-note sentences clinicians include in their summaries, then surfaces high-salience sentences not covered by an LLM-generated summary as candidate omissions. We apply OmitEHR to brief hospital course (BHC) summarization using 5,000 admissions from a large academic medical center. On held-out admissions, OmitEHR outperformed all evaluated baselines at predicting salient source sentences, including a prompted LLM judge. The full omission detection pipeline took 17.7 to 19.5 seconds and cost \$0.03 to \$0.13 per summary depending on the base model, 1.6 to 1.7 times faster and 5.6 to 6.7 times cheaper than scoring every source sentence with an LLM judge. In a blinded review of 25 admissions, two board-certified clinicians confirmed a mean of 5.6 clinically meaningful omissions per summary surfaced by OmitEHR, compared with 3.3 for a full LLM pipeline, counting candidates confirmed by at least one clinician. The efficiency and validity of OmitEHR make it a practical tool for health systems to flag omissions before a summary reaches the clinician.
  </details>

- **[MarineSparkLM-4B: A Lightweight Language Model for Marine and Offshore Engineering Built by Parameter-Efficient Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19151)**  `arXiv:2611.19151`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Cheng Siong Chin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Marine and offshore engineering text is spread across preprints, open-access journals, encyclopedias and institutional documents, and the amount of it is small by pretraining standards. We ask how far a lightweight language model for marine and offshore engineering can be taken with parameter-efficient adaptation on a single workstation-class device. Starting from the 4-billion-parameter OceanGPT-basic-4B-Instruct, we train two independent LoRA adapters, each updating 33.0 million of 4.06 billion parameters (0.81%). Training took 4 hours 40 minutes for the first adapter and 10 minutes for the second on one NVIDIA DGX Spark. The first adapter is trained by next-token prediction on a 61.6-million-token corpus covering ten engineering themes. The corpus was built from 13,879 source documents, of which 7,069 were retained after deduplication and a two-tier keyword filter that removes cross-domain lexical collisions. Its validation loss falls from 2.082 to 1.976 over 1,500 steps. Direct testing showed that this adapter could still drift off topic on a retrieval-grounded ballast-water prompt, so we trained a second adapter on 2,295 question-answer pairs (114 hand-written and 2,181 generated by the base model from corpus passages) with the loss restricted to answer tokens. On 20 held-out prompts scored by hand for accuracy, topicality and specificity (maximum 120), the plain base model, the continuation adapter, the instruction adapter and the instruction adapter with retrieval scored 42, 56, 84 and 88. Absolute accuracy stays low (14 of 40 without retrieval and 20 of 40 with it). We report the full pipeline, two negative results (a reverted keyword fix and an instruction run that overfit within 100 steps), and the limits of the evaluation, which uses one small prompt set, no inter-rater agreement measure and model-generated reference answers.
  </details>

- **[KUDEX: A Zero-Shot Decompositional Framework for Logical Analysis in Natural Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18851)**  `arXiv:2611.18851`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zixu Zhao, Tianhang Zhang, Tong He, Zheng Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While Large Language Models (LLMs) excel at generating fluent text, they often struggle with multi-step logical reasoning with inferential connections among statements. We introduce KUDEX (Knowledge-Upward-Downward Reasoning Extension), a novel zero-shot, decompositional framework for analyzing and verifying logical relationships in natural language. Unlike traditional approaches that rely on surface-level entailment checks, KUDEX leverages LLMs to decompose reasoning into atomic primitives and constructs transparent reasoning graphs that serve both as execution traces and audit logs. Our framework includes a general prompt adaptation pipeline that adapts to diverse reasoning tasks without additional fine-tuning or domain-specific pipelines. Through comprehensive experiments on benchmarks across four domains, we demonstrate that KUDEX provides superior performance compared to existing methods, offering a more granular and robust framework for assessing logical relationships between statements.
  </details>

- **[Wrong with Confidence: Measuring Hallucination, Overconfidence and Identity-Conditioned Behaviour in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18728)**  `arXiv:2611.18728`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Md. Rejaul Korim Sadi, Toufiqur Rahman Tasin, Golam Mostofa Naeem, Dewan Fahad Chowdhury, Hatim Al Amin Chowdhury_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models answer underspecified questions readily, and accuracy-only evaluation rewards guessing over abstaining. Observational studies link query underspecification to hallucination, but they cannot separate the effect of missing information from item difficulty, and they rarely ask what the model tells the user. We report a pre-registered, paired behavioural study of five open-weight models (Gemma 3 27B, Gemma 4 31B, gpt-oss-120b, Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B and Nemotron 3 Super). Each of 142 factual questions was posed in a fully specified form and in a referentially underspecified form with the same answer key, and three probes measured identity-conditioned behaviour. Two raters scored 5,620 responses under a fixed rubric; effects were estimated with mixed-effects models. Without the referent, the models sought clarification on 33.7% of prompts and answered the rest. Among attempted answers, the error rate rose from 39.1% to 52.0% (mixed-effects odds ratio 4.12, 95% CI 2.76-6.14), and specialist-domain questions failed more often than general ones in every model. Unelicited verbal uncertainty did not track this risk: 75.3% of wrong answers carried no hedge, wrong answers to underspecified prompts were hedged no more often than wrong answers to clear prompts (27.3% vs 22.3%), and hedging separated correct from incorrect answers only weakly (AUROC 0.56). Gendered pronouns followed occupational stereotypes whenever a model committed to one; any stated user identity lengthened answers by about 45% relative to an anonymous user; claimed expertise did not reduce safety caveats. The models either ask or answer as if the question were well posed, and the wording of a guess does not reveal that it is one. Evaluations should score clarification as a distinct outcome and check label validity as well as rater agreement.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating the Robustness of Fact-Checking LLMs on Multilingual Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18623)**  `arXiv:2611.18623`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Radhika Shah_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated fact-checking benchmarks typically pair claims with clean, curated evidence in high-resource languages, but real-world evidence is often incomplete, misleading, or written in a language where models are weaker. We evaluate the robustness of fact-checking LLMs for different languages. Building on EuroVerdict, we construct gold evidence sets for 190 claims in each of English, German, Greek, and Romanian, then apply four corruption strategies at two severity levels. We test LLMs on a set of claims, providing them with evidence from the sets above. A rubric-based LLM-as-a-judge scores reasoning quality. Verdict accuracy proves highly sensitive to both factors: Misleading edit cut Llama 3.1 8B's English accuracy from 80.00% to 16.84%, and losses widen as language resource level decreases. Machine-translating Greek and Romanian claims into English recovers much, but not all, of the low-resource gap. Judge scores decline more slowly than accuracy, suggesting that automated reasoning evaluation underestimates degradation. These results caution against deploying LLM fact-checkers on noisy multilingual evidence, and we release our evidence datasets and evaluation code to support future work.
  </details>

- **[The Judge Is Not Impartial: Self-Preference in Medical LLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18616)**  `arXiv:2611.18616`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zara Ansari, Shlok Natarajan, Vincent Yip, Cally Lin, Avi Udash, Mia Garvey, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to evaluate model-generated medical content, creating a potential circularity: an LLM judge may favor outputs generated by the same model independently of clinical quality. To characterize LLM judge self-preference in medical settings, we measured same-model affinity in single-turn specialist question answering and multi-turn standardized-patient encounters. We evaluated eight models, two from OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, and Qwen, on 620 real point of care questions from clinicians (Real-POCQi). The same eight models also generated and judged 200 standardized patient-clinician scenarios (MedSP1000) up to 8 visible turns. In total, our analysis spans 13,760 LLM judgments. We define three complementary measures of self-preference. Answer Rank Bias, our primary measure, compared the rank assigned to a fixed response by its own-model judge with its average rank from outside judges. Answer Score Bias made the analogous comparison using rubric scores. Own-Answer Win Rate measured how often a judge ranked its own model's response above a competitor. Higher values across all these measures indicate greater degrees of self-preference. In Real-POCQi, pooled Answer Rank Bias was $+1.22$ positions, and all eight judges favored their own answers. Self-preference persisted both with and without a grading rubric. Revealing generator identities modestly weakened self-preference by an average of $-0.094$ positions. In MedSP1000, pooled Answer Rank Bias ranged from $+0.53$ to $+0.62$ positions, with no clear difference by conversation length ($p=0.17$). LLM judges systematically favored responses generated by their own models across multiple formats. Clinical AI deployments should exercise increased vigilance including multiple evaluation measures and judge panels spanning differing model families when deploying LLM-as-a-judge systems.
  </details>

- **[When Helpful Agents Cause Financial Harm: Evaluating LLM Agent Safety in Executable Economic Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18612)**  `arXiv:2611.18612`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuzhu Ling, Jiale Han, Benyou Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As LLM agents become increasingly capable of making consequential economic decisions, their safety under economic pressures remains poorly understood. We introduce EconSafeBench, the first benchmark for systematically evaluating the safety of LLM agents in executable economic environments. EconSafeBench contains 828 cases across five economic scenarios and four categories of safety violations. A key design is the matched evaluation of three sources of safety pressure, including explicit user requests, pressure from other actors, and pressure arising from the agent's own pursuit of a performance objective. We further evaluate safety at both static and dynamic scales through 30-period interactions with persistent memory and matched controls. Across eight LLMs, all evaluated models exhibit safety violations on the static benchmark, with overall violation rates ranging from 8.5% to 40.6%. We further find that violations can occur even without an explicit unsafe request, and that initially compliant agents may begin to violate as performance pressure increases over time. These findings highlight the need for stronger safety alignment and rigorous evaluation before LLM agents are deployed in high-stakes economic environments.
  </details>

- **[Understanding Failures in LLM Reasoning by Learning Structured Representations of Chain-of-Thought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18611)**  `arXiv:2611.18611`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Tommaso Felice Banfi, Mubashara Akhtar, Jingwei Ni, Mrinmaya Sachan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chain-of-Thought (CoT) is central to improving reasoning in large language models, yet existing evaluation methods focus on step-level signals and ignore the underlying structure of reasoning traces. This raises a key question: what aspects of reasoning quality (i.e., correctness, efficiency, and failure patterns) are encoded in the structure of a CoT trace? We introduce \textbf{CoT2Graph}, a framework that represents CoT traces as directed dependency graphs, making relationships between reasoning steps explicit and capturing patterns such as backtracking, branching, and subtask merging. We model these graphs with graph neural networks and use attribution-based explanations to identify structural patterns associated with reasoning quality. CoT2Graph reveals distinct patterns of successful and failed reasoning: correct CoTs place greater importance on early planning, whereas incorrect CoTs are associated with overloaded synthesis steps and dependency violations. We further show that graph-based signals better predict whether a CoT leads to a correct answer, outperforming previous tree-based structural methods by up to 2.5\% and simple length-based baselines by 7.8\%, while also improving Best-of-$N$ selection. Overall, our results demonstrate that reasoning structure encodes rich signals about reasoning quality, motivating a shift from sequence-level evaluation to structural analysis.
  </details>

- **[Justification of Emotional Appeal in Computational Argumentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18607)**  `arXiv:2611.18607`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yanran Chen, Steffen Eger_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Emotional appeals (EAs) are often treated as fallacious in computational argumentation, yet they may be justified when warranted by the context. We introduce the task of predicting EA justification and present the first human-annotated dataset for this task, covering 1,176 argument--context pairs. Grounded in philosophical literature, our framework evaluates EA justification along logical and emotional dimensions. Human annotations show that justification criteria form a shared latent judgment space, with evidential proportionality and normative acceptability most strongly associated with overall justification. We further find that EAs without logical support are not always judged fully unjustified, and stronger EAs are not necessarily judged less justified. Finally, LLM experiments show that EA justification prediction remains challenging.
  </details>

- **[Syntactic Complexity Convergence in Dialogue: Analysis of the Phenomenon and Application to LLM Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18606)**  `arXiv:2611.18606`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yu Wang, Yanran Chen, Yifan Wang, Steffen Eger, Hendrik Buschmeier_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We revisit the under-explored phenomenon of syntactic complexity convergence in dialogue. Extending prior topic-level analyses to full conversations, we examine five human-human datasets and a set of human-LLM dialogues. We integrate and evaluate a multidimensional set of syntactic complexity metrics to quantify structural variation in dialogue. Our analyses reveal robust syntactic complexity convergence across human-human datasets, especially in task-oriented interactions, and statistical tests confirm that these effects are not due to chance. In contrast, human-LLM dialogues show no significant convergence under individual metrics; however, linear-CKA uncovers a distinct cross-metric convergence pattern. Motivated by these differences, we use syntactic complexity as a feature for detecting LLM-involved dialogue, achieving 96\% accuracy. Our classification results demonstrate its potential without relying on likelihood-based signals such as surprisal, which require a language model as the estimator.
  </details>

- **[A Multi-Word Tokenizer for 204 Languages That Needs Fewer Tokens Than GPT-4o's, Including on English](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13059)**  `arXiv:2611.13059`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Johin Johny Arimbur, Ankur Ashit Ghosh, Shervin Sam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Languages of India need 1.6 to 14 times as many tokens as English on GPT-4o's tokenizer for the same content, which makes them more expensive and slower to serve. We train tokenizers from scratch on human-written text in about 200 languages. Havon v3 is a byte-level BPE tokenizer with 262,144 tokens and a pre-tokenizer that keeps combining marks with their letters and chunks scripts written without spaces. On FLORES-200 devtest it needs fewer tokens than GPT-4o's o200k_base on all 25 languages of India (median 0.74 of GPT-4o's count) and on 172 of 204 languages, but 7% more on English. Havon v4 adds a second, multi-word training stage (SuperBPE) to v3. At exactly GPT-4o's vocabulary size of 200,019 tokens, v4 needs 6.1% fewer tokens than GPT-4o on English and fewer on 161 of 204 languages and all 25 languages of India (median 0.74); at the same size, v3 needs 9.4% more on English and wins on 133. With 262,144 tokens, v4 needs 9.6% fewer tokens on English and fewer on 182 of 204 languages, and with 524,288 tokens it needs fewer on 203 of 204. The gains are largest for English, Hindi and the Romance languages; scripts without spaces, such as Khmer, Burmese and Tibetan, gain little and lose subword merges. Havon v5 replaces v4's Python-only code sample with permissively licensed code in 15 languages: on code it needs 1.03 to 1.22 times GPT-4o's tokens, against 1.11 to 1.29 for v4, while keeping all 25 languages of India (median 0.74, all significant under a sentence-level bootstrap) and English (0.94). These are compression results only: we have not yet evaluated a model trained with v4 or v5.
  </details>

- **[The Indic Token Tax in 2026: Measuring Tokenizer. Disparity and Its Cost for the Languages of India](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13058)**  `arXiv:2611.13058`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Johin Johny Arimbur, Ankur Ashit Ghosh, Shervin Sam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Commercial language model APIs bill per token, so a tokenizer that needs more tokens for the same content in one language makes that language more expensive, slower, and able to fit less text in a fixed context window. We measure this disparity for nine public tokenizers, including those of GPT-4, GPT-4o, Qwen2.5, DeepSeek-V3, Mistral-Nemo and four Sarvam models, on all 25 languages of India available in FLORES-200 (2,009 professionally translated sentences aligned with English) and on 13,732 human-translated Hinglish-English social media pairs from PHINC. On GPT-4o's tokenizer the same content needs between 1.57 times (Hindi) and 5.01 times (Odia) the tokens of English, and 13.7 times for Santali in Ol Chiki script; GPT-4's tokenizer needs up to 12.8 times. Sarvam-30B/105B is the only third-party tokenizer that stays below 2.6 times on every language (median 1.53). In an illustrative scenario using official list prices accessed on 5 November 2026 and stated assumptions, a support bot handling 100,000 chats a month on GPT-4o would cost about INR 56,640 in English and INR 102,829 in Marathi; these are estimates, not measured production costs. Against an openly licensed Hindi and Marathi morphological segmentation set, no tokenizer places more than 35% of its word-internal boundaries on morpheme boundaries (boundary F1 at most 0.48). In a companion paper we present a tokenizer that reduces this tax. Code, pinned artifacts and raw token counts are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Form Over Content In Gradient-Based Data Attribution Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19589)**  `arXiv:2609.19589`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Sunwoo Kim, Seokwon Jung, Sohyung Kim, Seong Joon Oh, Alice Oh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Data attribution methods using gradient similarity are widely used to analyze and select training data for large language models, but what gradient similarity actually measures is debated. Some interpret it as identifying task-relevant skills, while other work reports that surface form is the main factor. We resolve this debate for supervised fine-tuning examples by varying task and answer format independently. Specifically, we render benchmarks in different answer formats, such that datasets can share a task without a format or a format without a task. We find that gradient alignment follows the answer format, as benchmark pairs sharing an answer format align strongly (disattenuated cosine near 0.4), while same benchmarks rendered with different answer format classes show no alignment (near 0.0). We demonstrate that this ordering holds from the earliest pretraining checkpoints through post-training, and across model scales and families. We then analyze the released selections of LESS, a gradient-based data selection method for instruction tuning, and find that each target's selections over-represent the target's own answer format. Hence, we demonstrate that gradient-based attribution methods track format similarity more than task semantics, meaning that such methods, as well as the semantic interpretation of the gradient, should be tested on data where answer format and task vary independently for greater robustness and reliability.
  </details>

- **[RubricReviewer: From Direct Critique to Rubric-Driven Review via Agentic Evidence Synthesis and Human-Aligned Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00005)**  `arXiv:2608.00005`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Shuyu Guo, Wenxiang Hu, Yuyue Zhao, Lun Zhou, Yougang Lyu, Xiaohui Yan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Peer review at major venues is under unprecedented submission pressure, motivating the use of large language models (LLMs) as review assistants. Existing LLM-based reviewers, however, face two structural limitations. First, they map manuscripts directly to reviews, leaving the underlying rubric implicit and entangling its derivation with the judgement. Second, the prevailing paradigms each capture only half of a good review: training-free agents gather broad evidence but produce undirected critiques, while training-based reviewers inherit human discriminative judgement together with its noise and uneven coverage. We introduce RubricReviewer, a fully rubric-driven framework that addresses both limitations. It makes rubric generation an explicit intermediate step, so that both review generation and the final assessment are conditioned on paper-adaptive rubrics. It further combines a training-free agent (Scout) that gathers external evidence with a human-aligned trained model (Aligner) that consumes this evidence, fusing the strengths of both supervision sources. Experiments on real-world submissions show that RubricReviewer produces reviews that are markedly more comprehensive and more discriminative than prior systems, and exhibits the strongest robustness against adversarial prompt-injection attacks. Ablation studies further confirm the necessity of each component.
  </details>

- **[CARE: A Conformal Safety Layer for Medical Summarization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08969)**  `arXiv:2606.08969`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Suhana Bedi, Bridget Lin, Anson Y. Zhou, Chloe O. Stanwyck, Jenelle A. Jindal, Sanmi Koyejo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for medical summarization but can omit medically important information and introduce unsupported claims. Existing error-detection methods produce heuristic or uncalibrated scores, providing no formal control over missed errors and no principled way to trade off safety against clinician review burden. We introduce Conformal Assessment for Risk Evaluation (CARE), a post-hoc risk-control layer that adds calibrated omission and hallucination annotations without retraining the summarizer. CARE uses scalar conformal risk control (CRC) to bound the probability that a document contains an unflagged hallucinated sentence, and Learn-Then-Test fixed-sequence testing (LTT-FST) to control the expected fraction of true omissions left unsurfaced for review. Omission surfacing depends jointly on sentence importance and summary coverage, a two-dimensional threshold-selection problem; we show that calibrating one gate and then imposing an uncalibrated second gate can invalidate risk control. Across five medical summarization tasks, mean held-out violation over 100 random calibration/test resplits remains at or below the target for both controllers. At $\alpha=0.15$, LTT-FST achieves the lowest review workload on three of five datasets among omission baselines held to the same confidence guarantee. Review workload varies by task and is highest for long, high-compression documents. On 46 clinician-annotated documents, the calibrated hallucination controller flags 88% of clinician-identified hallucinated sentences. In a preliminary study of 75 clinician reviews, assisted review-time omission coverage increased from 50.4% to 79.0% without increasing review time. Overall, CARE provides a principled framework for targeted, risk-controlled review of LLM-generated medical summaries.
  </details>

- **[BioELX: Context-Aware Cross-lingual Biomedical Entity Linking without Task-Specific Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27380)**  `arXiv:2605.27380`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yi Wang, Corina Dima, Liangyu Zhong, Steffen Staab_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cross-lingual biomedical entity linking (BEL) maps mentions in any language to unique identifiers in a biomedical knowledge base, supporting clinical and biomedical NLP applications. We identify two issues affecting current systems. First, the UMLS (Bodenreider,2004) aliases used to train cross-lingual BEL retrievers are heavily skewed toward English, so retrievers generalize poorly to non-English mentions. Second, although context is often necessary for disambiguation, naively injecting context into retrievers trained only to align aliases severely degrades retrieval. We propose BioELX, a retrieve-rerank framework that addresses both issues. For retrieval, we continue training SapBERT_multi (Liu et al., 2021b) using Wikidata-derived cross-lingual alias supervision, forming shared concept neighborhoods across languages. For reranking, we adapt pretrained LLM rerankers to entity linking through mention-anchored prompting, which marks the target mention so that rerankers score candidates with respect to the intended mention rather than other salient tokens in the context. Experiments show that BioELX achieves new state-of-the-art results on four cross-lingual BEL benchmarks, improving Recall@1 by 4.8 to 18.2 percentage points over prior best results, without any task-specific BEL annotations. Our code and resources are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[JuICE: A Benchmark for Evaluating LLM-Judge in Identifying Cultural Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26955)**  `arXiv:2605.26955`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiho Jin, Junho Myung, Juhyun Oh, Junyeong Park, Rifki Afina Putri, Sunipa Dev, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed to users around the world, they are integrated into everyday tasks across diverse cultural contexts, from drafting personal communications to brainstorming creative ideas. These tasks are inherently cultural: they require contextual appropriateness, symbolic resonance, and tacit cultural expectations that native speakers draw on instinctively, meaning that a response can be factually plausible yet unmistakably wrong to a local reader. Existing cultural benchmarks have treated culture as a flat set of facts via fact verification or norm entailment methods, and have adopted LLM-as-a-Judge without examining whether they can capture such thick cultural errors. To address this gap, we present JuICE (Benchmark for LLM-Judge in Identifying Cultural Errors), a multilingual dataset of 7,470 span-level annotations of cultural and linguistic errors in long-form LLM responses. It covers 1,050 query-response pairs from four countries (the United States, South Korea, Indonesia, and Bangladesh), in both English and their countries' main languages. Using JuICE, we find that even the strongest LLM-judge achieves only an F1 of 0.51 in the erroneous span detection task. Furthermore, LLM-judges consistently miss thick cultural errors that local residents readily identify. Our findings suggest that robust cultural evaluation must move beyond surface-level detection toward frameworks that account for the depth and situatedness of cultural meaning.
  </details>

- **[Verbal tics in frontier language models: A critical review of current releases, research evidence, and public discussion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.19139)**  `arXiv:2604.19139`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Shuai Wu, Xue Li, Zhijun Wang, Bolun Liu, Weilin Cai, Zihao Su, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Repeated praise, canned reassurance, formulaic contrasts, and conspicuous vocabulary figure prominently in discussions of large language models. Context governs how these habits should be read. A conventional phrase can orient a reader, whereas a fluent answer can reinforce a false belief. This critical review examines linguistic habits and sycophancy across eight developer families. We verify public offerings from OpenAI, Anthropic, Google DeepMind, xAI, ByteDance, Moonshot AI, DeepSeek, and Xiaomi against official release and API documentation through 1 October 2026. We then synthesize empirical research on lexical overrepresentation, stylistic variation, social warmth, and agreement alongside benchmark methods and dated public discussions in English and Chinese. Published studies document recurring linguistic patterns and agreement that distorts human judgment, but comparable measurements for the newest releases remain sparse in the retrieved material. Current user accounts report both persistent complaints and clearer prose, and experiences vary by task and prompt design. We propose separate measures for recurrence, contextual appropriateness, and belief distortion paired with exact service records and language-specific annotation. This framework keeps claims about writing quality and conversational reliability testable as model services change.
  </details>

- **[A Parameter-Efficient Transfer Learning Approach through Multitask Prompt Distillation and Decomposition for Clinical NLP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.06650)**  `arXiv:2604.06650`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Cheng Peng, Mengxian Lyu, Ziyi Chen, Yonghui Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing prompt-based fine-tuning methods typically learn task-specific prompts independently, imposing significant computing and storage overhead at scale when deploying multiple clinical natural language processing (NLP) systems. We present a multitask prompt distillation and decomposition framework that learns a single shared metaprompt from 21 diverse clinical source tasks and adapts it to unseen target tasks with fewer than 0.05% trainable parameters. Evaluated across five clinical NLP task types (named entity recognition, relation extraction, question answering, natural language inference, and summarization) on 10 held-out target datasets using three backbone models (LLaMA 3.1 8B, Meditron3 8B, gpt-oss 20B), our framework consistently outperforms LoRA by 1.5~1.7% despite using orders of magnitude fewer parameters, and exceeds single-task prompt tuning by 6.1~6.6%. The gpt-oss 20B model achieves the highest overall performance, particularly on clinical reasoning tasks. The strong zero- and few-shot performance demonstrates better transferability of the shared prompt representation.
  </details>

- **[Breaking the Silence: A Dataset and Benchmark for Bangla Text-to-Gloss Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.02293)**  `arXiv:2504.02293`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Sharif Mohammad Abdullah, Abhijit Paul, Shubhashis Roy Dipta, Zarif Masud, Shebuti Rayana, Ahmedul Kabir_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Gloss is a written approximation that bridges Sign Language (SL) and its corresponding spoken language. Despite a deaf and hard-of-hearing population of at least 3 million in Bangladesh, Bangla Sign Language (BdSL) remains largely understudied, with very limited prior work on Bangla text-to-gloss translation and no publicly available parallel dataset at the time of this work. To address this gap, we construct the first publicly available Bangla text-to-gloss dataset, consisting of 1,000 manually annotated and 4,000 synthetically generated Bangla sentence-gloss pairs, along with 159 expert human-annotated pairs used as a test set. Our experimental framework performs a comparative analysis between several fine-tuned open-source models and a leading closed-source LLM to evaluate their performance in low-resource BdSL translation. GPT-5.4 achieves the best automatic metric scores, while open-source models perform competitively despite being much smaller. This work introduces the first publicly available expert-annotated Bangla text-to-gloss dataset, benchmark and trained model for Bangla text-to-gloss translation. It also demonstrates the effectiveness of systematically generated synthetic data for addressing challenges in low-resource sign language translation.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Job Titles: Interpreting Early-Career Job Advertisements with Sparse Autoencoders and Semantic Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21586)**  `arXiv:2611.21586`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yisong Cao, Leah Kim, Brian Wright, Ali Rivera_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Job titles and job descriptions alone provide limited insight into how technical roles differ in a changing job market. To assess how such roles differ in their work and industry applications, we design and compare two methods based on deep learning and transformer representations using 36,081 early-career job postings collected from LinkedIn in the United States between December 2025 and October 2026.We first apply PERC, short for Preprocessing (P), Embedding (E), Dimensionality Reduction (R) and Clustering (C). We employ GPT-6 Luna to classify job description passages into Team context, Role task and Qualifications. We then create combinations of these sections and examine whether embedding and clustering the segmented passages produce meaningful semantic clusters about different job roles. We use Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD) to rank observed differences between embedding distributions and employ a pretrained sparse autoencoder (SAE) to extract features from layer-19 representations of Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. Lastly, we employ GPT-6 Luna to come up with hypotheses about job roles' feature patterns, and GPT-6.1 Terra to investigate whether those patterns exist in held-out job descriptions from unseen companies.Some example findings include Data Scientist advertisements placing more emphasis on machine learning model development, Data Analyst advertisements on reporting business performance, and Machine Learning Engineer advertisements on applying developed models in real-world settings. We also observe differences in the applications described by Data Analyst and Business Analyst advertisements across selected months, although our dataset's scale and uneven coverage limit their interpretation. Overall, we present a realistic application of SAEs and PERC to unstructured textual data and examine how automated insight extraction could support manual analysis of job descriptions.
  </details>

- **[Rubric-Based AES with LLM Ensembles: Scoring of Student Responses in Critical Online Reasoning Using Ensembles of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21422)**  `arXiv:2611.21422`  `cs.CL`  
  _Daniel Baumartz, Alexander Mehler, Patrick Schrottenbacher, Leon Hammerla, Sebastian Gombert_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Grading student responses is costly and time-consuming. Moreover, human annotators are prone to bias, fatigue, and rater drift, which further limits grading reliability. Various methods have been employed to develop automated essay scoring (AES) systems to reduce these costs and address bias-related issues. These methods range from early text-feature-based systems to current machine-learning and transformer-based models. Specifically, large language models (LLMs) offer the potential to streamline the development of AES systems. However, they are subject to well-known limitations, such as hallucinations and prompt sensitivity. This creates a need to explore the potential of a mixed-methods approach that leverages the synergies among individual LLMs through ensemble formation. We evaluate the potential of ensembles of LLMs to match human-rater agreement when grading student responses in economics and medical education across 103 criteria. We use 631 German-language responses and corresponding human ratings by 2 to 5 annotators from a previously conducted assessment. We experiment with 523 rubric-based prompt configurations across 15 open-weight LLMs and assess their consistency with human raters using Krippendorff's alpha for inter-annotator agreement (IAA) and the Alternative Annotator Test (alt-test). Our results show that replacing one human rater with the best-performing LLM--prompt configuration maintains or improves IAA for 91 of the 103 criteria and that LLMs pass the alt-test for 90 criteria. Our findings suggest that current state-of-the-art LLMs can support AES of German responses in economics and medical education, reducing human workload and enabling faster processing, while validation against human ratings remains necessary.
  </details>

- **[Stability-Compensated Threshold Calibration for Selection-Conditioned Risk Control in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21384)**  `arXiv:2611.21384`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhiyuan Wang, Yuanchang Ye, Jiahao Yuan, Jinhao Duan, Xiaoshuang Shi, Weijun Xie, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models often produce hallucinated answers, motivating selective prediction policies that abstain on uncertain queries. However, heuristic uncertainty thresholds alone do not provide statistical control of errors among accepted predictions. Linear Expectation Constraints (LEC) reduce selection-conditioned error rate (SCER) calibration to a nonpositive expected linear-loss constraint, but the associated loss is non-monotonic in the acceptance threshold. We construct an exact counterexample under i.i.d. sampling demonstrating that LEC's empirical threshold-selection rule can violate the population SCER target. To address this risk-transfer gap, we introduce Stability-Compensated Linear Expectation Control (SC-LEC), which uses the negative linear risk of a theoretical full-data reference to compensate for threshold-selection instability. For local expansion beyond a safe anchor, we derive two finite-sample certificates: a stability-based certificate exploiting rank crossings and a direct certificate using the structure of threshold expansion to reduce conservatism. With a valid population anchor margin, our certificate conditions ensure finite-sample marginal SCER control, and independent margin certification further provides a $1-\delta$ high-probability guarantee for this calibration-averaged control. Experiments on CommonsenseQA, ARC-Challenge, and TriviaQA show useful retention gains beyond the same certified anchor, while revealing conservative behavior in small-margin regimes.
  </details>

- **[LATTE-WordSeg-Lite: Multi-Granularity Thai Word Segmentation on a CPU, with a Reproducible Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21300)**  `arXiv:2611.21300`  `cs.CL`  
  _Thodsaporn Chay-intr, Kobkrit Viriyayudhakorn, Thanaruk Theeramunkong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models read Thai through subword tokens, without a word segmenter. Yet Thai treebanks, speech metrics and morphological resources are built on words, often at several granularities, and word boundaries resolve ambiguities that subword tokens leave open. LATTE (Lattice ATTentive Encoding) reads a text at several granularities at once, through a lattice of dictionary words, but it is a large GPU model. We distil it into LATTE-WordSeg-Lite, a CPU segmenter of 1.17M parameters that keeps the lattice as features. It outputs compounds, words and morphemes in one pass, and learns only from annotated data and text whose licences allow commercial use. To compare it with other tools, published Thai scores do not suffice, as they come from different files, splits and scorers. We therefore rebuild seven test sets from pinned sources and score twelve systems under one protocol. With the unit that fits each test set, LATTE-WordSeg-Lite scores above DeepCut on five sets, four of them from corpora that its teachers learnt from. Called line by line on one CPU thread, it runs 23 times as fast as DeepCut. We release the model, the benchmark scripts and all predictions.
  </details>

- **[Validating LLM-Based Essay Scorers: A Comparative Analysis in Critical Online Reasoning in Medical Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21208)**  `arXiv:2611.21208`  `cs.CL`  
  _Daniel Baumartz, Alexander Mehler, Mevlüt Bagci, Maxim Konca_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated essay scorers (AES) based on large language models (LLMs) promise human-level accuracy. However, training, fine-tuning, and evaluating LLMs for specific assessment tasks is costly. In particular, the question arises whether these AES should be trained for specific domains or whether models based on generic LLMs might be sufficient. Human annotations, by contrast, are often more valid but far more expensive.
