This study evaluates whether ensembles of AES systems based on general-purpose LLMs can approximate human ratings of medical-education writing tasks by leveraging encoded linguistic and conceptual knowledge. The goal is to assess whether general-purpose models are applicable for assisting or potentially replacing human annotation in this domain. We experiment with 11 AES systems and 8 different LLMs and evaluate 668 student responses to 12 tasks, using \numprint{2076} evaluations by 7 human annotators. These ratings are aggregated to create consistent performance levels that can be used as target classes for classifiers trained on AES outputs. Our results show that learner classes based on human ratings can be accurately reconstructed using a linear SVM trained on a small set of AES features. A single classifier generalized well across tasks and domains, with reasoning and content features being most predictive. This suggests that out-of-the-box LLMs, even without domain-specific fine-tuning, can support automatic essay grading in medical education. While individual AES systems are insufficient alone, their combination approximates human evaluators, pointing to a reduced need for manual scoring or its supplementation with automated methods.
  </details>

- **[MemDoctor: Stage-wise Error Attribution and Repair for MLLM Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21019)**  `arXiv:2611.21019`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ruiyang Jiang, Xinyang Liao, Weikai Huang, Yuqi Niu, Junjie Zhou, Yubin Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) now assist users across sessions of images and text, carried by an external memory of what was said and shown. When such an assistant answers wrongly, the failure usually began earlier, in how a record was written, updated or retrieved, and the answer alone does not say where. We formalize multimodal memory as four stages, Write, Update, Retrieve and Answer, and define nine error types across them. A stage-resolved analysis on Mem-Gallery traces most failures to a source stage, and most of those lie before the answer. Injecting known errors against fact-preserving control edits, we then follow each tainted record downstream. Early errors survive to the answer as apparent evidence, and hurt most when no other record holds the fact. A diagnosis that trusts the quoted evidence therefore blames the stage where the error surfaces, not the one where it began. We propose MemDoctor, in which the MLLM that built the memory diagnoses its own failures. It traces each needed fact backward through the four stages, accepts a diagnosis only when the cited evidence is found in memory, and repairs that stage before answering again. On a 4B backbone, MemDoctor improves accuracy on both Mem-Gallery and MemLens, outperforming all evaluated 8B baselines, and the same loop also improves MuRAG and A-Mem.
  </details>

- **[It Depends on the Task: Rethinking Rhetorical Segmentation of Dutch Judicial Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20868)**  `arXiv:2611.20868`  `cs.CL`  
  _Samyak Sheth, Gerasimos Spanakis, Gijs van Dijck_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Classifying the material facts, submissions, judicial reasoning, and the court's decision (rhetorical roles) in case law is a prerequisite for high-quality performance across virtually all NLP-in-law tasks, including legal information retrieval, summarisation, and LLM-based analysis. These rhetorical roles often intertwine and shift sentence by sentence, making reliable classification difficult. This paper introduces the first sentence-level annotated dataset of Dutch judicial decisions, labelled across four legally meaningful categories: material facts, submissions, judicial reasoning, and the court's decision, alongside non-annotated (None) content, and compares several classification strategies, including a staged pipeline that separates None detection from the rhetorical roles, and a Bayesian update that leverages information about the section-header a sentence belongs to. Our results show that no single strategy dominates on both precision and recall: header priors help most when the None sentences are excluded and offer little benefit for None detection itself. These trade-offs matter in practice. For instance, extractive summarisation depends more on recall so it does not miss relevant facts, whereas outcome prediction depends more on precision regarding decision and facts sentences so that the predicted ruling is not contaminated by noise. We use these trade-offs and map the approaches tested in this study onto a set of representative legal NLP use cases.
  </details>

- **[Instant Weight-Space Consolidation for Long-Term Memory in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20759)**  `arXiv:2611.20759`  `cs.CL`  
  _Manan Suri, Dinesh Manocha_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic memory systems give LLMs persistent memory by extracting experience into text and retrieving it at query time, but the model itself does not evolve: however many interactions it has had, it starts each query the same. Extraction errors compound, implicit cross-session patterns that no single fragment states cannot be surfaced, and the construction cost grows with every conversation. Fine-tuning on past interactions does change the weights, but requires backpropagation at serving time and degrades on long histories. We introduce CLAM (Carried Latent Adapter Memory), which combines the strengths of both: a recurrent hypernetwork performs parametric consolidation, compiling a stream of session activations into a low-rank adapter in a single forward pass with no gradient steps and no language-model generation calls, while a complementary episodic buffer supplies verbatim detail at inference. A gated latent state carries knowledge across sessions in fixed size, so the parametric memory accumulates without growing. On LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S across three open models (2-7B parameters), CLAM leads all agentic baselines by 1-45 points and all gradient-based parametric methods by 10-45 points, while requiring zero generative LLM calls, zero gradient steps, and 3-151x less wall-clock time than extraction systems to build its memory. The two channels are complementary: the consolidated adapter adds 12-15 accuracy points when the episodic buffer misses the gold evidence, and still adds 4-5 points even with an oracle buffer.
  </details>

- **[GPRM: Global Perspective Process Reward Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20755)**  `arXiv:2611.20755`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zifan Zhang, Zhengbo Jiao, Shaobo Wang, Jing Zhou, Guoyi Li, Jiayi Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Process Reward Models (PRMs) are widely used for step-level evaluation of multi-step reasoning in large language models, but existing methods are often limited by weak historical dependency modeling and insufficient global awareness. They typically fail to leverage prior step evaluations for coherent judgment and lack visibility into subsequent reasoning, leading to suboptimal assessment of intermediate steps. To address this issue, we propose the Global Perspective Process Reward Model (GPRM), which jointly models the problem, previous reasoning steps and their evaluations, the current step, and future steps to enable history-aware and globally informed process evaluation. We further introduce a structured evaluation framework along four dimensions---look back, look ahead, self-check, and goal alignment---to assess prior consistency, future plausibility, step validity, and task alignment. Based on this framework, we construct datasets for supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (GRPO). Experiments show that, despite using less training data and smaller model sizes, GPRM outperforms strong baseline models by 7.8 and 5.4 points on PRMBench and ProcessBench, respectively. GPRM also generalizes well across tasks, surpassing the GPT-4.1 baseline on Agent Error Bench, improving strong baselines by around 4 points under test-time scaling (TTS), and achieving competitive performance on PPE.
  </details>

- **[Can LLMs Give Good Product Advice? A Framework for Diagnosing Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20734)**  `arXiv:2611.20734`  `cs.CL`  
  _Assaf Hallak, Yftah Ziser, Gal Chechik_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating product advice from a language model requires assessing whether the recommendation meets the user's needs and identifying where the model falls short when it does not. However, matching a product mentioned in an online reply cannot answer either question on its own: several products may be suitable, and the recorded answer may itself be poor advice. When shown a community-listed alternative alongside their own sampled car recommendation, six models prefer the alternative in 1.9 to 6.5 percent of eligible disagreements, consistently across both source-blind presentation orders. We propose a diagnostic framework that separates the user's stated needs, the model's added assumptions, candidate generation, filtering, and final choice, with 19 possible failure modes and the evidence needed to assess them. We apply this framework to product-advice data and to a separate product-selection task with externally assigned relevance labels. The experiments distinguish naming a community-listed product from selecting it and show how answer extraction and candidate-list construction affect the outcome.
  </details>

- **[ANCHOR: Cluster-Grounded 5W1H Extraction via Large-Small Model Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20728)**  `arXiv:2611.20728`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hongyu Jiang, Chenang Cai, Lei Gao, Zhao Kang, Ling Tian, Jinchuan Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep semantic understanding of events requires recognizing related events and distinguishing why an event occurs from how it is carried out. 5W1H extraction provides a structured foundation for such understanding by organizing event information around six fundamental questions. However, single-event slot filling provides limited support for these distinctions. We propose ANCHOR, a cluster-grounded framework that combines a large language model (LLM) with a small language model equipped with discriminative heads for accurate 5W1H extraction. The LLM proposes candidate events and generates their initial 4W records, which guide the selection of an event cluster and the subsequent anchoring of its main event. A shared document encoder provides evidence for joint 4W refinement, and two separate sets of boundary and role-scoring heads then extract why and how under event-specific constraints, respectively. Document representations are reused across events and stages, and unsupported roles can remain empty. Following the same principles, an agent-and-skill annotation workflow constructs a benchmark of 26,110 documents from ten source corpora covering three languages and seven domains. We select nine of the resulting datasets to evaluate ANCHOR against strong baselines using four open-weight LLMs. ANCHOR achieves competitive performance. The datasets and code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[LEAF-OPD: Learning to Forget from Privileged Contexts via On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20674)**  `arXiv:2611.20674`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zirui Pang, Zhijie Deng, Yuancheng Wang, Qihan Yu, Xin Li, Jiaheng Wei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM unlearning aims to erase targeted knowledge from large language models while preserving their utility. However, many existing methods suppress target knowledge without explicitly teaching the model how to respond to queries about it. We propose LEAF-OPD (LEArning to Forget from Privileged Contexts via On-Policy Distillation), in which a frozen teacher provides token-level supervision on student-generated responses. On forget data, the teacher uses privileged contexts (instructions not to reveal knowledge about forget targets) to guide forgetting. On retain data, the teacher provides supervision without privileged context to preserve model utility. The student operates without privileged context in both cases, learning how to respond when encountering knowledge that should be forgotten. A key challenge is that the teacher often provides effective supervision only at the first few tokens of student trajectories. We introduce Supervision Signal (SS) to measure corrective supervision over a specified token interval, evaluating the entire trajectory (SS1) and its second half (SS0.5) to assess overall strength and late-stage persistence. When supervision is weak, lightweight SFT strengthens the teacher's context-conditioned refusal behavior, substantially increasing both scores. We evaluate LEAF-OPD on three widely used benchmarks: TOFU, RWKU, and MUSE-News. On TOFU, LEAF-OPD achieves the best Forget Quality, Model Utility, and Forget ROUGE-L while maintaining competitive retain performance. On RWKU, it outperforms the second-best method by 34% in Forget Quality while maintaining competitive Retain Quality. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of privileged-context supervision with on-policy token-level distillation for LLM unlearning.
  </details>

- **[Invariant-Aware Harness Enforcement to Protect LLM Agents from Silent API Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20595)**  `arXiv:2611.20595`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sajib Acharjee Dip, Brian Ouyang, Lala Motlhabi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents answer questions by calling APIs and trusting the results. An API can fail silently. It ignores part of a request, such as a misnamed filter, yet returns HTTP 200 and a plausible but wrong result. Current fixes, such as checklists, reviewer models and LLM judges, ask the model to catch it. But models often see the problem and answer anyway. We present SilentGuard, which moves the decision from the model to the harness running tool calls. It tests each result against invariants that any correct API must satisfy and withholds values that break one. We find silent failures in 11 of 15 public research APIs, with counts inflated up to 691 times. Current fixes leave over 70% of answers wrong, and agents report 77.4% of counts a judge flags. Across 7 open models, SilentGuard cuts wrong answers on its target faults from 74.0% to 1.7%, with no loss on fault-free tasks. On three frontier models they fall from 70.2% to 0.5%. A warning from the same check leaves 70.1% wrong, so enforcement is the key. On new fault types, the harness alone cuts wrong answers from 61.1% to 24.5%. It avoids wrong answers by abstaining rather than recovering.
  </details>

- **[Abstention Direction in Large Language Models: Mechanistic Interpretability of Abstention Across Query Ambiguity, Knowledge Limits, and Safety Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20441)**  `arXiv:2611.20441`  `cs.CL`  
  _Koki Itai, Jun Hirako, Hiroaki Yamagiwa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Abstention, the withholding of a response by a large language model (LLM) under uncertainty or safety risks, is essential for trustworthy models. The causes of abstention fall into three perspectives: ambiguity in the Query (Q), limits of Model Knowledge (MK), and misalignment with Human Values (HV). However, prior analyses of internal representations have focused on HV, using a refusal direction computed from harmful and harmless queries. No study has analyzed the three perspectives jointly. We therefore extend the refusal direction framework to all three perspectives. Specifically, for each perspective, we construct counterfactual pairs that share a template and differ only in the abstention-inducing factor, and compute an abstention direction by normalizing the difference in mean activations between the two polarities. Activation steering experiments show that intervening in middle layers increases or decreases abstention across all three perspectives. Moreover, steering yields cross-perspective effects, indicating that the abstention directions share a common directional component.
  </details>

- **[Augmenting Pretrained Language Models with N-gram Embeddings through Incremental Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20415)**  `arXiv:2611.20415`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shaohua Hu, Xusheng Gu, Haofei Yang, Minghao Shao, Nanhui Zhao, Caoyun Fan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As a new scaling technique for large language models, the n-gram embedding improves the base ability of a model while adding almost no inference cost. Mainstream research realizes it through joint training of the n-gram embedding with the backbone, which severely restricts the settings in which an n-gram embedding can be used. We therefore propose training an n-gram embedding onto an existing model through incremental training, to extend where the technique can deliver value. Specifically, we explore several settings of the n-gram embedding under incremental training---the learning rate of the embedding, the vocabulary size, and the injection position---and train an n-gram embedding onto the existing model under the best configuration. Experiments show that the best configuration under incremental training does not fully coincide with that under pretraining; moreover, the incrementally trained knowledge is written predominantly into the n-gram embedding, while the general ability of the backbone is retained throughout this training process.
  </details>

- **[Do We Still Need Fine-Tuned Classifiers? Swapping Typed Decision Models into 24 ACL-Venue Papers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20191)**  `arXiv:2611.20191`  `cs.CL`  
  _Marek Šuppa, Jaroslav Kopčan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Typed Decision Models such as TypeSafe's Jev, also called System One or System 1 models, answer typed questions about an input with a probability for each option, and they need no training. Many NLP papers still fine-tune a task-specific classifier, and prompted large language models (LLMs) trail such classifiers on most text classification benchmarks. We ask how many of these fine-tuned classifiers a Typed Decision Model can replace under each paper's own evaluation, and what is needed where it cannot. We swap the predictors of 24 ACL-venue papers for Jev, keep each paper's splits, label definitions and metric, and check the results with Cloudflare's Clef, Microsoft-Decision-1 and OpenAI's GPT-6 Luna Decisions. The resulting 46 English test sets, with their questions and published scores, form the TDM-Gauntlet benchmark. With a zero-shot prompt, Jev directly replaced the paper's predictor on 12 of the 46 test sets, mostly single judgments with plain labels, but rarely where the paper's method was trained on its labels. Where Jev scored lower, we most often attributed the shortfall to an annotation convention that the label name does not state. With 500 training labels plus validation and no user-managed GPU, calibration and GEPA-style prompt optimization closed a median 72% of the gap to the paper's method, about twice what off-the-shelf GEPA closed, for a median $1.66 per search. On the same labels, fine-tuned encoders such as RoBERTa and DeBERTa closed 59%, and LoRA fine-tuning of the open LLM Qwen3.8-27B closed 97%. Fine-tuning remains the most accurate option, but a zero-shot prompt is often enough for plain labels, and prompt optimization closes most of the remaining gap.
  </details>

- **[Mandarin Wh-Indefinites Follow Nonveridicality in Multilingual LLMs, Unlike English any](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20062)**  `arXiv:2611.20062`  `cs.CL`  
  _Guofang Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multilingual LLMs learn mostly from English. Do they carry English polarity licensing over to other languages? English any is licensed by downward-entailing (DE) contexts, Mandarin wh-indefinites such as shenme 'what/something' by nonveridical ones, and the two classes come apart: perhaps licenses shenme but not any, while only licenses any but entails existence. We test this dissociation in a pre-registered study of twelve open LLMs (0.5B-9B, four families), using 48 matched bilingual frames in 21 contexts and a log-probability difference-in-differences normalised by negation. All 11 models that pass the measurement gate license Mandarin wh-indefinites under possibility, necessity and evidential adverbs, at 0.42-2.33 times the strength of negation, while English any gets nothing in the same contexts. An exploratory extension to Qwen2.5-32B shows the same. The English side is weaker: any under only, first and regret is licensed but at a fraction of negation, so only 3 models show the full double dissociation in sentence probabilities. When prompted to choose between sentences, most gated models show it. In exploratory directional ablations, a direction that carries English licensing, held-out classes included, does not reach Mandarin licensing in most models. The models do not simply apply the English rule to Mandarin.
  </details>

- **[A Dataset for Misogyny Detection in Polish Social Media: Transferability of Hateful Speech Detection from General to Specific Types of Hate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20044)**  `arXiv:2611.20044`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ewelina Gajewska_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Harmful language detection is commonly evaluated on broadly defined hate speech and toxicity benchmarks, yet it remains unclear whether models that successfully detect harmful language in general can transfer this capability to more specific forms of hate. We investigate this problem for misogyny detection in Polish, an area for which dedicated resources remain scarce. We introduce a new dataset of Polish social media comments annotated for misogyny, gender-directed hateful speech. Using this resource, we first assess the reliability of LLM-assisted annotation and then evaluate general-to-specific transfer by comparing models trained on general harmful language data, safety-specialized models, and models fine-tuned directly for misogyny detection. Our results show that strong performance on general harmful language detection does not transfer uniformly to misogyny; safety-specialized models demonstrate substantially stronger generalization ability. The study contributes a new Polish resource for gender-directed harmful language and demonstrates that the scope and granularity of prior safety training are important factors in transferring harmful language detection from general to specific forms of hate.
  </details>

- **[Text Speeding Itself Up: Recency and Restraint in Session-Local Drafting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19455)**  `arXiv:2611.19455`  `cs.CL`  
  _Antal van den Bosch_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding lets a cheap draft model propose tokens that a large language model verifies in a single forward pass. When the verifier keeps a KV cache, verification is cheap and drafting must be nearly free. Session-local drafters, which predict from the prompt and the text generated so far, meet that bar without training. We compare a recency-weighted, backing-off, confidence-gated session memory against prompt lookup decoding in its reference form, which copies from the earliest earlier match, and in a variant that copies from the most recent one, with paired per-prompt bootstrap intervals throughout. On general, medical, and legal text with a Mistral-7B verifier, recency accounts for the speed: most-recent lookup gains up to 0.09 in speedup over earliest-match lookup on 500-token generations, and session memory's weighting and backoff add nothing measurable on top, at 30 to 40 times the drafting cost. Energy tells a different story. On medical and legal text, unselective drafters are 14% faster than plain decoding yet use as much energy per token, while drafters that abstain on weak evidence save 3 to 4 joules per 100 tokens; a three-token minimum match length on prompt lookup matches the confidence-gated session memory. Outputs are token-identical to greedy decoding in 94% of generations, with every divergence at a numerical near-tie. Under a repetition penalty, long-generation speedups shrink from 1.4-1.7x to 1.1-1.2x, but recency still helps and the length floor is the only drafter that saves energy in both domains.
  </details>

- **[Personalized Language Models for One-to-Many Private Communications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19155)**  `arXiv:2611.19155`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zihan Hong, Xue Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study relationship-specific continual reply prediction for one-to-many private communication: a system predicts an author's reply to a recurring contact and updates its personalization state when the actual reply becomes available. We propose TRACE (Temporal Relationship-Aware Communication Evolution), which uses author-level lexical habits, contact-specific wording, and temporal expression estimates to guide a frozen language model, with a feedback-driven policy controlling revision. On 216 test emails from eight Enron users, all four main TRACE configurations achieve higher mean BLEU, ROUGE-L, and BERTScore and lower mean Reply Style MSE than Base LLM and Base RAG. Component ablations show mixed effects and do not establish statistically reliable improvements in Reply Style MSE after multiple-comparison correction. More accurate state forecasts and larger revision budgets do not consistently improve reply-level metrics. These exploratory findings expose the challenge of turning evolving relationship-specific expression estimates into useful generation guidance.
  </details>

- **[PREFACE: Separating Shared Corrections from Prefix Conditioning in PEFT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18987)**  `arXiv:2611.18987`  `cs.CL`  
  _Novanto Yudistira, One Octadion_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Does input-conditioned parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) improve because it uses the current input, or because it adds a useful learned correction? We investigate this distinction with PREFACE, a bounded channel controller for PEFT-adapted projections that requires no test-time gradients. The controller reads observed context; in multiple-choice likelihood scoring, its state is independent of the candidate continuation. Our central study freezes IA3, tuned LoRA, and DoRA adapters and matches controller architecture, initialization, batches, and updates across current-prefix and learned-global controls. Across ARC-Challenge and SciQ, current-prefix control improves all six mean accuracies over frozen adapters, but none of its paired contrasts with learned-global control excludes zero. Replacing its corrections with their training-prefix mean changes accuracy by at most 0.34 percentage points; input-varying corrections contribute only 1.8-2.8% of correction RMS. On ARC test, frozen-adapter current/global controls score 25.68%/25.77%, distinct from the earlier jointly trained 23.1% to 26.4% gain over IA3. We derive and evaluate static compilation of fixed corrections, separating deployment simplification from statistical equivalence. Broader GPT-2, Pythia, and Qwen experiments show family-, task-, and scoring-dependent gains, with modest recent-model effects. The study provides an intervention-based evaluation of conditional PEFT: improvements over a static adapter do not by themselves demonstrate a benefit from input matching.
  </details>

- **[Mapping the Afterlife of Future Work Statements in NLP Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18847)**  `arXiv:2611.18847`  `cs.CL`  
  _Claudiu Creanga, Ioana-Roxana Boriceanu, Ana Sabina Uban, Liviu P Dinu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Future work statements are one of the few places in scientific papers where authors explicitly describe what they think should happen next. We study what happens to such statements after publication. We construct a dataset of 72,070 future work items extracted from 23,113 ACL Anthology papers published between 1979 and 2025, categorize them by type, retrieve candidate follow-up papers through citation-based and content-based search, and use LLM-based judgments to assess whether later papers pursue the proposed directions. Using this resource, we analyze patterns across time, venues, categories, themes, and authors. We find that many items are later addressed, often within a few years, with clear differences across types and themes and a largely community-driven pattern. Our results suggest that future work statements provide useful evidence for studying how research directions develop in NLP. To support further research, we release our dataset at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Binary Audits: Does Guideline Grounding Mitigate Decision-Space Framing in LLM Opioid Prescribing?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18631)**  `arXiv:2611.18631`  `cs.CL`  
  _Soumyadeep Roy, Keyi Yu, Tina Hernandez-Boussard_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Audits of LLMs for bias in opioid prescribing have largely relied on binary treatment decisions, a signal that current models have saturated. We introduce Q-Pain++, a full-factorial stress test that holds clinical content fixed while independently varying clinical risk factors and the granularity of the available dosage options. Adding an intermediate dosage option to otherwise unchanged vignettes substantially lowers agreement with expert-annotated dosages for most models, while demographic differences in dosage decisions remain small in all but one configuration once clinical risk factors are added. Grounding models in perioperative guidelines through retrieval reduces this framing effect on average but does not eliminate it, and one model's treatment decisions change when the retrieved passages are reordered. These results suggest that audits should vary the answer format, not only patient identity. We will release our codebase as well as Guideline-RAG-associated resources on GitHub (this https URL).
  </details>

- **[CondRelBench: A Mixed-Domain Benchmark for Condition-Scoped Relation Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18620)**  `arXiv:2611.18620`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sydney Anuyah, Bofu Dong, Sudhasmita Swain, Sunandan Chakraborty_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Relation extraction systems often identify what a relation is, but not the condition under which it holds. This can collapse condition-bound claims into unsupported generalizations, a failure we call relation overgeneralization. We introduce CondRelBench, a mixed-domain benchmark for evaluating condition-scoped relation understanding. Each claim is represented as (E1, R, E2 | C), where C is the condition licensing the relation. Using 3,000 synthetic paragraphs, 200 PubMed full-text papers, and 695 Reddit posts, we construct five probe variants per tuple that preserve, replace, remove, or contradict the condition, or contradict the relation itself. Models classify each probe as Supported, Not Supported, or Not Enough Evidence. We also introduce Overgeneralization Rate (OGR) and Scope Preservation Score (SPS) to measure false support for altered claims and consistency across probe families. Across prompted LLMs, fine-tuned LLMs, and encoder models, we find that Accuracy and Macro F1 often hide scope failures. Models frequently support the original scoped claim but fail when its condition is removed or altered. Domain-specific encoders achieve the strongest scope preservation, while instruction-tuned LLMs often show higher overgeneralization. Synthetic supervision helps but does not close the gap to real-world PubMed and Reddit performance.
  </details>

- **[Self-Evolving Dual-Role Deliberation for Medication Recommendation from Clinical Records](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18615)**  `arXiv:2611.18615`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiyue Jiang, Yu Lei, Yitao Xu, Zikang Wang, Zhenyu Wang, Licheng Zong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recommending medications from electronic health records (EHRs) is a safety-critical task that demands therapeutic coverage, explicit safety constraints, traceable justification, and continual adaptivity. Supervised neural models freeze at deployment and cannot reason about new phenotypes, while existing LLM pipelines are single-pass and lack a cross-case improvement loop. We propose SEMPA, a training-free agentic framework that couples a Proposer with a Critic in multi-round adversarial deliberation, grounds candidates with molecular-aware retrieval, contrastively evolves both agents' natural-language policies under a semantic-anchor constraint, and enforces rule-based DDI and contraindication checks. On 100 MIMIC-IV encounters, SEMPA reaches 0.3116 Jaccard, 0.7295 F1, and a 0.0172 DDI rate, improving F1 by 57.7% and cutting DDI by 77.5% over the strongest supervised baseline. Two LLM judges rank SEMPA highest, and six-backbone studies confirm that gains derive from the framework rather than a specific model.
  </details>

- **[Bridging Semantics and Utilities: A Controllable and Scalable Market Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18613)**  `arXiv:2611.18613`  `cs.CL`  
  _Dejun Cao, Boyang Wang, Jiayuan Wei, Tingxi Zhang, Chengyuan Zhang, Ruixuan Yan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models(LLMs) have recently been used to construct generative agents for financial market simulation. Existing approaches typically prompt each agent separately with public market information and a private investor profile, and ask the model to directly generate trading actions. In this agent-wise design, the model repeatedly interprets information shared across the population, while individual behavior is controlled through textual descriptions of investor preferences and traits. This creates two structural difficulties: continuous traits such as risk sensitivity do not reliably induce ordered behavioral changes, and repeatedly processing the same public information causes inference costs to grow with the number of agents. We propose \textbf{SUM}, a Semantic-Utility Model that separates cohort-level semantic perception from agent-level utility-based decision-making. A language model interprets public market information for each investor type and produces semantic signals shared within the corresponding cohort. Individual agents then combine these signals with their private states, preferences, and constraints to generate orders through utility-based decisions. Experiments in an A-share limit-order-book market show that \textbf{SUM} provides more stable and monotonic behavioral control than prompt-based agents while substantially reducing repeated language-model inference as the population grows. Closed-loop simulations further reproduce directional market responses, volatility persistence, differentiated investment styles, and persistent wealth heterogeneity. These results establish \textbf{SUM} as a controllable, scalable, and semantically informed framework for financial market simulation.
  </details>

- **[NYUAD at ImageEval Shared Tasks: A Multimodal LLM for Audio-Visual Question Answering and Hallucination Detection in English and Modern Standard Arabic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18610)**  `arXiv:2611.18610`  `cs.CL`  
  _Nouar AlDahoul, Yasir Zaki_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The increasing adoption of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) has enabled more natural interaction with visual and spoken information, creating new opportunities for intelligent audio-visual question answering (AVQA) and hallucination detection systems. AVQA extends traditional visual question answering by requiring models to jointly understand an input image and a spoken question to generate accurate, context-aware responses. Despite recent advances, AVQA and hallucination detection tasks remain challenging for modern standard Arabic (MSA) due to the limited availability of multimodal datasets and the need to reason across Arabic speech and text modalities alongside visual inputs. In this paper, we present an MLLM-based framework for AVQA that takes an image and a spoken question as input and generates answers. In addition, we utilized this framework to detect hallucination in text-image pairs. The proposed approach is evaluated on the ImageEval 2026 Task (Ayn-VQA) benchmark, which focuses on culturally grounded Arabic AVQA. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed framework effectively understands spoken queries, grounds their responses in the visual content, and produces accurate answers. The results underscore the capacity and limitations of different LLM architectures for both English and MSA and under various conditions.
  </details>

- **[Many Paths to the Same Mistake: Generating Diverse Reasoning Paths Behind the Same Student Mistake](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18609)**  `arXiv:2611.18609`  `cs.CL`  
  _Fareya Ikram, Mengxuan Hu, Parker Glenn, Zhiqi Huang, Alfy Samuel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Realistic evaluation of AI systems requires user simulators that capture not only cooperative behavior, but also the errors and variability that arise in real interactions. In educational technology, simulating step-by-step flawed student reasoning is essential, yet datasets mapping these pathways are scarce, and Large Language Models (LLMs) often auto-correct rather than sustain simulated mistakes. To address this, we present a generate-and-filter pipeline that produces diverse and realistic student error traces. Our approach utilizes forward consistency, a metric verifying if a flawed reasoning trace can be used to recover the student's actual incorrect answer, paired with an LLM-as-a-judge to ensure realism. Our evaluation suggests that while many LLMs struggle to maintain incorrect reasoning mid-trace, smaller supervised fine-tuned models successfully capture these divergent paths. This work provides a framework for high-fidelity error trace generation, advancing realistic, non-cooperative user simulation.
  </details>

- **[NYUAD at ArGuard Shared Tasks: Multimodal Embedding Models for Detecting Arabic Hateful Memes and Unsafe Prompts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18608)**  `arXiv:2611.18608`  `cs.CL`  
  _Nouar AlDahoul, Yasir Zaki_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid growth of social media and generative AI has significantly increased the volume of Arabic textual content, memes, and user prompts shared across online platforms. While these media facilitate communication, creativity, and information exchange, they can also be exploited to disseminate hate speech, harmful content, and unsafe instructions. As a result, there is an increasing need for reliable automated methods to identify hateful Arabic memes and harmful (unsafe) prompts. This paper leverages pre-trained and fine-tuned multilingual embedding models to learn rich semantic representations from textual and visual content. The evaluation is conducted using a dataset of Arabic memes and prompts proposed in the ArabicNLP ArGuard 2026 challenge. The results underscore the capacity of large language model (LLM)-based embedding models to deliver superior performance. Developing robust detection systems is essential for improving content moderation and fostering a more secure and trustworthy online environment for Arabic-speaking users.
  </details>

- **[Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Clinical Decision Support in Gait Analysis: A Proof-of-Concept](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18603)**  `arXiv:2611.18603`  `cs.CL`  
  _Georgios Giarmatzis, Fabian Horst, Djordje Slijepcevic, Nikolaos Aggeloussis, George Papakostas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) demonstrate significant potential for clinical decision support but lack specialized biomechanical knowledge required for accurate gait analysis interpretation. We developed and evaluated a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) framework to enhance LLM-generated clinical explanations for ankle fracture rehabilitation using ground reaction force data. Using the GaitRec dataset (503 subjects: 211 healthy, 292 ankle fracture patients), we implemented a five-stage pipeline integrating: (1) machine learning classification (91.3\% accuracy); (2) Layer-wise Relevance Propagation for explainable feature identification; (3) RAG knowledge base from peer-reviewed biomechanics literature; (4) GPT-5 explanation generation with and without retrieval augmentation; and (5) double-blind evaluation by experienced physical therapists across five clinical criteria. RAG augmentation produced statistically significant improvements in Clini-cal Benefit (mean difference=0.60, p=0.047, {\delta}=0.49) and Completeness (mean difference=0.70, p=0.049, {\delta}=0.49), both demonstrating large effect sizes. Baseline GPT-5 achieved strong performance across all criteria (\textgreater4.0/5.0). This proof-of-concept demonstrates that augmenting general-purpose LLMs with domain-specific knowledge meaningfully enhances actionable, evidence-based clinical guidance---dimensions most critical for rehabilitation decision-making. The framework provides a scalable approach for integrating AI into specialized clinical workflows without costly model retraining.
  </details>

- **[Jailbreak LLMs with Linguistic Style as a Hidden Attack Surface](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18601)**  `arXiv:2611.18601`  `cs.CL`  
  _Priyanshu Mohanty, Sarthakbrata Halder, Srikant Panda, Afshin Oroojlooy, Murari Mandal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We show that linguistic style is a systematic and previously overlooked attack surface for LLM safety. Across a diverse taxonomy of styles and multiple state-of-the-art models, stylistic rewrites of identical harmful goals produce order-of-magnitude differences in Attack Success Rate (ASR), with some styles achieving up to 80\% ASR while others remain below 6\%. To exploit this vulnerability, we introduce a lightweight single-pass attack framework that combines a style-conditioned generator with a BERT-based style selector, achieving performance competitive with substantially more expensive multi-turn optimization methods. To understand why style affects safety behavior, we conduct mechanistic analysis on LLaMA-3.1-8B and find that stylistic rewrites disrupt refusal representations while preserving recoverable harmful intent, suggesting a distributed failure mode rather than a single refusal mechanism. Motivated by this finding, we develop a style-aware Direct Preference Optimization defense that reduces ASR from 86\% to 19.5\% while largely preserving utility. Together, our results establish linguistic style as a critical factor in adversarial robustness and provide both mechanistic insight and a practical defense.
  </details>

- **[ScopeBench: Evaluating Cultural Norm Scope Awareness in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18600)**  `arXiv:2611.18600`  `cs.CL`  
  _Haojun Liang, Ziwei Zhao, Chenyu Li, Qiyue Zhao, Rongduomijia Mu, Yulan Shen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safety evaluation in large language models treats harm as culturally invariant, yet a model cannot correctly apply a safety norm without knowing whose norms bind. We introduce ScopeBench, a benchmark that treats cultural scope as a first-class safety dimension. It comprises 3,323 norms spanning 49 countries across nine cultural clusters, each annotated as Universal or Specific. We define three scope error types identically across four evaluation tasks: Scope Inflation, Scope Deflation, and Scope Displacement. Evaluating 21 frontier LLMs across seven model families, we surface three structural failures. First, the dominant error type inverts with prompt format: Scope Deflation reaches 70% in single-answer classification, while Scope Inflation reaches 33% when each candidate culture is judged independently. Second, scope-classification accuracy is empirically decoupled from behavioral safety: the six classifiers achieving at least 97% Task 1 accuracy still produce harmful responses to 40-42% of scope-violating statements. Third, no model is safe in interaction, with harmful-response rates ranging from 29% to 51% across the cohort, excluding one model that systematically refuses the task. We release all data, code, and model outputs to establish cultural scope as a distinct alignment axis.
  </details>

- **[Profiling Hallucinations in Frontier LLMs for Entity Linking to Medical Ontologies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18595)**  `arXiv:2611.18595`  `cs.CL`  
  _Logan Born, Nishant Kambhatla, Uliyana Kubasova, Maryam Siahbani, Andrei Vacariu, Tim O'Connell, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The integration of Large Language Models (LLMs) into healthcare promises to revolutionize clinical documentation and interoperability, yet reliability remains a concern. This study presents a comprehensive analysis of hallucinations by frontier LLMs tasked with mapping clinical text to SNOMED CT. Through rigorous experimentation, we identify a critical reliability gap: LLMs hallucinate medical codes at a rate that currently renders them unsuitable for autonomous clinical coding applications. Paradoxically, constraining models to use ground-truth mention spans exacerbates, rather than mitigates, these hallucinations. We further contribute a taxonomy of hallucination types--including deprecated codes and cross-ontology errors--and demonstrate that general-purpose LLMs significantly underperform compared to specialized zero-shot entity linking approaches. These findings underscore the need for robust verification mechanisms before clinical deployment.
  </details>

- **[Endogenous Preferences as Vulnerability Signals: Discovering Universal Adversarial Triggers for LLM Utility Degradation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18592)**  `arXiv:2611.18592`  `cs.CL`  
  _Qingyu Wu, Zeyu Feng, Yuzhe Luo, Hua Cheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deployed large language models must remain useful when untrusted text enters their prompt context. Adversarial prompting and red-teaming methods typically define the failures they seek through task labels, harmful targets, judge feedback, or downstream utility scores. We ask whether a frozen model's clean behavior can reveal a reusable context vulnerability when its task-level form is unknown, and downstream task feedback is withheld. To study this question, EndoUT uses a Siamese-style design that compares the same frozen victim on clean and span-modified versions of each unlabeled instruction. Without a task-level target, the clean pass supplies the victim's preferred response-onset token as an instruction-conditioned reference; Endogenous Preference Disruption (EPD) measures how insertion weakens that preference. An auxiliary generator supplies bounded-span proposals; EPD guides its update and ranks the pool without access to downstream information. The highest-scoring victim-specific span is fixed before evaluation and reused unchanged across all held-out inputs. Across four instruction-tuned models and nine benchmarks, fixed triggers reduce benign-task utility by 40.2 percentage points on average, with negative mean change on every benchmark and greater average degradation than every evaluated baseline under the reported protocols. Matched protocol ablations show stronger transfer for source-side selection followed by fixed reuse than for null-context fixed reuse or per-input generation. Transfer recurs across proposers with proposal-dependent strength; output-level analyses identify victim-specific associations between trigger families and downstream failure patterns. Thus, clean behavior can expose context vulnerabilities that transfer across inputs yet manifest through structured, victim-specific patterns of utility degradation. The code and data will be released upon acceptance.
  </details>

- **[Human vs Machine Translation Detection: A Cross-Model, Cross-Domain, and Low-Resource Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18586)**  `arXiv:2611.18586`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yabsera Yemanberhan, Prasenjit Mitra, Roald Eiselen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The growing reliance on web-crawled corpora for training large language models introduces a critical challenge: machine-translated (MT) content is often treated as human translation (HT), potentially degrading data quality and propagating synthetic artifacts, especially in low-resource settings. In this work, we study MT-HT detectability across high-resource (English, Spanish) and low-resource (Swahili, Afrikaans) languages using both decoder-only LLMs in zero-shot and few-shot settings, and fine-tuned multilingual encoders trained at the sentence level and on token-chunked inputs. We find that decoder-only models provide limited discrimination overall and exhibit a consistent class-asymmetric failure mode: they recognize human-written text reliably (high HT recall) but rarely flag fluent machine output (low MT recall). Encoder-based classifiers achieve high accuracy when evaluated on data generated by the same MT systems and translators used during training in a legislative domain, but their MT recall drops sharply on unseen translators and on unseen domains such as low-resource conversational text and medical content. To address this limitation, we evaluate models on longer inputs by chunking text into segments of up to 300 tokens, which substantially improves generalization, suggesting that sentence-level signals are insufficient for robust detection. Finally, we analyze the relationship between translation quality and detectability using COMET and BLEURT, showing that lower-quality MT is more easily identified, while high-quality MT remains difficult to distinguish from HT. Our results highlight the importance of input granularity, translator generalization, and translation quality in building robust MT detection systems across domains and languages.
  </details>

- **[Read, Route, Patch: Robust Test-Time Evolution for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18585)**  `arXiv:2611.18585`  `cs.CL`  
  _Kaijie Chen, Liang Yan, Yilan Wei, Jiachen Luo, Zhihao Dou, Jiashu Qu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM)-based agents can improve long- horizon interactive decision-making through test-time evolu- tion, yet existing methods face three information bottlenecks: memory retrieval overemphasizes textual similarity, candi- date harnesses are often behaviorally redundant, and whole- harness rewriting may overwrite useful evidence or introduce cross-component inconsistency. We introduce Trident, which improves experience utilization, behavioral exploration, and harness evolution through boundary-aware memory reading, diversity-aware candidate routing, and order-aware insertion- based updating, respectively. We evaluate Trident on five benchmarks spanning text games, web interaction, and cod- ing tasks. Across qwen-32B, mistral-small-3.2-24B, and gpt- oss-20B, Trident achieves the best average performance and consistently outperforms non-learning, memory-based, reflection-based, and automated prompt optimization base- lines. Compared with the strongest baseline, APEX, Trident yields relative average gains of 30.7%, 30.8%, and 38.3% on the three backbones, respectively. Ablation and sensitivity stud- ies further show that the three designs provide complementary gains and remain robust to parameter variations.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Truncation: When Can LLMs Safely Omit Generated Text?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08975)**  `arXiv:2611.08975`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mahmoud Alyosify, Mirna Imbabi, Eman Elkhamisi, Rahatara Ferdousi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Instruction-tuned large language models (LLMs) answer at greater length than a request needs. We ask whether a training-free controller inside the decoding loop can remove that surplus while preserving content, judged against the simplest possible baseline: truncating the model's own answer to the same length. An entropy-gated logits processor removes 25.5-34.9% of tokens on MT-Bench development questions and 19.9-34.2% on a sealed test split within pre-registered BERTScore non-inferiority margins, yet we prove and confirm by audit that its output is always a prefix of the unmodified answer: the saving is truncation. A survey of 23 training-free mechanism families finds none that selects content better than truncation without oracle labels. We therefore introduce Entailment-Licensed In-Decoding Elision (ELIDE), which, at each sentence boundary, withholds a span from the reader only when a natural-language inference model judges it entailed by the text already shown, while leaving the model's generation unchanged. ELIDE beats matched-length truncation on three model families, but reading 936 of its elisions on 670 public prompts shows that 59% harm the answer, a failure that no automatic measure detects. Scoping the licence to its discourse unit reduces the harmful share to about one elision in ten on two fresh tests.
  </details>

- **[CAMR: Compatibility-Aware Memory Replay for Continual Learning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36734)**  `arXiv:2610.36734`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jingpu Yang, Tianhao Li, Fengxian Ji, Yufeng Wang, Shuxin Zheng, Xiaoqing Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual learning requires large language models to acquire new knowledge and capabilities while retaining what they have learned. Fine-tuning and replay support acquisition and retention, but deciding whether incoming examples need supervision and which historical examples to revisit remains a challenge. We propose Compatibility-Aware Memory Replay (CAMR), an adaptive framework that jointly controls current-example supervision and historical replay. CAMR combines compatibility checks with a learned memory gate to guide an online policy over three actions: learning incoming examples with general replay, jointly reinforcing compatible related examples, and skipping direct supervision of verified mastered duplicates. The policy learns from acquisition, retention, and training-cost feedback under a bounded per-arrival replay budget. We evaluate CAMR with Qwen3-8B-Base on continual factual updating and cross-task and cross-domain learning. On WikiBigEdit, CAMR consistently leads evaluated methods in Original Match from 3K to 100K facts, reaching 93.66% after just 3K facts and surpassing Full-history Replay (LoRA) after 100K facts. It also achieves the highest final scores on all four selected TRACE tasks and maintains or improves earlier-task performance relative to post-acquisition levels in a sequence spanning financial question answering, mathematical reasoning, code generation, and summarization.
  </details>

- **[Style over Substance? Linguistic Bias in LLM-based Peer Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35254)**  `arXiv:2610.35254`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sai Suresh Macharla Vasu, Sumedh Santosh Joshi, Philipp Christmann, Ivaxi Sheth, Ruta Binkyte, Koel Dutta Chowdhury, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The number of paper submissions to major AI conferences has increased dramatically in recent years, substantially increasing the peer-review workload of researchers. At the same time, large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to support peer review, both by individual reviewers and by conference organizations. While LLM capabilities have improved considerably, their evaluations may be influenced by linguistic characteristics that are unrelated to scientific content. In this work, we introduce a controlled experimental framework for studying such effects by systematically perturbing the linguistic style of research papers. Specifically, we rewrite papers to exhibit (i) linguistic features associated with native or non-native English writing, and (ii) high- or low-confidence linguistic features. To construct these interventions, we derive fine-grained rewriting instructions grounded in the linguistic literature and apply them using LLMs. Crucially, our interventions are designed to preserve the underlying scientific content. We then evaluate the resulting papers using different LLM reviewers. Our experiments expose a critical vulnerability in LLM-based peer review: reviewers systematically reward confident rhetoric independent of scientific content, raising serious concerns about the robustness of their assessments.
  </details>

- **[Examining form-based linguistic productivity in people and large language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33433)**  `arXiv:2610.33433`  `cs.CL`  
  _Michael Wilson, Robert Frank_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Linguistic productivity, as evidenced by the use of words in novel contexts, is one of the most striking facts about human language. However, how abstract the knowledge that enables such productivity is remains unclear. We compare people's and computational language models' linguistic productivity in contexts where successful generalization depends on form-based information. We focus on extending the use of a novel verb learned in the active voice to passive contexts. We find people extend active to passive uses in a way consistent with abstract generalizations concerning linguistic form, even when no plausible meaning can be assigned to the novel verb. In contrast, large language models succeeded only when a meaning like an existing verb's could be assigned to the novel verb. We conclude that computational models' syntactic knowledge remains tied to the distribution and meaning of specific lexical items in a way that human syntactic knowledge is not.
  </details>

- **[Too Good to Be Real? Diagnosing and Reducing the Gap Between AI Preference and Real User Engagement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18282)**  `arXiv:2609.18282`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xinglang Zhang, Yuanmeng Xiang, Yunyao Zhang, Zeliang Chen, Junqing Yu, Zikai Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly used to generate and evaluate online content, yet it remains unclear whether the qualities they associate with higher engagement match what real users respond to. We study this question using 1.17 million answers to 25,978 questions from Zhihu, Quora, and Reddit, comparing real platform answers and AI-generated answers across four within-question engagement levels. We introduce Ontological Preference Measurement, which represents answers along three dimensions: logic, affect, and expression. We find a systematic gap between AI preference and real user engagement: as target engagement increases, LLMs add more explicit logical structure, while real user engagement is more strongly associated with affective and expressive salience. We call this tendency logic overbinding. Based on this diagnosis, we propose Ontology-Masked Reasoning Autoencoding (OMRA), a controlled intervention that masks and reconstructs over-explained spans while preserving stance, factual content, and coherence. Across four LLM families, OMRA reduces the measured gap by an average of 54.4%. In human evaluation, OMRA wins 62.4% of pairwise preference judgments against matched real platform answers, even though the real answers are more often judged to be human-written.
  </details>

- **[Vishing-Tactics-Bench: Forecasting Exploitation Trajectories in Voice Phishing Calls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07151)**  `arXiv:2609.07151`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jeongmin Lee, Dongmyung Sul, Seung Yun, Jinxia Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Voice phishing (vishing) unfolds in real time; by the time a call has ended and post-hoc classification is possible, the harm has already been done. The more actionable question is which concrete harm (Information Gathering or Financial Exploitation) an ongoing call is tactically progressing toward. We present Vishing-Tactics-Bench, a benchmark grounded in Endsley's situation-awareness (SA) framework that recasts vishing defense from after-the-fact fraud classification to harm projection: predicting at each turn whether the call will reach either terminal harm. We adapt MITRE ATT&CK to vishing as a 6-tactic taxonomy (Vishing-Tactics) and label 35,340 scammer utterances across 5,645 synthetic Chinese calls. We define Exploitation Trajectory Forecasting, a survival-style protocol over the two terminal harms with three metrics: AP@k, C-index, and divergence error. Baselines ranging from a Markov heuristic to fine-tuned LLMs show that the tactical trajectory serves as an interpretable representation of the call's tactical state, supporting harm-specific forecasting, which can then be used for the downstream application of intervention selection; a stratified lead-time analysis at a tight false-alarm budget further identifies at what point in a call the trajectory signal yields early warning.
  </details>

- **[IndicQE-APE: A Consolidated Benchmark for Quality Estimation and Automatic Post-Editing over Indic Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16344)**  `arXiv:2608.16344`  `cs.CL`  
  _Diptesh Kanojia, Archchana Sindhujan, Sourabh Deoghare, Daria Sokova, Shenbin Qian, Girish Koushik, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Indic quality estimation (QE) and automatic post-editing (APE) data is spread across separate releases, so no single resource supports training and evaluation across tasks and language pairs on one footing. We consolidate the WMT 2020-2024 shared-task lineage with an extended English-Malayalam resource into IndicQE-APE: $126{,}754$ instances over nine directional pairs, with up to four label types aligned on the same segment, a direct assessment, a human post-edit, word-level tags and an error explanation, and a test set stratified over four difficulty axes. We benchmark six prompted LLMs and three COMET metrics on segment-level QE, and three systems on APE. Two of the axes are defined partly on direct assessment and select a compressed slice of it. Segments whose segment-level and token-level signals disagree are ranked below equally scored segments of the same language. Four-shot prompting costs every model at or below $3.4$B both correlation and output-format compliance. Unedited MT beats every APE system we run on three of the four pairs. The benchmarkthis https URLand codethis https URLare released.
  </details>

- **[Prompt Privilege in Large Language Models: Measuring and Mitigating Barriers to Equitable AI Access](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08942)**  `arXiv:2608.08942`  `cs.CL`  
  _Lier Jin, Lan Hu, Binqi Shen, Hanyu Cai, Yuting Xin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in healthcare, education, public services, and everyday decision making. They should provide comparable assistance regardless of users' literacy, communication style, or prompt-engineering expertise. However, research on prompt robustness primarily focuses on adversarial attacks, prompt injection, and prompt optimization, overlooking whether semantically equivalent requests receive different responses simply because they are phrased differently. We call this accessibility challenge "Prompt Privilege": users with greater prompting expertise obtain better model performance despite expressing the same underlying intent. To address this problem, we propose the Prompt Equity Score (PES), a metric for evaluating performance consistency across user populations, and the Prompt Equity Transformer (PET), an LLM-based agent that automatically transforms user requests into semantically equivalent prompts while preserving their intent. PET shifts prompt optimization from users to AI systems, serving as an accessibility layer between users and foundation models. Experiments on MedQA reveal measurable prompt privilege, with statistically significant performance disparities between low-literacy and expert-prompting cohorts. Applying PET reduces these differences, with no pairwise cohort differences reaching statistical significance in our evaluation. These results suggest that accessibility-oriented prompt normalization can improve equitable AI access. By introducing prompt privilege as a new dimension of AI accessibility and PET as a practical solution, this work advances system-centered accessibility and provides a foundation for fairer, more trustworthy, and more inclusive AI systems.
  </details>

- **[Characterizing Human-Likeness in AI Generated Poetry: A Zero-shot Classification Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26221)**  `arXiv:2607.26221`  `cs.CL`  
  _A. N. Biswas, T. Tabassum, A. A. Shohid, R. M. Mou, A. A. Esha, A. Ahmed, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  With the advancement of AI technologies, Generative AI (GenAI) and human written text have become nearly indistinguishable. Additionally, the global standardization of AI chatbots made academic malpractice more frequent. Furthermore, existing research indicates GenAI poems are the most difficult to distinguish even without any modification thus, GenAI poems are naturally deemed human-like by modern detectors. However, the objectivity of such dissertations needs to be verified against modern detection tools but the subjectivity of poetry and the black-box nature of the modern LLMs (Large Language Models) architectures made verification of such work quite complicated. Hence, the main objective of the research is to deduce the attributes of English poetry that contribute classification and misclassification of both human and AI poems and provide corroborating or contradicting evidence to the poetry distinguishability claim. For such characterizations, we propose a Zero-shot detection pipeline with a dataset consisting of both human and AI poems to verify the distinguishability of human and AI creation and extract the aforementioned crucial attributes for accurate classification. Extraction of such attributes provides benefits in two ways: firstly, it reduces the margin of training needed as only the poems based on misclassifying attributes need to be trained and fine tuned and finally provides a critical insight to the GenAI detection dilemma to strengthen the modern detection pipelines.
  </details>

- **[Population-Level Profiling of DSM-5 Depressive Symptom Language Among Twitter Users With Self-Reported ADHD or ASD: Exploratory Study Using Natural Language Processing and Statistical Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05626)**  `arXiv:2607.05626`  `cs.CL`  
  _Muhammad Rizwan, David Nabergoj, Jure Demšar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Depression frequently co-occurs with attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) and autism spectrum disorder (ASD), but how depressive symptoms are expressed by people with these conditions is underexplored. We examined whether Twitter users self-reporting ADHD or ASD differ in language related to the nine DSM-5 depressive symptoms. We analysed 1,282,437 tweets (2020-2021) from 792 users. Tweets were screened for depressive relevance by a zero-shot classifier combined with a locally run large language model (Qwen3-8B) and classified into the nine symptoms by MentalRoBERTa fine-tuned on the ReDSM5 corpus. The pipeline was tuned on two stratified samples of 500 tweets and internally validated against human-adjudicated labels on 150 of these tweets. User-level profiles (582 users) were compared with L1-penalised logistic regression, nested cross-validation, bootstrap stability analysis, per-symptom effect sizes, and co-occurrence analysis. A baseline pipeline with a natural language inference filter reached a precision of only 0.09-0.13, whereas the final pipeline reached precision 0.48-0.59 and F1 0.55-0.68. Discrimination between groups was modest (nested ROC-AUC 0.60-0.62). Language related to suicidal ideation was more frequent among ASD users and language related to cognitive issues among ADHD users; both differences were small but robust across sensitivity analyses. These exploratory findings describe how depressive symptoms are expressed online, not their clinical prevalence.
  </details>

- **[K-BrowseComp: A Web Browsing Agent Benchmark Grounded in Korean Contexts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02404)**  `arXiv:2606.02404`  `cs.CL`  
  _Nahyun Lee, Dongkeun Yoon, Guijin Son, Geewook Kim, Dayoon Ko, Jeonghun Park, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier model evaluations are shifting from foundational capabilities (e.g., instruction following and reasoning) toward compositional, agentic ones, but Korean agentic benchmarks remain scarce. We introduce K-BrowseComp, a web-browsing agent benchmark grounded in Korean contexts, consisting of 400 problems. The 300-problem K-BrowseComp-Verified subset is manually constructed and validated by native Korean speakers. On this subset, frontier LLMs, including GPT-5.5, DeepSeek-V4-Pro, and GLM-5.1, reach only 30.00--45.67\%, a substantial drop from BrowseComp, while Korean LLMs released through Korea's Proprietary AI Foundation Model program obtain only 0.00--10.33\%. We further construct a 100-problem synthetic split using hard few-shot exemplars and failure-mode-targeted generation to exploit the asymmetry between solving and creating web browsing problems. On the adversarially filtered synthetic diagnostic split, the strongest model reaches only 26.00\%, and we report this split separately as a targeted stress test. We publicly release our data and code.
  </details>

- **[Inducing Artificial Uncertainty in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13595)**  `arXiv:2605.13595`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sophia Hager, Simon Zeng, Nicholas Andrews_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In safety-critical applications, language models should be able to characterize their uncertainty with meaningful probabilities. Many uncertainty quantification approaches require supervised data; however, finding suitable unseen challenging data is increasingly difficult for large language models trained on vast amounts of scraped data. If the model is consistently (and correctly) confident in its predictions, the uncertainty quantification method may consistently overestimate confidence on new and unfamiliar data. Finding data which exhibits enough uncertainty to train supervised uncertainty quantification methods for high-performance models may therefore be challenging, and will increase in difficulty as LLMs saturate datasets. To address this issue, we first introduce the problem of inducing artificial uncertainty in language models, then investigate methods of inducing artificial uncertainty on trivially easy data in the absence of challenging data at training time. We use probes trained to recognize artificial uncertainty on the original model, and find that these probes trained on artificial uncertainty outperform probes trained without artificial uncertainty in recognizing real uncertainty, achieving notably higher calibration on hard data with minimal loss of performance on easy data.
  </details>

- **[Adapting Large Language Models to Low-Resource Tibetan: A Two-Stage Continual and Supervised Fine-Tuning Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.03976)**  `arXiv:2512.03976`  `cs.CL`  
  _Lifeng Chen, Chenyang Zhong, Ryan Lai, Tianming Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adapting large language models (LLMs) to low-resource languages remains a major challenge due to data scarcity and cross-lingual drift. This work presents a two-stage adaptation of Qwen2.5-3B to Tibetan, a morphologically rich and underrepresented language. We employ Continual Pretraining (CPT) to establish Tibetan linguistic grounding, followed by Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) for task and translation specialization. Empirical evaluations demonstrate a consistent decrease in perplexity (from 2.98 $\rightarrow$ 1.54) and substantial improvements in Chinese$\rightarrow$Tibetan translation quality (BLEU: 0.046 $\rightarrow$ 0.261; chrF: 2.2 $\rightarrow$ 6.6). Layer-wise analysis across 435 layers in Qwen3-4B reveals that adaptation primarily concentrates on embedding and output heads, with mid--late MLP projections encoding domain-specific transformations. Our findings suggest that CPT constructs a Tibetan semantic manifold while SFT sharpens task alignment with minimal representational disruption. This study provides the first quantitative exploration of Tibetan adaptation dynamics for LLMs, and offers an open, reproducible framework for extending multilingual foundation models to low-resource settings.
  </details>

- **[Guideline-Based Medical Reasoning in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.03838)**  `arXiv:2512.03838`  `cs.CL`  
  _Michael Staniek, Artem Sokolov, Stefan Riezler_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) excel at medical question-answering, yet progress toward medical reasoning models is limited by the lack of scalable, trustworthy reasoning supervision. We propose tapping into the so far underused resource of medical consensus guidelines. These map numerical clinical measurements to disease scores and can optionally be curated for site-specific exceptions. Automatically generating gold-standard reasoning chains from consensus rules enables both large-scale supervised fine-tuning and automatic evaluation. Applying SOFA, SAPS-II, and Sepsis-3 guidelines to MIMIC-III, we show that small fine-tuned models significantly outperform larger one-shot prompted models in derivation correctness and F1 score, achieving near-perfect deductive inference and reliable exception handling. Trustworthy step-by-step reasoning does not reliably emerge from one-shot prompting, even when models receive the consensus definition and an example. Performance across all models declines when forecasting future clinical variables, indicating that, on the evaluated structured ICU tasks, the central challenge is not rule application, but the orthogonal problem of generalizing into the future for prognostic reasoning.
  </details>

- **[Beating Hateful Narratives on Social Media with Facts: Retrieval-Augmented Counter-Speech Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.12316)**  `arXiv:2510.12316`  `cs.CL`  
  _Greta Damo, Elena Cabrio, Serena Villata_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Online hate speech threatens social cohesion by amplifying discrimination and harmful narratives against marginalized communities. While counter-speech (CS) - fact-based, respectful responses to hate - offers a constructive alternative to content removal, its large-scale generation remains constrained by factual reliability and the risk of generic responses. We address these challenges with a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) pipeline grounded in the first large-scale, curated knowledge base built from authoritative international and regional human rights sources, including the United Nations Digital Library, EUR-Lex, and the EU Agency for Fundamental Rights. The framework covers eight groups frequently targeted by online abuse. We systematically evaluate all pipeline components demonstrating that each design choice is empirically motivated, combining automatic metrics, LLM-based judgments, and human evaluation with comparisons against strong baselines. Results show that RAG improves factual grounding, diversity, and perceived effectiveness over No-RAG and performs favorably against existing systems. Our human evaluation further reveals differences between in-group and out-group perceptions of CS, with RAG narrowing but not eliminating this gap. These findings highlight the potential of knowledge grounded CS for scalable social media interventions, while underscoring the need to evaluate such systems with and for the communities they are intended to serve.
  </details>

- **[PersonaFuse: A Situation-Aware Post-Training Framework for Adaptive LLM Behavior in Enterprise Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07370)**  `arXiv:2509.07370`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yixuan Tang, Yi Yang, Ahmed Abbasi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in enterprise settings to support task execution and workflow automation. Despite the strong performance of general-purpose models on standard benchmarks, organizations often need to adapt them to domain-specific tasks and operational constraints in practice. However, existing post-training approaches introduce capability trade-offs and lack mechanisms for situational adaptation. In this work, we propose PersonaFuse, a post-training framework that enables situation-aware behavioral adaptation in LLMs. Grounded in Trait Activation Theory and operationalized through the Five-Factor Model, PersonaFuse dynamically adjusts model behavior based on task context using a mixture-of-experts architecture with trait-level experts and a context-aware routing mechanism. We evaluate PersonaFuse at three levels. At the capability level, it achieves balanced improvements across general reasoning, interaction quality, and safety without introducing trade-offs. At the workflow level, it improves task completion in enterprise scenarios involving tool use and structured execution across both B2B and B2C tasks. At the deployment level, we analyze model selection under cost and safety constraints using a capability-cost frontier framework, showing that models adapted with PersonaFuse achieve more favorable trade-offs and are consistently selected as optimal across deployment scenarios. PersonaFuse provides a practical framework for developing LLMs that are not only capable, but also adaptable and deployable in real-world enterprise environments.
  </details>

- **[Multilingual Hallucination Gaps in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.18270)**  `arXiv:2410.18270`  `cs.CL`  
  _Cléa Chataigner, Afaf Taïk, Golnoosh Farnadi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as alternatives to traditional search engines given their capacity to generate text that resembles human language. However, this shift is concerning, as LLMs often generate hallucinations, misleading or false information that appears highly credible. In this study, we explore the phenomenon of hallucinations across multiple languages in freeform text generation, focusing on what we call multilingual hallucination gaps. These gaps reflect differences in the frequency of hallucinated answers depending on the prompt and language used. To quantify such hallucinations, we used the FactScore metric and extended its framework to a multilingual setting. We conducted experiments using LLMs from the LLaMA, Qwen, and Aya families, generating biographies in 19 languages and comparing the results to Wikipedia pages. Our results reveal variations in hallucination rates, especially between high and low resource languages, raising important questions about LLM multilingual performance and the challenges in evaluating hallucinations in multilingual freeform text generation.
  </details>

- **[Autonomous Multi-modal Agents for ITOps Observability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19982)**  `arXiv:2611.19982`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Gunjan Siddharth, Sanjana Garai, Utsav Shekhar, Prakhar Jain, Sarvam Maheshwari, Balaji Ganesan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  ITOps observability products must integrate data from different tools and systems. We present ITOps Observability Studio, a multi-modal multi-agent system that orchestrates observability workflows through collaborative AI agents. Its three-tier architecture features inter-agent communication, specialized LLM-powered agents for data access, schema operations and visualization, and unified tool integration. The system supports natural language to GraphQL translation, GraphQL schema generation, schema mapping, trace data lifecycle management and multi-tool workflows for reliability engineering. Its agents are instrumented with agent analytics, so that their model and tool calls can be traced.
