We evaluate each agent on its own benchmark, scored against gold answers where they exist and by a model judge for schema mapping and chart quality. Query generation on a public benchmark and routing work well with a current open model, and the data lifecycle management agent solves most of its scenarios. The evaluation exposed defects that only appear when prompts, parsers and serving stacks are tested together, and we report them together with their fixes.
  </details>

- **[LLM Trading Agents Earn Beta, Not Alpha: A Look-Ahead-Free Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19530)**  `arXiv:2611.19530`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Jackson Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) trading agents now trade stocks for their users, and benchmarks rank models by their agents' returns. Yet a single run's return says little about skill. In our study, two runs of the same model that differed only in the random seed ended 5.2 points apart on average, so telling skill from luck takes many backtests across markets, models and seeds. However, live arenas trade each day only once, and backtests either use the few months after a model's training cutoff or run on years the model may remember, often with news joined to prices by calendar date. In this paper, we present rta-bench-1, a backtest over three US markets (a rally, a bear market and a crash). It shows each headline only after its publication minute, plays each period blind and named, and checks every run for look-ahead. It also splits each return into factor exposure and alpha and scores every decision against random choices of the same day and sector. In 91 runs of six models from four vendors, no alpha was significant, market and style exposure accounted for a median 78% of the return, and the decisions did no better than random stocks of the same sector. The agents bought the stocks with the most positive news, yet in ablations they earned as much without their headlines. Still, the agents carried out rules reliably, since a single cash rule in the prompt cut a GPT-based agent's share in stocks by 30.3 points in all six pairs. Builders should therefore supply the edge themselves and report exposures and alpha, and benchmarks should replay news by publication time.
  </details>

- **[Consensus Within, Competition Between: Decentralized LLM Teams for Perimeter Defense](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41044)**  `arXiv:2610.41044`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Khawaja Abaid Ullah, Ananya Acharya, Mohammad Javad Khojasteh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The use of large language models (LLMs) to coordinate multi-agent teams raises a fundamental architectural question: whether task allocation should be centralized in a single LLM controller or distributed across autonomous LLM agents, particularly when communication is unreliable. In a deliberately simplified perimeter-defense simulation against LLM-controlled intruders, we compared a central commander, an auction-based consensus team and independent agents under five network conditions. Breach rates were 15% for the consensus team, 32% for the commander and 29% for independent agents. Burst message loss, falsified reports and limited radio range raised the commander's breach rate by 10 to 18 percentage points (pp), whereas the consensus team's changed by at most 5. In the consensus team, giving a low-cost LLM more authority over the bid computation raised its breach rate by 38 pp. With birds and civilian drones in airspace, we added an image for each to the input text state. Only the near-frontier model treated them apart from intruders. Intruders disguised as existing taxidermy bird drones were mistaken for birds by both low-cost and near-frontier LLMs, being dropped from pursuit in 59-61% of near-frontier decisions (1-16% before). However, a one-sentence forewarning in the prompt lowered it to 0-25%. We find that the LLM team with a dedicated external conflict-resolution protocol had the fewest breaches. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[GRACE: Parallel Best-of-N Multi-Agent Analysis of Closed-Form Questions over Dataframes with Guardrail Selection and Adaptive Retry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20983)**  `arXiv:2611.20983`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Fabio Picariello, Salvatore Cipolla, Susanna Bonura, Vito Morreale_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents answer closed-form questions over a tabular dataframe by writing and executing Python, almost always as a single rollout: one interpretation, one script, one result, accepted without comparison. The few pipelines that produce several candidates reconcile them by answer-agreement voting, which needs a single comparable scalar and always returns one, even when every candidate is wrong. We present GRACE (Generative, Ranked, Adaptive Code Ensemble), a production-oriented pipeline that instead runs parallel best-of-N selection specialised to dataframe code. N agents run concurrently under a strict, machine-checkable output contract, diversified by persona hints, per-retry rotation, and a two-regime temperature schedule. Rather than vote, GRACE judges the executed artefacts: a pairwise tournament pre-filters the pool and a domain-specialised guardrail either selects the best candidate or, when none is defensible, rejects the whole pool and abstains with a diagnosis (an option voting structurally lacks), which in turn drives an adaptive retry with per-agent feedback. On InfiAgent-DABench (DAEval, M = 246), using Qwen3.6-35B-A3B as the backbone, GRACE raises answer match from 39% for a single-rollout baseline to 71% (+32 pp, 1.8x), recovering 53% of previously wrong answers. On the same DAEval task, the smaller dense Qwen3.5-9B lifts answer match from 38% to 63% (+25 pp, 1.7x). A controlled 3 x 3 study that sweeps the number of parallel agents against the retry budget finds, on the 35B backbone, the two levers largely substitutable: once either one is generous, the other adds little, identifying a cheap operating point for deployment. The same directional pattern replicates on the independent DataBench benchmark, again on the Qwen3.6-35B-A3B backbone, where answer match rises by 15 percentage points (from 70% to 85%) across 400 sampled questions.
  </details>

- **[AgentFaultBench: State-Verified Evaluation of Tool-Fault Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20711)**  `arXiv:2611.20711`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Sachin Gupta_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Does recovery advice help an agent finish a task after a tool fails? AgentFaultBench evaluates LangGraph and CrewAI agents performing specified tasks in an isolated GitLab installation. We inject tool errors and check both the resulting files and issues and the required reads, verification and final response. The prompt supplies the expected issue and report text, so the task tests execution rather than content generation. In the primary comparison of 1,944 planned runs, advice changes success by -8.33, 0.00 and +1.06 percentage points relative to a neutral reminder; missing outcomes leave the latter two comparisons inconclusive. Later experiments test a revised recovery policy. GPT-4o mini improves, while Sol and Claude already complete the recoverable cases. In a separate 624-run study on six related tasks not previously used by that policy, the guided condition records 161 correct final states and one unknown outcome in 192 planned runs, versus 142/192 with neutral feedback. The gain lies between +9.90 and +10.42 pp when missing outcomes are included. Removing issue IDs gives 47/48 successes versus 46/48 with IDs; other execution information remains. A second checker agrees on 1,879 observed primary outcomes and 241 constructed test cases. No human validation was collected. The findings support testing advice against explicit recovery controls rather than assuming it helps.
  </details>

- **[Interactive Structured Environment Scaling for Large Language Model Agentic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19871)**  `arXiv:2611.19871`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Peng Yu, Luoyi Fu, Xinbing Wang, Ying Wen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning via environmental interaction is a cornerstone post-training paradigm for evolving large language models into autonomous agents capable of multi-turn tool use and exploration. Because the environment defines what the policy observes and how actions are verified, its structure directly bounds the capabilities an agent can acquire. Yet existing structured and retrieval environments rely on upstream pre-filtering---extracting a compact subgraph around the gold answer or retrieving fixed passages before the policy acts. We show that such pre-filtering introduces a fundamental measurement confound: because per-question subgraphs enclose ground-truth paths, they compress the candidate space by orders of magnitude and leak answer locations via local topology, allowing a question-blind degree heuristic to surface gold answers far above chance. To restore path selection, candidate pruning, and optimal stopping as genuine policy decisions, we propose RISE (Interactive Structured Environment), which transitions structured reinforcement learning from static subgraphs to full-scale interactive databases along two coupled axes. First, Decision-Space Scaling exposes the unpruned knowledge graph via a five-stage graph-agnostic typed intent adapter under a bounded call budget and a multiplicative grounding reward gate. Second, Learning-Frontier Scaling overcomes within-group variance collapse under group-normalized objectives by uniting model-independent structural tier progression with a self-evolving competence-frontier bandit. Across nineteen benchmarks and three model backbones, RISE substantially advances in-domain and unseen-schema knowledge-graph reasoning, transfers zero-shot to open-domain passage retrieval, and significantly enhances out-of-domain mathematical and logical deduction.
  </details>

- **[SpecRelay: Amortize or Avoid the Round Trip in Edge-Cloud Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18963)**  `arXiv:2611.18963`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jai Jain, Chihyo Ahn, Yong Liu, Charles Chen, Duc N.M Hoang, Ajay Kumar Jaiswal, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Edge-cloud speculative decoding moves autoregressive drafting to an edge device while a larger cloud model verifies draft tokens in parallel, so that edge drafting and cloud verification overlap under a synchronous draft-verify schedule. Beyond low-latency local networks, however, the next verification request of that schedule can be sent only after the previous reply arrives, so every round still waits out a full round trip, and throughput falls as the network RTT grows. To recover this loss, we present SpecRelay, an edge-cloud LLM inference system that addresses this cost at two levels: it amortizes round trips when cloud verification is necessary and avoids them when local reasoning suffices. For cloud-bound queries, an asynchronous verification pipeline keeps fixed-budget draft trees in flight while earlier replies are still outstanding, and the cloud rejects stale requests before the target-model forward pass (the asynchronous schedule). At the query level, an RTT-conditioned router (the Latency-Aware Router) estimates quality and completion latency for three on-device modes (direct generation, Chain-of-Draft, and full reasoning) and cloud speculative decoding, selecting among them under a configurable accuracy-latency objective without adding latency during inference, since the router runs concurrently with the draft-model prefill. The asynchronous schedule alone improves decoding throughput by up to 50% over a reproduced synchronous baseline across multiple benchmarks. At matched target-model accuracy and 250 ms of added round-trip latency, the full system with the router delivers 1.95x and 1.55x the throughput of the same baseline on SPROUT and OlympiadBench, respectively, while sending only 47% and 39% of the queries to the cloud.
  </details>

- **[MARBO: Relational Belief Grounding for LLM Agents in Social Deduction Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06563)**  `arXiv:2609.06563`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yechan Hwang, Sangjun Bae, Jeongmo Kim, Sangwoo Bang, Seungyul Han_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Social deduction games (SDGs) require agents to reason under partial observability by maintaining relational beliefs about hidden roles and team alignments. While recent LLM-agent approaches improve gameplay through prompting and preference optimization, they often optimize actions and in-game speech without explicitly grounding them in such beliefs. This frequently leads to strategically inconsistent behavior, especially for compact LLM agents. We introduce Multi-Agent Relational Belief Optimization (MARBO), a belief-grounded preference optimization framework that leverages relational beliefs to guide strategic decisions and in-game speech. MARBO provides preference feedback only when behaviors are supported by reliable relational beliefs and lead to strategically favorable social outcomes, encouraging more consistent learning under uncertainty. Experiments on representative SDGs show that MARBO enables compact LLM agents to consistently outperform existing baselines. The Code is available onthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[LLMs Can Design Near-Optimal OR Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27296)**  `arXiv:2608.27296`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jackie Baek_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We ask whether large language models (LLMs) can design effective algorithms for well-specified operations research (OR) problems. We study inventory control, queueing network control, and assortment optimization, using benchmark instances from recent papers in each area. We evaluate two levels of LLM use: at level 1, the model receives one problem instance and returns a solution for that instance; at level 2, it receives only the problem class description and broad parameter ranges, and returns an algorithm that maps instance parameters to solutions. Human input is minimal: we give one untuned prompt that describes the problem, and the model has access to a Python sandbox tool with a fixed compute budget.The strongest model we test, gpt-5.6-sol, matches or outperforms the best method reported in those papers on almost all evaluated instances. This holds even at level 2, where the returned algorithm is fixed before seeing the evaluation instances. Performance also improves sharply across models released less than eight months apart, suggesting that this capability is moving quickly. Thus, for the well-specified operations problems we study, a single untuned LLM query can already produce algorithms competitive with specialized methods. These results suggest that frontier LLMs can be a serious empirical baseline for algorithm design in well-specified OR problems.
  </details>

- **[SkillBoost: A Constrained Exploration-Exploitation Process for Mitigating Skill Overfitting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26643)**  `arXiv:2607.26643`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Hongqiang Lin, Chao Liu, Xiaofan Bai, Xuan Jin, Yuhong Li, Nenggan Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enabling large language model (LLM) agents to accumulate and reuse experience from past interactions remains a central challenge in real-world applications. A promising solution is to treat skills as trainable states and optimize them in the same way as model parameters in neural network training. However, data-driven skill optimization is prone to overfitting to the limited trajectories collected from real environments. Overexploiting these trajectories overfits the current batch, while unconstrained exploration causes regression on previously solved cases. This tension motivates a constrained search view of skill self-evolution, governed by an exploration--exploitation trade-off. We propose SkillBoost, a three-stage framework that mitigates both risks: structured exploitation localizes observed failures to editable skill components, prior-guided exploration draws on prior knowledge in the LLM to generate diverse repair candidates, and verified acceptance commits a candidate only when it improves performance within a regression bound. Experiments across 23 model--benchmark configurations show that SkillBoost achieves state-of-the-art performance while mitigating overfitting, outperforming both human-crafted and LLM-generated skills. Transfer experiments further show that optimized skills can be reused by other agents on similar tasks. The code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[SIRI: Self-Internalizing Reinforcement Learning with Intrinsic Skills for LLM Agent Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02355)**  `arXiv:2606.02355`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhongyu He, Yuanfan Li, Fei Huang, Tianyu Chen, Siyuan Chen, Xingyang Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon LLM agents can benefit from reusable skills, yet existing skill-based methods often rely on external skill generators during training or persistent skill retrieval at inference, increasing engineering complexity, context length, and deployment latency. We propose Self-Internalizing Reinforcement learning with Intrinsic skills (SIRI), a three-phase framework that enables agents to discover, validate, and internalize skills without external skill generators or inference-time skill banks. SIRI first warms up the policy with GiGPO to acquire basic interaction ability and collect successful skill-free trajectories. It then performs self-skill mining, where the current policy summarizes compact skills from its own successful plain rollouts and validates them through paired skill-augmented and skill-free rollouts. Finally, SIRI distills only beneficial skill-guided action tokens into the plain policy using trajectory-level utility and action-level advantage. At inference, the agent runs with the original prompt only. On ALFWorld and WebShop with Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, SIRI improves GiGPO from 0.908 to 0.930 on ALFWorld and from 0.728 to 0.813 on WebShop, outperforming prompt-based, RL-based, and memory-augmented baselines. Further analysis shows that our self-mining strategy can achieve performance comparable to distillation with closed-source large model.
  </details>

- **[Debate as Reward: A Multi-Agent Reward System for Scientific Ideation via RL Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16723)**  `arXiv:2604.16723`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Moein Salimi, Babak Hosseini Mohtasham, Amin Aghakasiri, Mahdi Naieni, Amir Hossein Qeysarbeigi, Mohammad Masih Shalchian Nazer, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated potential in automating scientific ideation, yet current approaches relying on iterative prompting or complex multi-agent architectures often suffer from hallucination or computational inefficiency. A critical bottleneck in applying Reinforcement Learning (RL) to this open-ended domain is reward hacking---where models exploit imperfect evaluation proxies to maximize scores without producing genuine scientific innovation. To address these limitations, we propose an RL framework explicitly tailored for high-quality scientific idea generation. We propose the first multi-agent reward function designed to serve as a judge, decoupling methodological validation from implementation details while providing strict binary rewards that are robust to reward hacking. To effectively optimize against this sparse signal, we utilize an unbiased variant of Group Relative Policy Optimization to mitigate artificial length bias. We ground our training in a curated dataset of problem-solution pairs extracted from ICLR 2024 and NeurIPS 2025 proceedings. Experiments demonstrate that our framework significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across expert-evaluated metrics of novelty, feasibility, and effectiveness.
  </details>

- **[ReST-RL: Reinforcing LLM Reasoning through Unified Self-Training and Value-Guided Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.19576)**  `arXiv:2508.19576`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Sining Zhoubian, Dan Zhang, Jie Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  With respect to improving the reasoning accuracy of LLMs, the representative reinforcement learning (RL) method - Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) - has achieved critical success, yet it still suffers from the issue of insignificant reward signals. This paper introduces ReST-RL, a unified Reinforced Self-Training (ReST) policy-value framework that reconnects policy optimization and value-guided search to improve LLM reasoning ability. Firstly, ReST-GRPO adopts an optimized ReST-style algorithm to reshape the policy-induced trajectory distribution by increasing the reward variance of GRPO sampling and exposing the policy to more informative partial states, thereby improving training efficiency and effectiveness. Then, we further introduce a decoding optimization method, VM-MCTS, which trains a Value Model (VM) from self-collected Monte-Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) targets and deploys it through an adapted MCTS algorithm to provide precise process signals and verification scores, further enhancing reasoning accuracy. These two stages are internally dependent - ReST-GRPO yields higher quality trajectories for value learning with VM-MCTS, which in turn enables more effective inference-time search. We validate our framework on multiple coding benchmarks (e.g., APPS, BigCodeBench, and HumanEval), where it significantly outperforms other reinforcement training baselines (naive GRPO, DAPO, and ReST-DPO), as well as decoding and verification baselines (e.g., PRM-BoN and ORM-MCTS), indicating its power to strengthen LLM reasoning capability. Moreover, we further evaluate ReST-RL on out-of-domain math and science reasoning tasks, where it achieves improved performance without target-domain tuning and favorable end-to-end efficiency trade-offs, providing preliminary transfer evidence beyond our primary coding domain.
  </details>

- **[CivilBench: Benchmarking Vision-Language Models for Multimodal Information Integration in Civil Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21105)**  `arXiv:2611.21105`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Dan Yuan, Yue Wen, Zhili He, Zhenkui Zhou, Shicen Liu, Ziqiang Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Artificial intelligence (AI), particularly large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs), is increasingly applied in civil and structural engineering for disaster assessment, structural analysis, and engineering decision support. However, the extent to which current VLMs possess the reasoning capabilities required for reliable engineering judgments remains unclear. In this work, we introduce CivilBench, a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating VLMs in civil engineering and disaster-related scenarios. CivilBench covers three major task families: Sequential Reasoning, Image-based Structural Understanding, and Open-world Understanding, encompassing numerical reasoning, structural damage assessment, risk inference, causal analysis, and repair planning. The benchmark is constructed through a systematic pipeline of domain-specific data collection, engineering-oriented captioning, expert annotation, question design, and quality verification. Evaluation of representative state-of-the-art VLMs reveals substantial limitations in domain-specific reasoning, with Sequential Reasoning emerging as a major bottleneck, particularly when models must infer numerical relationships from sparse structured observations. Further experiments show that numerical priors and external knowledge can alleviate these limitations, although their effectiveness depends on the model's ability to reliably interpret and use the available evidence. CivilBench provides a capability-oriented testbed for identifying reasoning limitations and evaluating progress toward reliable AI-assisted engineering decision-making. The benchmark will be publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Video-as-Experience: Learning Computer Use via Audio-Visual Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18747)**  `arXiv:2611.18747`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Guangzhi Sun, Zhonghao Zhai, Yixuan Li, Chao Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (LLMs) have enabled capable computer-use agents that perceive graphical user interfaces, reason over instructions, and execute actions. However, existing approaches to learning from video demonstrations typically convert videos into screenshot-based action trajectories, rather than directly leveraging their rich audio-visual information. We introduce VEX (Video as EXperience), a framework that uses audio-visual understanding to unify learning from external demonstrations and reflecting on an agent's own behavior. Following a watch-act-rewatch paradigm, the agent extracts procedural guidance from tutorial videos containing visual demonstrations and spoken explanations, executes tasks, and verifies its progress by rewatching its execution as video. We instantiate VEX with video-SALMONN-VEX, a 9B-scale audio-visual computer-use agent built on the video-SALMONN 2 architecture with a Qwen3.5-9B backbone. We further introduce Action-Grounded Guide Optimization (AGGO), a reinforcement learning method that optimizes video-derived guidance for downstream action prediction, and augment AgentNet, OSWorld, and CUA-Gym with over 24K real-world tutorial videos. Experiments on OSWorld and a complementary subset of CUA-Gym demonstrate consistent improvements, with video-SALMONN-VEX achieving a 22% relative increase in task success over its Qwen3.5 backbone on OSWorld. Moreover, its generated guidance improves Gemini-3.5-flash by 10% relatively on OSWorld. These results highlight the potential of audio-visual understanding as a unified interface for procedural learning and self-verification in computer-use agents.
  </details>

- **[Macro Control and Capability Evaluation of Black-Box World Models: A Two-Regime Framework for LLM-Agent Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19311)**  `arXiv:2611.19311`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Zhiyang Niu, Chuanjie Li, Huayuan Shen, Mengmeng Zhao, Jiajia Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents can be organized into multi-agent markets that act as world models, mapping macro information to emergent macro dynamics while the evaluator observes only macro inputs and outputs. This paper separates a model's capability into two stages that a single aggregate score cannot separate: information simulation, from macro information to micro behavior, and macro emergence, from micro behavior to the macro outcome. We introduce a soft macro-control method that announces a public reference path day by day without touching individual decisions, orders, or matching, and a two-regime evaluation framework that measures both stages from macro observations alone. In the reference-only regime the symmetric true and flipped references share a common level R, which measures realization under the announced reference, while their paired gap B measures structural bias; in the information-injection regime external information dilutes the reference, so only the true-flipped gap Delta remains interpretable. On five single-index LLM-agent markets, announcing a target path shifts the realized path toward the announced target on both announced references under both information conditions. Relative to the no-reference path, the tracking score is higher by 0.306 and 0.415 without news and by 0.183 and 0.329 with news; within the stock/cash scan structural bias is lowest at 70/30 (B=0.042); realization peaks at a 3:7 technical share (R=0.894 versus 0.358 for technical-only); and Delta is 0.0341 with fundamental data and 0.0487 with news. Both stages use the same two observable scores, each read in the regime where it is interpretable.
  </details>

- **[Staged Semantic Distillation: Preserving Free-Form Semantics in a Low-Compute Non-LLM Surrogate of a Staged LLM Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19299)**  `arXiv:2611.19299`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Zhiyang Niu, Huayuan Shen, Chuanjie Li, Mengmeng Zhao, Jiajia Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Staged LLM agents make market and social simulators realistic, but one 102-day run of a 200-agent, ten-index market simulator issues 203,100 language-model calls and returns one realization. Conventional low-compute distillation drops their free-form intermediate state. We present staged semantic distillation, a low-compute method that preserves non-structured semantics in a non-LLM student trained from one recorded trace. A frozen encoder maps each teacher output to a 1024-d vector; the student sees typed stage-tagged vectors with stage-specific visibility, is jointly supervised, and trains with scheduled self-history. One TwinMarket trace yields a 13.2M-parameter deterministic student that trains in 1,658 seconds on one 8-GB GPU and runs with zero teacher or decoder calls and no text generation (0.14 ms per decision). Its analysis stays close to the teacher (cosine 0.943), and swapping semantic inputs between agents moves 64.7% of decisions against 12.8% for a matched donor, so the channel is preserved and live. We use the surrogate to study what the transfer carries. It reaches 0.8347 balanced accuracy on the teacher-forced replay and 0.7783 [0.766,0.791] on the deployment decoder; the five-seed gain over a no-semantic retrain is +2.2 [1.21,3.14]. Against the teacher's own re-sampling level, round-one R is 97.1% [95.8,98.4] and 92.3% [89.0,95.4]; R is a diagnostic, not an accuracy. A five-seed content-by-schedule study isolates the gain: scheduled self-history carries it (+2.2 [1.21,3.14]); shuffled and constant content controls reproduce it, while content matters teacher-forced. The design rule: invest in the deployment schedule, let content earn its place at the teacher-forced operating point. A 20-seed, 200,000-decision sweep takes 12 minutes with zero LLM calls, versus three hours for the teacher. The paper is a method-and-case-study contribution on one simulator and trace.
  </details>

- **[MindTopo: Can Foundation Models Reason in Topological Space?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11900)**  `arXiv:2609.11900`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Yunfei Ge, Anbang Liu, Qineng Wang, Johnalbert Garnica, Jianwen Lyu, Zihan Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial reasoning depends not only on metric properties such as distance, angle, and shape, but also on topological relations that remain invariant under continuous deformation. Cognitive science identifies these relations as foundational to spatial understanding, yet foundation-model evaluations largely focus on metric or viewpoint-dependent relations. We introduce MindTopo, a benchmark of topological intuition across five properties grounded in cognitive science and formal topology, namely continuity, separation, order, enclosure, and knots. MindTopo evaluates each property at two cognitive levels. Reasoning asks a model to identify topological relations or infer how they change. Planning instantiates a foundation model as a closed-loop agent whose policy selects environment actions. MindTopo provides a Core Set of 3,900 instances across 13 procedurally generated task types, split into an Easy and a Hard level, and an Extended Set of 11,035 original instances. On the Core Set, we benchmark 13 multimodal large language models (MLLMs) and study agent configurations augmented with image and video generation, covering seven video generative models in planning settings. Every MLLM performs better on reasoning than on planning. On the Easy level, where human scores are available, the strongest model trails humans in both reasoning and planning. On Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct, supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning improve reasoning more than planning. Generated observations retain local cues and reach solved endpoints, yet none of the 1,890 evaluated video rollouts passes all process-validity checks.
  </details>

- **[LatentRouter: Can We Choose the Right Multimodal Large Language Model Before Seeing Its Answer?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11301)**  `arXiv:2605.11301`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Xueqi Cheng, Yushun Dong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have heterogeneous strengths across OCR, chart understanding, spatial reasoning, visual question answering, cost, and latency. Effective MLLM routing therefore requires more than estimating query difficulty: a router must match the multimodal requirements of the current image-question input with the capabilities of each candidate model. We propose LatentRouter, a router that formulates MLLM routing as counterfactual multimodal utility prediction. Given an image-question query, LatentRouter extracts learned multimodal routing capsules, represents each candidate MLLM with a model capability token, and performs latent communication between these states to estimate how each model would perform if selected. A distributional outcome head predicts model-specific counterfactual quality, while a bounded capsule correction refines close decisions without allowing residual signals to dominate the prediction. The resulting utility-based policy supports performance-oriented and performance-cost routing, and handles changing candidate pools through shared per-model scoring with availability masking. Experiments on MMR-Bench and VL-RouterBench show that LatentRouter outperforms fixed-model, feature-level, and learned-router baselines. Additional analyses show that the gains are strongest on multimodal task groups where model choice depends on visual, layout-sensitive, or reasoning-oriented requirements, and that latent communication is the main contributor to the improvement. The code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Jev-as-a-Reward: Validating Probabilistic Safety Judges for Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20955)**  `arXiv:2611.20955`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Eunkyeong Lee, Junhyung Moon, Seunggwan Hong, Dongsuk Oh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Probabilistic safety judges return the probability of a safety label and cheaply score reinforcement learning rollouts. A confidence gate sends only uncertain verdicts to a stronger LLM judge, which leaves confidently passed harm in a blind spot. Policies can exploit this blind spot, and commercial judges release new versions. We propose a protocol that validates such a judge before and during training, separates the share of confidently passed harm from its amount, and re-validates it after each judge change. We apply it to two versions of Jev, a commercial decision-only judge, and to an open guard classifier. Under the probability reward of Jev, passed harm shrinks in all 11 runs under the primary reference, mostly more slowly than caught harm, and concentrates in the blind spot. Binarizing this judge meets the escape criterion on Llama, and the open guard classifier shows an escape signal in one seed under a second reference. Guard classifiers share this blind spot, and even frontier LLM judges catch at most one of 10 human-labeled confident misses. Without a trained reward model, the probability reward with a helpfulness term lowers HarmBench attack success on Llama-3.1-8B from 0.287 to at most 0.033 at the final checkpoint.
  </details>

- **[Objectify, Don't Delete: Complaint-Derived Evaluation of Ambient Clinical Notes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19525)**  `arXiv:2611.19525`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Nikita Gupta, Narayanan Unny Edakunni, Vikram Okaly, Suranjana Datta, Navya Penubadi, Karl Eric Swanson, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating the quality of AI-generated clinical notes is hard: standard metrics like ROUGE measure lexical similarity to reference notes, while expert checklists encode what committees believe matters. Neither captures what clinicians actually complain about. Recent work has shown that evaluation criteria can be mined from user feedback and filtered to criteria that LLM judges can reliably enforce. We argue that this is where much of the value is lost. The criteria that fail enforceability are disproportionately the ones clinicians raise most often, because high-frequency complaints are stated as several questions glued together. No yes/no judgment can grade such composites as posed. We introduce an objectification step that rewrites such criteria into sets of scoped, determinate decisions. We demonstrate the approach through an evaluation pipeline that mines criteria from complaints, objectifies them, and deploys LLM judges against both planted defects and production notes. Across eleven defects planted into ACI-bench reference notes, objectified judges localize violations with median recall of 0.95, while reference metrics change by at most 0.037. Given the complaint as one broad question, the same judges still find every planted defect, but they also flag 70% of the unperturbed reference notes; one section-pair question per call raises Youden's J from 0.30 to 0.72 (by 0.19 to 0.26 with prompt effort matched). We release ACI-Defect, a perturbation benchmark; clinician-reviewed instance-level labels showing quality gaps in the ACI-bench gold standard (32 of 40 notes contain hallucinations; 40 of 40 omit discussed facts); and grounding-judge verdicts. On 1,446 production notes, mean instance-rate burden falls at every clinician-rating step (rho=-0.15), a small association. Defect counts have to be kept per span rather than collapsed into one pass/fail bit per note.
  </details>

- **[Large Language Models for AI Infrastructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19061)**  `arXiv:2611.19061`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Bolin Shen, Shaoyi Huang, Yushun Dong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to design, optimize, and operate the infrastructure behind AI training and serving, yet the literature remains fragmented across hardware design, machine learning systems, electronic design automation, and high-performance computing. We present a comprehensive survey of LLM-assisted AI infrastructure across its lifecycle, from accelerator design and kernel engineering to compilation, deployment, operations, data management, and energy optimization. We organize the literature by the primary resource the LLM manages: artifact-centric methods for computational artifacts, operational-centric methods for deployment context and system state, and resource-centric methods for data and energy. These categories span the lifecycle and cover AI-specific concerns such as distributed-training configuration, inference serving, GPU-cluster scheduling, and data loading. Building on this taxonomy, we conduct a cross-study quantitative synthesis of reported results. Because studies adopt heterogeneous benchmarks, baselines, hardware, and metrics, we compare methods only where evaluation conditions permit and align results along common dimensions: functional correctness, execution performance, throughput, resource and energy consumption, operational outcomes, and optimization cost, keeping each result's native metric, baseline, and setting rather than collapsing them into a single score. This comparison reveals where LLM-driven methods yield consistent gains, how reported gains in workload orchestration depend on the evaluation environment, from simulators to real clusters and production, and where evidence is still lacking. Together, the taxonomy, lifecycle perspective, and quantitative evaluation provide an evidence-grounded reference for LLM-driven engineering of AI infrastructure.
  </details>

- **[How to make a moral agent: sentimentalism and rationalism as lenses on AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18714)**  `arXiv:2611.18714`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _David Gottlieb, Jared Moore_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can AI systems be moral agents? That is, can they undertake actions that can be correctly assessed as morally right or wrong? There is no settled normative criterion of moral agency that applies neatly outside paradigm human cases. Because of the difficulties with a normative account, we instead propose a research program to explore descriptive moral agency. Most basically, we ask, what are the capabilities we humans possess that underlie our moral agency? To do so, we draw on historical scholarship in philosophy and moral psychology to isolate candidate capabilities. Then we suggest refining candidate capabilities so that they are testable in the context of AI systems. Finally, we offer specific empirical recommendations to test AI systems, especially large language models (LLMs), for those refined capabilities. Our approach offers several benefits. Firstly, it may demonstrate whether the behaviors of AI systems are aligned, or in a broad sense desirable. Secondly, the debate may inform policy questions (e.g., what models should be deployed in which contexts?). Thirdly, our approach may complement engineering and design efforts: when capabilities can be precisely characterized, they can inform the development of models. Fourthly, applying our moral reasoning concepts in the new context of AI systems should improve our understanding of those concepts.
  </details>

- **[The Granularity Axis: A Micro-to-Macro Latent Direction for Social Roles in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06196)**  `arXiv:2605.06196`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Chonghan Qin, Xiachong Feng, Ziyun Song, Xiaocheng Feng, Jing Xiong, Lingpeng Kong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are prompted to adopt social roles from individuals to institutions, but whether their representations encode this difference in social granularity remains unclear. We discover a contrast-based Granularity Axis, the difference between mean macro- and micro-role hidden states, that aligns with the dominant geometry of role space: in Qwen3-8B, its cosine with PC1 is $0.972$, and PC1 explains $52.6\%$ of the variance. Across 75 roles at five levels, with 90,000 role-conditioned and 1,200 default responses per model, projections increase monotonically and remain stable across layers, prompts, and response-quality filters. The structure transfers to Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and scales to Qwen3-32B and Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct, whose PC1 shares are $45.99\%$ and $31.23\%$. Independent five-rater validation ($\alpha=0.961$) preserves the ordering; identical continuations and affect/formality controls confirm scale-related variation beyond generated wording. The axis is also behaviorally causal: positive steering moves Llama from $2.00$ to $3.17$ on a five-point macro scale for prompts admitting local responses. Steering strength and stability depend on the model's default operating regime. Together, these findings establish social granularity as a representational primitive: a continuous, ordered, causally manipulable direction organizing role-conditioned generation across model families.
  </details>

- **[Generative AI and the Crisis of Higher Education Assessments: Examining 'AI-Proofing' Strategies for College-Level Assessments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21476)**  `arXiv:2611.21476`  `cs.AI`  
  _Bonam Mingole, Davis Yadav, Hui Min Lee, S. Shyam Sundar, Amulya Yadav_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Higher education is confronting an academic-integrity challenge unlike any in its history. Students can now outsource homework, take-home assignments, and online examinations to large language models (LLMs) that complete them in seconds. Existing research on evaluating LLM performance on academic assessments has focused largely on standardized examinations, or on questions from college-level courses drawn from a single discipline. Far less is known about how LLMs perform on college-level assessment questions spanning multiple disciplines, that instructors have purposely designed to be AI-resistant, and about which features of such questions, if any, meaningfully constrain LLM performance. We address this gap by collecting 75 questions from authentic college coursework assessments, spanning 17 disciplines, which were contributed by 37 faculty members from ten U.S. universities and one U.K. university. Of these, 61 questions were intentionally designed by faculty members to be 'LLM-proof', while the remaining 14 questions were sourced from online courses. We then (i) evaluate three state-of-the-art LLMs' ability to correctly answer these questions with and without supplementary course materials; (ii) identify five strategies, through thematic analysis, that faculty used to make their questions harder for LLMs to solve; and (iii) test whether these strategies impact LLMs' ability to answer the questions accurately. Our experimental results show that the best-performing model scored approximately 83% without supplementary course materials and 85% with them, and we observed noteworthy differences across models, answer formats, and instructor tweaking strategies. We discuss implications for assessment design, academic-integrity policy, and the broader ethics of deploying LLMs in educational settings.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Reason with Predictive Gains for Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21441)**  `arXiv:2611.21441`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhuoyang Li, Teng Shi, Jiakai Tang, Weijie Yu, Guofu Xie, Xiao Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning-enhanced generative recommendation (GR) has attracted increasing attention for leveraging the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) to improve next-item prediction. However, existing methods primarily optimize reasoning through Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with rule-based rewards determined by final recommendation performance, which faces two key limitations: (i) unmeasured predictive contributions within reasoning, where the predictive contributions of individual reasoning steps are not directly reflected in optimization signals, and (ii) weakly discriminative learning signals within groups, where different reasoning trajectories within the group often receive indistinguishable rewards, limiting effective policy optimization. In this paper, we propose GainReason, a simple yet effective training framework that optimizes reasoning through Predictive Gain (PG). Specifically, PG measures changes in target Semantic ID (SID) log-likelihood induced by individual reasoning sentences, providing contribution-aware and discriminative rewards. We further theoretically establish the alignment between expected PG and the recommendation ranking objective. Building on this signal, we develop PG-based Reasoning Optimization (PGRO) to optimize reasoning generation with sentence-level rewards, supported by a custom causal attention mechanism for efficient PG computation. Additionally, an auxiliary PG-based Reasoning Utilization (PGRU) task adaptively strengthens reasoning-conditioned prediction to complement PGRO. Extensive experiments on three public benchmarks demonstrate that GainReason consistently outperforms competitive baselines and achieves substantial performance gains from reasoning.
  </details>

- **[VisualArtifactSpec 1.0: Evaluating Visual Artifacts under Task Specifications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21382)**  `arXiv:2611.21382`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shuyi Ouyang, Xiaojun Tang, Zijian Zou, Xintong Zhang, Mingli Song, Guannan Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual artifact tasks test agents' ability to translate task specifications and source materials into outputs that meet practical needs. Evaluation across artifact families helps distinguish broadly applicable authoring capabilities from strengths confined to a particular medium. We introduce VisualArtifactSpec 1.0 (VAS), a benchmark of 211 creation and modification tasks spanning presentations, static graphics, animations and videos, and web interfaces. Each task specifies authoring objectives, source materials, constraints, and deliverables. Existing benchmarks provide limited support for systematically evaluating how authoring choices suit different audiences, purposes, and media across artifact families. VAS evaluates two aspects separately: Task Fulfillment verifies explicit requirements and applicable functionality, while Artifact Performance assesses Content, Organization, Visuals, and Delivery. Task-specific rating anchors instantiate shared criteria for the intended audience, purpose, and medium, with judgments grounded in source materials and artifact inspection records. We evaluate nine model-harness configurations on all 211 tasks. Overall scores range from 42.12 to 70.52, with Artifact Performance accounting for 73.4% of the gap between the highest- and lowest-scoring configurations.
  </details>

- **[Turing Agent: Agentic Formal Verification for Mathematical Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21299)**  `arXiv:2611.21299`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yang Chen, Qianyi Wang, Ronghui Yang, Zhanhao Li, Yu Cong, Chen Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are rapidly advancing mathematical discovery, moving beyond benchmark-level problem solving to tackle frontier research problems. This progress is ushering mathematics into an era of proof abundance, raising a fundamental challenge: how can we ensure the correctness of the ever-growing volume of AI-generated proofs and transform them into trustworthy, machine-verified mathematical knowledge? We introduce Turing Agent, an agent harness designed to orchestrate end-to-end formal verification of research-level mathematics. Its wo-scale architecture integrates theorem formalization and proof formalization agents, empowered by structured proof decomposition, parallel verification, accumulated proof experience, and selective semantic review. Leveraging our collaborations with theoretical computer science (TCS) researchers, we focus our evaluation on TCS, a particularly demanding domain characterized by intricate quantification, complex dependencies, and stringent semantic requirements. Against direct generation and general-purpose coding harnesses, including Codex CLI and Claude Code, Turing Agent achieves approximately 57-62% faithful formalization success on Formal TCS Bench and 83% overall pass@1 on a TCS-focused subset of RLMEval, using GLM-5.3 and DeepSeek-V4-Pro. Notably, Turing Agent, built on DeepSeek, successfully completes the formal verification of three previously unresolved TCS tasks from OpenAI's October 2026 collection. One particularly challenging TCS problem required about a hundred formally verified auxiliary claims. Moreover, by actively exploring and refining the formalization process, Turing Agent can uncover simpler alternative proofs, expose unnecessary dependencies in original proofs, and generate reusable mathematical knowledge as a byproduct of verification.
  </details>

- **[CAE-Bench: Benchmarking AI Agents for Reusable CAE Solver Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21273)**  `arXiv:2611.21273`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiyu Zhou, Tianyou Liu, Xinping Yang, Yiyao Liu, Jiayi Dong, Yihan Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We developed CAE-Bench, a benchmark to evaluate AI agents on Computer-Aided Engineering (CAE) solver development. A CAE solver is more than a PDE solver; it must be able to generalize across various inputs, such as different boundary conditions, geometries, and material properties. CAE-Bench evaluates the generalizability of AI-developed CAE solvers by providing the same task, such as a heat transfer problem, multiple cases, with each case specifying an input. The AI agent develops a solver for a fixed task using only a few public cases, and the resulting solver is then tested on held-out cases, i.e., cases it has never seen before. We evaluate 12 models across a range of reasoning settings on 80 computational tasks. Our experiments show that even if a solver developed by an AI agent performs well on the public cases, it may even get 0 score on held-out cases. For tasks such as fluid dynamics problems, even the solver developed by GPT-6 Astra performs poorly on held-out cases. A data format and an SDK for task registration are provided, so the researcher can use the benchmark on their own infrastructure. We hope that CAE-Bench will help the community to develop AI agents that can develop reusable CAE solvers that generalize to various inputs.
  </details>

- **[InclusionMed: Benchmarking AI for Inclusive Healthcare](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21218)**  `arXiv:2611.21218`  `cs.AI`  
  _InclusionMed Collaboration_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Advancing medical AI for inclusive healthcare requires evaluation across diverse healthcare roles, tasks, and care settings, but benchmark coverage remains uneven. We introduce InclusionMed, an open medical AI evaluation initiative for inclusive healthcare. The InclusionMed Atlas organizes benchmarks across three healthcare roles and three AI capabilities. For the first release, we selected four public benchmarks from 388 candidates through structured audits and independent reproduction. With InclusionMed-DxFiles, our private diagnostic reasoning benchmark, the Atlas contains five benchmarks covering three of its nine role--capability cells. We evaluated frontier large language models on the Atlas and aggregated their results into the InclusionMed Index, with overall scores ranging from 17.5 to 49.1 out of 100. The Index also reports scores by healthcare role and AI capability, showing that a model's rank varied with the role or capability considered. A within-benchmark analysis using task-level tags showed that the ten highest-ranked models in the Index all scored higher on patient communication than on clinical decision-making. Models with similar Index scores also differed substantially in estimated inference cost. These findings highlight the need for evaluation covering more diverse healthcare needs and for context-specific model selection. Through open collaboration and community contributions, InclusionMed seeks to ensure that diverse healthcare needs worldwide shape how medical AI progress is defined and measured.
  </details>

- **[System 1 agents: a method for local, reliable and fast agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21053)**  `arXiv:2611.21053`  `cs.AI`  
  _Eleftherios Tzanis, Michail E. Klontzas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic artificial intelligence could automate analyses of sensitive clinical data, but current agents depend on large language models (LLMs). Cloud LLMs expose patient data. Local LLMs hallucinate, and an invented value in a tool call propagates through every later step. Here we present a method to build System 1 agents: local agents that call tools without generating text. A deterministic compiler turns each tool's schema and the request into multiple-choice questions whose options come from the request and the schema, then a one-pass decision model answers them. On 1,952 requests to 157 biomedical tools, the best System 1 agent chose the right tool, or correctly declined, for 97.0% of requests, significantly ahead of all 18 local LLM-agent settings (best 95.2%). The System 1 agent filled 97.0% of 6,056 stated argument values. No System 1 call contained invented content, against 5.5-19.7% of LLM calls. The System 1 Agents framework is freely available as open-source software atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[K2-Horizon: A Frontier Fleet of Open Source Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21003)**  `arXiv:2611.21003`  `cs.AI`  
  _K2 Team, Zhengzhong Liu, Haonan Li, Xuezhe Ma, Alexander Moreno, Subham Sekhar Sahoo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present K2-Horizon, a fleet of six fully open language models, from a 0.9B dense model to a 375B-A23B MoE, built for general-purpose reasoning and agentic work across math and science, software engineering, deep research, office automation, and beyond. The models lead or are competitive with strong open models of their size on agentic and coding benchmarks. On the modeling side, to strengthen reasoning, we introduce Mixture-of-Value Attention (MoVA), applying MoE to attention value projections, and softplus attention gating, a more flexible, theoretically motivated gate. Our 36B-A4B model uses both and nearly matches a 32B dense model trained on the same data, with one-seventh the active parameters. We also introduce Uno, lossless diffusion adapters that speed up decoding by up to 2.2x without a separate draft model. Our release follows the LLM360 definition of open source for transparency and reproducibility: the full development history, including intermediate checkpoints from every training stage, pre-training data, training logs, and all training, evaluation, and infrastructure code, notably our production-scale xLLM framework for dense and MoE training and the RL360 reinforcement-learning stack. Beyond the data itself, we release and document in detail every pipeline that built it, the part of model development that open releases most rarely disclose. These pipelines produced roughly 10T synthetic pre-training tokens, showing that synthetic data can be a major ingredient of pre-training at scale. Four of the models share one 22T-token pre-training corpus and recipe, revealing consistent scaling behavior and enabling controlled studies of scale and sparsity. All artifacts are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Razavi-Bench: Tracking AI Progress in Analog Circuit Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20935)**  `arXiv:2611.20935`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhishuai Zhang, Oscar Toomey, Taekwang Jang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose Razavi-Bench, a benchmark built from the 50 questions in Razavi's two Analog Design Experiments With AI articles and graded by GPT-6 Luna against reference answers and rubrics, and use it to track how fast AI models improve at analog circuit analysis. Across 73 multimodal large language models released between May 2024 and November 2026, the top score rose from 28.83% to 98.33% in 30 months, mostly before the reference answers were published. The leading models now answer almost every textbook question correctly; the hardest questions show a circuit that resembles a textbook one but works differently, or ask about behavior the obvious analysis misses. Within two and a half years, a set of questions that exposed the limits of AI models has become one they answer almost perfectly.
  </details>

- **[Multi-LLM Agentic AI at the Edge: Joint Agent Scheduling and Resource Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20915)**  `arXiv:2611.20915`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuanyan Song, Kezhi Wang, Xinmian Xu, Yongmin Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, an edge-enabled multi-large language model (LLM) agentic AI architecture is proposed, where one supervisor AI agent and multiple worker AI agents cooperatively execute users' requests at the edge. The supervisor AI agent decouples the user request into a series of sub-tasks and dynamically dispatches them to LLM-equipped worker AI agents via two agentic workflows, i.e., parallel and serial workflows, with a two-level retry mechanism to enhance execution reliability. To prolong the service duration of worker AI agents, the energy minimization problems are formulated by jointly considering AI agent selection and resource allocation, subject to task execution latency and quality of service (QoS) constraints. A resource allocation scheme based on alternating optimization (AO) is proposed. Specifically, for the parallel workflow, the scheme decouples the original problem into bandwidth allocation, retrieval selection and AI agent selection sub-problems, which are iteratively solved by the proposed successive convex approximation (SCA), Lagrangian relaxation (LR), and Gale-Shapley (GS) or LLM-based algorithms, respectively. For the serial workflow, the problem is similarly decoupled into service time allocation, retrieval selection and AI agent selection sub-problems; the optimal service time allocation is obtained via Karush-Kuhn-Tucker (KKT) conditions, while the latter two sub-problems are addressed by extending the proposed algorithms to the serial scenario. Simulation results show that the proposed resource allocation scheme can reduce system energy consumption compared with other benchmarks, while experiments on a multi-LLM agentic AI system based on Qwen and Llama demonstrate improved task execution success rates.
  </details>

- **[Conversing with a Conversational Agent: A Real Conversation, Not a Simulated One](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20826)**  `arXiv:2611.20826`  `cs.AI`  
  _Christophe Denis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The claim that interactions with artificial conversational agents merely simulate human conversation rests on a philosophical distinction between reproducing the observable features of an activity and actually performing it. Although this distinction is appropriate for certain physical phenomena, its application to linguistic and symbolic activities is far less straightforward. This article argues that conversations with large language model (LLM)-based agents are genuine conversational activities rather than mere simulations, without thereby attributing consciousness, subjective understanding, or human intentionality to artificial systems. Drawing on the distinction between subjective and objective simulation, the article first examines the limitations of defining conversation exclusively through the intrinsic properties of its participants. It then develops a relational and semiotic account of conversational activity, informed by linguistic pragmatics, Lacan's Conversing with a Conversational Agent theory of the mirror, Baudrillard's concept of the simulacrum, Simondon's philosophy of individuation, and Peirce's semiotics. These perspectives make it possible to distinguish the reality of conversational interaction from the appearance of artificial subjectivity and from the epistemic validity of the meanings produced. The argument culminates in the concept of Hybrid Semiotic Fabrication, which characterizes meaning-making as a distributed process involving human participants, artificial intelligence, corpora, and technical arrangements. Recognizing the reality of human-AI conversation therefore entails neither anthropomorphizing artificial agents nor granting their outputs epistemic authority. Rather, it requires a more precise account of the relational, semiotic, and normative conditions under which conversational meaning is produced and justified.
  </details>

- **[Regularizing Treatment Leakage for LLM-based Uplift Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20794)**  `arXiv:2611.20794`  `cs.AI`  
  _Bojing Feng, Jinqiu Deng, Zihan Hong, Yuan Xue, Wenqi Xiang, Shuhan Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Uplift modeling estimates the conditional average treatment effect (CATE) and supports industrial decisions such as promotional pricing. When large language models (LLMs) encode product text, treatment-revealing content can introduce shortcuts that do not transfer to deployment. We propose \emph{LLM-based Uplift Leakage Regularization} (\textsc{LULR}), which uses a CLUB-based penalty on the causal head's trainable shared representation while keeping the LLM encoder frozen. Controlled proxy experiments show a positive ranking-score interaction and higher scores than the tested weight-decay configuration; treatment-dependent evaluation limits their interpretation to exploratory model behavior. Structured-only comparisons provide reference performance, without establishing a general advantage over tree-based estimators. On a million-record industrial pricing RCT, \textsc{LULR} improves offline $\text{lift@}0.3$ in $4/5$ seeds, although the difference is not statistically significant and estimated policy values are near-tied. A separate one-week online A/B experiment records $5.45\%$ higher sales volume and $1.91\%$ higher GMV than the control arm. These aggregate results provide short-term business evidence for the complete deployed pricing policy; they do not isolate the regularizer's contribution.
  </details>

- **[GainCtrl: Predicting Remaining Gains for Efficient LLM Agent Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20736)**  `arXiv:2611.20736`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kangwen Zhao, Jianfeng Cai, Ruopei Sun, Mingxiao Feng, Peng Zhang, Wengang Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are widely used to power agents that interact with tools and environments to solve complex tasks. Repeated attempts improve the quality of tool-using agents, but continued execution incurs additional costs even when it is unlikely to improve the best result ultimately returned. This raises the question of when to stop an attempt to reduce execution cost while preserving task-level Best-of-N quality. To address this question, we introduce GainCtrl, a gain-based execution controller for LLMs. In GainCtrl, a lightweight CPU predictor uses interaction histories to estimate expected positive gains over retained scores, which are then used to guide stopping decisions with calibrated thresholds to limit the loss in mean Best-of-N task quality. We evaluate the controller through trajectory replay across three environments and three LLM actors. Comparisons of interpolated quality-cost Pareto frontiers show that GainCtrl generally uses fewer tokens than baselines at matched target quality. Fixed policies also achieve quality close to that of full execution while substantially reducing token consumption. The predictor runs at millisecond latency on a single CPU thread, supporting repeated monitoring without access to LLM internals.
  </details>

- **[Routing Beyond Task Categories: A Cost-Aware LAB-Bench Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20732)**  `arXiv:2611.20732`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ahmed Tariq, Leo Guinan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We tested whether features of biology questions or of a model's first answer can improve accuracy within an inference budget. In a LAB-Bench pilot, we compared a category router with one that adds six structural features. Neither reached the primary 80% accuracy target on development. At the 50% and 60% secondary targets, both chose Haiku for all 120 held-out questions and scored 71/120. We then froze a new comparison before collecting responses on 144 additional questions. Semantic routing scored 86/144. A policy that could defer after reading Haiku's answer scored 87/144. Both stayed within the inference budget of USD 0.678 per 1,000 queries. Against a 95% Haiku and 5% GPT mixture, the accuracy gains were 0.66 percentage points for semantic routing and 1.35 points for deferral. The paired 95% intervals were [-0.28,2.25] and [-0.14,3.47] points. A simple rule that escalated Haiku nonanswers scored 92/144 at USD 0.710 per 1,000 queries, above the budget. A retrospective nonanswer rule with a calibration-selected escalation probability gave an expected 85.63/144. At the learned deferral policy's observed inference cost, post hoc interpolation gave an expected 86.14/144. In a later development check, Astra and Gemini each scored 30/40. The best tested policy in each router family matched that score. The pair covered 33/40 in hindsight. The routers missed the 32/40 criterion, so the proposed 120-question evaluation did not start.
  </details>

- **[GEAR$^2$: Pruned Experts Live On through Gated Reuse of Activated Experts and a Missing-Mass Residual](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20616)**  `arXiv:2611.20616`  `cs.AI`  
  _Linliang Chen, Yan Zhong, Wang Kang, Sai Li, Ji Wu, Ji Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) Large Language Models (LLMs) activate only a few experts per token while generally keeping all the experts in GPU memory for high efficiency. Expert pruning reduces this GPU memory footprint cost, yet discards the contribution of pruned experts. Existing remedies either rely on static substitutes, merge experts at the cost of altering their functions, or require costly recovery training. In this paper, we find that the functionalities of pruned experts can be efficiently reconstructed from the activated remaining experts. We propose GEAR (Gated Expert Activation Reuse), which reuses the scaled outputs of reliable activated experts without any training or additional expert computation. In addition, we propose GEAR$^2$ to further recover what reuse cannot express with an ultra-lightweight residual. On five MoE LLMs from 16B to 106B parameters with 25% and 50% of experts pruned, GEAR improves over its training-free counterparts in nearly every setting (up to 4 pp.) in terms of accuracy, and GEAR$^2$ outperforms baseline approaches (up to 2.75 pp.) in every setting on nine zero-shot benchmarks, with larger advantages (up to 2.72 pp. higher) under more aggressive pruning (up to 50%).
  </details>

- **[ToolGraph as a Q-Function for Test-Time Scaling of Tool-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20569)**  `arXiv:2611.20569`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ke Shen, Ankita Bhaumik, Li Dong, Shengjie Liu, Shuzhen Li, Naveed Ahmed Janvekar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-use agents must chain interdependent calls, yet which outputs satisfy which later inputs in a deployed catalog is not something a general-purpose language model reliably infers from tool descriptions alone. We compile that structure once into a reusable external representation. ToolGraph is a directed, weighted dependency graph over a tool catalog: parameter matching and interventional probes, which execute a candidate chain with and without its proposed predecessor, propose edges whose evidence is combined into an edge confidence. Prior work uses such graphs to retrieve tools, search for tool paths, or score the next call. We ask instead whether the same structure can supply a \emph{multi-step} value by composing graph paths into a goal-conditioned action value \(Q(s,a,g)\). We study three estimators (dynamic programming over entity sets, a GraphSAGE estimator distilled from it, and an LLM scorer conditioned on graph evidence) plus a verbalizer that returns action--goal feedback to the agent, which keeps final action selection. Across two tool-use benchmarks and three backbones, we compare value ranking against personalized-PageRank retrieval over the identical graph, and against direct and reflective agents. Composition pays off only when the estimator also reads the concrete state: the LLM estimator improves on PageRank in every benchmark--backbone setting, by \(8.3\) points on average, while the two purely structural estimators fall below it, because a value defined over tool types cannot separate a correct tool called with a wrong argument. Graph construction is a one-time cost; we report it separately rather than assume it amortized away (at our scale it is comparable to request-time cost) and sweep four knobs controlling how much graph evidence to expose against success and tokens.
  </details>

- **[Agent-Guided Configuration Tuning for SMT Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20484)**  `arXiv:2611.20484`  `cs.AI`  
  _Cong Wang, Qiaoyi Zheng, Zhaoguo Wang, Fan Long_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  SMT solvers expose many configuration choices that determine how well their existing algorithms perform on a workload. We present, to our knowledge, the first workflow that uses general-purpose LLM agents to tune SMT solver configurations. Agents read solver documentation, source code, and persistent experiment records to propose option changes, and a deterministic harness measures each proposal, validates the returned models, and selects changes separately for each competition Division. Over roughly nine weeks, our tuning campaign recorded 4,545 proposals for cvc5 across 13 Divisions and 36 logics.We evaluate the tuned configurations on the 2026 and 2025 SMT-COMP ModelValidation benchmarks under a 24-second cutoff. On problems not used for training, they validate more problems than official cvc5 in ten of the 13 Divisions in 2026 and in eight in 2025, with geometric-mean gains of 7.8% and 6.8% across Divisions. On the complete official selections, which include training problems, the tuned floating-point counts exceed those of every individual competition participant in both years. Further studies show how configuration changes make existing algorithms effective by changing access to strategies, scheduling, and option compatibility, and how benchmark families trade off against one another. The agents' experiment traces contain hypothesis-based proposals in every tuning group, and 64.3% of the extracted hypotheses draw on prior measurements. These results show that agent-guided tuning can specialize a mature SMT solver and that accumulated experimental evidence informs sustained search.
  </details>

- **[FinVerify: A Verification Framework for Financial Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20423)**  `arXiv:2611.20423`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mao Guan, Qian Chen, Ge Meng, Xiao-Yang Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Financial agents produce numerical claims that can be wrong even when their calculations execute correctly, due to mismatched reporting periods, unsupported sources, incompatible units, or incorrect financial definitions. We introduce FinVerify, an evidence-grounded framework for verifying financial agents' numerical claims. Based on an analysis of 190 incorrect trajectories from the Agent Finance Reasoning dataset, we define five error categories--temporal integrity, source fidelity, comparability, accounting integrity, and financial-model fidelity--and operationalize them as verification protocols. An LLM structures claims and supporting evidence, while deterministic validators check financial requirements; when authoritative formulas are available, Z3 checks consistency with grounded inputs. FinVerify accepts only if all applicable checks pass, rejects proven violations, and abstains when evidence is insufficient. We evaluate the framework against a Direct baseline and an LLM judge across five fixed-candidate benchmarks. On Agent Finance Reasoning, accepted-claim precision increases from 56.4% for the LLM judge to 76.7% at 81.2% coverage. On XBRLFiling, FinVerify achieves 99.8% accepted-claim precision and 97.0% decision accuracy; on FinanceBench-67, it reaches 87.9% accepted-claim precision versus 69.7% for the judge. Improvements are smaller on FinQA and TAT-QA, where authoritative evidence is limited. These results suggest that explicit, evidence-backed verification can improve financial-agent reliability while exposing trade-offs between correctness, false rejection, and abstention.
  </details>

- **[Deploying a Control-Logic LLM Assistant in Air-Gapped Power Plants: Lessons in Source Authority and Bounded Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20398)**  `arXiv:2611.20398`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yiwei Fu, Nicholas Gabriel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deploying Large Language Model (LLM) assistants in industrial operational technology environments poses constraints largely absent from cloud applications: site policies restrict engineering and operational data from leaving the plant, on-premises compute is limited, and incorrect causal attribution can mislead incident response. We report lessons from deploying a control-logic evidence layer that underpins a broader on-premises operator assistance system at two gas power plants. Two prototypes revealed where generative models added avoidable uncertainty and cost. The first used a Vision Language Model (VLM) to reconstruct control topology from PDF printouts routinely exchanged by engineers. Across the full 6,979-page document, extraction reached 0.51 connection precision and required 3.1 hours on 4xH200 GPUs; consultation with controls engineers then revealed a native structured export from the authoring system. The second used an LLM tool-calling agent to plan repeatedly over eight predefined graph operations. In a controlled benchmark of 40 template-generated questions, replacing this agent with explicit execution plans reduced mean token use by 20x and median latency by 3x, with identical retrieved node sets across repeated runs. The deployed design ingests the authoritative export into a versioned knowledge graph, executes bounded graph operations in fixed software, and confines the local LLM to fallback intent classification and evidence-grounded explanation. At one site, the graph contains 221K nodes across seven controllers. Our experience suggests a practical division of labor for high-consequence AI: LLMs interpret natural-language requests and explain results, while deterministic software constructs and retrieves technical evidence grounded in authoritative sources. Abstention, entity disambiguation, graph versioning, and read-only integration provide additional safeguards.
  </details>

- **[Towards Agentic Scientific Discovery in Distributed Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20031)**  `arXiv:2611.20031`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rashmita Soni, Abhirup Ghosh, Soumya Banerjee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We investigate when adaptive tool selection helps identify a shared equation across clients with different input coverage. Query-ATLAS (query-conditioned Agentic Tool-based Learning for Aggregating Symbolic Equations) uses an LLM to guide symbolic search from client errors and previous proposals in a simulated distributed system. A four-architecture comparison reveals task-dependent opportunities: relative to fixed PySR-LLM, validation acceptances rise from 3/12 to 7/12 on CRK3 and from 11/12 to 12/12 on oscillator2, while MatSci3 favors the fixed workflow. Across 72 settings in a post-hoc six-task subset of eight development tasks, Query-ATLAS achieves 64 validation and 64 in-distribution passes, versus 62 and 59 for the fixed workflow; both pass 47/60 eligible out-of-distribution tests. The fixed workflow retains lower median errors and cost. Broader query coverage emerges as a promising benefit, motivating task-aware tool selection and client-aware fitting for scientific agents.
  </details>

- **[When Facts Change, Which Decisions Break? Dependency-Aware Context Graphs for Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19859)**  `arXiv:2611.19859`  `cs.AI`  
  _Paul Deepakraj Retinraj_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When an observed fact changes, an agent must identify and repair the decisions that depend on it while preserving unaffected outputs. We investigate the reliability-cost trade-off of field-level dependency graphs extracted from actual agent read traces. A controlled benchmark spans three executable workflow domains and four successive updates, with independent imperative and expression-based references. Across 120 workflows, two models and two repeated runs, we compare full recomputation, rolling summary memory, lexical retrieval, trace-extracted dependencies and verified dependency upper bounds. Trace-derived graphs reduce reviewed decisions and estimated total cost, but omitted alternative supports and missing repair context can reduce complete-plan correctness. Paired ablations isolate the effects of edge deletion, spurious edges, transitive propagation and context scope. An exploratory enhancement supplies the existing record ID/field directory during extraction without revealing unread values or reference edges.
