We evaluate this enhancement on 60 fresh workflows with two repeats per model. On this fresh set, the directory-aware graph matches observed full-context complete-plan correctness for gpt-6.1-sol (100%) with an estimated 4.0% total-cost reduction, but reaches only 84.8% versus 99.6% for gpt-6-luna. The results distinguish potential dependency impact from actual decision invalidity and show why selective repair must be evaluated with both executable correctness and full memory-processing cost. The evidence concerns explicit procedural workflows; broad real-world agent-memory generalization remains untested.
  </details>

- **[Read Before You Fit: Text-Derived Constraints for Discovering Differential Equations from Noisy Biological Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19779)**  `arXiv:2611.19779`  `cs.AI`  
  _Pronoma Banerjee, Md. Masudur Rahman, Juan P. Wachs_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly used for scientific equation discovery. However, most existing methods are strongly data-driven and assume sufficiently clean and representative observations. This assumption is problematic in biological systems, where individual-level measurements are often sparse, noisy, heterogeneous, and expensive to obtain. While the underlying mechanisms are extensively described in scientific text, they are not explicitly used for the LLM based modeling of such systems. We introduce P2C, an agentic framework that translates textual descriptions of biological systems into a typed set of formal constraints, and use these constraints to restrict candidate ordinary differential equation (ODE) structures before numerical fitting. P2C consists of five stages: Text2Constraint extraction, ODE discovery, symbolic verification, parameter fitting, and model scoring. We evaluate P2C on four published ODE systems of acute inflammation and immune regulation, under clean and noisy observations. By progressively degrading the numerical evidence, we isolate when textual domain knowledge contributes beyond regression alone. Across the four systems, adding Text2Constraint reduces the worst form error from 1.397 to 0.499 on clean data and from 13.096 to 1.095 for data perturbed with Gaussian noise of $\sigma=0.1$; P2C also achieves lower form error and trajectory error than several LLM-based baselines over the tested noise range.
  </details>

- **[Data-Driven Heuristic-Guided Business Process Redesign](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19623)**  `arXiv:2611.19623`  `cs.AI`  
  _Nan Sai, Karolin Winter, Remco Dijkman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Business process redesign (BPR) has a long-standing catalog of best-practice redesign heuristics, yet, applying them remains a mostly manual task that requires extensive domain knowledge. Despite recent advances using Large Language Models (LLMs) for conversational process model redesign, limitations remain. For example, redesign is user-driven instead of data-driven and is not guided by best practices such as the redesign heuristics. Moreover, only one change can be handled at a time. To address these limitations, given an event log and a BPMN model, we present the DH-BPR framework to realize data-driven heuristic-guided BPR using LLMs. To design DH-BPR, we first conducted an analysis of which redesign heuristics can be automatically detected and applied given information contained in an event log such as the fraction of cases that bypass an activity. Based on this, DH-BPR detects and validates heuristic candidates through a combination of rule-based techniques and an LLM. After that, DH-BPR jointly designs and records an application plan for valid candidates, before finally yielding a redesigned BPMN model and an updated resource model (the way resources execute the process). Evaluation based on simulation using a synthetic benchmark and two real-world event logs shows that the framework's redesign reduces the mean case completion time by up to 61.5%.
  </details>

- **[Continuation-Guided LLM Evolution of Program Policies and Goal Compilers in Craftax](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19537)**  `arXiv:2611.19537`  `cs.AI`  
  _Víctor Gallego_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents can synthesize and optimize code-based policies from simulator feedback, allowing the resulting program to act with zero inference model calls. But for long horizon tasks, two challenges arise: local improvements can hurt final success and switching to a new objective requires another expensive synthesis run. We propose a framework that first develops competence by refining the policy through a feedback cascade, moving from episode returns to hierarchical goals and belief updates which can be scored densely from simulator. In the Craftax game, a partially observed and procedurally generated environment, refinements via episode level feedback stall at <1% final-level arrival, whereas with the feedback cascade the optimized policy reaches the final stage 50% of time (28% final boss defeats). Furthermore, to make this competence reusable towards new goals, we factor the previous controller into an environment interpreter $I$ (models, memory, skills) and a rule program $p_0$, and we show that coding agents can optimize a reusable goal compiler $C_{\theta}$ that maps structured tasks $g$ into new rule programs $p_g$. Across five independent compiler searches trained only on short tasks, the resulting compilers successfully transfer zero-shot to longer tasks without any task-specific tuning nor inference-time LLM calls: continuation-scored feedback and a fixed machine with an editable program together turn a single-task competent policy into a reusable one.Code at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Program Distillation: Transferring LLM Capability from System 2 to System 0](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19461)**  `arXiv:2611.19461`  `cs.AI`  
  _Pujun Zheng, Wanying Ren, Guoxiu He_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent studies leverage large language models (LLMs) to construct executable programs through environment interaction, feedback analysis, and code revision. However, prior work has mainly treated LLMs as optimizers, overlooking their ability to transfer knowledge across system levels. We propose Program Distillation (PD), a framework that formalizes the transfer of abilities from System 2 to System 0. During learning, the LLM provides task knowledge and draft strategies; at deployment, a student program executes the resulting policy. We show that this approach can improve performance while reducing inference time. We introduce PDBench, a benchmark comprising 27 discrete- and continuous-control tasks with fixed initial policies, and PDMetaHarness, a model-harness-independent meta-system for monitoring and guiding the learning process. The strongest model achieves a normalized mean score of 0.728, surpassing PPO (0.656) and Jev-like decision models (0.239), while running exclusively on CPUs and reducing inference costs. Our analysis shows that performance is largely driven by the first breakthrough during learning, and structural modifications have a slightly lower success rate than parameter modifications but typically yield larger gains. We further introduce style distillation, which enables interpretable stylistic control over the learning process. Overall, we present an analytical framework for PD and demonstrate its practical viability.
  </details>

- **[Does a completed simulation run mean a usable urban wind result for AI agents?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19385)**  `arXiv:2611.19385`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhongke Qu, Yunwei Zhang, Junwei Su, Yabin Zhang, Chengwei Li, Qingxiang Shui, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents based on large language models (LLMs) can now configure and run computational fluid dynamics (CFD) solvers, but a completed run does not ensure that delivered metrics can be recomputed from raw outputs. This gap was examined for urban wind simulation, where multi-case comparisons must hold non-target conditions fixed and reported values must be traceable to raw samples. UrbanWind-AgentBench, comprising 200 tasks and 367 cases in 10 engineering task families, was constructed. Public task contracts specify variable factors, fixed conditions, numerical thresholds and deliverable requirements, and two-layer scoring separates a structural score for deliverables, run provenance and raw evidence from a numerical score obtained by independent recomputation against references. WindPilot was then proposed as an execution framework that incorporates contract parsing, execution provenance, deterministic verification and bounded acceptance review into the LLM execution loop. On 30 OpenFOAM tasks with independent references, at least the structural maximum of 60 points was reached by a base agent on 28 tasks, but numerical credit was obtained on only 6. With the same model name and budget, WindPilot increased the number of tasks with numerical credit to 17, full-score tasks from 0 to 3 and the mean task score from 62.5 to 72.5. Mean action steps fell from 130.4 to 104.9, and mean token consumption decreased by 12.9%. The mean gain for atmospheric boundary layer (ABL) tasks was 18.5 points. A completed simulation run is therefore not sufficient for a usable urban wind result; usability depends on checks that tie reported metrics to raw evidence during execution.
  </details>

- **[Zero-Shot Weight-Space Programming for Prediction and Classification of Target Variables in Structured Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19292)**  `arXiv:2611.19292`  `cs.AI`  
  _Eduardo C. Garrido-Merchán_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Weight-space programming fixes every parameter of a feed-forward network by hand, with no gradient descent anywhere in the construction. Every demonstration so far starts from a target function handed to the model as a rule system, making it a compilation problem. A supervised dataset withholds that specification, so the procedure must do the statistical inference itself and then express the result in the parameterisation it was given. We ask whether a large language model can do both. It is shown a small dataset in full, with the architecture of a feed-forward network, and writes out every weight and bias as a literal number, with no optimizer, no gradient, no code execution, and no chance to evaluate it first. Each construction is sealed and pre-registers its expected score. The graded suite runs from two toy problems, through nine synthetic problems disguised by a hidden rotation, to the classical benchmarks and one real engineering regression. The frozen network matches the closed-form optimum wherever that optimum is linear, reaches the irreducible error of the generating process on three rungs where it is not, and beats every fitted baseline on two of those, including the same architecture trained by gradient descent. It fails on two rungs, both times because the structure to be found is oblique to the coordinates it analysed. Three further constructors separate two failure modes a single score conflates: one returns no parameter vector at all on the hardest rungs, another delivers everywhere while writing networks worse than predicting the mean. One of those failures proves avoidable: another constructor solves the oblique-hinge rung by a margin that survives multiple-comparison correction. What predicts whether the hidden layer earns its keep is the training error of a linear separator, which the composite complexity index misses.
  </details>

- **[Agentic On-Policy Distillation Implicitly Reshapes LLMs' ReAct Patterns](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19244)**  `arXiv:2611.19244`  `cs.AI`  
  _Deyang Kong, Zhaoyi Li, Sijian Ren, Yuan Wei, Ying Wei, Defu Lian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) transfers specialized capabilities to language-model agents, but its cross-domain effects on interaction remain unclear. We investigate how OPD reshapes ReAct interaction patterns across mathematics, ALFWorld, and $\tau^2$-bench, focusing on reasoning, actions, and observation acquisition. General-reasoning supervision reduces mathematical tool attempts from 70.4% to 14.4% while increasing accuracy from 16.7% to 38.0%; code-interpreter supervision improves household actions and retail queries. In contrast, ALFWorld supervision reduces $\tau^2$-bench accuracy from 41.5% to 6.6%, accompanied by frequent tool calls that violate user-information dependencies. To address negative transfer using only source-domain training trajectories, we explore selective OPD guided by teacher preference changes before and after domain-specific RL. Retaining the top 95% of positions raises $\tau^2$ accuracy from 11.5% to 29.9% with a source-learning trade-off.
  </details>

- **[Auditable Authorization for LLM Agents: Empirical Evidence Across Model, Harness, and Workflow Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19237)**  `arXiv:2611.19237`  `cs.AI`  
  _Bangjie Deng, Xiaoxuan Lu, Wenjun Chen, Chengge Wen, Yulin Wang, Jiayue Meng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human approval provides effective control only if rejected work stops and authorized work completes. We formalize refusal safety and bounded authorization completeness, and audit three execution boundaries: the model selects a call, the harness schedules and resumes it, and the workflow routes the decision to a protected effect. Paired decisions, exact traces, and native business-state witnesses support experiments with three models, five prompt arms, four runtime approval designs, two additional framework replays, and 100 public workflow sources.The paired model panel leaves seven of 300 approvals unfinished despite zero denied effects; parallel confirmation loses an approved branch in three tasks per model, restoration omits 336/672 CrewAI approvals, and four of 59 comparable n8n workflows execute after Reject. Comparing intent, generated calls, dispatch, and native effects identifies the first observable divergence and its repair target. This yields a practical audit contract that makes both refusal safety and authorization completeness independently verifiable, converting human approval from a procedural formality into an enforceable control boundary between intent and real-world effect.
  </details>

- **[CompoundingKernel: Self-Improving from Zero to First](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19230)**  `arXiv:2611.19230`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qian Qu, Hongyang Wang, Wenbo Chen, Yichuan Li, Jingguo Liu, Xian Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present CompoundingKernel, a self-improving agent system that writes GPU kernels by search. As of 2026-11-11, its generated kernels rank #1 on the public L1, L2 and Quantization boards of SOL-ExecBench (NVIDIA B200). They have held #1 on L1 since 2026-10-27, albeit a short loss on 2026-11-07. All 235 problems have a site-verified correct kernel; on the 209 public-board problems they are 4.40x faster than the benchmark's reference baseline. The search starts from zero: agents get each problem's PyTorch definition but no reference kernels or official scores. A per-problem archive outside the model holds every correct kernel and its lineage. An LLM controller reads it, picks a parent or a new design, and gives each parallel child agent one direction. Checks outside the model decide correctness. At equal wall time, it beat a coded UCB style tree on 35 of 38 problems by making 2.4x as many correct attempts. Over a thousand agents ran at once, and one warm restart fix raised their evaluation rate 3.6x. We find that the rate of reliable measurements, not the number of agents, limits how far such a search scales. Our code and kernels are available on URL.
  </details>

- **[STaSBO: LLM-Guided Strategy Planning and Configuration Selection for Bayesian Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19214)**  `arXiv:2611.19214`  `cs.AI`  
  _Youngseok Yoon, Peng Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bayesian optimization (BO) enables sample-efficient search for expensive black-box problems, but its performance depends strongly on surrogate-acquisition configurations whose suitability varies across problems and optimization states. We take a significant step toward LLM-driven strategic management of BO with STaSBO, a task-agnostic meta-controller that plans search behavior and jointly selects surrogate-acquisition configurations, while numerical BO retains responsibility for probabilistic modeling and candidate generation. STaSBO comprises two coordinated stages: strategy planning and strategy-guided configuration selection. A structured memory system connects prior plans, configuration selections, and candidate behaviors to optimization states, allowing the controller to revise its trajectory summary and behavioral plans during planning. To navigate the large joint configuration space, the selection stage shortlists configurations using surrogate fit evidence. Prospective BO-action diagnostics then expose the candidate behaviors, supporting final joint selection against the behavioral plans. Across 12 synthetic mathematical and 10 scientific and engineering application tasks, STaSBO achieves the best average rank against 11 baselines: 2.17 and 2.60, compared with 4.08 and 4.10 for the strongest baseline in each suite, respectively.
  </details>

- **[What Moves a LoCoMo Score? Memory Context, Answer Prompts and Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19199)**  `arXiv:2611.19199`  `cs.AI`  
  _Nikhil Kumar Jha, Amitoj Cheema_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LoCoMo is a widely reported benchmark for long-term conversational memory, but published scores mix memory quality with choices of answer prompt, judge and baseline layout that are rarely reported together. We measure how much these choices move a score, and evaluate Becos, a team memory system for coding agents, as a black box under all of them: ten conversations (1,540 questions), two answer models (gpt-4o-mini, gpt-4.1-mini), two answer prompts and three LLM judges, against a whole-conversation baseline rerun under identical conditions. Changing only the judge moves a score by up to 7.2 points and changing only the answer prompt by up to 9.3. A blind human check of 150 answers finds the judges reliable when they agree, and the primary judge's errors on disputed answers working against Becos rather than for it. Becos answers from a context with 90% fewer characters than the whole conversation. Its configuration was developed on LoCoMo itself, so there is no held-out set. With gpt-4o-mini answers it scores clearly higher than the whole conversation (79.7 vs 73.0 with the short prompt, paired 95% interval for the difference +3.8 to +9.5; 88.8 vs 77.5 with a step-by-step prompt). With gpt-4.1-mini answers the two score about the same (83.2 vs 83.1; 92.5 vs 92.1). We release every answer and verdict and the scoring script.
  </details>

- **[Agent-Guided Process Verification for Mathematical Self-Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19122)**  `arXiv:2611.19122`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rui Zou, Mengqi Wei, Xin Zhao, Ji-Rong Wen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models have made substantial progress in mathematical reasoning, yet in complex multi-step reasoning, an error at any step may propagate along an incorrect path, and the resulting solution may omit necessary branches. Existing natural-language reflection methods often struggle to locate critical errors precisely, while outcome verification typically checks only whether the available candidate answers satisfy the problem conditions and can therefore overlook necessary branches. To address these limitations, we propose Agent-Guided Process Verification (AGPV) for mathematical self-correction. AGPV decomposes the current solution into an ordered sequence of key claims, verifies them individually while invoking a shared mathematical toolbox when needed, and stops upon detecting the first error. A summarization agent converts the failure evidence into a diagnosis that guides a correction agent to re-derive the subsequent solution from that point; AGPV then adapts the next verification iteration to the revised solution. AGPV also verifies solution completeness. We further prove that AGPV guarantees solution correctness and completeness when key claims provide sufficient coverage and local verification is reliable, provided that all key claims pass verification. The implementation compresses long working contexts and reuses successful tool results within each problem. We evaluate AGPV on five challenging mathematical reasoning datasets with four models from DeepSeek, Qwen, and GPT. AGPV achieves an average accuracy of 63.8%, outperforming the strongest among the representative baselines evaluated (50.3%) by 13.5 percentage points, with higher repair coverage than the programmatic-verification baselines.
  </details>

- **[Guiding AI Agents to Generate Conjectures by Encoding How Theorems Build on One Another](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19108)**  `arXiv:2611.19108`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hita Kambhamettu, Daniel Krashen, Lyle Ungar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recently, LLMs have been making headlines, and headway, in mathematical research. Much of this work focuses on proving existing open conjectures. Deciding what to prove is harder and less studied, because of the lack of a clear criterion for what makes a conjecture worth pursuing. We present a method for conjecture generation that builds on the observation that mathematical theorems build on one another: new theorems can modify the setting or hypotheses of earlier theorems, strengthen their conclusions, or extend their scope. We encode these relationships in a new kind of graph, the Theorem Evolution Graph (TEG), which we instantiate as an online search method for LLM agents. An agent builds a TEG in two steps: (1) identifying relevant theorems from papers found by following citations backward and forward from a seed paper; and (2) comparing those theorems to record how one builds on another. LLM judges and an expert mathematician preferred TEG-generated conjectures over those from an unconstrained baseline. LLM judges favored TEG conjectures for significance, ambition, and insight, and favored baseline conjectures for plausibility. Ablations suggest a benefit from constructing and using a TEG even when the available papers are fixed.
  </details>

- **[Policy Optimization With Multiple Verifiers via Pareto Rankings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19064)**  `arXiv:2611.19064`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kai Yamashita, Shohei Taniguchi, Hiroki Furuta, Masahiro Suzuki, Yutaka Matsuo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has become an important approach to improving the reasoning capabilities of large language models. In tasks such as code generation and instruction following, multiple verifiers evaluate whether a response satisfies different requirements. Their outcomes provide detailed feedback about partial success, yet this feedback is commonly aggregated into a scalar reward for policy optimization. Rewarding only complete success discards distinctions among partial solutions, while counts and weighted sums impose comparisons between responses that satisfy different subsets of requirements. We introduce Partial-Order Likelihood Policy Optimization (POLPO), which learns from the comparisons established by verification without first constructing an aggregate reward. Pareto dominance identifies responses that satisfy additional requirements without losing any already satisfied, while leaving responses with conflicting outcomes incomparable. POLPO extends the listwise likelihood underlying Direct Preference Optimization by maximizing the total probability of all complete rankings consistent with these partial orders. This objective provides supervision from verified improvements even before complete solutions are available, without requiring a prescribed ordering of incomparable responses. Experiments on code generation and instruction following demonstrate improved learning efficiency and higher rates of complete requirement satisfaction compared with scalar-reward training. These results support partial-order likelihood as an effective approach to learning from multiple verifiers.
  </details>

- **[Ask Precisely, Assume Wisely: Interactive Evaluation of Ambiguity Resolution in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18905)**  `arXiv:2611.18905`  `cs.AI`  
  _Avik Dutta, Aayush Kumar, Zahra Ashktorab, Hosein Hasanbeig, Austin Z. Henley, Gustavo Soares_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Ambiguity is a core challenge in human--AI interaction, where models must decide when to make assumptions and when to seek clarification. Existing approaches to ambiguity evaluation largely focus on coverage over multiple interpretations using static metrics, offering limited insight into how efficiently a model converges toward a single user-intended interpretation through interaction. We introduce a preference-grounded simulated user framework for controlled, multi-turn evaluation of ambiguity resolution, grounding simulator responses strictly in user disambiguation preferences to avoid hallucination and over-disclosure. Alongside this, we define three observable dissatisfaction signals --- incorrect assumptions, repeating clarifications, and irrelevant clarifications --- that characterize interaction quality beyond task accuracy. Our analysis reveals a clear trade-off: models that frequently seek clarification tend to over-clarify and prolong interactions, while assumption-driven models are more prone to misaligning with user intent. We demonstrate that system prompts optimized on these dissatisfaction signals achieve a Pareto-optimal balance across all three metrics, reducing overall error rates by up to 7.65%, underscoring the value of our framework in designing agents that ask precisely and assume wisely.
  </details>

- **[Skill Adapter: Personalized Modification and Online Routing of General-Purpose Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17007)**  `arXiv:2611.17007`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qijia Zhuang, Yanyin Gou, Wenbin Ma, Yuxiang Ren_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents use external skills to reuse task procedures and experience with tools. General-purpose skills describe shared execution procedures, while specific tool choices, operating practices, and output requirements vary across users. In long-horizon tasks, subgoals, available inputs, and environmental states change as execution progresses. Agents must select currently applicable skills from an expanding skill pool and prepare candidates for subsequent steps.We propose Skill Adapter, which enables personalized modification and online routing of general-purpose skills. The system distills local adjustments from user requirements and execution feedback and stores them as Adapters linked to general-purpose skills. The routing agent selects general-purpose skills and applicable Adapters based on the current subtask, execution progress, and environmental feedback, incrementally maintaining a task-level online skill library that the main LLM reads on demand. The system preloads candidates for subsequent nodes and rechecks their applicability once actual inputs are determined. Reinforcement learning optimizes the two types of decisions using modification gains and routing outcomes, respectively.We evaluate Skill Adapter on ALFWorld, WebShop, and SkillsBench. Compared with SkillRL, it improves task success rates by an average of 11.43 percentage points and reduces mean execution time on all three benchmarks. Skill Adapter retains its performance advantage when the skill pool grows to 1,000 skills.
  </details>

- **[Can MLLMs Reason over Surround-View Driving Videos? A Multi-View Autonomous Driving QA Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15225)**  `arXiv:2611.15225`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shunming Liu, Jianyi Peng, Cheng Li, Lu Hou, Pei Huang, Yajing Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Surround-view video understanding in autonomous driving is a crucial capability for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to achieve reliable driving reasoning. Real-world driving often requires information beyond the front-view camera. Existing MLLMs primarily focus on front-view information, while their spatio-temporal understanding and reasoning abilities remain unknown. To address this gap, we propose MVDriveBench, a high-information-density video QA benchmark for autonomous driving, characterized by synchronized surround-view inputs, spatio-temporal understanding, and reasoning-chain-oriented evaluation. We design 12 categories of QA tasks that cover the complete reasoning pipeline from low-level visual perception to high-level driving decision-making, enabling a systematic evaluation of MLLMs surround-view spatio-temporal understanding and reasoning capabilities in complex driving environments. We conduct a comprehensive evaluation of 23 open-source, closed-source, and expert MLLMs on MVDriveBench. The results show that current MLLMs still exhibit insufficient surround-view spatio-temporal reasoning ability. Meanwhile, closed-loop evaluations of specialist models in AlpaSim reveal a correlation between surround-view spatio-temporal reasoning capabilities and closed-loop driving performance, highlighting the importance of comprehensive multi-view understanding for reliable vehicle control.
  </details>

- **[Early Signals, Strong Decisions: Prefix-Guided Sampling for Parallel Test-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14965)**  `arXiv:2611.14965`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jie Ren, Jonathan Rosenfeld, Neil Thompson_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parallel test-time scaling (TTS) has been shown to enhance reasoning in large language models by generating and aggregating multiple independent reasoning paths. Internal confidence scores can further improve this process by identifying paths of varying quality. However, existing methods remain computationally inefficient, as low-quality paths are fully expanded before being evaluated. In this work, we aim to improve the use of internal confidence signals for better model performance and computational efficiency. We begin by offering a new perspective on the role of confidence in aggregation, showing that it is most impactful for questions near the decision boundary. Notably, when amplified by multi-sample aggregation, even small confidence signals derived from short prefixes can meaningfully influence outcomes in these boundary cases. Building on this insight, we introduce Prefix-Guided Sampling (PreG), a method that reallocates computation by first generating short prefixes, ranking them using internal confidence scores, and then completing only the most promising candidates. This strategy reduces token usage while maintaining gains in path quality. We provide theoretical analysis demonstrating that, under a fixed compute budget, PreG strengthens the effective signal by widening the decision boundary margin. Empirically, our method consistently outperforms standard parallel TTS approaches across benchmarks, while significantly improving token efficiency and reducing overall computation.
  </details>

- **[From Questions to Decisions: Benchmarking How LLM Agents Acquire Decision-Relevant Information from Human Beings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13015)**  `arXiv:2611.13015`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuhan Tang, Ke Fan, Xinkai Zou, Baichuan Mo, Kangxin Cui, Ao Qu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are increasingly asked to make plans for people, such as scheduling drivers or assigning nurses. However, people's requests are often incomplete, and the supporting information for decision-making may be outdated, requiring agents to ask for clarification. Existing benchmarks typically provide fully specified problems or rely on uncalibrated LLM-simulated users, leaving this ability insufficiently tested. We introduce Ask2Decide, a benchmark of 43 planning tasks that evaluates whether agents ask for missing information that matters for their decisions. An operator holds facts absent from the agent's request and records, and optimization solvers determine which facts change the optimal plan. We also include similarly worded facts that leave the optimal plan unchanged to test whether agents distinguish useful information from irrelevant details. We collect 79 interaction sessions from 19 participants and find that, after an incorrect plan, human replies omit the correcting fact far more often than replies from an uncalibrated simulated user (43% versus 7%). Using these interactions, we calibrate a simulated operator that more closely matches human reply patterns. Evaluating four frontier LLMs, we find that their initial plans are infeasible on 58-98% of tasks when information is missing, compared with 5-28% when all facts are provided upfront. Agents uncover facts that change the optimal plan only slightly more often than facts that leave it unchanged (39-52% versus 32-41%). These results show that obtaining decision-relevant information through interaction remains a major bottleneck for LLM planning. This is ongoing work and will be updated. The date of this version is shown on the first page.
  </details>

- **[BiVerify: Closing the Loop Between Backward and Forward Verification for Mathematical Self-Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11368)**  `arXiv:2611.11368`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rui Zou, Xin Zhao, Mengqi Wei, Ji-Rong Wen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have made substantial progress in mathematical reasoning but still produce incorrect solutions to complex problems, indicating the need for more reliable self-correction mechanisms. Mathematical self-correction typically identifies errors through verification, which can proceed backward or forward. The two directions provide complementary verification capabilities. Backward verification checks a candidate answer against the original constraints, bypassing complex intermediate reasoning, but cannot ensure correct reasoning or objective completeness. Forward verification follows the problem conditions and current solution, enabling it to locate reasoning errors and assess objective completeness, but may overlook key constraints in complex solution chains. We propose BiVerify, a dynamically scheduled framework for bidirectional mathematical verification. BiVerify begins with backward verification to screen the answer against the original constraints, followed by forward verification of the reasoning process; subsequent direction switching, state resetting, and re-verification are governed by verification outcomes. Upon failure, the system revises the candidate or verification flow until the same candidate passes both directions. We also introduce shared mathematical tools that separate logical reasoning from precise computation. We systematically evaluate BiVerify on five challenging mathematical reasoning benchmarks using multiple mainstream DeepSeek, GPT, and Gemini models. BiVerify exceeds the strongest representative public baseline evaluated in this work by 13.5 percentage points in average accuracy, with the margin reaching 31.2 percentage points on GPT. Further analysis shows that BiVerify's overall performance exceeds that of either verification direction alone.
  </details>

- **[Cancellation Is Not Enough: What Retries Leave Behind in an Overloaded LLM Engine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08684)**  `arXiv:2611.08684`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xindan Zhang, Fei Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clients and proxies in front of an LLM engine retry calls that time out. After a transient overload, these retries can keep the engine from recovering even when calls arrive below its capacity. Since the engine may keep computing for clients that have left, propagating cancellation looks like the natural remedy. In this paper, we test it on vLLM with overload experiments on three open models, and with scheduler traces of one that follow each client attempt to the execution it creates in the engine. We find that with retries on, cancellation alone did not restore service in any run, and only 12-38% of the calls that arrived after the overload were delivered. The traces suggest why: retries that vLLM ran typically started prefill less than half a second before they timed out. Executions that vLLM later aborted took 79-85% of the prompt tokens it computed, and vLLM's prompt-token counter missed a third to half of all computed prompt tokens. A retry budget restored service by shedding retries but left many of the overload's calls undelivered. Request coalescing delivered every call from the overload onward, some late, unless cancellation had already aborted the running execution. Overload controls should thus be judged by the executions that retries add and the calls that clients receive.
  </details>

- **[LUCA: A Synthetic World for Experimental Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03100)**  `arXiv:2611.03100`  `cs.AI`  
  _Anna Gogleva, Thomas Charman, Martin Balla, Karl Gemayel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  [Note, November 2026: the experimental results in this version are affected by implementation issues in the simulation environment and should not be relied upon pending correction. See the author note on page 1.]An experimental agent can succeed for the wrong reasons. Evaluations, however, often focus on the outcome, not the experiments, evidence, and inferences that produced it. We introduce LUCA, a configurable synthetic world for evaluating this process under fully known ground truth. Agents learn unknown regulatory systems through budgeted experimentation, then predict unseen interventions, diagnose hidden faults, and attempt rescues. This setup allows us to evaluate experiment selection, prediction, diagnosis, and intervention separately.Across seven frontier LLMs and three baselines, these capabilities dissociate sharply. Agents frequently rescue malfunctioning systems while diagnosing the wrong fault; diagnosis varies 7x across models while prediction varies only 1.4x. Models also adopt surprisingly different experimental strategies, and greater exploration does not reliably yield better performance. In one failure mode, agents often perform the experiment needed to distinguish competing causes, but still fail to use the resulting evidence. LUCA turns these hidden failures into measurable behaviours, providing a controlled environment to study where experimental agents succeed and fail. Code:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[SGR-OPSD: Learning from Subgoal Plans with Graph-Regularized Credit Assignment for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02414)**  `arXiv:2611.02414`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shu Liu, Yang Chong, WenShi Chen, Man Lan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  OPSD provides dense supervision for agentic reinforcement learning through privileged information feedback. However, existing privileged information often lacks explicit task-progression planning, while teacher-student log-probability gaps reflect shifts in model preference rather than true task progress. To address these limitations, we propose Subgoal-Guided Graph-Regularized OPSD (SGR-OPSD), a framework that combines subgoal supervision with interaction-derived evidence for turn-level credit assignment. Specifically, SGR-OPSD uses LLM-generated subgoal plans to provide dense action-level guidance, converting them into advantage weights via a constrained, KL-regularized objective. To ground credit in observed outcomes, we construct a Subgoal Progress Graph that estimates empirical returns from sampled transition frequencies. The graph compares empirical action returns against the state average to derive preferences that calibrate the teacher's credit weights. Evaluated on ALFWorld, WebShop, and Search-QA across two model scales (Qwen2.5-3B and 7B), SGR-OPSD consistently outperforms GRPO and strong self-distillation baselines, improving success rates over GRPO by up to 7.6 percentage points and reaching 85.8% on ALFWorld with Qwen2.5-7B. Ablation studies confirm that subgoal guidance and graph-based weight calibration are key to these performance gains.
  </details>

- **[PatentAgentBench: Benchmarking LLM Agents with Professional Patent Task Suites](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35031)**  `arXiv:2610.35031`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yijie Hu, Wei Li, Junjie Zhou, Zhiqi Jiang, Yi Jin, Licong Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Patent practice lies at the intersection of technical, legal, and linguistic work, making it challenging yet crucial to technological innovation. Recently, rapid advances in large language models (LLMs) and agentic systems have demonstrated the potential to complete realistic business workflows and even patent works. This raises a question: can current AI systems perform patent work reliably? Existing benchmarks cover a limited range of patent tasks and simplify evidence acquisition by providing reference materials or candidate documents. In this paper, we introduce PatentAgentBench, a benchmark comprising 510 instances across eight task suites: novelty search, freedom-to-operate (FTO) search, design FTO, invalidity search, claim charting, patent drafting, office-action response, and patent translation. Curated from real patent records and professional materials, the benchmark combines task-specific metrics with expert-reviewed annotations and rubrics. Retrieval tasks such novelty search, FTO, etc., require agents to search and compare patent collections without a supplied candidate pool. The drafting and OAR tasks assess the quality of agents' work given technical disclosure and office action notice. We evaluate seven LLMs across four agent harnesses on PatentAgentBench, finding that current systems still struggle to identify novelty, assess risk, and understand practitioner intent throughout patent workflows.
  </details>

- **[ARCANA: A Benchmark for Abstraction and Analogical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29271)**  `arXiv:2610.29271`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ruqi Chen, Zilei Wang, Wei Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Analogical reasoning is widely considered a fundamental component of human intelligence. Few benchmarks are designed to explicitly test far analogical reasoning - reasoning between domains that are superficially dissimilar yet share deep relational or structural similarities. In this work, we introduce an analogical reasoning benchmark called Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus of ANAlogy (ARCANA). The tasks of ARCANA require performing far analogical mappings from real-world objects or physical phenomena to abstract grid-based transformations, different from the well-known ARC-AGI benchmark which primarily emphasized innate human priors. We validate ARCANA through a human study, demonstrating that humans solve these tasks reliably. In contrast, evaluations across a range of large language models show a distinct performance gap compared to humans, revealing systematic failure modes that remain challenging for current models. To address this gap, we propose ConceptSIR, a reasoning framework based on concept slippage, introspection, and reflection. Experiments demonstrate that ConceptSIR enables standard LLMs to solve several challenging ARCANA tasks that were unsolved by direct prompting in our evaluation. Overall, our results suggest that far analogical reasoning remains an open problem for contemporary LLMs. All the materials are available atthis http URL. The complete codebase, including benchmark, and ConceptSIR, is publicly available atthis https URL. All ARCANA data and task instances can be accessed and explored atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MINDSET: Energy-based Memory Evolution for Long Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08586)**  `arXiv:2610.08586`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sujato Dutta, Sreekruthy Tummala, Shashank Vanga, Ayushmi Pavani, BHVSP Subrahmanyam, Neeraj Choudhary_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term conversational memory requires more than retrieving past dialogue. A system must determine whether new evidence replaces an existing fact, applies in a different context or represents an unresolved contradiction. We introduce MINDSET, a local memory controller that makes these decisions without any LLM calls. It preserves original dialogue episodes and organizes them into provenance-linked, versioned schemas. For each incoming episode, a feasibility-constrained energy objective governs four possible transitions: reinforcement, supersession, contextual branching and new-schema creation. Stability rules prevent isolated conflicts from prematurely displacing established states while version links preserve access to historical evidence. At inference time, retrieval follows these structures back to original dialogue. On a frozen 700-question LoCoMo subset, MINDSET achieves the highest observed answer F1 among six evaluated systems and significantly improves retrieval ranking. Without retuning, it achieves the highest judge accuracy on LongMemEval knowledge-update cases, compared with the other baselines evaluated. Additional experiments with GLM-4.7 and Gemma-4-31B demonstrate compatibility across answer backbones. These results support explicit, non-generative memory-state control as an effective approach to address evolving conversational knowledge.
  </details>

- **[Memadapter: Counterfactual Adaptation Against Memory-induced Sycophancy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05162)**  `arXiv:2610.05162`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ruqing Ning, Haibo Meng, Zhishang Xiang, Zerui Chen, Jinsong Su, Xin Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term memory enables LLM-based agents to retain and reuse information across tasks and sessions, supporting personalization and long-horizon interactions. However, persistent memories can also induce sycophancy, causing agents to over-align with users' historical beliefs even when they are inaccurate, outdated, or inconsistent with objective evidence. Existing mitigation methods assume that memory-induced sycophancy originates from biased or incorrect memories and attempt to reduce this risk by filtering such memories at different stages of the memory pipeline. However, in the real world, objective and correct memories can still induce sycophancy, and the same memory can warrant different influence across different contexts. To this end, we propose MemAdapter, a novel framework that adaptively integrates retrieved memories to support objective and reliable reasoning. Specifically, MemAdapter consists of three components: (i) Counterfactual Induction, which leverages counterfactual reasoning to uncover the potential risk of retrieved memories; (ii) Context-Aware Reflection, which calibrates the inferential influence of each retrieved memory in light of the current task via self-reflection; and (iii) Evidence-Based Reasoning, which grounds the final response in appropriate evidence while preserving the legitimate influence of memory. Extensive experiments on three benchmarks demonstrate that MemAdapter consistently improves memory reliability across diverse scenarios. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Agentic AI with Structured CoT for Enhancing AI's Spatial Intelligence: Visualization and Reasoning of Rotation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04188)**  `arXiv:2610.04188`  `cs.AI`  
  _Uttamasha Monjoree, Wei Yan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent studies show that artificial intelligence (AI) with language and vision capabilities still experiences limitations in spatial reasoning. In this paper, we have studied the spatial capabilities of advanced generative AI to understand the rotations of objects in 3D space, utilizing AI's image processing and language processing features. We trained and examined the spatial intelligence of a generative Agentic AI model (GPT-5.6) to understand the spatial rotation process with rotation diagrams based on the revised Purdue Spatial Visualization Test: Visualization of Rotations (Revised PSVT:R). We improvised the Revised PSVT:R by superimposing additional graphical and contextual features to evaluate how different Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning strategies influence model performance. The results indicate that structured CoT reasoning improves the spatial reasoning performance of the base GPT-5.6 model in both datasets (PSVT:R and PSVT:R with coordinate system). We used three CoT approaches - (1) Structured CoT, (2) few-shot Structured CoT, and Structured CoT with Self-optimized Prompt. The three CoT approaches evaluated in this study showed no significant performance difference. Results showed that combining structured CoT reasoning with relevant contextual information leads to considerable improvements in VLM performance on 3D rotation tasks, demonstrating the potential of agentic AI for more effective spatial reasoning. However, when contextual information is removed, structured CoT reasoning alone provides limited improvement, and the models continue to exhibit notable difficulties in understanding spatial transformations. These findings suggest that effective spatial reasoning in VLMs relies on the integration of visual, textual, and reasoning-based information in future agentic AI systems for spatial intelligence.
  </details>

- **[Build2SPARQL: A Large-Scale Text-to-SPARQL Benchmark Dataset for Building Knowledge Graph Querying](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00224)**  `arXiv:2610.00224`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wooyoung Jung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Building automation systems are increasingly represented as semantic knowledge graphs (KGs) using ontologies such as Brick and ASHRAE 223P, creating a machine-readable substrate for artificial-intelligence applications. One promising application is translating natural-language questions into SPARQL (text-to-SPARQL), which would let building operators query these graphs through language agents, but progress is limited by the scarcity of large natural-language/SPARQL benchmarks. This paper presents Build2SPARQL, a large-scale benchmark for building KGs generated by a KG-grounded pipeline: SPARQL queries are produced and validated entirely by graph-traversal code, while large language models generate only the natural-language questions, keeping query correctness independent of model behavior. The pipeline mines six query-pattern families -- linear chains, branching, UNION, aggregation, OPTIONAL, and attribute-filtered -- and phrases each query across five vocabulary registers. Applied to 201 building KGs (180 Brick, 21 ASHRAE 223P), it yields 6,136 executable SPARQL queries and 30,680 questions. A two-rater human validation of 300 questions found 98.8% semantic fidelity, 98.8% naturalness, and 84.0% operational plausibility. A retrieval-augmented evaluation across three open-weight language models raised exact-match accuracy from 0.2-20% (zero-shot) to 56-65% (three-shot retrieved).
  </details>

- **[Externalized CPDAG Summaries Improve LLM Causal Deduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31071)**  `arXiv:2609.31071`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wentao Sun, João Paulo Nogueira, Dominique Verchere, Mathieu Acher, Alonso Silva_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Observational data rarely identify a single causal structure. Such structures are typically modeled as directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) whose arrows point from direct causes to their effects, and observed correlations and conditional independencies usually leave a whole family of DAGs that fit the data equally well. A causal claim follows from the data only if it holds in every one of them. Classical causal discovery methods such as the Peter--Clark (PC) algorithm summarize this family as a completed partially directed acyclic graph (CPDAG): an edge is directed when every compatible graph orients it the same way, and undirected when the data leave its direction open. On the Corr2Cause benchmark, large language models (LLMs) that reason in free text often lose track of this family and decide from a few local edges. We propose \emph{Structured Thinking}, a two-turn method in which the model first summarizes the CPDAG in a constrained schema and then checks the claim against that summary. Without fine-tuning or an external solver, it raises Qwen3.5-27B from $77.5$ to $86.5$ F$_1$ (mean of three seeds) over a baseline that receives explicit PC-algorithm instructions, whereas a second turn of free-text analysis does worse than that baseline. The gain holds on test splits with renamed variables or paraphrased hypotheses and on other models. Given only the graph and the claim, the model's answer flips when the graph is replaced by a valid CPDAG under which the claim has the opposite truth value. Moreover, replacing the model's graphs with reference CPDAGs on a fixed subset shows that most remaining errors arise in building the graph rather than in reading it. Making causal structure explicit can help LLMs reason more reliably, provided the intermediate representation is itself trustworthy.
  </details>

- **[REVISE: Validity-Guided Recovery for Online Revisions in Agent Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00643)**  `arXiv:2609.00643`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ruoling Qi, Xuaner Wu, Penghang Liu, Jian Chen, Yirui Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent revisions expose a fundamental correctness--efficiency trade-off during concurrent execution. Discarding ongoing work preserves latest-version correctness but wastes progress that may remain valid, whereas reusing prior work preserves efficiency but risks propagating stale state into outputs and tool effects. Existing recovery strategies resolve this trade-off in an imbalanced way with coarse-grained policies: they either favor efficiency by allowing potentially stale work to continue, or favor correctness by restarting the workflow or recomputing a linear suffix from the earliest conflict, thereby discarding unaffected progress. We present \textsc{Revise}, a validity-guided runtime for fine-grained recovery in structured agent workflows. When a revision arrives, \textsc{Revise} first intersects its delta with recorded data and control dependencies and propagates the resulting impact through the partially executed DAG to identify affected work. It then stops invalid work, preserves validity-established progress beyond the earliest conflict, and recomputes only the affected region. Incomplete provenance conservatively expands recovery, while reused results are revalidated before commit. Analysis of real coding-agent traces show online recovery opportunities: 118 sessions retain observable work before a queued later message is delivered; across 167 overlapping assistant responses, enqueue-to-completion overlap reaches 56.55~s at p95. Across 300 challenging revision/commit executions, \textsc{Revise} matches a latest-version oracle with no stale outputs or effects. On unmodified LangGraph and LLMCompiler applications using Qwen3-14B, it reduces model calls by 40.6--56.0\% relative to full restart and by 31.3--43.6\% relative to suffix recomputation. Under serving pressure, it further reduces revision-to-correct-completion tokens by 13.26\% and improves SLO goodput by 3.07--5.43\%.
  </details>

- **[RISE-RL: Rubric-Informed Selective Exploration for Open-Ended Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09123)**  `arXiv:2608.09123`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jinkun Hou, Zhuo Liu, Huimin Ren, Kuan Zhang, Zirong Chen, Hongsheng Xin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aligning Large Language Models (LLMs) for open-ended tasks is challenging because responses must satisfy multidimensional criteria without following a single correct generation trajectory. Existing rubric-based reinforcement learning (RL) methods compress fine-grained criterion-level feedback into scalar rewards, making persistent capability gaps difficult to target under limited on-policy exploration. We propose $\textbf{RISE-RL}$ (Rubric-Informed Selective Exploration), which uses repeatedly missed rubric criteria to elicit privileged trajectories that are difficult to discover through unguided exploration alone. RISE-RL retains only trajectories whose complete-rubric reward exceeds the mean reward of natural rollouts, and then re-evaluates them under the original prompt to emphasize behaviors that remain weakly supported by the natural policy. The resulting guidance signal is optimized through a separate auxiliary objective and removed once its additional benefit diminishes. Experiments with 4B and 14B models across writing, chat, health, and science show that RISE-RL achieves the highest mean score on every evaluated benchmark under guidance-free evaluation. Compared with standard Rubric-RL, it improves the average score by 1.3 points at the 4B scale and $\textbf{3.3 points at the 14B scale}$, including a $\textbf{6.0-point}$ gain on CreativeWriting-V3. It also improves creative-writing diversity and yields gains on objectively scored medical and scientific benchmarks. These results indicate that selective internalization through reward filtering and policy support shaping is effective for open-ended reinforcement learning.
  </details>

- **[Analysis of Inverse Relation Directionality in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03512)**  `arXiv:2608.03512`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sefika Efeoglu, Adrian Paschke_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have achieved strong performance on text-to-knowledge graph generation and related tasks. Nevertheless, it is still unclear whether they accurately model the direction-dependent semantics of inverse relations, in which reversing the order of the arguments alters the meaning of a relation (e.g., \textit{mother} versus \textit{child}). To the best of our knowledge, this work presents the first systematic study of inverse relation directionality in LLMs, using a benchmark consisting of 5,457 instances spanning 27 distinct inverse relation labels. We evaluate five open-source LLMs under a multiple-choice prompting framework and further examine the influence of relation descriptions and entity representations by substituting the original entities with synthetic and masked entities. Our findings reveal systematic asymmetries in inverse relation classification across LLMs, indicate that relation descriptions do not consistently improve performance, and show that model performance can be sensitive to variations in entity representations.
  </details>

- **[AMTFV: Agentic Mathematical Tool-Flow Verification for LLM Self-Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29549)**  `arXiv:2607.29549`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rui Zou, Yutao Zhu, Mengqi Wei, Ji-Rong Wen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models have demonstrated strong mathematical problem-solving capabilities, yet reliably verifying their candidate answers remains challenging. Existing representative methods mainly revise outputs through natural-language reflection or assist verification by directly generating verification programs; the former may not reliably support exact computation, whereas the latter prematurely couples mathematical modeling with low-level implementation. We propose AMTFV (Agentic Mathematical Tool-Flow Verification). By introducing Mathematical Tool Flow (MTF) as an interrupt--execute--resume interface, AMTFV decouples verification modeling from concrete execution and supports exact computation through a mathematical toolbox. Specifically, the verification agent first constructs a verification workflow, encodes the mathematical objects and computational intent requiring reliable execution in an MTF request, and sends it to the mathematical toolbox agent. The latter parses the request, generates executable calls, and dispatches them to the backend for exact computation. Tool outputs then support candidate-answer adjudication, answer revision, and verification-workflow revision. We evaluate AMTFV on five challenging mathematical reasoning datasets with seven model configurations from DeepSeek, GPT, and Gemini. Experimental results show that AMTFV outperforms the representative baselines evaluated in this study overall; under an individual model configuration, it improves average accuracy over the strongest baseline by up to 8.3 percentage points, with larger gains on samples of medium and high verification complexity.
  </details>

- **[CADENCE: A Cardiac Atom Dictionary for Interpretable Neural Concept Extraction from ECG Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25244)**  `arXiv:2607.25244`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yixuan Duan, Arjun Naik, Sadeer Al-Kindi, Wei Qiu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Foundation models for 12-lead electrocardiograms (ECGs) transfer well across clinical tasks, but the physiological knowledge encoded in their representations remains opaque. We present CADENCE, a framework that decomposes an ECG foundation model into a human-interpretable, queryable dictionary of physiological concepts. Using a BatchTopK sparse autoencoder, CADENCE factorizes Layer-6 embeddings from more than nine million ECG tokens into 8,192 sparse cardiac atoms. These atoms align better than individual dense embedding dimensions with clinical phenotypes and waveform morphology, recovering arrhythmias, conduction abnormalities, infarction and repolarization patterns, chamber and axis findings, and lead- and beat-phase-specific waveform primitives. At Layer 6, the best atoms achieve mean AUROCs of 0.88 for clinical phenotypes and 0.90 for morphology, versus 0.78 and 0.83 for the best dense dimensions. Sparse atom probes match or outperform dense probes for phenotype, morphology, and age prediction while attributing each prediction to a small set of interpretable atoms; phenotype AUROC improves from 0.93 to 0.95. Atom-space geometry recovers physiologically coherent relationships, and targeted atom ablation selectively changes frozen downstream outputs. An automated LLM pipeline generates and quantitatively validates atom descriptions by predicting held-out activations. On independent external ECG datasets, CADENCE recovers overlapping concepts and maintains consistent phenotype-prediction performance. CADENCE provides a scalable framework for discovering and auditing the physiological knowledge encoded by ECG foundation models.
  </details>

- **[Agentic Neural Architecture Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07984)**  `arXiv:2607.07984`  `cs.AI`  
  _Seokhoon Jeong, Mijung Kim, Taehwan Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural architecture search (NAS) methods have grown increasingly efficient, but they still operate within manually designed search spaces that require domain expertise and must be rebuilt for each new task. Large language models (LLMs) can write architectures in an open-ended code space, yet it is unclear how the work should be divided between LLM-driven design and NAS-driven search. We propose a way to combine the two. An LLM first designs a seed architecture and then decomposes it into a slotted architecture: a scaffold with named, interchangeable module slots that defines a bounded, task-specific search space, which a conventional NAS algorithm then explores without any manual engineering. We implement this in AgentNAS, a three-phase pipeline whose phases can be evaluated separately. On 17 tasks from NAS-Bench-360 and Unseen NAS, covering classification, dense regression, segmentation, and multi-label tagging, AgentNAS outperforms published baselines, including task-specific expert designs, on 11 tasks. Extensive ablation studies show that LLMs find strong architectures quickly but soon plateau, and that switching to NAS at this point yields further gains, indicating that the two approaches are complementary. Experiments with five LLMs show that seed architecture design is effective across all of them, whereas constructing a usable search space requires a minimum level of model capability. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[LLM-as-a-Tutor: Policy-Aware Prompt Adaptation for Non-Verifiable RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04412)**  `arXiv:2607.04412`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yujin Kim, Namgyu Ho, Sangmin Hwang, Joonkee Kim, Yongjin Yang, Sangmin Bae, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) for non-verifiable instruction following increasingly relies on LLM judges with prompt-specific rubrics as reward signals. While recent methods adapt these rubrics to the evolving policy during training, the training prompts themselves remain static, drawn from fixed corpora. This static approach often results in a critical misalignment between prompt difficulty and policy capability, leaving the judge unable to recover a discriminative reward signal when prompts fail to elicit quality variance among rollouts. To address this misalignment, we introduce LLM-as-a-Tutor, a framework that extends the LLM's role from judge to tutor: a single model serves as an examiner that pairwise-compares policy rollouts to detect non-challenging prompts, and as a generator that appends atomic constraints to them. This append-only design preserves the original prompt while adding constraints to increase difficulty in step with the policy's capability, producing a self-calibrating training signal without external difficulty schedules. On three complex instruction-following benchmarks, our method outperforms both policy-unaware baselines and prior policy-adaptive methods that adapt rubrics or rewrite prompts, on average and on four of five metrics, suggesting prompt adaptation as an underexplored axis of policy-awareness in non-verifiable RL for constraint-heavy instruction following.
  </details>

- **[Adversarial Concept Search: Predicting Compositional Errors From Feature Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13934)**  `arXiv:2606.13934`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jennifer Meng Lu, Ruochen Zhang, Isabelle Lee, David Alvarez-Melis, Ellie Pavlick, Naomi Saphra_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans cannot always intuit what scenarios are most challenging to LLMs. Hoping to capture challenging edge cases, developers either design problems to be difficult for humans or curate extensive benchmarks. What if we could instead anticipate which scenarios a model will fail on? In this paper, we use an LLM's representational geometry to predict which concept combinations it will fail on. We attribute this compositional failure to interference between salient features. In tasks that require systematic composition - toy programmatic settings, multihop reasoning, multilingual factual recall - we find that when a pair of concepts is encoded near-orthogonally, the model reliably composes them. When their linear encodings are close, producing interference, the model fails to compose them. Our method reliably anticipates failure modes across different compositional tasks, without evaluating specific inputs. These results lay the groundwork to use representational geometry to identify high-risk examples, construct targeted stress tests, and provide a scalable foundation for active learning in real-world deployment.
  </details>

- **[OmniToM: Benchmarking Theory of Mind in LLMs via Explicit Belief Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26322)**  `arXiv:2605.26322`  `cs.AI`  
  _Adam Bawatneh, Sagar Sapkota, Amrit Singh Bedi, Santu Karmaker, Mubarak Shah_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Theory of Mind (ToM), the ability to infer others' knowledge, intentions, and emotions, is commonly evaluated in large language models (LLMs) using end-point question answering, where performance is judged solely by the final answer to a social reasoning query. This paradigm obscures whether the model actually constructs the underlying mental-state representations required for robust reasoning, particularly in scenarios involving divergent, evolving, or mistaken beliefs. In order to address this research gap, we introduce OmniToM, a benchmark that directly evaluates these representations by requiring explicit modeling of belief structures for all relevant actors within a narrative. These structures are composed of belief propositions: minimal statements of what an actor takes to be true about the world or another actor's mental state, allowing knowledge, intentions, emotions, and false beliefs to be analyzed in a common format. Models are evaluated in two stages: Stage 1: Belief Extraction, which extracts from the story the beliefs relevant to its social dynamics, and Stage 2: Belief Labeling, which assigns each belief a seven-dimensional schema label covering recursive order, truth status, knowledge access, explicitness, content type, mental source, and context. Built from 895 stories from the existing ToMBench story corpus and augmented with 22,343 labeled belief propositions, OmniToM uses a human-calibrated LLM-assisted annotation pipeline. Across diverse models in zero-shot evaluation, OmniToM reveals an actor-specific belief-tracking bottleneck: current LLMs struggle with the knowledge-access and representational decisions required to transform narrative facts into actors' beliefs and shared mental states.
  </details>

- **[Scaling Retrieval-Augmented Reasoning with Parallel Search and Explicit Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13534)**  `arXiv:2605.13534`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiabei Liu, Wenyu Mao, Junfei Tan, Chunxu Shen, Lingling Yi, Jiancan Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep search agents have proven effective in enhancing LLMs by retrieving external knowledge during multi-step reasoning. However, existing methods often generate a single query for retrieval at each reasoning step, limiting information coverage and introducing high noise. This may result in low signal-to-noise ratios (SNR) during search, degrading reasoning accuracy and leading to unnecessary reasoning steps. In this paper, we introduce MultiSearch, an RL-based framework that addresses these limitations through multi-query retrieval and explicit merging of retrieved information. At each reasoning step, MultiSearch generates queries from multiple perspectives and retrieves external information in parallel, expanding the scope of relevant information and mitigating the reliance on any single retrieval result. Then, the agent consolidates and refines retrieved information at the merging process, improving the SNR and ensuring more accurate reasoning. Additionally, we propose a reinforcement learning framework with a multi-process reward design to optimize agents for both multi-query retrieval and information consolidation. Extensive experiments on seven benchmarks demonstrate that MultiSearch outperforms baseline methods, enhancing the SNR of retrieval and improving reasoning performance in question-answering tasks.
  </details>

- **[PDEAgent-Bench: A Multi-Metric, Multi-Library Benchmark for PDE Solver Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09636)**  `arXiv:2605.09636`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhen Hang, Yushan Yashengjiang, Junhui Li, Huanshuo Dong, Yang Wei, Zhezheng Hao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  PDE-to-solver code generation aims to automatically synthesize executable numerical solvers from partial differential equation (PDE) specifications. This task requires not only understanding the mathematical structure of PDEs, but also selecting appropriate discretization schemes and solver configurations, and correctly implementing the resulting formulations in finite-element method (FEM) libraries. Existing code generation benchmarks mainly evaluate syntactic correctness, or success on predefined test cases. To our knowledge, there is currently no publicly available benchmark specifically for PDE-to-solver code generation, and general-purpose code benchmarks do not fully capture the unique challenges of numerical PDE solution, such as ensuring solver accuracy, efficiency, and compatibility with professional FEM libraries. We introduce PDEAgent-Bench, to the best of our knowledge, the first multi-metric, multi-library benchmark for PDE-to-solver code generation. PDEAgent-Bench contains 645 instances across 6 mathematical categories and 11 PDE families, with common FEM libraries for DOLFINx, Firedrake, andthis http URL. Each instance provides an agent-facing problem specification, a reference solution on a prescribed evaluation grid, and case-specific accuracy and runtime targets. PDEAgent-Bench adopts a staged evaluation framework in which generated solvers must sequentially pass executability, numerical accuracy, and computational efficiency checks. Experiments with representative LLMs and code agents show that models can often produce runnable code, but their pass rate drops substantially once accuracy and efficiency requirements are enforced. These results indicate that current agents remain limited in producing numerically reliable and efficient PDE solvers, and that PDEAgent-Bench provides a reproducible testbed grounded in the practical requirements of numerical PDE solving.
  </details>

- **[Joint Consistency: A Unified Test-Time Aggregation Framework via Energy Minimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06219)**  `arXiv:2605.06219`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yunzhen Yao, Hongye Wang, Yahong Wang, Michael C. Gastpar, Bo Jiang, Lie He_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper studies test-time aggregation, an approach that generates multiple reasoning traces and aggregates them into a final answer. Most existing methods rely on evaluation signals collected from candidate traces in isolation or answer frequencies, while ignoring comparative interactions among candidates. We propose Joint Consistency (JC), formulated as a constrained Ising-type energy minimization problem, which employs both independent evaluation signals and pairwise comparisons using LLM-as-a-judge. JC provides a unified framework for test-time aggregation that subsumes typical voting and weighted aggregation methods as special cases. Moreover, we develop an efficient approximation strategy that makes interaction modeling practical for large-scale test-time aggregation. Experiments on math and code reasoning benchmarks show that JC outperforms existing baselines across tasks, judge models, trace budgets, and trace-generation settings, with particularly pronounced gains on challenging problems.
  </details>

- **[CODE-SHARP: Continuous Open-ended Discovery and Evolution of Skills as Hierarchical Reward Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.10085)**  `arXiv:2602.10085`  `cs.AI`  
  _Richard Bornemann, Pierluigi Vito Amadori, Antoine Cully_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A core quality of general intelligence is the ability to open-endedly expand and evolve its set of mastered skills autonomously. While recent Foundation Model (FM) driven approaches have shown promising results towards this goal, they typically rely on significant human-in-the-loop engineering, limiting their transferability to novel environments. To address this, we introduce Continuous Open-ended Discovery and Evolution of Skills as Hierarchical Reward Programs (CODE-SHARP), a framework that leverages FMs to open-endedly grow and evolve an archive of Python programs encoding skills to train a generalist agent policy entirely from scratch via reinforcement learning, directly from source code. These programs, termed Skills as Hierarchical Reward Programs (SHARPs), each encode a local success condition and a set of prerequisites delegated to previously discovered SHARPs. At runtime, SHARPs dynamically route the agent through their prerequisite chain based on the current state, rewarding each completion along the way, requiring the agent to learn only the marginal behaviour each new SHARP introduces, enabling efficient learning of long-horizon skills without any pre-defined rewards. On Craftax-Classic and XLand-MiniGrid, agents trained fully autonomously by CODE-SHARP outperform previous works by 6x and 2.7x in median performance and are the only agents capable of crafting iron tools and mining diamonds. On AI2-THOR, CODE-SHARP achieves 66.7% median success versus 0% for ELLM and OMNI, using only the initial environment state instead of the full source code. Scaled to Craftax-Extended, CODE-SHARP trains a generalist agent on over 90 discovered SHARPs, enabling the agent to solve challenging long-horizon tasks zero-shot, matching agents trained on ground-truth rewards.
  </details>

- **[PieArena: Ranking and Profiling Language Agents in Realistic Negotiation Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05302)**  `arXiv:2602.05302`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chris Zhu, Sasha Cui, Will Sanok Dufallo, Runzhi Jin, Zhen Xu, Linjun Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present an in-depth evaluation of LLMs' ability to negotiate, a central business task that requires strategic reasoning and theory of mind to create and claim economic value. To do so, we introduce PieArena, a large-scale negotiation benchmark grounded in multi-agent interactions over realistic scenarios adapted from MBA negotiation courses at an elite business school. We evaluate language agents across three pairing regimes: mirror-play, cross-play, and human-LM play. We develop a ranking model for continuous negotiation payoffs that yields order-invariant, uncertainty-quantified leaderboards while correcting for systematic experimental asymmetries. We further study the effects of shared-intentionality agentic scaffolding and find asymmetric gains, with large improvements for mid- and lower-tier LMs and diminishing returns for frontier LMs. As calibration anchors, we collect human-human and human-LM negotiation data from trained business school students, finding that a representative frontier language agent matches or exceeds this human baseline in our evaluation settings. Beyond deal outcomes, PieArena provides a multi-dimensional behavioral profile that reveals cross-model heterogeneity in instruction compliance, computation accuracy, as well as judge-assessed deception and reputation, illustrating the value of evaluation beyond outcome-only leaderboards.
  </details>

- **[Advancing ESG Intelligence: An Expert-level Agent and Comprehensive Benchmark for Sustainable Finance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.08676)**  `arXiv:2601.08676`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yilei Zhao, Wentao Zhang, Lei Xiao, Yandan Zheng, Mengpu Liu, Wei Yang Bryan Lim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Environmental, social, and governance (ESG) criteria are essential for evaluating corporate sustainability and ethical performance. However, professional ESG analysis is hindered by data fragmentation across unstructured sources, and existing large language models (LLMs) often struggle with the complex, multi-step workflows required for rigorous auditing. To address these limitations, we introduce ESGAgent, a hierarchical multi-agent system empowered by a specialized toolset, including retrieval augmentation, web search and domain-specific functions, to generate in-depth ESG analysis. Complementing this agentic system, we present a comprehensive three-level benchmark derived from 310 corporate sustainability reports, designed to evaluate capabilities ranging from atomic common-sense questions to the generation of integrated, in-depth analysis. Empirical evaluations demonstrate that ESGAgent outperforms state-of-the-art closed-source LLMs with an average accuracy of 84.15% on atomic question-answering tasks, and excels in professional report generation by integrating rich charts and verifiable references.
