These findings confirm the diagnostic value of our benchmark, establishing it as a vital testbed for assessing general and advanced agentic capabilities in high-stakes vertical domains.
  </details>

- **[An Empirical Investigation of Robustness in Large Language Models under Tabular Distortions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.05009)**  `arXiv:2601.05009`  `cs.AI`  
  _Avik Dutta, Harshit Nigam, Hosein Hasanbeig, Arjun Radhakrishna, Sumit Gulwani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Distortions are a natural consequence of tables collected from real-world sources, where scraping, extraction, or manual intervention can introduce semantic and structural inconsistencies. Humans can often identify and mentally correct such errors autonomously before reasoning over the data. In this work, we investigate whether Large Language Models (LLMs) possess a similar capability by evaluating them on 506 distorted table question answering (TQA) samples derived from canonical tables. Our findings reveal systematic weaknesses in current LLM pre-training paradigms, where models exhibit a strong inductive bias toward assuming tables are well-formed, often overlooking distortions or discarding inconsistent entries instead of repairing them. Even frontier models like GPT-5.4 and Opus-4.6 achieve only 68% accuracy under distortion. While distortion-aware prompting partially mitigates these failures, errors persist. Through lightweight finetuning, we demonstrate that models can learn to autonomously detect and repair human-recoverable distorted tables.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Multi-Step Legal Reasoning and Analyzing Chain-of-Thought Effects in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.07979)**  `arXiv:2511.07979`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wenhan Yu, Xinbo Lin, Lanxin Ni, Jinhua Cheng, Lei Sha_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong reasoning abilities across specialized domains, motivating research into their application to legal reasoning. However, existing legal benchmarks often conflate factual recall with genuine inference, fragment the reasoning process, and overlook the quality of reasoning. To address these limitations, we introduce MSLR, the first Chinese multi-step legal reasoning dataset grounded in real-world judicial decision making. MSLR adopts the IRAC framework (Issue, Rule, Application, Conclusion) to model structured expert reasoning from official legal documents. In addition, we design a scalable Human-LLM collaborative annotation pipeline that efficiently produces fine-grained step-level reasoning annotations and provides a reusable methodological framework for multi-step reasoning datasets. Evaluation of multiple LLMs on MSLR shows only moderate performance, highlighting the challenges of adapting to complex legal reasoning. Further experiments demonstrate that Self-Initiated Chain-of-Thought prompts generated by models autonomously improve reasoning coherence and quality, outperforming human-designed prompts. MSLR contributes to advancing LLM reasoning and Chain-of-Thought strategies and offers open resources for future research. The dataset and code are available atthis https URLandthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Large Language Modelsfor Injury Surveillance: Evidence from E-Bike Incidents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04654)**  `arXiv:2506.04654`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhichao Yang, Faeze Momeni Rad, Jiashu He, Mohammad B. Al-Khasawneh Darshan Pandit, Cinzia Cirillo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This study analyzes e-bike-related injury incidents using two complementary data sources: the Consumer Product Safety Risk Management System (CPSRMS) and the National Electronic Injury Surveillance System (NEISS). We develop an LLM-assisted framework that converts unstructured incident narratives into structured variables, notably human-, equipment-, and environment-related contributing factors, for use alongside demographic, locational, temporal, and weather-derived predictors. To improve cross-source comparability, injury severity is harmonized into three ordered categories. Separate ordered logit models are estimated for CPSRMS and NEISS, together with two supplementary joint scaled specifications. In CPSRMS, human-related factors are associated with higher injury severity, whereas equipment- and environment-related factors, riders under age 18, spring incidents, and recreation/sport locations are associated with lower severity categories. In NEISS, riders under age 18 and female riders are associated with lower treatment severity, whereas riders above age 59 are associated with higher severity. Likelihood-ratio tests support selected coefficient-sharing restrictions but reject equality of the source thresholds. The findings demonstrate that narrative-rich incident reports and standardized emergency-department surveillance data capture complementary rather than directly interchangeable dimensions of e-bike safety. The proposed framework illustrates how LLM-derived variables can be integrated with econometric models to support injury surveillance, safety intervention design, and evidence-based micromobility policy.
  </details>

- **[NeuronGen: Neural Network Parameter Generation via Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.12470)**  `arXiv:2505.12470`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiaqi Wang, Yusen Zhang, Xi Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Acquiring the parameters of neural networks (NNs) has been one of the most important problems in machine learning since the inception of NNs. Traditional approaches, such as backpropagation and forward-only optimization, acquire parameters via iterative data fitting to gradually optimize them. This paper aims to explore the feasibility of a new direction: acquiring NN parameters via large language model generation. We propose NeuronGen, a generalized and easy-to-implement two-stage approach for NN parameter generation conditioned on descriptions of the data, task, and network architecture. Stage one is Parameter Reference Knowledge Injection, where LLMs are pretrained on NN checkpoints to build foundational understanding of parameter space, whereas stage two is Context-Enhanced Instruction Tuning, enabling LLMs to adapt to specific tasks through enriched, task-aware prompts. Experimental results demonstrate that NeuronGen effectively generates usable NN parameters. Our findings highlight the feasibility of LLM-based NN parameter generation and suggest a promising new paradigm where LLMs and lightweight NNs can coexist synergistically
  </details>
